# What the Dead Remember

*A Star Wars story*

A work of fan fiction. Star Wars and all associated characters and settings belong to Lucasfilm and Disney. No infringement is intended; no money is made.



# Part One: The Returned



## Chapter 1: The Weight of Breath

The deck was tilting. That was the first thing Anakin understood and the last thing that made any sense: that the floor of the hangar bay was going over under him like the deck of a sea-ship, and that it was not the floor at all but the suit, the whole black tonnage of him, listing in the boy's arms while the station shook itself to pieces around them.

He had never known how heavy he was. He had carried it for twenty-three years and never once felt it, because the servos did the carrying; now the servos were dying one by one down the length of his legs and the weight came back to him joint by joint, as if his body were being handed to him in instalments. His son had him under the arms. His son was dragging him. The boy's boots skidded on the plating, found purchase, skidded again. Somewhere behind them a conduit let go with a sound like tearing cloth and the air went hot and then cold.

The respirator was failing. He heard it before he felt it: the in-breath coming a half-beat late, the out-breath ragged at the end, the machine in his chest hunting for a rhythm it could no longer find. Lightning had done that. It had gone through the suit's circuitry the way water goes through sand, and the lights on the chest-plate were guttering green, amber, nothing. Each breath was now a thing the machine did badly for him, and he could feel, under the doing, the old ruined lungs trying to help and not being able to.

Luke got him to the foot of the ramp and could get him no further. The boy knelt. His face was very close, and it was still red; everything was still red, the red of the lenses, twenty-three years of a world the colour of a wound.

Anakin asked for the mask.

He did not have the breath to say it well. He said it in pieces, and the boy argued, as he had known the boy would argue, because the boy was Padmé's and had her way of standing in front of a thing that was going to happen and telling it no. And then the boy stopped arguing, because he was also Anakin's, and understood a lost cause when he was holding one.

The seals went one at a time. There was a pressure in the ears, a sound like a held breath let out, and then the boy lifted the helmet away and the air of the hangar came onto his face.

It was cold. That was all, at first. He had forgotten cold as a thing that could touch skin. It smelled of ozone and burnt insulation and the sweat of the boy who was holding him, and it moved; air moved; he had not felt air move on his face since a river of fire. His eyes watered. He thought it was the air and then knew it was not.

And there was the boy's face. Not red. Not through a lens. The colour of it, the plain colour of a human face in bad light, tired and dirty and twenty-three years old, with his mother's mouth and a cut above one eyebrow that had bled and dried. Blue eyes. He had not known the boy's eyes were blue. He had seen them through the mask a dozen times, across the carbon-freezing chamber, across a gantry at Bespin, across the throne room an hour ago, and the mask had made them black. They were blue. They were wet. The boy was looking at him as though he were something worth looking at.

He had so much to say and no breath to say it with. He had the boy's hand; he had taken that hand once, on a gantry, with a blade. He tried to say that he knew that. He tried to say the boy's mother's name. What came out was smaller and truer, and it was all he could carry.

"You were right about me," he said. "Tell your sister... you were right."

The boy said something. Anakin did not hear it. The hangar was going white at the edges, and the white was not the lights, which were failing, and not the fires, which were orange. It came from no direction. It came up through the deck and down through the ruined ceiling and in through the boy's hand, and it was warm, and it did not hurt, and for the first time since Mustafar he was not afraid of anything.

He took a breath. The machine did not help him with it. It was his own.

He let it out.

* * *

He was breathing.

In, and out. The in-breath and the out-breath, and nothing doing them for him.

For a long time that was the only fact. He lay in the dark and listened for the machine and the machine did not come. There was no hiss at the top of the breath, no click at the bottom, no faint whine of the pump that had been the floor of every silence he had known since he was twenty-three. There was only air going into him and air coming out, and the sound it made was a soft, wet, animal sound, the sound a sleeping creature makes, and it was coming from his own mouth.

Something was wrong with the respirator. He reached for the control on his chest-plate.

His fingers closed on cloth.

Cloth, warm, a little damp. Under the cloth a chest that rose and fell. Under the chest, a heart going fast. He pressed his hand flat against it and felt the ribs, each one, and felt the hand doing the pressing, and lay very still.

Fingers. He had fingers. He lifted the hand in the dark and could not see it, but he could feel it: the five of them, the joints, the small pull of tendon when he closed them. He closed them. He opened them. He brought the hand to his face and touched his mouth, and it was a mouth, lips, dry; and above it a nose, and the skin of a cheek, and no seam anywhere, no edge where the mask bit, no ridge of grafted hide. He touched his own eyelids. They were soft. He had not touched his own face in twenty-three years and he did it now the way a blind man reads, slowly, by the fingertips, and when he came to the hair at his temple it was hair, short and warm and sticking up with sleep.

The other hand. He was afraid of the other hand, and he lifted it anyway, and it was a hand.

He lay with both hands held up in the dark above his face, as a child does, and the breath went in and out of him.

He thought: I am dead. This is what comes after. He had been promised nothing and had expected less, and if what came after was a dark room and two hands and no pain, then it was more than he had any right to, and he would lie here and be grateful and not ask.

But a dead man did not need to piss. His mouth was dry in the specific way of a mouth that has slept with its lips parted, and there was an itch at the back of his neck where something fine and plaited lay against the skin, and the body he was in had a bladder and a dry tongue and an itch, and the body was not negotiable.

He put a hand to the back of his neck and found the braid.

Thin. Tight-plaited. Running from behind the right ear down over the collarbone, with a bead in it that his fingers knew before he did. He held it. He had cut it off himself once, in a Council chamber, and handed it to Obi-Wan; and before that he had worn it for ten years and hated it for most of them and pulled at it when he was thinking, exactly as his fingers were pulling at it now.

The breath went in. The breath went out.

He made himself do the rest of the catalogue, because he had been a soldier and a soldier inventories the body before he moves it. Legs: he sent the thought down and the legs were there. Two of them, long, lying under a thin blanket, and when he told the toes to move the toes moved, and he felt the blanket against the skin of his shins. Skin. The shins had skin. He put a hand down under the blanket and ran it along one thigh and it was a thigh, bony, a little hairy, cool at the knee, and nowhere on it was there a port or a socket or a ridge of scar. He ran the hand up over the belly, which was flat and hollow with hunger, over the ribs, over the chest again, and nowhere was there the hard plastoid shell, and nowhere was there a tube.

Lungs. He drew the deepest breath he could, down to the bottom, past the place where for twenty-three years the breath had stopped against a wall of ruined tissue. There was no wall. The air went all the way down and the ribs spread to take it and nothing tore, and he held it, and let it go, and the sound it made going was a sob, and he let that go too.

There was no pain.

That was a sensation in itself. He had not known it could be. For twenty-three years pain had been the room he lived in: the burns that never finished healing under the suit, the grind of the prosthetics in the stumps, the ache in the chest from the pump, the headaches from the lenses; a constant low noise, so constant that he had stopped hearing it the way one stops hearing a ventilator. Now the noise had stopped, and the silence where it had been was so loud that he lay rigid in it, listening, waiting for it to come back.

It did not come back.

He turned his head. The pillow was cool against his cheek, which was a thing a pillow had not been for a very long time, and there was a smell to it, and the smell undid him more than the hands had.

Stone. Old, dry, faintly warm stone, the kind that has been breathed on by ten thousand years of people sitting still. Under it the recycled air of a vast building, with its mineral tang, and under that, so faint he would not have been sure of it if he had not known it with his whole childhood, a green note, damp, of growing things and moving water, carried through a kilometre of ductwork from the Room of a Thousand Fountains. Incense, days old, soaked into the plaster. Starch from a tunic folded over a chair. And his own sweat, which was the sweat of a boy, sharp and thin and nothing like the sweat that had cooked inside the suit.

The Jedi Temple.

He had burned it. He had walked in through the front gate with a legion at his back and gone up the Processional Way with his blade already lit, and the stone had smelled of this, and then of smoke, and then of other things. He knew exactly what this building smelled like when it was dying. It was not dying now. It was asleep, and he was in it, and he was in a bed.

The bed was narrow. He stretched and his feet found the end of it and his head the other, and that was the measure of him: he fitted. There was a ceiling close above, a slit of window to his left with the city's light coming through it in a dull orange bar, and in the bar of light he could just make out a chest, a chair, a tunic over the chair, the shape of a datapad on a shelf. Two small rooms off a common room. The Padawan's room was the one nearer the door.

He knew where he was now. He did not yet dare to know when.

* * *

He felt Obi-Wan before he thought of him.

It was at the back of his mind, where it had always been, where there had been nothing for so long that he had forgotten the shape of the absence: a presence, a mood, a weather. Asleep. Thirty paces away, through two walls, lying on his side as he always lay, dreaming something mild and orderly. There was no thought in it. There never had been; it had never carried words. It carried warmth, the way a wall carries the warmth of a room on the other side, and it was carrying warmth now, into the dark, into him, the way it had every night of the ten years he had been a boy in this building, so that he had never once in those years lain awake and been alone.

Obi-Wan.

Alive, and asleep, and thirty paces away.

The last time he had felt him through anything was on a bank of black rock, with the river roaring, when the bond between them had not broken so much as burned, and in the burning he had felt Obi-Wan feel it, had felt the exact moment Obi-Wan turned away. After that, nothing, for nineteen years. Then one flicker, on a corridor of a station the size of a moon, as an old man lowered his blade and smiled at him and went out like a lamp.

Now this. Warmth. Sleep. A man dreaming of nothing in particular, thirty paces off, who did not know he was being felt.

For the space of three heartbeats he stood wide open. There was no wall and no mask and no Master's hand on the latch; there was only a man's mind in a boy's skull, with twenty-three years of grief in it and nothing to hold the grief in, and it went out of him in every direction at once, down the bond and through the stone and into every sleeping room of that vast building, the way the note goes out of a bell that has been struck too hard. It rang. He felt it ring, and felt how far it carried, and did not yet understand that he was the one who had been struck.

Anakin slammed the shield down.

He did not decide to. The decision would have taken a thought, and there was no thought; there was only the reflex of twenty-three years, the thing Sidious had taught him first and best, before the saber work, before the choke. Close the mind. Close it the way a blast door closes, all at once, from the top, and let nothing through, not a mood, not a colour, not the fact of being awake; because anything that got through was a thing the Master could use. It came down now as it had come down ten thousand times, black and total, a wall of old stone with the scars of old sieges on it, and the warmth at the back of his mind stopped as though a hand had been laid across a candle.

He lay in the silence he had made.

Then he understood what he had done, and his whole body went cold.

Obi-Wan would have felt that. Even asleep. Especially asleep, when a master's mind lies open to the bond because the bond is how a master knows his Padawan has not fallen out of a window in the night. For six years that bond had been the one thing in the Temple that Anakin had never been able to hide from, and he had just shut it in the man's face. Not thinned. Not dimmed, as a boy does when he is sulking or hiding a cut lip. Shut. With a discipline that did not exist in this building, that no one in this building had ever felt, that any Jedi who had ever stood near a Sith would know in his teeth for what it was.

The first thing he had done in this body was give himself away.

He made himself breathe. In; out. The wall held. He could not take it down now; taking it down would be worse, would be a boy's mind with a man's grief in it spilling out across the Temple at the hour when the younglings slept lightest. He had to leave it up and pay for it. He had been paying for things his whole life. He would find out what this one cost in the morning.

He got up.

The legs did not want him. He had forgotten that a body has to be asked: that one does not simply decide to stand, with a flesh body, but negotiates it, and that the knees have opinions. These knees were a fifteen-year-old's. They had grown four centimetres in a year and had not yet been told, and when he put his weight on them they wobbled, and he put out a hand to the wall and found it closer than it should have been because his arm was shorter than the arm he had reached with. He stood against the wall until the floor agreed to be a floor. The flagstones were cold under his bare feet. He had forgotten feet. He had forgotten cold under them, the specific cold of stone at the fourth hour, and he stood on it and let it hurt him in that small clean way, because it was the first thing since the white light that had hurt at all.

Three steps to the shelf. He caught his shin on the corner of the chest, hard, and swore in Huttese in a voice that cracked on the second syllable.

He stopped.

That voice. High, and then not, and then high again: a voice in the middle of changing, a voice that had not finished deciding what it was. He had not heard it in thirty years. He had forgotten he had ever had it. He put his hand over his mouth, as though he could catch the sound and put it back, and stood there in the dark with a boy's hand over a boy's mouth and his shin throbbing, and did not laugh, and did not weep, and did neither for long enough that he could move again.

The datapad on the shelf woke when he touched it. Its glow was blue and small and showed him a chrono in the corner, and he looked at the chrono for a long time before he let himself read it.

Then he read it.

He read the year. He read it again. He did the arithmetic that a man does when he has been told the date and cannot make it fit, counting on from the only fixed point he had, which was the morning of Geonosis, which he would never not be able to find; and the count came out the same every time he did it, and it was four years.

Four years before the war.

He put the datapad down with a care that was almost violence, because if he did not put it down carefully he would throw it, and went to the window.

Coruscant. The slit gave him a narrow slice of it, a vertical strip of the city from the Temple's flank, and he put his face to the transparisteel and looked. The Senate dome, lit from beneath, pale and swollen. The lanes of traffic above it, moving at the thinned pace of the fourth hour. The towers of 500 Republica, with their rich windows dark. He looked for the things he knew and they were not there: no orbital batteries picking out the sky in a lattice; no grey wedge of a warship parked above the Senate like a thumb over a mouth; no searchlights. The air above the city was clear of everything but weather and traffic and the ordinary haze of fifty billion people breathing. It was a city before. It did not know yet. Nothing in it knew.

Four years.

The Chancellor was in his office, six years into his term, with a window that looked across at this spire, and he had been in this boy's head every tenth day for six years and would expect to be in it again. Dooku was at Serenno with a curved blade in a drawer. On Kamino, under the rain, Rex was six years old and looked twelve, and had a thing the size of a grain of sand behind his eye. His mother was alive. His mother was alive on Tatooine, under a different name, and would be alive for four more years.

Everyone was alive. Everyone in this building, every one of them, was asleep and alive, and he had killed them, and they did not know.

He stood with his forehead against the cold glass and his breath fogging it and his hands, both of them, flat against the wall on either side.

* * *

The knock, when it came, was soft. Two knuckles, twice.

He had heard the man wake. He had not been able to help it; even through the wall he had built, there was the small physical sound of a body turning over in the next room, the pause, the bare feet on stone. He had heard him stop outside the door and stand there, and he had known what the man was doing, because he had done it himself a thousand times outside a hundred doors: deciding whether to be the kind of person who knocks.

"Anakin?"

He turned from the window. He did not trust himself to speak yet. He said, "Yes," and it came out in the boy's voice, and it was enough.

The door slid back. The light from the common room was low, one lamp left burning as it was always left burning, and the man in the doorway was backlit, and then he stepped in and was not.

Obi-Wan Kenobi. Thirty-one years old. His hair flattened on the side he had slept on, his beard short and uncombed, his sleep-tunic creased, one hand still on the frame of the door. His face without the lines the war would cut into it. His eyes without the thing that would be in them on the bank of the river, that would still be in them nineteen years later in a corridor of a battle station, that Anakin had carried like a stone in the chest and would have given everything he had, twice over, to have never seen. There was none of it. The man had not seen anything yet. He was simply tired, and a little rumpled, and concerned, in the ordinary way of a man who has been woken at the fourth hour by a feeling he could not name.

"Nightmare?" Obi-Wan said.

The word was gentle. It was offered the way one offers a hand to someone who has stumbled: not insisting, not grabbing, just there. He said it as though he had said it before, in this doorway, more than once, and as though the answer had sometimes been yes.

Anakin looked at him. He had the boy's body between himself and the man, and he was grateful for it, because it meant Obi-Wan was looking at a narrow fifteen-year-old in a sleep-tunic with his hair on end and his shin bruised, and not at what was behind the eyes.

He could have said: I died. I died an hour ago, in my son's arms, and I had just stopped being the thing I was, and I was so glad, Master, I was so glad, and then I woke up here and you were asleep and I could feel you, and I shut you out because the Emperor taught me to, because I spent twenty-three years as his dog, and I have killed every person in this building, and I have killed you, and I am sorry, and I do not know why I am here.

He said, "Yes, Master."

Obi-Wan's hand stayed on the doorframe.

"You shut the door rather hard," he said. Mildly. He did not say *the bond*; he had never liked to speak of it, it had always embarrassed him a little, as though it were a form of eavesdropping. "I thought you'd fallen off something."

"I'm all right."

"Mm." The man looked at him. It was a longer look than Anakin had expected. It went over his face and down to his hands, which he realised he was holding clasped in front of him, the way a soldier holds his hands when he has nothing to do with them, and he unclasped them and did not know what to do with them instead. Obi-Wan's eyes came back to his face. "Do you want to tell me about it?"

"No." Too fast. He heard it. He softened it, in the boy's voice, with a boy's shrug. "I don't really remember it. It's gone. It was just... I woke up and I didn't know where I was. For a second."

That part, at least, was true.

And here was the thing he had not reckoned on, standing at the window doing his arithmetic: that the man would believe him. That Obi-Wan would take the lie the way he had taken a hundred lies from a boy in this room, with a slight tilt of the head and a slight narrowing of the eyes and then a nod, because the boy had the right to his own nightmares and a master who pried would get less, not more. He saw Obi-Wan decide to believe him. He saw it cost the man nothing. It was small; it was the smallest lie he had ever told anyone; he had told lies that burned planets. And it went into him like a splinter and he knew at once that he would be feeling it for days, for the simple reason that the last time Obi-Wan had asked him a question and he had answered, he had answered with a blade, and this time the man had asked him gently in a doorway at the fourth hour, and he had lied to him again, and the man had said all right.

"All right," Obi-Wan said.

He did not go. He stood a moment longer with his hand on the frame, and the look he gave the room was, Anakin thought, a strange one for a man to give a Padawan's room at night: a look that took in the window, and the datapad, still faintly lit on the shelf, and the chest, and the boy, and seemed to be counting them. Then it was gone, and the man was only tired.

"There's tea in the pot if you want it. It'll be stewed." A pause. "I'm just there. You know that."

"I know, Master."

"Try to sleep, Anakin."

The door slid to. The feet went away across the common room. The pause, the turning-over, the settling. And then, through the wall, through the black wall he had built, nothing: the man lying awake, as Anakin was lying awake, and neither of them able to feel the other. For the first time in six years. He had done that.

He sat down on the floor with his back against the bed.

* * *

He did not sleep. He had not expected to. He sat on the cold flagstones with his knees up and his arms around them, folded small as the body wanted to be folded, and watched the bar of light from the window crawl across the floor as the city turned, and thought.

He thought first about whether it was a trick.

It would be like the Master. It would be exactly like him: to let the boy die, and let him think he had died well, and then to put him here, in the one place and the one time where he could be made to do it all again, and to sit in his office across the city and watch. There were old stories about Sith who had done such things. Anakin had read them, in the years when he had read everything, in the archives that the Emperor had opened to him as one opens a cage to a trained animal. None of the stories had been true. He did not think this one was either. He had felt Sidious go into the reactor shaft. He had felt him go the way one feels a tooth go when it is pulled: a wrench, a hole, and then an absence that the tongue keeps finding. The absence was still there. He put his mind to it carefully, like a tongue, and it was still a hole.

So: not the Master. Or not in any way he could see, which with the Master had never meant much.

Something else, then. He did not know what. He had been offered no explanation and expected none. The Force did not explain itself; it had never explained itself to him, not on Mortis, not on the lava bank, not in the throne room. It had shown him a thing and let him choose. If it had shown him this, then it was showing him something and he did not yet know what, and until he knew he would be a fool to act.

So he would not act.

He turned it over, because it went against everything in him; it always had. He wanted to go now, tonight, barefoot, across the city to the Senate Office Building and up the lift and in through the red door and put his hands around the old man's throat while he slept, and he could do it, he knew exactly how it was done, where the guards stood and which lift the staff used and what the man's mind felt like when it was asleep. He wanted to go to the Archives and find the hole where Kamino had been and shout about it until the Council listened. He wanted to go to Obi-Wan's room, thirty paces away, and wake him, and tell him everything, and be held.

He could not do any of it. He went through each one, sitting on the floor, and took it apart.

The old man was not asleep; the old man was never asleep, and a fifteen-year-old who went across the city at night and died in a red office would change nothing except the date of the next boy's apprenticeship. The Council would listen to a Padawan who talked of an army on a planet that was not in the Archives for exactly as long as it took to call a healer. And Obi-Wan would believe him. That was the worst of it. Obi-Wan would believe him, because Obi-Wan loved him and did not yet have any reason not to, and then Obi-Wan would look at him and know what he was. He would get the look. He would get it ten years early, from a man who had not yet earned the right to give it, and he found that he could survive almost anything in this body, the breathing and the hands and the smell of the stone, except that.

Hide, then. All of it. The shield would stay up, thinned to something that could pass for a boy's sulk, and he would learn by morning how to hold it at that thickness, because he had held it at every thickness for twenty-three years for an audience that forgave nothing. The body would learn to fight like a boy. The mouth would learn to say *Master* and mean the right man. He would go to his classes and stand in a line of children and hold a training saber that did not weigh enough and let them hit him with it. He would sit in the Chancellor's office every tenth day and let that hand touch his mind and give it a boy to touch. He had been a mask for twenty-three years. He could be a face.

Change nothing. Not yet. Not until he understood why he was here, and whether he was alone.

That was the thought he came to last, as the bar of light reached the far wall and began to pale, and it was the one that sat with him longest. He did not know why it came. Perhaps it was the look Obi-Wan had given the room. Perhaps it was only that a man who has been a monster learns to assume that where there is one, there are others. He sat with it and could not make it into anything. He was here, alone, in a boy's body, in a sleeping building, and no one in the galaxy knew what he was.

He had just got that far when he felt it.

Far off. Not in the room; not in the quarters; not, he thought, on this level of the Temple at all, but somewhere below him and to the east, through many floors of stone. Something rang.

It was not a sound. It was the thing a sound is before it reaches the ear: a struck bell, a single note, clear and sudden and enormous for its size, as though a very small thing had been hit very hard. It went through the Force the way a bell's note goes through water. It had no words in it and no direction and it was not aimed at him; it was, he thought, not aimed at anything. It was simply the sound something makes when it wakes all at once.

Then it was gone, and the building was asleep again, and the grey of the window had become the grey of dawn.

He sat very still, with his arms around his knees, and did not know what it was. He thought it might be the Temple itself, which was old and had heard things. He thought it might be himself: some echo of his own arrival, going round the walls of this place and coming back to him. He thought, for one cold moment, that it might be the Master, somewhere across the city, opening his eyes.

He did not think it was a child.

He had no reason to. He was fifteen, and alone, and dead, and the morning was coming, and somewhere below him in the dark a bell had been struck, and he put it away with everything else, in the place where he kept the things he would have to understand later, and got up off the floor to find his boots.


## Chapter 2: Too Many of Them

Obi-Wan was already up, and Anakin heard him before he saw him: the small domestic noises of a man in a kitchen who has done the same thing every morning for years, the click of the heating element, the faint rattle of a tin, the particular silence of someone waiting for water. He stood in the doorway of his own room with his boots in his hand and listened, and the listening was so sharp and so entire that he had to tell himself to stop it. A boy does not stand in a doorway listening to his master make tea as if it were the last transmission from a dying ship. A boy yawns. A boy scratches.

He scratched. His scalp, under short hair that was his own. He had been doing that all night, on and off, finding things: the braid behind his right ear, thin and tight and slightly greasy; the ridge of a bone in his wrist; the fact that both wrists were there to have bones in them.

He had done the thing with the bond before dawn, because it had to be done before Obi-Wan woke and he had not known when Obi-Wan would wake. It had taken him most of an hour. The wall he had built in the first instant of waking was the wall he had always built, the one Sidious had taught him and he had spent twenty years improving out of spite, and it was not a thing a fifteen-year-old carried. He had taken it down a course at a time, as if dismantling a gun emplacement in the dark, and built in its place something low and sullen and adolescent: a door shut not because there was anything behind it but because the boy inside wanted it known he was shutting doors. Resentment at a volume Obi-Wan would recognise and sigh at. He had been that boy, once. He remembered the exact temperature of it.

What he did not remember was Obi-Wan's face when he came into the common room.

The man was at the counter with his back to the door, in a plain under-tunic with the sleeves pushed up, pouring. He looked over his shoulder when Anakin's feet touched the floor and he smiled, and the smile was wrong.

It was not wrong in any way Anakin could have pointed to. It was Obi-Wan's smile: the slight one, more in the eyes than the mouth, that said he had seen you coming a long way off and had decided to be amused by it. But it arrived too fast. The Obi-Wan of this year, of the year Anakin had been fifteen, had not smiled when his Padawan came into a room. He had looked up. He had said *good morning* in the tone of a man confirming a fact. He had been kind in the way a well-made tool is kind, by doing its job without comment, and Anakin had spent that year and the three after it trying to make him say something that was not a correction.

This Obi-Wan said, "You look as though you slept in a ventilation duct."

"I slept fine."

"You slept the way a Wookiee sleeps. I could hear you through the wall." He set a cup on the table. Two cups. He had always made two, Anakin remembered, or thought he remembered, and Anakin had always let his go cold. "Drink that. You have Drallig at the tenth hour and I will not have you dropping a training saber on a nine-year-old because you could not be bothered to wake up properly."

Anakin sat down.

He sat because his knees had told him to, in the half-second in which the sentence went in and his body decided what it would do before he could stop it. The cup was in front of him. It steamed. He put his hands around it, which was something a boy did, and the heat went into his palms and through them into a man who had not felt heat in his hands for twenty-three years without a gauge telling him the number, and he breathed, which was still a shock each time, and said, "Drallig."

"The Hawkbat Clan. Their saber class." Obi-Wan sat down across from him with his own cup and looked at him over it. "You assist, every tenday. You have done for the better part of two years. I am aware that it is not your favourite morning, but I do not believe it has ever before caused you to forget it existed."

"I didn't forget."

"No?"

"I was thinking about something else."

"That," said Obi-Wan, "I believe." He drank. The beard was shorter than Anakin remembered, and redder; the grey had not come into it yet. It would come in at the war. Anakin had watched it come. "You shut the door rather hard last night."

There it was again, the same words as before, and he knew them the way one knows a step on a stair.

He had expected it and it still went through him. He kept his hands on the cup. He let the sulk he had built behind his eyes thicken a little, the way a boy's would when touched. "I was tired."

"Mm."

"I'm allowed to be tired."

"You are allowed to be anything you like, within reason and the Code." Obi-Wan set the cup down. "I only mention it because it was rather like being in a room when the lights go out. One notices." He looked at Anakin for a moment longer than the sentence needed. Then he reached across the table, reached across with his right hand, in a plain under-tunic in the morning light, and put it on Anakin's shoulder, and squeezed, once, and let go. "Drink your tea, my friend. You are going to be late, and Cin Drallig does not forgive lateness; he merely stores it."

Anakin drank his tea.

He drank it and it was tea, and it was hot, and the hand had been on his shoulder for perhaps a second and a half, and he could still feel the four fingers and the thumb as if they had been pressed into wet clay.

Obi-Wan had never done that.

Not at fifteen. Not at nineteen. There had been a hand on his shoulder on a landing platform once, when the Chancellor's people were watching, a hand that was performing. There had been one on Mustafar. No. There had not. There had been the opposite of a hand. He had gone looking for this touch for a decade, had wanted it so badly that he had made a whole life out of not being given it, and here it was, at a kitchen table, at fifteen, from a man who had just said *my friend* to a boy with a braid.

My friend. Obi-Wan said that to equals. To Dex, to Bail Organa. He had said it, at the last, to a man in a mask on a dark landing, and Anakin cut that off before it finished, because he knew where it ended and he could not go there with a cup in his hands.

He thought: *I have forgotten. I am remembering a different year. He was always like this and I made him cold in my memory because cold was easier to hate.*

He thought: *No.*

He thought: *Write it down.*

"You're staring," Obi-Wan said.

"You've got tea in your beard."

"I have not." He checked, though. Anakin watched him check and something in the watching nearly broke: the small vanity, the hand going up to the jaw, the slight affronted dignity. He had not seen this man do this since he could not have said when. He had not seen this man. Not this one. He had seen an old man in a desert, through a helmet, for the length of one fight, and the old man had done nothing so human as check his beard.

"Go," Obi-Wan said. "Boots. Belt. Both of them. The children can smell fear and so can Drallig, and he has a longer memory."

* * *

The training halls were on the fourth level, on the eastern side, under the Council towers. He knew the way. His feet knew it, which was not the same thing, and he let them have it.

The Temple in the morning was noise. He had not been prepared for that. Twenty-three years in a place where every sound was his own breath, and then a night in a sleeping building, and now this: the corridors full of people, robes and boots, the low voices of Masters and the high voices of younglings being shepherded somewhere in lines, a door hissing, a laugh, somebody's name called from a stairwell. Light through the tall windows in slabs. The smell of stone and cleaning solvent and, somewhere, bread. A Twi'lek Knight nodded to him in passing and he nodded back and did not know her name and was certain he had once. Two Padawans his own age went by arguing about astrogation. A youngling of four or five sat on a bench outside the Halls of Healing with one boot off, crying with enormous dignity, while a crèche-master knelt in front of her and said something about socks.

It was so ordinary and so alive that he could not breathe in it. He stopped at the top of a stair and put his hand flat on the wall and the wall was cool and the stone was the stone and under his hand, for a second, it was warm, and black with smoke, and the light on it was orange.

*The 501st came up the Processional Way in two columns and he walked at the head of them. Their boots on the steps. The Temple's doors stood open; someone had opened them, someone had thought it was a delegation; and the light inside was the same gold it always was, and he went into the gold with his saber in his hand and it was already lit.*

He took the hand off the wall.

A boy of twelve or so, a Padawan, bumped him from behind and said *sorry* without looking and went on. Anakin went on. He went down the stair and along the gallery and through the archway into the hall of the initiates, and he counted the children as he came in because counting was a thing he knew how to do.

Twenty. Sand-coloured tunics, brown belts, small boots. Hawkbat Clan was the nine-to-eleven cohort, and they stood in four rows of five in the middle of the long hall with training sabers in their hands and the sabers not yet lit, and they were loud. That struck him first and hardest: the noise of them. A human boy was telling a Rodian girl something at a volume that suggested he thought she was a floor away. A Nautolan boy and a human boy were comparing their hilts and shoving. A Mirialan girl near the back, with her hair in a tight dark braid, stood very correctly with her saber at her side and looked bored out of her mind. Somebody's belt was done up wrong. Somebody was yawning. The smallest of them barely came past his belt; the tallest was a lanky human boy who might one day be Anakin's own height and was all elbows getting there.

Children. He said it to himself the way he had once said *target* and *range*. Children. A room of children, with sticks.

Battlemaster Cin Drallig stood at the far end of the hall with his hands clasped behind him, watching Anakin come in with the expression of a man noting the time.

He was alive. That was the thing. He was alive and his hair was still mostly brown and he was wearing the plain dark tabard he taught in and there was a training saber tucked in his belt, and he said, in exactly the voice Anakin had heard across this hall ten thousand times and once, "Padawan Skywalker. You honour us."

"Master Drallig. Sorry."

"You are not late. You are merely not early, which in you I have learned to treat as a kind of lateness." Drallig looked him over. The look was quick and professional and it did not find anything; Anakin had built himself at the top of the stair to be looked at. "You look peaky. Kenobi feeding you?"

"Tea."

"Yes. Well." Something almost like humour. "Take the back two rows. Grips and stances; we are on the second Shii-Cho sequence and half of them are still holding the thing like a spoon. Correct what you see. Do not demonstrate anything I have not taught them, however much they beg, and they will beg."

"Yes, Master."

"Hawkbat Clan," Drallig said, not loudly, and the hall went silent as if a blade had come down through the noise.

Anakin took the back two rows.

He did it. That was the thing he would remember, afterwards, when he could remember anything about the hour: that he did it. He walked down the side of the hall along the ranks of children while Drallig called the sequence from the front, and his body knew the walk and his voice knew the words, and he said *thumb along the emitter, not around it* and *weight forward, you're sitting on it* and *that's your left foot, Dorreth, I need the other one*, and the children did as they were told, or did not, or did for three seconds and forgot, and he came back and said it again. Ten children in his two rows. He learned their names from Drallig's roll and from their own mouths, when they told him, which they did without being asked, because children did. Ilyo. Brannick. Wix. Tam Dorreth, who was the loud one. The Mirialan girl who stood so correctly was Kessa Ruun and her grip was the only decent grip in the hall and he told her so and she flushed up green and did not know where to look.

*The Council chamber. The round room, the twelve chairs, the dark outside the windows because it was night. They had hidden behind the chairs. Six of them, seven. Someone had told them to go up as high as they could, and they had, and they had waited, and the door had opened.*

"Sorry," he said to the Nautolan boy, who had said something. "Say that again."

"I said is it true you built a podracer."

"Yes."

"Did it win?"

"Once." He looked at the boy's hands. "You're choking it. Here." He put his own fingers over the boy's fingers and moved them an inch down the hilt, and the boy's fingers were damp and small and bony and the hilt was a training hilt, light, blunt-ended, meant for a child's reach, and it was, for the length of a breath, the lightest thing he had ever held. "Feel that? The balance is in the hand now, not the wrist. You'll last longer."

"Last longer than what?"

"Than you did yesterday."

The boy grinned. He had very sharp small teeth. Anakin went on down the row.

There was a girl, Rodian, Ilyo, whose stance was close to right and whose feet were a hand's width too narrow, and he knelt to show her, because she was small and a thing shown from above is not the same as a thing shown at one's own height. He knelt on the mat on one knee, and the knee went down and the joint took his weight and did not grind or lock or report a fault, and he had to let that pass through him too, and said, "Look at my feet." She looked. Her eyes were enormous and wet and black and interested. "Yours are like this." He put his boots together. "Where does the ground go if I push you?"

"Away?"

"Where do *you* go?"

She considered. "Also away."

"So." He opened his stance. "Now push me."

She pushed him. She put both hands on his shoulder and shoved with her whole body, and she weighed about as much as a loaded rifle, and he did not move, and she laughed, a loud, surprised, honking laugh that made Drallig at the front say *Ilyo* without turning round, and then did it herself, feet apart, and looked down at them, and looked up at him, satisfied.

He stood up.

Drallig was walking the front ranks now, calling the sequence with his hands clasped behind him, *one, and two, and guard, and over*, and Anakin heard the voice as he had heard it as a boy in this hall, calm and dry and infinitely patient, the voice of the Order's own patience, and he turned his head towards it the way a man turns towards a lamp.

*The Room of a Thousand Fountains. Dark, the lamps out, and the water still falling, all of it, a thousand small voices in the black. The Battlemaster came along the edge of the pool with his blade already up, and the pool took both blades and gave them back, and he was older and his hair was white and he said nothing at all; he had seen what was behind Anakin and had done his sums. He was good. He was the best the Order had with a blade, and he was old, and he had taught the boy who was killing him every stroke the boy did not use. It took eleven seconds. Anakin had counted. He had always counted. The last thing Drallig's face did was understand.*

"Over, and guard, and *reset*," said Drallig, alive, at the front of the hall. "Skywalker, the back row."

He looked at the back row.

He had been working the fourth rank from one end and had reached the third child in it when the thing with the Rodian girl had happened, and he had not yet got to the far end. The far end was in the corner, by the wall. There was a Togruta initiate in it.

She was small. Smaller than Ilyo; the smallest in her row, perhaps the smallest in the clan. Her montrals were short, barely risen, white and blue, and her lekku came down only to her shoulders and curled at the ends against the sand-coloured cloth of her tunic. White markings on an orange face. Her tunic was a size too large and had been taken in at the belt by someone who did not sew. She was holding her training saber the wrong way round, hilt in a backhand grip, the blunt emitter pointing down and back along her forearm instead of up and out, and she had been holding it that way, he saw, for some time, because the stance she had built around the grip was complete, and comfortable, and hers.

"Tano," said Drallig, from the front, without heat. "The other way. We have discussed it."

Anakin's knees went.

They did not go all the way. The body he was in was fifteen and strong and did not know what its mind knew, and it caught him, somewhere above the floor, with a lurch like a ship catching on its repulsors a metre from the ground. He put a hand on the wall. The wall was cool. He had stopped breathing and he started again, and the breath went in like something swallowed wrong, and nobody looked at him, because Drallig had said a child's name and the children were all looking at the child.

She turned the saber over. It took her a moment. She was not slow about it; she was, he thought, precise, in the way of someone doing a thing properly that she had done improperly on purpose. She held it the right way up and looked at the front of the hall and waited.

He knew her.

He had known her from the back of the room, from the curl of the lekku against the tunic, before Drallig spoke. He had told himself it was any Togruta, that there were Togruta in every clan, that the Temple had taken in dozens of them in the years before. Before. And then Drallig had said the name and the name had gone through him and out the other side and taken something with it, and what was left standing by the wall in a Padawan's tunic with his hand on the cold stone was not a boy and not a man but a thing in a black suit on a stone bridge in a temple that was falling down, looking through a crack in a lens at a woman who had said she would not leave him.

He had told her what that meant. He had meant it.

She had been thirty-three. She had been a Padawan of fourteen with a saber too short for the grip she wanted, and a girl of seventeen walking down the Temple steps away from him, and a voice on a comm in a war that was over, and the thing on the bridge. He had had a name for her. He had not used it since.

She was ten.

He made himself look. The hilt was too long for her wrist; he could see it from here, the way the pommel sat past the heel of her hand, the way she had had to shift her grip to keep the emitter from dipping. Her boots were planted. There was a scuff on the left one. A child in the second row had turned round to look at her and she looked back without expression until the child turned away.

He did not reach for her. In the Force she was one small bright thing among twenty and he did not reach for her, because he could not have borne what he found, or did not find.

"Skywalker," Drallig said again, mild.

"Yes, Master."

He walked down the row.

He did not know, afterwards, how. He walked, and the children parted for him, and he came to the end of the row and stood in front of her, and she had to tip her head back to look at his face and he could not let her, so he knelt. He went down on one knee on the mat as he had for the Rodian girl and it brought his eyes level with hers, more or less; hers were blue, and very steady, and they went to his face for one half of one second and then away, to the hilt in her hand, to the thing he was presumably there to do.

"Let me see," he said.

She held it out. The right way up. He took her wrist.

His hand was shaking.

It was not a tremor; it was a shake, the kind that lives in the forearm and comes out through the fingers, and he could not stop it. He watched his own hand close around her wrist, around the bone of it, which was small, which was a child's, which he could have broken by closing his fist, and he saw that she was watching it too. Not his face. His hand. Her eyes were on his fingers where they shook against her skin, and they stayed there, and she did not flinch and she did not speak and she did not, in any way at all, look up.

He heard himself say, "Thumb along the emitter."

"Yes," she said.

It was the only word. Her voice was a child's voice: high, a little hoarse, Togruta vowels. It went into him like something cold into a wound and lay there.

He moved her thumb. The joint of it was tiny under his. He opened her fingers one at a time with his own and laid them along the hilt where they were meant to go, and he was not teaching her anything, and they both knew it; her fingers went where he put them and lay there as obediently as a doll's. He did not look at her face. She did not look at his. He thought of the way she had been holding it, reversed, the emitter back along the forearm, the grip he had watched her build an entire form around in a war that had not happened, and he thought that if he corrected it he would be correcting her out of the only thing in this room that was true. And he thought that was madness. She was ten. She had picked it up wrong the way children picked things up wrong; she would be told, and told, and would grow out of it or be made to.

He let go of her wrist.

He put his hand on his knee where it could shake against something, and he said, "Good," and then, "Carry on," which was not something a Padawan said to a youngling, and stood up.

She turned the saber over.

He saw her do it. It was not defiance; there was nothing in her face at all. She simply turned the hilt in her hand, deliberately, the way she had before, until the emitter pointed down and back along her forearm, and set her feet, and looked at the front of the hall, and waited for the sequence.

He did not correct it.

He stood beside her for the length of a breath with his back to the children and his hand at his side and he did not correct it, and then Drallig said *one* and the room moved, and he moved with it, back along the row, past the Mirialan girl, past Wix and Brannick and the loud boy, and corrected a grip, and corrected a stance, and said *weight forward* to somebody, and did not look behind him.

*The round room. The chairs. He had come in and they had come out from behind the chairs because he was Master Skywalker and they had been told that the Jedi would come, and one of them, a boy, fair, nine or ten, with his training saber held in both hands exactly as he had been taught, had stepped forward out of the others and looked up at him. "Master Skywalker," the boy had said. "There are too many of them. What are we going to do?"*

*He had not answered.*

*He had never answered.*

"And guard," said Drallig. "And reset. Better. Dorreth, I said better, not louder. Again."

* * *

The class ran forty minutes. He finished it.

He could not have said how. He would try, later, lying on his back in the dark in a boy's bed, to reconstruct the second half of that hour and he would find he had almost none of it: the Nautolan boy asking again about the podracer; a lit training saber catching Tam Dorreth across the knuckles and Tam yelping and the clan laughing and Drallig not laughing; Kessa Ruun's braid. The clan bowing. Drallig's hand lifted in dismissal and the noise coming up again like water into a lock. Twenty children making for the door and the small one in the corner going with them, not first and not last, somewhere in the middle, where a child who did not want to be noticed would go. He did not watch her go. He watched the wall.

"Skywalker."

"Master."

Drallig was looking at him. The look was longer this time. "You did well enough. The Rodian girl's feet were a good catch." A pause. "The Tano child."

"Yes, Master."

"Was there something?"

He thought about it. He actually thought about it, with the Battlemaster's eyes on him and the hall emptying behind him, and the thinking took no time at all and arrived nowhere. "She holds it backwards."

"She does. She has done since she was seven. Her clan-master has had words, I have had words, Master Plo has had words." Drallig's mouth did something that in another man would have been a smile. "She holds it correctly for exactly as long as she is being looked at. I have known Knights with less discipline." He unclasped his hands. "You were kind to her. I saw. That is not a fault. Go and eat something, you look like a wet rag."

"Yes, Master."

He bowed. Drallig bowed. He walked out of the hall the way he had come in, past the mats and the racks, through the arch and along the gallery, and his legs did it and he was grateful to them, and at the second stair he turned left instead of right and went into the refresher at the end of the corridor and locked the cubicle door and was sick.

There was not much. Tea. He had not eaten; Obi-Wan had said *eat* and he had not. He knelt on the tiled floor with his hands on the rim and brought the tea up, and then nothing, and then nothing again with his whole narrow body clenched around it, and the sound he made in the small tiled room was a boy's sound, thin and awful, and he could not stop it. His eyes ran. His nose ran. He hung there with his forehead on the cool edge of the bowl and waited for the body to be finished with him.

Somebody came in and used the basin and went out. He stayed where he was.

She was ten.

He said it to the bowl. He said it in his head and then, very quietly, aloud, in the voice he had, which cracked in the middle of it. *She is ten.* She was ten years old and she weighed nothing and her tunic was too big and she held a training saber the wrong way round because she was a child and children did that, and she had looked at his hand because his hand had been shaking and a child would look at a shaking hand, and she had said *yes* because he was a Padawan and she was an initiate and that was what one said. There was nothing in it. There was a child in it. The last Togruta he had touched had been thirty-three years old and had put her hand against the cracked lens of his mask and he had told her she would die, and that was not in this room, and had never been in this room, and would not be for twenty-three years, if ever, if he could stop it, if he could keep her from ever standing on that bridge with that face.

She was a child. She was the thing he had come up the steps to kill.

He sat back on his heels. The cubicle was very small. His knees were against the door. He wiped his face with the heel of his hand and looked at the hand, which had stopped shaking, which was fifteen years old and had never held anything heavier than a training saber and a hydrospanner, and he thought, with a clarity that was almost peace, that he did not know what she had seen.

Not the girl. The thing she had seen was his hand, shaking. He did not know what she made of it. Children made things of things. He did not know what Obi-Wan had seen across a kitchen table, or Drallig across a hall; he did not know why there was a hand on his shoulder where there had never been one, or a child in the corner who held the blade the way she had held it in a life that no one in this building had lived. He did not know what was wrong, or whether anything was, or whether it was only him: a dead man's memory, gone soft in the suit, remembering a colder master and a later child and calling the difference a sign.

He could not tell. That was the whole of it. He could not tell, and he would have to find out, and in the meantime he would have to go and eat something so that his master would not look at him again the way his master had looked at him over the tea.

He got up. He flushed the bowl and unlocked the door and rinsed his mouth at the basin and did not look in the mirror, because there was a boy in it, and he was not ready for the boy.

Malachor was seven years ago.

Malachor was twenty-three years from now.

He went to find food.


## Chapter 3: The Youngling

Ahsoka Tano had been awake since the bell.

Not the Temple's bell; the Temple had no bell, only the chimes that went through the dormitories at the sixth hour like someone running a fingernail along a comb. This had been the other kind. It had come up through the floor and the bunk frame and the thin mattress and the ten-year-old body lying on it, somewhere past the middle of the night, and it had rung in the Force like a struck bell, one note, enormous, and gone. She had sat up with her heart going so hard she could feel it in her montrals. She had put her hand flat against the wall, because the wall was cold and real. She had not got out of the bunk.

That was the discipline. You did not get out of the bunk.

Around her, nineteen children slept. Hawkbat Clan: twenty bunks in two rows, a door at each end, a night-glow strip along the skirting that made everything the colour of weak tea. Tam Dorreth snored. Ilyo breathed through her mouth with a tiny click. Kessa Ruun, in the next bunk, had thrown an arm over her face the way she always did, as if sleep were a bright light. None of them had stirred. Whatever it was had not been loud; it had been deep, the way a thing is deep when it is very far down and very large.

Above and to the west, she thought. The Padawan floors.

She lay down again and put her hands on her stomach and counted her breaths, which was a thing a youngling was taught at four and most of them had forgotten by six. She did not forget things. She counted to a hundred and started again, and her heart did not slow, and somewhere around the third hundred she stopped pretending that it would.

The note had been gone a long time by then. What stayed was the shape it had left behind, like a dent in a cushion where someone has sat. She lay and looked at the shape and did not name it. There was a feeling that went with it, and she did not name that either, because the feeling was far too big for a girl in a bunk in a dormitory, and if she let it have a name it would have her.

Her feet did not reach the end of the mattress. She noticed that, lying there: the gap of cold sheet past her toes. She had noticed it most nights for seven years and it had never once stopped being strange.

Under the mattress, between the slats and the foam, was a strip of flimsi folded in four. She did not reach for it. She knew what was on it; she had written it.

When the chimes came she sat up with the rest of them and rubbed her eyes with her fists, which was what a child did.

* * *

"You look awful," said Kessa, which was what Kessa said most mornings and meant kindly.

"Didn't sleep."

"Bad dream?"

"No." Ahsoka pulled her tunic over her head. It was the sand-coloured one every initiate wore, belted brown, and it had fitted her at the start of the year and did not now; the hem came to the middle of her thighs and the sleeves stopped short of her wrists by the width of two fingers. The quartermaster droid issued new ones at the turn of each quarter, and the turn was still nine days off, and in the meantime she walked about the Temple with her wrists showing like a girl who had been let out of somewhere. "Just awake."

"You're always just awake." Kessa was Mirialan, green-skinned, the black diamonds on her cheeks not yet complete; she was ten, and loud in the mornings, and she had decided two years ago that Ahsoka was her friend, and Ahsoka had let her. It was easier with a friend. A girl alone was watched; a girl with a friend was a pair, and a pair was ordinary. "Sit. Your left one's twisted."

Ahsoka sat on the edge of the bunk and let Kessa straighten the silka bead on the end of her left lek, which had turned in the night. Kessa's fingers were quick and careless. Ahsoka kept her face still. She had learned, early, that she did not like to be touched there by anyone she did not trust, and that a child who flinched from her clanmates was a child who went into a file. So she did not flinch. She looked at the wall above Tam Dorreth's bunk, where someone years ago had scratched a hawkbat into the plaster with a stylus, and she thought about nothing.

"There," Kessa said. "Now you're only half awful."

"Thanks."

"Saber day."

"I know."

"Drallig'll have the Padawan again. The Skywalker one."

"I know," Ahsoka said, and bent to do her boots, so that she was looking at the floor.

* * *

The Bear Clan boy was waiting in the corridor outside the refectory, as he was most mornings, standing against the wall with his hands behind his back the way the very small ones stood when they had been told not to run.

Caleb Dume was seven. He was too thin for his tunic the way she was too big for hers, and he had a cowlick that no amount of water would flatten, and a way of looking up at her that she could not bear and could not stop looking at. He had attached himself to her the previous winter, for no reason either of them could have given; he had simply started being wherever she was, a few paces behind, like a thing she had dropped. The clan-masters thought it was sweet. Kessa thought it was funny. Ahsoka thought nothing about it at all, very carefully, every single day.

"Hawkbat has sabers," he informed her. He knew all the timetables. "Bear has Master Yoda."

"Lucky Bear."

"He makes us sit still."

"That's the point of him."

"Is the Padawan coming? The tall one?"

"I don't know," she said, which was a lie, and put her hand on the top of his head to steer him round a pillar he had not seen, because he was looking up at her and not where he was going. His hair was warm. She took her hand away a half-second later than she should have and felt the half-second go through her like a draught through a door.

He walked beside her to the refectory, talking about Master Yoda's stick. She let him. In the doorway she stopped, and he bumped into the back of her legs, and she stood for a moment looking at the long room: the long tables, the sand-coloured ranks of younglings with their bowls, three Knights at the far end with their tea, the tall windows going grey with the morning. She looked at all of it. She did that in doorways. Then she went in and got her bowl and sat beside Kessa and ate, and when a girl from the clan above them, Mirialan too, older, quiet, with an earnest braid down her back, passed behind their bench, Ahsoka looked into her porridge until she had gone by.

"Was that Offee?" Kessa said. "She's going to Ilum next quarter, I heard."

"Probably," said Ahsoka, and ate.

* * *

Master Plo found her in the colonnade before the class, as he did perhaps once a tenday, and she let him see her light up because it was not a performance. It was the only thing in the Temple that was not.

"Little 'Soka."

"Master Plo."

He was very tall. Everyone was very tall, but Master Plo was tall in a way that made her feel small on purpose, and she liked it; it was like standing in the shade. His breath mask hissed softly. His tusks, behind it, made the shape of what passed for a smile. He had found her, once, on a world she did not remember and did remember, and had carried her up the ramp of a ship, and she had been three years old and had put her arms round his neck and known, with a certainty that a three-year-old could not have had, exactly who he was. He did not know that. She intended that he never should.

"You are tired," he said.

"A bit."

"The night was long?"

"I woke up." She looked at the Processional Way through the columns, the first speeder traffic going by at the level of the cornices. "Did you feel anything, Master? Last night? Late."

She had not meant to ask it. She asked anyway. It was the way she asked all her questions of him, as a child asks: idle, looking elsewhere, one foot scuffing.

Plo Koon was quiet. The goggles turned towards her and stayed. "Feel what, little one?"

"I don't know. Something. Like something being dropped. A long way off." She made a small gesture with the hand that did not reach the end of her sleeve. "It woke me up. I thought maybe someone..." She let it go. "Nothing. I was dreaming, I expect."

"The Temple was quiet," he said, slowly. "I felt nothing. But I was in meditation, and in meditation I am not always listening." The mask hissed. "Does it trouble you, that I did not hear it?"

"No, Master."

"It troubles you that you did."

She did not answer that. He put his hand, briefly, on the top of her montrals; it was the only touch there she did not have to make herself bear.

"You asked me once," he said, "whether a dream could be a true thing. You were five. Do you remember?"

"I remember."

"I told you that the future is always in motion. I have thought since that it was not a kind answer. It was the Council's answer." He looked down at her. "Was it a dream, 'Soka?"

"I don't know what it was," she said, which was true, and was the first true thing she had said that morning, and she felt it sit between them like a stone she had set down and would have to pick up again later. "I'll be late for Master Drallig."

"Go, then." And as she went: "Hold the thing the right way round, child, for the sake of my ears. He complains of you."

"Yes, Master Plo," she said, and did not look back, because her face would have told him something.

* * *

The hall was the long one on the fourth level, with the high gallery and the sprung floor, and it smelled, as it always did, of twenty children and the ozone of the training sabers. Drallig had them in four ranks before the chimes had finished. Ahsoka took the back corner, as she always did, left-hand end, where the light from the clerestory did not quite reach and where a girl could hold a thing however she pleased for a full minute before anyone with authority noticed.

The training saber was too long for her. They all were; they were made for a twelve-year-old's wrist, and the grip was fat in her hand, and the weight was wrong, and she had stopped minding years ago. She held it reversed, blade down along her forearm, the way she had held it since she was seven, since the first morning they had put one into her hand and her hand had turned it round before she could stop it. Drallig had had words. Her clan-master had had words. Master Plo had had the gentlest words of all, and she had listened to every one of them and turned it back round the next morning, and they had put it down, eventually, to a Togruta thing, or a stubborn thing, or a thing she would grow out of, and she had let them.

The Padawan was already there.

She felt him before she saw him. That was the thing she would not think about afterwards: that she had been standing in a corner with her eyes on Drallig's boots, and her whole body had turned, as a plant turns, half a second before she knew which wall he was standing by, and she had known.

He stood there white. That was the first thing. He was a tall boy, fifteen, narrow in the shoulders, with the thin braid behind his right ear and the short sandy hair, and she had seen him across the refectory and along corridors and a dozen times in this hall for the better part of two years, and he had always stood like a boy: loose, restless, apologising to Drallig with a grin that said he was not sorry. He did not stand like that. He stood at the side of the hall as if the floor were heavy, and he looked at them, the four ranks, the twenty sand-coloured children with their sabers, and his eyes went along the first rank and the second and the third, one by one, not fast, and she thought: *he is counting us.*

She did not know why she thought it. She thought it the way one knows a smell.

Then his eyes reached the fourth rank and the corner and she looked down at the floor, hard, at the grain of the sprung wood, and her heart did the thing it had done in the night.

"Padawan," said Drallig. "The back two rows."

"Yes, Master."

A boy's voice. A boy's voice with something gone out of it.

The class went on. Drallig called the first form and the hall filled with the hum of twenty low blades and the squeak of boots, and Ahsoka moved through it with the rest of them, the sixth cut and the seventh, her reversed blade making the same shape the right way up would have made and nobody in the back rank caring. She kept her eyes on her own hands. She did not watch the Padawan. She felt where he was the whole time, the way one feels the sun on one side of one's face with one's eyes shut, and she did not watch him.

She watched him with Brannick.

She had not meant to. He had come down the third rank correcting grips, a word here, a hand there; he had passed Kessa with a word and come to Brannick, the Nautolan boy, who held his hilt like a rope, and he had put his hand over the boy's and moved the small fingers down the grip. He had said something too low for Ahsoka to hear, and Brannick had grinned all his sharp small teeth, and he had smiled at him, and the smile was the wrong smile: not a Padawan's smile for a youngling, careless and brief, but the smile of someone who had done this before, a great many times, and had done it once too many. He kept his hand there over the boy's for longer than the grip needed. Then he went on to Ilyo, the Rodian girl, and knelt to show her her feet, and Ilyo put both hands on his shoulder and pushed with her whole body, and he did not move, and she laughed, loud and honking, and Drallig at the front said *Ilyo* without turning round. He stayed a moment on his knee looking at her, as if to check that she was still there, and then he stood and moved on.

Ahsoka looked at the floor.

*Stone falling. A red light moving. Someone saying her name as if it cost him to say it.*

"Tano," said Drallig.

He said it as he always said it: a name and a sigh, from the front of the hall, over the heads of the ranks. Her grip. Of course. She turned it round, the right way up, neither slowly nor fast, and stood with the blade along her wrist and waited for the Padawan, who was already in the fourth rank, three children away, two, one, and she was looking at her hands, and then he was in front of her and she was looking at his.

He went down on one knee. He was still too tall for her, kneeling; she would have had to look up a little. She did not look up. "Let me see," he said, and she held it out, and she looked at his right hand as it came out and closed round her wrist.

It was shaking.

Not much. Not so a child would see it. A tremor in the fingers, in the tendons on the back of the hand, the kind a hand gets after it has carried something too heavy for too long and been told to put it down. He held her wrist and the shaking went into her through the bones, and she stood very still, the way one stands when an animal one has not expected has put its mouth round one's hand and has not yet decided to bite.

He moved her thumb along the hilt, to where it was meant to go. His fingers did it gently and they did it well, and then they stopped, and he did not let go.

He looked at it. Her hand, the hilt, the way her fingers had been arranged. He looked for a long time. The hall went on around them, Drallig calling the ninth cut, the hum of the blades; it went on a long way off. She could hear him breathing. She did not look at his face. She thought that if she looked at his face something would happen that she could not take back, and so she looked at his hand on her wrist, and the shaking in it, and she thought, with a clarity that was almost cold: *you are not. You cannot be. He is a boy.*

"Thumb along the emitter," he said. Barely a voice at all.

"Yes," she said.

It was all she said. One word, in a child's flat voice, to a boy's hand. He let it go.

He let her wrist go and he stayed on his knee a second longer looking at the saber, the right way up now, in her small hand, and then he got up, too fast, the way a boy gets up when his knees have been on the floor too long, and he moved on down the rank to Wix. He did not look back. He did not correct her again.

She turned the blade back round.

She did it slowly, so that he would feel it if he was feeling, and he was feeling; she saw the line of his back go still for the width of a breath, three children away, and then go on. "And guard," said Drallig, from the front, and the ninth cut went on, and Ahsoka stood in the corner with her saber reversed along her arm and her wrist burning where he had held it, and told herself nothing.

That was the discipline. She told herself nothing, the whole of the rest of the hour. She did the forms. She let Tam Dorreth beat her at the paired drill, twice, because Tam was loud and a loud boy who won was a boy who did not look at you. She bowed to Drallig at the end and she filed out with the rest and she did not look for the Padawan, who had stayed by the wall, white, with one hand against it. Behind her, at the door, she heard Drallig's dry voice say, "Was there something?" and then the Padawan's, barely there: "She holds it backwards." She did not turn round.

* * *

She wrote the line at midday, when the dormitory was empty and the others were at history.

The flimsi was the kind that came out of the mechanics workshop, grey, thin, the stuff the droids wrapped spare parts in. She had taken a sheet of it the first year and folded it in four and kept it between the slats and the mattress ever since, and she wrote on it in Togruti, in the small square script that nobody in Hawkbat could read and that Master Plo could read a little, which was a risk she had decided was worth running, since there was no one else in the Temple she would rather be caught by.

She took it out. She unfolded it on her knee. It was not long. Six lines, seven, in a hand that had got steadier as the years went by; the first line had been written by a child of four who could barely hold a stylus and had wanted very badly to put a thing down before it went. She read them, all of them, quickly, the way one checks that a locked door is still locked.

Then she took the stylus and wrote the eighth line, small, at the bottom. It took her longer than it should have. She looked at it when it was done and did not like how it looked, and did not change it, and folded the flimsi in four and put it back under the mattress and smoothed the blanket over it, and sat for a moment with her hands between her knees.

Her hem came to the middle of her thighs. Her wrists showed. She was ten years old.

She went to history.

* * *

The small dojo on the third level was never used after the ninth hour. She had learned that in her second year, and she had been going there, perhaps one night in five, ever since: down the dormitory corridor in her socks with her boots in her hand, past the night-glow strips, up the service stair that the droids used, along the upper passage, and in.

It was a square room with a sprung floor and a rack of training sabers on the wall and a gallery along one side, high, railed, where a master could stand to watch a bout without being in it. Nobody stood there at night. The lights came up to a quarter when the door opened and no further. She had asked a maintenance droid about it once, carelessly, as a child asks, and it had told her the dojo's lighting was on a fault ticket that had been open for three years. She had thanked it. She had not told anyone.

She took two sabers off the rack.

They were too long for her. Both of them. The grips were fat in her hands and the balance was high and wrong, and when she thumbed them on the low blue hum of them filled the room and she felt the familiar thing go through her: not pleasure, exactly. Rightness. The feeling of a key going into a lock it was cut for. She turned them both in her hands so the blades ran down along her forearms, points towards the floor, and she stood for a moment in the quarter-light in her socks with her wrists showing and her tunic hem at the middle of her thighs, a small girl holding two sabers backwards in a room where nobody could see her, and she let her breath out.

Then she moved.

It was not the forms. The forms were for the hall, the right way up, in a rank. This was something else, something that had no name in any class she had sat in, a thing of turns and crossed blades and the body going low and coming up from the other side, both hands at once, the blades making shapes in the air that no one had ever taught her and that her hands knew the way they knew the stylus. Her arms were too short for it. Her wrists tired. The sabers were a half-hand too long and she felt it in every turn: the end of the blade going where she had not meant it, the weight dragging the stroke a fraction wide. She corrected. She went on. She was strong for ten, everyone said so, and she was not strong enough for this, and she did it anyway, every fifth night, in a room with a fault ticket, because her body did not know how to do it yet and some other part of her could not bear that it did not.

She did not think about the hall. She did not think about a hand shaking on her wrist, or a boy who counted children, or the wrong smile over Brannick's small hands. She thought about the blades. The left came up and the right came round and she turned, low, and came up from the other side, and sweat ran down between her montrals and she thought about nothing at all for the first time all day.

She stopped because of the gallery.

It was not a sound. There was no sound. It was the feeling that comes on the back of the neck when a room one has believed empty has stopped being empty: a change in the air, a weight. She stood with both blades down and her chest going and she looked up.

The gallery was dark. The quarter-light did not reach it. There was the rail, and the black above the rail, and nothing moved there, and nothing breathed that she could hear over her own breathing. She stood and looked at it for a long time. She reached out, carefully, the way one puts a hand into cold water, and there was nothing: no presence, no mind, no shape of anyone, only the Temple's great slow hum and, far above, the particular quiet of a place where thousands of people were asleep.

And something else. Not in the gallery. Not anywhere she could point to. Something at the edge of what she could feel that had not been there last night, the way a new piece of furniture is not there and then is, and the whole room is arranged around it.
