Something that was shielded very hard, and very well, and by someone who had done it for a long time. Something that was, she thought, holding on.

She thumbed the sabers off. The hum died. She stood in the quarter-light in a room that was empty, that she was sure was empty, that she could not make herself believe was empty, and she put the two training sabers back on the rack, exactly where they had been, and picked up her boots.

In the doorway she looked back once at the gallery. There was nothing there. She told herself nothing.

She went down the service stair in her socks, and along the dormitory corridor with the night-glow making everything the colour of weak tea, and in past nineteen sleeping children, and she put her boots under her bunk and got in and lay with her feet not reaching the end of the mattress and her hands flat on her stomach and her heart going, exactly as it had gone the night before, exactly as it had gone in the hall with a shaking hand round her wrist.

She did not sleep.

She knew what she knew, and it was this, and it was only this, and she would let it be nothing else: a bell had rung, and something had come into the Temple that night, and it was wearing Skywalker's face.


## Chapter 4: The Garden

Every tenth day the Chancellor saw the boy.

It had been so since Anakin was nine and newly scrubbed, standing in the red office with Qui-Gon's death still in him like a swallowed stone, and the man behind the desk had come round it to crouch to his height and say that he hoped they might be friends. Six years of tenth days. Anakin had been back nine of them, and had spent most of those nine days knowing that the tenth was coming, the way a man in a cell knows the hour the door will open.

He dressed for it as a boy would. That took thought. The fifteen-year-old he was wearing had owned, he found, a better tunic kept for the Senate and a worse one kept for everything else, and he put on the better and found the collar sat wrong and left it. He looked in the glass over the basin and a narrow sandy child looked back with a braid behind his ear and no scar, and he tried the face he would need, the face of a boy who was a little proud of where he was going and would not say so, and the child in the glass did it well enough that Anakin had to look away.

Obi-Wan was waiting in the common room with his cloak already on.

"You needn't," Anakin said.

"I'm going that way." He was not. He had never, in the six years of tenth days that this body remembered, been going that way; he had always been going that way. "Dex owes me a conversation."

"Dex is in CoCo Town."

"Which is, if you consult a map, in the direction of the Senate. Loosely." He held the door. "Come along."

They went down through the Temple in the thin early light, past the Thousand Fountains where the water-noise came out into the corridor like a draught, down the Processional Way between the pillars with the city opening in front of them, and Anakin kept his shield at the depth he had settled on in the first week, a boy's sulk, a door pulled to and not latched, and felt Obi-Wan not quite lean on it. That was new. The Obi-Wan he remembered had, at this age, left the bond alone the way one leaves a cat alone. This one touched it, lightly, every few minutes, as if checking that a pocket still had a thing in it.

Anakin added it to the list in his head. *Things that are different.* The list was getting long.

They took an air-taxi to the edge of the Senate District because that was what they always did, and then they walked, because that was also what they always did; Obi-Wan said once that a Jedi should arrive on his feet, and had never said why. The district rose round them. The dome of the Senate sat on the skyline like a held breath. Monument Plaza was already busy with aides in grey and clerks in blue and a Rodian selling something hot out of a cart, and the air smelt of it and of speeder exhaust, and far above the lanes were full and moving and the light came down between them in bars.

Anakin had walked this plaza under a black cloak with the crowd going out from him like water from a dropped stone. He walked it now as a thin boy whose master had to shorten his stride, and a woman with a stack of flimsi walked straight into him and said *sorry, love* and went on. He stood for a moment feeling where her shoulder had struck him. It did not matter. It was only that nobody had walked into him in twenty-three years.

"Anakin."

"I'm here."

"You looked, for a moment, as though you were not." Obi-Wan's tone was easy. His hand, Anakin noticed, had gone to his belt and come away from it. "What are you going to say to him?"

"Whatever he asks me. He asks about classes. He asks about you."

"I'm aware of what he asks about me." They had come to the foot of the steps. The Senate Office Building went up above them, pale and ribbed, a thousand windows, and somewhere high on the far side of it a red room with a man in it. The Senate Guards at the doors wore the blue of the old Republic and held their pikes like men who had never had to use them. "You know that you may leave whenever you wish. You need not wait to be dismissed."

"I know."

"If you were unwell. If you were tired. If you simply did not care to stay."

"Master." Anakin made the boy laugh. It came out thin. "I've been doing this since I was nine."

"Yes," Obi-Wan said. "You have."

He stood at the bottom of the steps and did not go up them. He had never gone up them. Anakin had known that as a fact for six years of this body's memory and understood it now for the first time as a thing with a shape: a man of thirty-one who had been in the Senate Office Building on a hundred errands and had never once walked his Padawan to the Chancellor's door, because to walk him to the door was to be asked to come in, and to be asked to come in was to be asked, pleasantly, by a Chancellor with all the time in the world, to wait outside. He had tried. Anakin remembered that too, now that he was looking for it: a request to the Council, two years ago, that the audiences be reduced; a kind note from the Chancellor's office regretting that the boy was such a comfort to him; Obi-Wan's face reading it. The Council had sided with the comfort.

"An hour," Obi-Wan said. "I'll be back here in an hour."

"Dex might keep you."

"Dex has never kept anyone. It is one of his few failings." He put his hand, briefly, on the back of Anakin's neck, where the braid was, which he had not done when Anakin was nine or twelve or fourteen, and took it away. "Go on."

Anakin went up the steps. At the top he looked back, because a boy would, and Obi-Wan was still there with the plaza moving round him, not looking at the diner he was not going to, not looking at anything, a Jedi in a brown cloak standing very still at the foot of a building as though he had been told to guard it and had not been told from what.

* * *

Sly Moore met him in the outer office. She was as he remembered her, pale and smooth and hairless and entirely unreadable, and she inclined her head the exact depth she had always inclined it and said, "Padawan Skywalker. He's expecting you," and Anakin realised with a small cold shock that she would be standing on the far side of a door, seven years from now, when a black-suited thing came through it from the other way.

He did not let that into his face. The doors opened and he went in.

Red walls. The wide window with the city in it and, far across the skyline, small and white, the Temple. The desk, and the two chairs angled towards each other on the near side of it, because the Chancellor had never once in six years spoken to the boy across his desk. A jug on a side table with condensation on it. And the man, rising, in the dark robes of his office, fifty-eight years old, with his hair greying out of its red and his face creased and kindly and his hands already out.

"Anakin. My dear boy. Come in, come in. Sit. You look taller. Is that possible in ten days? I think it must be."

"Chancellor."

"Sit. I've had them bring the muja juice. I remember you don't care for the tea. Master Kenobi's influence has its limits, it seems." He poured it himself. He had always poured it himself. The jug tilted, and the juice came out pale orange and cold, and he handed the glass across and sat, and arranged his robe, and smiled, and the touch began.

Anakin had known it would. He had told himself, in the taxi, on the steps, in the outer office, that he knew exactly what it would feel like and that the knowing was his armour. It was. It was also true that he had never before felt it from this side with his eyes open, and the first thing he had to do, with the glass cold in his hand and the Chancellor asking whether the astrogation module had improved since last time, was not shut it out.

It did not push. That was the thing Jedi never understood about it, in the years when Jedi were still alive to misunderstand: they expected a Sith to force a mind, and they shielded against force. Sidious did not force. He strolled. The touch came in at the edge of Anakin's mind the way a man comes in at his own garden gate, dry and patient and proprietary, in no hurry at all, and it moved along the paths it knew. Anakin had lived under that touch for twenty-three years. He knew it the way a man knows the weight of a hand that has rested on his shoulder every day of his adult life: the exact dryness, the exact warmth, the way it paused at things it liked.

He gave it things to like.

He had built the surface over nine nights, lying in the small room with Obi-Wan's presence the far side of the wall, and he laid it out now the way a man lays out a table for a guest who must not go into the kitchen. In front: a boy's irritation, broad and real-feeling, at having been made to go back to the Shii-Cho forms that morning because Drallig said his footwork had gone lazy; the resentment had edges; Sidious liked edges. Beside it, a speeder. A racing speeder in a showroom window on the Temple approach, a Mobquet with a twin-cell, which this body had in fact stood in front of for a quarter of an hour the week before Anakin arrived in it, so that the wanting was in the muscle memory and the touch found it warm. Beside that, lower, a hollow he did not have to invent: his mother. He let it stay generic. A boy's hollow, the shape of a woman in a doorway, nothing in it with a name or a date.

And beside that, because a boy of fifteen with nothing of that kind in him would have been a thing for the touch to pause at, a girl. He had thought hard about the girl. She needed to exist. He had chosen a Padawan a year older, a human girl called Mirren Dask who sat two rows in front of him in Vos-Kerrick's politics class and who he had never, in either life, spoken to, and he had given her a way of pushing her hair back that the body could plausibly have watched, and a small clumsy heat around her name. It was the most careful work he had done since Endor. It was embarrassing. It was meant to be.

The touch moved through it all like a man walking his borders in the evening. It touched the resentment, and warmed. It touched the speeder, and was amused. It touched the mother, and lingered, as it always had, as it had lingered on every wound he had ever let it see. It touched the girl, and Anakin felt something that in a different face would have been a smile.

"— the trade bills again, I'm afraid," the Chancellor was saying. "I don't know why I tell you these things. Because you listen, I suppose, and no one else does. The Outer Rim Free Trade Act, which is neither free nor, if the Trade Federation has its way, about trade. Forty-one systems want the tariff schedules revised and the Core wants them left alone and Nute Gunray wants, as ever, whatever is worst for everyone. We've had it on the floor three times. Do you know what it is to have a thing on the floor three times, Anakin? It is to have it on the floor a fourth."

"Does it pass?"

"Something passes. Something always passes. Whether it is the thing one wanted is a separate question." He drank his own juice, which he had poured a quarter-glass of for form's sake. "And of course the reformers are at it. Senator Amidala's faction. Very energetic."

Anakin's hand did not move on the glass.

He had known the name might be said. He had known it with his whole body, in the taxi, on the steps; he had built nothing for it, because there was nothing that could be built for it, because the only thing in him that could be given to that name was the whole of him. She was twenty. She was in a building he could see from this window. She had never been to Geonosis, or stood on a landing platform in the fire with her hand on her belly, or said his name in a room on Polis Massa he had not been in and had seen afterwards, every night, for twenty-three years. She was a Senator of Naboo in her second year, and she was alive, and the touch was in his mind, moving, looking at what the name did.

He let it find what a boy would have. That was the only way. A boy of fifteen who had met a queen when he was nine and told her, in a hovel on Tatooine, that she was an angel, would flush at her name and hate that he had flushed, and so Anakin let the body flush, let the heat come up his neck into the roots of his hair where the braid was, let a small sharp humiliation sit on the top of the surface like a dropped coin, and the touch found it and was delighted, and underneath it, where the touch could not go, Anakin held the rest of her in both hands and did not make a sound.

"Energetic," he said. It came out thick. That was all right. That was the boy.

"Oh, very. She and Senator Organa of Alderaan, and that very composed young woman from Chandrila. Mothma. The three of them have found each other, which is what idealists do, and they have a bill of their own, which is what idealists do next. An anti-corruption measure. Full disclosure of corporate contributions. Quite impossible, of course; the Senate would strike it down nine to one; but they're young and they say it beautifully, and the HoloNet loves them. I confess I rather love them myself. One needs the young to be unreasonable. It is how one finds out what is possible." He smiled. "You should hear her speak. Perhaps one day you shall."

*I have heard her speak,* Anakin thought, behind the wall, and said, "The Council says Padawans shouldn't go to the Senate floor."

"The Council says a great many things." The touch moved on. "And on the subject of people who say things beautifully. Have you seen the Count's latest?"

"Count?"

"Dooku. Of Serenno. You'll have heard of him; Master Kenobi's master was his Padawan, which I suppose makes him a sort of grandfather to you, in the Jedi way of things." The Chancellor reached to the desk without rising and came back with a slim bound thing with a plain cover and no title. "He publishes these. Privately; he can afford to. This one is on the Senate's ethics, or want of them. He is a very fine writer. He argues that the Republic is rotten, which is true, and that the Jedi serve the rot, which has a certain force, and that a man of conscience should say so, which he does, in sentences I would give a great deal to be able to write. I disagree with every word and I have read it twice."

He held it out. Anakin took it, because a boy would, and his hands did not shake, and in the place behind the wall there was a hangar on Geonosis and a curved hilt coming round faster than a fifteen-year-old could have followed and then nothing where his forearm had been, and a room on a ship years later and a man on his knees with his hands bound in front of him and an old man's voice saying, kindly, *do it*. He turned the pamphlet over. The Count's seal was on the back, small and elegant.

"He left the Order," Anakin said.

"He did. Some years ago. Over, I'm told, a difference of conscience. Those are the only kind of differences worth leaving anything over." The touch had gone back along the path to the speeder, idly, as a man will pick a leaf off a thing he has already looked at. "He writes to me, occasionally. We are both, in our different ways, critics of the thing we serve. I have an advantage over him, of course."

"Chancellor?"

"I'm leaving." He spread his hands. "My term. The law is quite clear, and I have never been one to argue with the law, and when the time comes I shall go back to Naboo and grow something. I find I'm looking forward to it. One cannot hold an office like this for ever; it wears a man thin; and there are days, Anakin, when I would give the whole of it for a garden and an afternoon." He said it with real warmth. He said it, Anakin thought, with real wanting, and that was the horror of him and always had been: that the lie and the truth came out of the same mouth in the same voice and even the mouth did not always know which was which. "But not yet. There's work to be done first. There is always, it seems, a crisis of some kind to be seen through before one is permitted to be tired."

*Yes,* Anakin thought. *I know which crisis.* He drank his juice.

And the touch, which had wandered back along the edge of the garden where the girl was, where the mother was, where it had always stopped, because there had never been anything beyond that to stop at, went past them.

Anakin felt it go. He had known it would. The surface was furnished; it had to be; a bare surface was a confession, a boy with nothing in his head was a locked room with a sign on it. But a furnished surface had an edge, and beyond the edge there had been, for six years, the open country of a child's mind, unfenced, going down and down to things the child did not know he had. The touch had walked there every tenth day. It had taken its time. It had planted what it planted.

Now it came to the edge of the furniture and there was a wall.

He had built it under Sidious's own hand, over two decades, in a chamber on a Star Destroyer with a respirator counting his breaths, because a thing that had been inside your mind would be inside it again, and the only privacy a man in a suit had was the privacy he could hold between his teeth. It was not a Jedi's wall. A Jedi's shield was a clean thing, a pane of glass, a held breath; he had felt a hundred of them crack. This was old. This was scarred. It had been built in layers by someone who knew how the other side worked and had mortared every course with hate, and it did not look like anything a fifteen-year-old could have made, because it was not.

The touch stopped.

It did not press. He had been afraid of that and had been wrong to be; pressing was not how the man worked, not here, not in this office with the window open on the city. It stopped at the wall the way a man stops at a wall in his own garden where there was no wall the week before. It stood there. It was very still, and in its stillness Anakin felt, with a clarity that twenty-three years had given him and nothing else could have, that the man in the chair across from him was not angry, and was not alarmed, and was for the first time in a very long time surprised, and that the surprise was giving him pleasure.

"— Master Kenobi well?" the Chancellor said. His voice had not changed at all. "I never see him. I think he would prefer that I never see him. How is he?"

Anakin heard himself from a long way off.

"He's well. My Master has me on forms again."

He heard it as it left him. Not the words. The words were a Padawan's, any Padawan's, in any corridor of the Temple; a boy said *my Master* forty times a day. It was the weight. It was the way the two words had gone down at the end, flat and low and complete, the cadence of a man on one knee on a black floor with his head bowed, saying a thing he had said ten thousand times to the only person he had ever said it to. *Yes, my Master.* For twenty-three years those two words in that voice had meant: I am yours. He had said them in this room. Not this room. A room that was this room, later, with the red gone to black.

The Chancellor's eyes rested on him.

They were pale and watery and kind, the eyes of a tired man who had been good to a boy, and they did not move, and nothing moved in them, and Anakin had seen those eyes without the kindness in them and knew precisely how little the kindness weighed and how little it took to lift it off. The touch was still at the wall. The eyes were on his face. Two seconds. Three. Behind the wall a man who had killed this man held perfectly still, and on the surface a boy flushed again, at nothing, at the attention, and looked down at his glass.

"Forms," the Chancellor said gently. "At your age. He is very thorough."

"He says my footwork's lazy."

"Then I am sure it is, and I am sure it will not be by the next time I see you." The touch withdrew from the wall. It did not withdraw from his mind; it went back along the paths, unhurried, and stood a moment at the mother, and a moment at the girl, and let itself out at the gate. "You must forgive an old man's curiosity, Anakin. You seem — quieter. These last ten days. Has something happened at the Temple?"

"No, Chancellor."

"No one has been unkind to you?"

"No."

"Because you may tell me. You know that. Whatever it is, and whatever the Council thinks of it, and whatever Master Kenobi, with the very best will in the galaxy, does not understand." The hand on the shoulder; every sentence a hand on the shoulder. "I have no children. I shall have no children; the office does not permit it, and I was never the sort of man who would have managed it well. But I have you, every tenth day, and I find I look forward to it more than to anything else in a very long week. You must let me be of use."

"I'm fine," Anakin said, as a boy says it. "I'm just tired. Obi-Wan says I've grown four centimetres."

"Then that explains everything. Growing is exhausting. I remember it, just." The Chancellor rose, which meant that it was over, and Anakin rose with him, and the man took the pamphlet gently out of his hand and put it back on the desk and then, as he always did, walked him to the door with a hand not quite touching his back. "Give Master Kenobi my regards. Tell him he need not stand in the plaza. There is a perfectly good anteroom."

Anakin looked at him.

"He has stood in Monument Plaza for an hour every tenth day for six years," the Chancellor said, with great fondness. "It is one of the things about him that I most admire. Off you go."

* * *

Obi-Wan was at the foot of the steps. He had not been to CoCo Town. He had not, Anakin thought, moved: he was standing where he had been standing, in the same place in the stone, with the same plaza going round him and the Rodian's cart a little further along, and when he saw Anakin come out he did not look relieved, which was how Anakin knew what the hour had cost him.

"Dex?" Anakin said.

"Was out." Obi-Wan fell in beside him. He did not ask. He never asked, on the walk back, and Anakin had taken it for six years of this body's memory as indifference and knew now what it was: a man who could not bear to hear the answer and could not bear to be seen not asking, and so said nothing, and walked. "You'll want something to eat."

"I had juice."

"Juice," Obi-Wan said, in a tone that put the word on a list.

They walked out of the district the way they had come in, under the lanes, with the light in bars. Anakin walked with the boy's loose stride and let the boy's tiredness be in his shoulders, and it was not entirely performance. His legs ached. His hands were cold where the glass had been. He kept the surface up, because the surface was a habit now and would need to be a habit for a long time, and he kept the bond thinned to a sulk, and he did not look back at the building, and he felt it.

Not the touch. The touch was gone; it had gone at the gate, the way it always went, and the Chancellor was behind a thousand windows doing whatever the Chancellor did in the hours of a very long week. This was something else. This was the thing a house feels when a man has come up the path in the night and tried the door, and found it locked, and gone away without knocking. Nothing was broken. Nothing had been taken. There was only the handle, which had been turned, and which had held, and which would be turned again.

He had been prepared for the wall to be found. He had told himself, over nine nights, that it was the one thing that could not be helped: that the child's mind had been open and was now closed, and the only question was whether it was found closed by a man who thought it was a child's wall, or by a man who knew what a wall was. He had hoped. The hope had been a boy's hope and he had let it be, because he had so little else to be a boy with.

The man knew what a wall was. The man had been pleased.

Anakin walked. A speeder went over low and the downdraught pushed the hair off his forehead and Obi-Wan said something about the tariff schedules and Anakin said *mm*, and his hands were cold, and he thought: he will send someone. He will not come himself; he never came himself; he will look at the wall and he will think about who could have built it in a child and he will think of the only other man in the galaxy who could have, and he will send that man to look. And that man will come and put his hand on my mind and find in it a hate with his own name on it, with a history it cannot have, and he will go back and say so.

And then.

He did not know and then. That was the thing about being here: he did not know and then. He had spent twenty-three years in a story he already knew the end of and he was standing now in the Senate District in a boy's boots in a story that had, in a red room, an hour ago, stopped being that story, and he could not see past the corner, and the not-seeing came up through him like water through a floor.

*Blue light. The whole of the room was blue light and his body was the colour of it, lifted, the lightning going in at the fingers and out at the spine, and the sound was his own voice coming apart. The old man's hands. The old man's face in the light of what came out of his hands, not angry, interested, the eyes pale and watery and kind. On the floor the boy, curled, screaming, holding his own hand. And then no floor; and then the weight, the astonishing weight of him, lighter than a man should be and heavier than anything Anakin had ever lifted, the robe and the bones in it and the lightning going through the armour into the arms that held him and the respirator counting, four, three, and the rail of the shaft coming up under them both, and the old man turning in his arms to look at him, and the look, the look at the end, which had not been fear.*

"Anakin."

The plaza. The light in bars. He had stopped walking. Obi-Wan was a step ahead and had turned, and his face had gone very still, the way it went when there was a thing in the room he did not want to see, and his hand was out, not touching, two centimetres from Anakin's arm.

"I'm here," Anakin said.

"You said that this morning."

"It was true this morning."

Obi-Wan looked at him for a moment longer. Then he took the hand back and put it in his sleeve and said, in the dry voice, "He asked after me."

"He sends his regards. He says you needn't stand in the plaza. There's an anteroom."

Something went over Obi-Wan's face and was gone. "How kind of him," he said. "Come along. You need to eat." And he walked, and after a moment Anakin walked with him, a boy and his master going home through the Senate District in the middle of a bright morning, and far above them the lanes were full and moving and nobody looked down.


## Chapter 5: Tea and Lists

The kettle had a note it reached just before it boiled, a thin rising sound like a question, and Obi-Wan Kenobi had learned to take it off the ring on that note and not on the boil, because the boil was too late and the tea went bitter. He had learned it in this kitchen. He had learned it, he would have said if anyone had asked, over four years of mornings, and no one had asked.

He poured. The leaves turned over once in the water and settled. Outside the window the sky over the Senate District was the colour of a knife left out in the rain, and the lanes were already full, and the Temple's shadow lay across the city to the west in a long five-fingered hand that would shorten all morning and be gone by noon. He had stood at this window often enough to know where the shadow fell at each hour. He did not think this was a useful thing to know. He knew it anyway.

The boy's door was shut. It had been shut when Obi-Wan woke and it would stay shut until the second bell, because Anakin now slept until the second bell precisely and came out dressed, with his braid tidy, and said "Good morning, Master," and ate what was put in front of him. He had done this for eleven days. Before that, for four years, he had come out at any hour between the first bell and the third in whatever he had slept in, with his hair standing up and his braid chewed at the end, and had eaten standing, and had said "Morning" to the room at large, and once, memorably, to the kettle.

Obi-Wan drank his tea and kept his accounts.

Every master kept accounts of a kind. It was a discipline, like breathing in fours; a way of looking at the day before it looked at you. He began where he always began, with the small things, because the small things were the ones that could be moved.

The letters. Three years now. A courier on the Temple staff who did not ask, a flimsi packet every month or so from a moisture farm on a world he would not think about before his second cup, and a reply that the boy wrote at this table with his tongue between his teeth and did not show him. He had argued for the letters in front of the Council in the voice he used for treaties. *A wound in the open heals, Masters. A wound under a bandage goes bad.* Master Windu had said that the Order had managed attachment for a thousand years without his assistance. Yoda had said nothing for a long time and then said, *Try it, we will,* in the voice that meant he was not sure, and Obi-Wan had gone back to his quarters and sat on the edge of his bed with his hands between his knees for an hour, because he had not expected to win, and because of what he would have done if he had lost.

The classes. Two years. Drallig had wanted a senior Padawan to help with the initiates and Obi-Wan had put Anakin forward before anyone else could, and said, *It is good for him,* and smiled, and had not been able to explain to Drallig or to himself why he had needed it so badly. The boy had hated it for a month. Then he had stopped hating it. Obi-Wan had gone to watch once, from the gallery, and seen Anakin on one knee with his hands round a six-year-old's hands on a training hilt, saying *Like this. No, like this, you're strangling it,* and had left before the class ended and walked round the Room of a Thousand Fountains twice without seeing any of it.

The Chancellor. Every tenth day for six years. He had never managed to end it. He had managed, by a long accounting of small refusals, to see that it was never a whole day: a morning audience, a luncheon once that he had cut short with a mission that did not strictly exist, a tour of the Works that he had declined on the boy's behalf with such courtesy that the Chancellor's aide had thanked him for it. He had stood in Monument Plaza in rain and in the white glare of midsummer and in one memorable grit-storm that had come off the industrial districts, and he had never once gone up the steps, and he had never once let the boy come back to the Temple alone.

Yesterday he had meant to go up the steps. He had decided it in the night: he would walk the boy to the door this once, and nod to the blue-robed guards, and wait in the anteroom that had been offered him with such kindness. He had got as far as the foot of the steps. His legs had done something he had no word for. Not weakness. A refusal, from the knee down, as if the body had read the stair and declined it. He had stood there at the bottom, not going up, long enough for Anakin to reach the top, turn round and look back at him, and see whatever there was to see.

He put that in the accounts under things to do better. There were a great many things under that heading.

There were other entries, further down, that he did not go into this morning. A name he had looked for in the Archives and not found, and the look Madame Nu had given him over the top of her spectacles. A friend in CoCo Town who had been asked a strange question over a plate of eggs and had answered it. A list, in a drawer, in a hand he had trained to look unlike his own, that he added to seldom and did not reread. Those were not small things, and the morning was for small things.

He drank his tea.

The bond he put nowhere, because it did not fit.

He would have described it, to a healer, as a presence at the back of the mind. A mood more than a voice. For four years it had been a boy's weather: sulks and bright patches and the occasional thunderstorm, and under them, always, the thing he had no better word for than *warmth*, a lamp left on in another room. Ten nights ago, at some hour before dawn, it had closed. Not faded. Not gone quiet, as it did when the boy slept. Closed, like a door, with a sound he had felt in his teeth, and on the other side of the door: nothing. Not a bad feeling. Nothing. A wall where a window had been.

He had lain still and listened and told himself every reasonable thing. By morning the door had opened a crack and let out exactly enough of a sulk to be convincing, and it had stayed at that crack for eleven days, and he knew the width of it to the hair, because he had never in his life stopped measuring anything.

He had told no one. He had, in four years of mornings at this window, acquired a certain fluency in telling no one.

The second bell went. The boy's door opened.

"Good morning, Master."

"Good morning. There's tea."

"Thank you." Anakin sat. He was in his tunic, with his braid lying flat behind his right ear and his hair wet from the refresher. He was fifteen and had grown five centimetres since the turn of the year and none of his clothes fitted at the wrists. He took the cup in both hands, the way he had never taken anything, and looked at the steam. He had the face of a boy who had slept, which was not the same as a boy who had.

Obi-Wan looked at his hands. He did it before he could stop himself doing it. Two hands, brown from last month's sun, the right one with a healing scrape across the knuckles from the speeder he was not supposed to be rebuilding. Two hands on a cup. He looked away.

"You have Drallig at the fourth hour," he said.

"I know."

"Bellas is back from Corellia."

"I know that too." A boy's flat tone, well done. "Are you coming?"

"I may. Eat something, my friend. Drallig doesn't feed you."

The boy ate. Obi-Wan watched him do it, and the shadow of the Temple went on shortening across the city, and neither of them said anything about steps.

* * *

The summons came by a Temple Guard, which was Yoda's way of being discreet, and it said only that Master Yoda would be glad of a word before the third hour, in the small room below the Council chamber, if Master Kenobi could spare it.

He could. He went up the long stair of the eastern spire with his hands in his sleeves, past the windows where the city turned slowly as the stair turned, and found the old Master alone with his stick across his knees and the room's one window open to the wind.

"Obi-Wan."

"Master."

"Sit, sit." The chair was too low, as it always was, and he sat in it, as he always did. Yoda looked at him for a while with the look that had been assessing Padawans since before the Republic had its present name. "Tired, you look."

"I'm told it's the beard."

"Hm." The ears moved. "Ten nights ago. Where were you?"

He had known, of course, that it would be this. He had known it when the Guard spoke. He said, in the voice he used for everything, "In my bed, I think. It seems the sort of place I would have been."

"Woke, did you?"

"I'm a light sleeper."

"Slept, the younglings did. Every clan. Woke, none of them." Yoda's claws tapped the stick. "Felt it, I did, all the same. A shadow, entering the Temple. A bell, struck, where no bell was. And here the shadow is still. New, it is. Close. Not an old darkness. Not a thing from outside." The green eyes did not move from him. "A stain, Obi-Wan. Of the dark side. Here, in the house."

The wind came in and moved the hem of Obi-Wan's robe. He let it.

He thought: I could say it. There was a door, Master, and it closed, and I have been standing outside it for eleven days knocking very quietly. He could see the sentence whole. He could see Yoda hearing it. He could see, with great clarity, as if it were a holo thrown on the wall opposite, the thing that would happen next: the Council in its circle; the boy in the middle of it; twelve faces turned on a child the way a city turns on a fire. He had watched that circle look at that boy before. Not for this. For less. He had watched what being looked at did to him.

He thought: I do not know that it is him. That was true. He held on to it the way a man holds a rail.

"A shadow," he said. "In the Temple."

"Say it twice, you need not."

"I only wondered whether you meant a person or a place. Shadows have both."

"A person, I think." The old eyes narrowed. "Your Padawan. Slept badly, that night. Said so, you did, to the healers' roster. Written down, it is."

"Anakin has slept badly since he was nine, Master. If the Council means to investigate every night he's had a dream, I'll need a larger datapad." He heard himself and did not like it; it was too easy, and Yoda was not easy. He made his voice slower. "He's fifteen. He has nightmares. He has them more often around the Chancellor's audiences, which I have said before, in this building, to no particular effect."

"Hm."

"If you are asking whether I have felt the dark side in my own Padawan—" He stopped, because he had set himself a rule, years ago, at this window or one like it, that he would not lie to this particular person outright if there were any other road. He looked for the road. "I would not call what I have felt in him that."

Which was true. He would not call it that. He would not call it anything. He had been very careful not to.

Yoda was quiet. Then: "Look, the Council will. All of it. Every corner of this house. The healers, I will ask. Master Windu already is asking." A small sound, not a laugh. "Hide, a thing cannot, that is close and new. Known, it will be. Only a question of when." He tilted his head. "Near, stay. Again I will want you. When I send, come."

"Then I hope the Council finds what it's looking for," Obi-Wan said, and stood, because his legs wanted to, "and I hope it is kind to whatever that turns out to be."

He was at the door when Yoda said, "Obi-Wan."

He turned.

"Your door, also, I felt close. That night."

The wind. The city turning. He put a hand to his face and found the beard short, and took the hand away.

"I'm a light sleeper," he said again, and went down the stair.

* * *

Drallig's hall at the fourth hour smelled of sweat and ozone and the particular dust of a floor that had been fallen on for a thousand years. Obi-Wan took the gallery, which ran round three sides above the sparring floor and was built, he had always thought, for exactly this: for masters to watch their Padawans and pretend they were not. There were two other masters up there, and a knot of initiates in sand-coloured tunics who had slipped in and would be found and sent out, and he nodded and said nothing and leaned on the rail.

Bellas was on the floor already. Seventeen, tall, with the kind of shoulders that arrive early and the kind of confidence that never leaves; Drallig's favourite, which Drallig denied and everyone knew. He had come back from Corellia with a new way of standing and was showing it to the room. Anakin stood opposite him with his training saber held the way a boy holds a thing he is good at and wants to be seen being good at: a little high, a little tight. He was a head shorter than Bellas and his tunic hem was uneven where he had caught it on something, and when he settled his feet on the mark he did it twice, because a boy who is growing does not trust his feet.

"Begin," said Drallig.

Obi-Wan had watched Anakin fight for four years. He knew what he was watching the way he knew the kettle's note. The boy fought forward. He fought with his whole face. He took a hit the way he took everything, as a personal remark, and came back harder, and lost his temper around the fourth exchange and won or lost on the strength of it. It was ugly and it was gifted and Drallig had said, in Obi-Wan's hearing, that in ten years it would be something no one in the Temple could stand in front of.

This was not that.

This was a boy fighting well. Correctly. A beat behind Bellas and never more than a beat, so that it looked like a good Padawan trying hard against a better one. The blade work was clean. The feet were clean. The face—the face was a boy's face, mouth open, breathing hard, flushed to the ears; it was so exactly a boy's face that Obi-Wan found himself watching only it, and so he saw the moment, in the third exchange, when Bellas overreached on a cut and left his whole right side open from hip to shoulder, and the boy's blade was already moving, and stopped, and went somewhere else.

Nobody else saw it. Drallig might have. Obi-Wan looked along the rail and Drallig was watching the floor with his arms folded and his face shut, and that could have meant anything.

Bellas took the point. Anakin went down on one knee with the sting of it, as a boy does, and got up, and said "Again?" with the right amount of breath in it, and they went again, and Obi-Wan stood with his hands on the rail and thought: he is losing. He is choosing to. He is choosing how.

He did not know why that frightened him more than if the boy had taken Bellas's head off.

He had seen Anakin lose. Badly, with his whole face, with a fist against the wall afterwards. He had never seen him lose quietly. He had never seen him *give* a point, and he thought, with the part of him that kept accounts, that if he had a list of everything in this boy he had never seen before, it was eleven days old and already longer than his arm.

The fourth exchange. Bellas was enjoying himself now, and the enjoyment made him generous, and generosity made him big: he came in with a high overhand stroke, the Corellian thing, all shoulder, the kind of blow that is meant to be seen coming and to arrive anyway.

Anakin took it one-handed.

He did not step. His left foot moved perhaps the width of a finger. The blade came up turned outward, the elbow tucked, the whole weight of Bellas's shoulder caught and sent down and sideways with no more effort than a man closing a door on a draught, and for half a second the boy stood inside the other's reach with his saber low and his free hand open at his side and his face entirely still, and the stillness was not a boy's. It was not anyone's. It was the stillness of something that had already decided and was waiting, out of some private courtesy, for the other party to realise it.

Then he stepped back, and stumbled on the step, and Bellas's blade caught him on the shoulder and he yelped like a fifteen-year-old and sat down.

Obi-Wan's breath had stopped.

He noticed that it had stopped as a fact about the room, as if someone else's breath had stopped nearby and he was being polite about it. His right hand had come off the rail and gone to his face, to his beard, and it had reached for something longer than what it found, and it stayed there against his jaw, cupped round a shape that was not there. His eyes had gone to the boy's right hand. The boy's right hand was on the floor, flat, taking his weight, with the healing scrape across the knuckles. Two hands. He looked at them both. He made himself.

He had seen that parry before.

He did not think where. He noticed himself not thinking it, the way he had noticed his breath. There was a place in him that he kept, as a man keeps a locked room in a house he otherwise lives in, and it was from that room that the recognition had come, clean and immediate and total, and he did not open the door to see what else was in there. He stood on the gallery with his hand on his face and the door shut, and below him Drallig said, "Enough. Bellas, your footwork is a disgrace, Corellia has made you vain. Skywalker—"

A pause.

"Skywalker. That block. Where did you learn it?"

The boy, sitting on the floor, looked up. His face was red and wet and open, and he said, with his breath still short, "Which one, Master?"

Drallig looked at him for a moment. Then he looked up at the gallery, straight at Obi-Wan, and raised his eyebrows half a centimetre, which in Cin Drallig was a speech.

"I didn't teach him that," Drallig said.

"No," Obi-Wan said. His voice came out perfectly level. "Nor did I."

Which was true, and was the kind of truth he had got very good at.

Anakin got to his feet, and bowed to Bellas, and bowed to Drallig, and did not look at the gallery at all, and Obi-Wan thought that this was the most frightening thing the boy had done all morning, because for four years he had looked at the gallery first.

* * *

He went back up the eastern spire because Yoda had said *when I send* and had not yet sent, and because the stair was long and he wanted it to be long, and because he did not want to go home and find the boy at the table with his tongue between his teeth, writing a letter he would not show him.

The small room below the Council chamber was empty. The window stood open as it had that morning. He stood at it with his hands in his sleeves and let the wind have his face.

He thought about the accounts. He thought that it was possible to keep very careful accounts for a very long time and have them come out, in the end, to a number you had not expected. He thought that there was a flaw in the whole enterprise that he had never properly faced, which was that you could only change the things you could see, and a man standing at a window, whatever he thought he knew, could not see what was coming up behind him on the stair.

Something moved on the ledge outside.

He looked. He had expected a pigeon, or one of the Senate District hawks that nested in the spires and brought the younglings feathers. It was neither. It was a bird he did not know: owl-shaped, broad-faced, green, a deep soft green from crown to tail like moss on the north side of a stone, with two long feathers lying back from its head and eyes that were, he saw, as it turned them on him, not an owl's eyes at all. They were yellow and whole and very old. It sat on the stone ledge a hand's breadth outside the open window and looked at him, and he looked at it, and neither of them moved.

He did not know the bird. He was certain of that; he knew what he knew, he kept lists. He had never seen a creature like it in the Temple or on this world or anywhere he could name.

And the feeling came anyway.

It came up through him the way the refusal had come up through his legs at the foot of the Senate steps, from below, without permission, and it was not dread. That was the extraordinary thing. He had been braced for dread all day; he had a long familiarity with dread and would have known how to hold it. This was the other thing. This was old and bright and enormous, like light off water a long way away, like the first warm day after a winter, like something he had once been given and had been sure he would not be given again. It had a shape and the shape was a person's and he could not see the face.

His eyes stung. He had not told them to.

He stood there, a Jedi Master of thirty-one with his hands in his sleeves and his eyes wet, looking at a green bird, and thought of nothing. He could not have said what he thought of. There was no name on it. There was only the brightness, and the sting, and under them both a feeling he recognised from somewhere with more certainty than he had recognised the parry, and liked less: the feeling of a door he had closed and locked and leaned against, moving, very slightly, in its frame.

The bird blinked, once, slowly, the way a thing blinks that has all the time in the world.

Then it opened its wings, which were wider than seemed possible, and stepped off the ledge into the wind, and was a green mark against the city, and then was nothing.

He stayed at the window for a while. The wind dried his face. The shadow of the Temple had gone to nothing under the noon and the city lay flat and bright and unshadowed to every horizon, and somewhere under it a boy was walking home from Drallig's hall with a stung shoulder and two hands and a face he had been using for eleven days, and somewhere a Council was beginning, in its slow, thorough, well-meaning way, to look.

He found that he had taken his hand out of his sleeve and put it on the stone of the sill, and that it was not quite steady, and he looked at it with interest, as at someone else's.

"Not again," he said, to the empty room.

It was not loud. The wind took most of it.

"Whatever this is," said Obi-Wan Kenobi. "Not again."


## Chapter 6: Letters from Tatooine

The courier had been and gone by the time Anakin got back to the quarters, and Obi-Wan was holding the letter out before the door had finished closing.

"This came for you."

He said it the way he said that the tea was made, or that the Council had moved a briefing. He did not look up from the datapad in his other hand. The flimsi envelope hung in the air between them at the height of a boy's chest, which was lower than it had been a year ago and would be lower still by the spring, and Anakin stood with his stung shoulder throbbing under the tunic and looked at it and did not take it.

It was a courier envelope, the grey kind with the Temple seal in the corner, the kind he had seen perhaps a dozen times in his first life and never addressed to him. Across the front, in the oblong Outer Rim hand that the Hutts' scribes taught and the slaves copied, was a name he had not seen written in thirty years.

*Skywalker, Anakin. Jedi Temple. Coruscant.*

"It's from your mother," said Obi-Wan, still not looking, as though the boy might have needed telling. "I believe the Mos Eisley relay was down for a tenday; that is why it is late. You will want to read it before supper, I expect, so I shall not ask you to do anything useful until after."

Anakin took the envelope.

He took it with his right hand, which was his own, and the flimsi was slightly rough and slightly warm from Obi-Wan's holding it, and it weighed nothing, and he thought with great clarity: *This did not happen.*

"Thank you, Master," he said, and his voice came out at the right pitch, and he went into his room and shut the door.

* * *

He sat on the bed. He did not open it at once.

In his memory there were no letters. He had searched the memory now, standing in the common room with the envelope in the air, and he searched it again sitting down, methodically, the way he had once gone through a captured ship's logs deck by deck. There had been the parting in Watto's yard. There had been the thing he had made himself not think about for three years, and then had thought about every night for the next seven, and then the dreams. There had been Padmé's ship coming down over the Dune Sea and the farmer in the hoverchair telling him a name he had never heard and a month he had not been there for. He had not seen his mother's face from the age of nine to the age of nineteen, and the second time he saw it, it was in the dark of a tent, and that was all. That was the whole of it. There was no slot in it where a letter could have gone.

And yet the Obi-Wan in the next room had handed him one as if it were the fourth or fortieth, and had known which relay was down.

He turned the envelope over. The seal had been broken and resealed by the courier office, as they did, and under the Temple's stamp there was a smaller stamp in violet ink that said *Lars Homestead, Anchorhead District*, and then, because the courier droids were thorough, *Tatooine, Arkanis Sector, Outer Rim.*

He opened it.

*Ani,*

*Your last came the day after the second harvest and I read it at the table with Cliegg and then on my own after, which is how I read them. He says to tell you the west vaporators are giving a third more since Owen rebuilt the condensers, and that he knows you will say the condensers were never the trouble, and he says he knows that too but it is what we could afford. Owen is sixteen now and taller than Cliegg. He does not say much. He fixed the south fence by himself and did not tell anyone until Cliegg found it done. I think you would like him and I think he would pretend not to like you for about a week.*

*I am well. I say that every time and you ask every time and I will keep saying it. I sleep through the night. I go into Anchorhead on my own and nobody asks whose I am. I have not got used to it and I do not mean to.*

*I keep your letters in the tin from the old shop, the one you kept the parts in. The last one I keep separate, in my apron, because I like to have it with me when I am out at the vaporators and the wind is bad. I have read it so often I think the flimsi will go. Write me another so I can wear that one out too.*

*You said you were taller than your master's shoulder. I have tried to picture it and I cannot. In my head you are still nine and your hands are too big for you.*

*Eat. You never say whether you eat.*

*Your mother,*
*Shmi*

He read it twice. The second time he read it with his thumb on the word *Owen*, as though it might be a mistake in the printing.

Then he sat with it in his lap and looked at the wall.

The light from the window had moved perhaps a hand's width across the floor before he thought to breathe properly, and when he did, it hurt, and he let it. His chest was narrow and his ribs showed when he stretched and the breath went into them with no hiss and no count and no machine deciding how much, and he had been back eleven days and it still surprised him, every time.

*Nobody asks whose I am.*

She had never said that to him. She could not have. She had been dead before she was ever in a position to say it, in the only version of her life he had been given, and he had learned that she had been free from a stranger, after, over a grave. Here was the sentence in her own hand, in violet-stamped flimsi, in his lap. *I have not got used to it and I do not mean to.* That was her exactly. He could hear it. He had not heard her voice in thirty years and he could hear it as though she were at the basin in the next room with her back to him.

He put the letter down on the blanket very carefully, as if it could bruise, and got the datapad out from under the pillow.

* * *

The courier office kept an archive. He had not known that either, but it was there when he looked, under his own name, a folder he had never opened, with a scan of every letter that had passed through the Temple in either direction. Hers were in one column. His were in the other.

Eleven of his. Nine of hers. Three years.

He opened the first of his because he had to start somewhere.

It was a scan of a flimsi sheet, and the sheet had been written on with a stylus pressed too hard, so that the back of each stroke showed through faintly as a shadow, and the lines went uphill across the page the way they had always gone uphill, and he had been twelve.

*Mum. Master Obi-Wan says I'm allowed to write to you and I don't know what to say so I'm going to say everything and you can skip the boring bits. I'm taller. I can do a full backflip from standing now and Master Obi-Wan says it's showing off and then he showed me how to land it better so I don't know what he thinks really. We went to a planet called Carnelion where there was a war and it was very bad and I'll tell you about it when I'm older but I'm all right. There's a room here with a thousand fountains. I counted. There aren't a thousand. Are you well. Does he hit you. You can say if he does. I'm a Jedi now nearly and I'll*

The scan ran on. He did not.

He closed it and opened the fourth, from a year later, because the courier's dates put it just after a letter of hers that was shorter than the others.

*Is it true. Master Obi-Wan read it to me twice because I couldn't make the words stay still. Is it true, are you free, did he really. Who is Cliegg Lars. What does he want. You can tell me if it's not a good thing. I'm thirteen and I've got a lightsaber and I'll know if you're lying because I always know.*

*If it's true then I'm*

And a gap, where a boy had not known what came after *then I'm*, and had put the stylus down and picked it up again, because the next line was in a different pressure, lighter, as if written later or by someone who had been crying.

*Tell him he has to be good to you. Tell him I'm a Jedi and I'll know.*

He put the datapad face down on the blanket.

It was the handwriting. He had thought it would be the words, and the words were bad enough, but it was the handwriting he could not stand: the forward lean of it, the loop on the *k* of his own name, the way the lines climbed. It was the hand of a boy who pressed too hard because he was afraid the words would not stay. He had two hands now and they were both that boy's, and he looked at the right one, which had held the letter, and it was the same hand, with the same callus on the second finger from the stylus and the same scar on the knuckle from Watto's shop. The mechanical one had never written anything. It had signed things. It had closed on things. It had not, in twenty-three years, formed a single letter that leaned forward.

He turned the datapad back over, because he was not a coward, and opened the last one. The eleventh. The one she kept in her apron.

*I'm taller than Master Obi-Wan's shoulder now, which he says is not an achievement, it's just time passing, but I think he minds. I beat Orrin Bellas in the hall last tenday and I didn't gloat, I want you to know that, I just beat him. Master Obi-Wan says that is also showing off.*

*I'll come. I don't know when, they don't let Padawans go anywhere on their own, but I'll come and I'll see the farm and I'll fix the condensers properly and I'll meet Owen and Cliegg and I'll*

*I'll come. That's all. I'll come.*

*Ani*

He had not come.

In the only life he knew, he had come three years from now, in a borrowed Naboo ship, with a Senator he was in love with and a dream he had been having for a month, and he had found the farm and the farmer and the stepbrother and the empty chair, and he had taken a speeder bike out into the Jundland Wastes alone, and

*The tent. The dark of it, and the smell, and her face when he got the hood off, the lines in it that had not been there in Watto's yard, the cord marks at her wrists gone black at the edges, her mouth working at the first half of his name and not finding the second. The weight of her head when it stopped holding itself up. The sound outside that went on and on, and was him.*

He was on his feet. He did not remember standing. His right hand had closed on the flimsi letter and crushed it, and he opened the hand and looked at the creases he had put into her sentences and made himself breathe, in, hold, out, the count the suit had done for him, which he could do himself now, which he had a mouth and lungs and a throat to do himself.

He smoothed the letter out on his knee. The creases stayed.

She was alive. She was in Anchorhead buying flour with nobody asking whose she was. Owen had fixed the fence. The vaporators were giving a third more. None of it was his, and all of it was in his lap, and the boy who had written *I'll come* was dead on a bank of black rock a long way from here and also sitting on this bed in his body, and he did not know which one of them was going to write back.

* * *

He wrote back.

It took him until the light had gone orange in the window and Obi-Wan had knocked once and said "Supper, when you want it," and gone away again without opening the door, which was another thing to think about, later.

The trouble was the hand. He sat with the stylus and the courier's flimsi and found that his hand wanted to make the letters square and fast and separate, the way the glove had signed things, and the first line he wrote looked like an order of the day and he tore it off. The second line was in the boy's forward lean, carefully done, and it was so obviously careful that it looked forged, which it was. He tore that off too. He had been a boy for eleven days now and could do the voice and the walk and the sulk through the bond and had sat across a desk from the man who had made him and lied in his face, and he could not write *Mum* on a piece of flimsi without his hand going strange.

The third sheet he wrote on without looking, as the boy would have, pressing too hard.

*Mum. Yours came late because the relay was down, Master Obi-Wan says, and I read it twice and then once more. Tell Cliegg the condensers were never the trouble. Tell Owen the south fence is probably fine but I'll know if it isn't.*

He stopped. He had written *I'll know* because the boy had written it, and it was a boy's threat and a boy's joke, and in the only other version of this he had not known. He had not known anything. He had been nineteen and in love and had not known for a month, and she had been in the tent for a month, and

*I have thought about you every day.*

He looked at it. It was true and it was not a sentence a fifteen-year-old wrote to his mother, or not one who had letters from her in a folder, who had Cliegg and Owen and the vaporators as ordinary news. It was a sentence from the other side of a grave. He drew a line through it, and then he had a letter with a line through a sentence in it, and she would see the line and wonder, because she always saw.

He tore that sheet off as well. There were three on the floor now. His hand was shaking very slightly and he was annoyed with it, and the annoyance was useful; he was fifteen and annoyed and his shoulder hurt, and he wrote from there.

*Mum. Yours came late, the relay was down. Tell Cliegg the condensers were never the trouble but I'm glad they're giving more. Tell Owen I'll check the fence myself when I come, and I will come, I said so. I lost to Bellas in the hall this morning, on purpose, and I'm only telling you because it's true. My shoulder's bruised, ignore that. Master Obi-Wan is well and says to send his regards, which he didn't say, but he would.*

*I do eat. I eat a lot, apparently. Master Obi-Wan says the Temple will go bankrupt.*

*Keep the last one. I'll send more so you've got spares.*

*Ani*

He sealed it before he could read it again, and put it on the table by the door where the courier would collect it in the morning, and stood with his hand flat on it for a moment as if it might get up and leave on its own.

Then he went back to the bed and sat down with the datapad and did the thing he had been putting off since the first morning, since the shaking hand in Drallig's hall, since the doorway of their quarters, where Obi-Wan had held out the letter as if it were nothing.

He started the list.

* * *

The question of what to write it in took longer than the first entries.

Basic was out; anyone could read Basic. Huttese was out; Obi-Wan read Huttese, badly, and the Chancellor read it well and would not say so. The Temple's own cipher for Padawan journals was a children's thing that Jocasta Nu could unpick in her sleep. He needed something that nobody in this building could read, that nobody in the Senate could read, that could sit face up on a table in front of the one man in the galaxy who had walked through his mind every tenth day for six years, and mean nothing.

He had one.

He had made it in the chamber on the *Executor*, in the hours when the mask was off and the air was a pressure and a smell, for the things he had not wanted the Emperor's slicers to find in his terminal when they went through it, as they went through it. It was a substitution cipher keyed to a number he had never told anyone and that no one alive now would know: the serial of the first respirator unit, the one from the surgical table, which he had read upside down on the housing while they were fitting it and had never afterwards been able to stop seeing. It was not a clever cipher. It had never needed to be clever. It had needed to be his, and it was the most entirely his thing he owned, because he had made it in a room where nobody else could breathe.

Nobody alive could read it. Nobody alive had ever read it. The man who had built it was dead on a floor in a reactor shaft, and the boy with the stylus had never had a respirator and never would, if he could manage it, and between them they knew the key.

He set it up. The datapad did not care what the characters meant; it took them as a private script and showed him his own ugly symbols on the screen, and he put the title at the top the way he had put a title on the first one, thirty years and a death away.

*Things that are different.*

Under it:

*Letters. Three years. Eleven of mine, nine of hers. Obi-Wan handed it to me like a ration bar. In the other one there were none and I did not see her for ten years.*

*Obi-Wan. The shoulder. "My friend." The waiting in the plaza, six years of it, and Palpatine knew. He put in a request to the Council to cut the audiences. He has never once, that I can find, let the Chancellor have me for a whole day. The Obi-Wan I remember did not do these things. The Obi-Wan I remember did not do any of these things.*

He stopped and looked at that, and did not soften it, and went on.

*The Tano child. Hawkbat Clan, ten. Reverse grip, Shien, since she was seven. Drallig has had words. She looked at my hand, not my face. I did not correct her and she turned it back round again.*

*The bell. The first night. From below and east. I do not know what it was.*

Then, because a list was no use without the thing it was a list for, he wrote the three theories, and numbered them, and did not like any of them.

*One. My memory is rotten. Twenty-three years in the suit, and what the Emperor did to it, and what I did to it myself. The letters happened and I do not remember them because I chose not to. Obi-Wan was always like this and I chose not to see it. The child is a child.*

*Two. This was never mine. Close, but not the same: a place where the small things were always slightly different, where my master always let me write home, where a Togruta youngling always held her blade backwards, and I have been put into it by whatever put me here, and the differences are not clues; they are only the furniture of a house that is not my house.*

*Three. Someone else is here.*

He looked at the third one for a long time. The symbols were ugly and meant nothing, and he knew what they meant.

He did not add anything to it. He put the datapad in the pocket inside his tunic, where it sat against his ribs, and went out and ate the supper Obi-Wan had left under a cover, cold, standing up, while Obi-Wan read in the other chair and did not ask him anything, and did not say *my friend*, and watched him eat with an expression Anakin did not turn round to see.

* * *

The trouble with the first theory was that it could not be argued with. If his memory was rotten, then his memory of his memory was rotten, and there was no floor to stand on. So he went to find a floor.

The Archives after the evening meal were nearly empty, which was the point. The lamps were down to their night level and the long galleries of blue-lit shelving went away into the dark on either side like the inside of a ship, and the busts of the Lost Twenty stood along the central aisle in their niches with the light coming up under their chins, and he did not look at the twentieth, the last. He had looked at that face across a kneeling man's blade once and did not need to see it in bronze.

He took a terminal at the end of the second gallery, where the chair was low enough for a youngling and his knees came up, and he put his hand on the reader and the Archive took his print and his rank and gave him what a Padawan was allowed.

He tested himself against the record.

Carnelion IV first, because he had put it in the boy's letter and the boy had been twelve, and because it was the only mission in his first life he could date to a tenday. The file came up. The dates were the dates. The crash was where he remembered the crash, the two sides had the names he remembered, the report was in Obi-Wan's careful hand with the paragraph about the Padawan's conduct that had been, he now saw at fifteen and forty-five together, a good deal kinder than the conduct. There was a line about the boy having raised the question of leaving the Order, and the Council's note that the Chancellor had taken an interest. All as it was. He felt nothing about it, which was its own information.

His own file next, which he was allowed to see in part. *Skywalker, Anakin. Tatooine; born 41; brought to the Temple by Master Jinn (deceased); accepted to training over the Council's initial objection; Padawan to Knight Kenobi, O., since the same year.* The midi-chlorian count was sealed. The healers' notes were sealed. The standing audience with the Chancellor was logged as a matter of record, every tenth day, six years of them, each one a line, and Obi-Wan's petition to the Council to reduce them was there too, two years old, with the single word *Declined*. There was nothing in it that he did not remember and nothing he remembered that was not in it, and he sat back in the child's chair and let the first theory go out of him like a held breath.

His memory was not rotten. Not for this. Whatever was wrong, it was not in here.

Footsteps in the aisle; he put his boy's face on before he turned.

"Padawan Skywalker." Jocasta Nu, with a stack of datacards against her chest and her glasses pushed up into the grey of her hair, looking at him the way she looked at everyone who was in her Archives after dark, as a possible thief. "You are not often here."

"No, Master Nu."

"Is there something you need?"

"No, Master. Just checking something. For my master."

She looked at the terminal, and at him, and at the terminal, and he had the sense of being filed. "The Archive closes to Padawans at the second bell," she said. "Master Kenobi knows that, if he has forgotten to tell you." And went on down the aisle with her datacards, and he heard her a long time after, because the Archives carried sound the way water did.

He should have got up then. He had what he had come for. The first theory was dead and he had two left and neither of them could be tested in a library.

He did not get up. He sat with his hand on the reader, in the blue dark, and thought, idly, the way one touches a bruise to see if it is still there, of the one word he could put into this machine that he knew for a certainty was not in it.

He typed it.

*KAMINO.*

The terminal thought about it for the length of a breath and told him what he already knew.

*No record found.*

He nodded. It was expected. It was almost a comfort. The Archive did not know Kamino because a dead man's codes had cut it out six years ago, and would not know it for another four, until a Besalisk with a frying pan told a Jedi Master to trust his friends over his library, and he had wanted to see the blank for himself, to see what Obi-Wan would see when he stood in front of Jocasta Nu and heard that if an item did not appear in the records it did not exist. He had wanted to see the hole.

He had seen it. He reached to clear the screen.

Under *No record found*, in the small, apologetic typeface the Archive used for everything it was sorry about, a second line had come up while he was looking at the first.

*Prior unfilled queries for this string: 1.*

And below that, because Jocasta Nu's system logged every search that failed so that the Chief Librarian might consider acquisitions, and because Jocasta Nu had never in her life thrown anything away:

*Requester: KENOBI, O. — Knight.*

And a date.

He read the date three times. The Archive used the Republic's reckoning and he had to do the sum, and he did it, and did it again, and it came out the same. Two years ago. Not quite to the tenday. Two years, and a season, before tonight.

He sat very still.

The lamps hummed at their night level. Somewhere along the aisle a datacard went into a slot with a sound like a tooth. His knees were up in the child's chair and his hands were on the reader and the terminal was patient, as terminals are, and showed him the two lines, and the name, and the date, and waited to be told what to do next.

Sifo-Dyas, he thought.

That was the first one, and it came quickly, and he was grateful to it. Sifo-Dyas had vanished when Anakin was nine, a Council member lost on a mission nobody discussed, and a Knight with a sense of debt to Qui-Gon's generation might have gone looking, might have traced the last transmissions, might have found a word in them and brought it here to see if the library knew it.

Except that the Jedi had never found the last transmissions. Except that in the only version of events he had, nobody in this Temple had connected Sifo-Dyas to that word until the war was three years old, and the man who had done the connecting had been Obi-Wan, and he had done it over a dead Pyke in a cave with Anakin standing beside him, and had been as surprised as anyone.

Qui-Gon, then. Qui-Gon's old friends, his old files, something in a dead master's effects, a note, a place name on a chart Obi-Wan had inherited and not understood and put into the machine out of thoroughness. Obi-Wan was thorough.

Except that Qui-Gon had never heard the word either. Nobody had. That was the entire point of it. That was why Dooku had used a dead man's codes to cut it out: so that no Jedi, going through any dead master's effects, would find a name that led anywhere.

A Senate note, then. A trade dispute. A shipping manifest with a destination misspelt. A Padawan's exercise. Some other world that happened to—

There was no other world. He knew the spelling. He had been there. He had stood in the rain on the landing platform and looked down the lines of them in the growing halls, the ones who were six years old tonight and looked twelve, and he knew exactly how the word was spelt because he had read it on the side of the cloning facility in letters taller than he was, and the man he had read it beside had been Obi-Wan Kenobi, who had come there following a dart, four years from now, and had not known the name until Dex said it, and had said it to Anakin afterwards as though it were new, because it was.

That was the last of them. He had run out.

He thought: he could have got it from somewhere I do not know about. He thought: there are things in a man's life that are not in the record. He thought: my memory is not rotten, I have just proved that, and so I know when my master first heard this word and it was not two years ago and it was not in this room.

He thought of a shoulder touched in Drallig's hall, and the man waiting in Monument Plaza for six years in all weathers, and a letter handed over like a ration bar, and *my friend*, and a door that had been tried and tried and never quite forced, and a knock on his bedroom door and the voice through it saying *Supper, when you want it* and going away.

He did not want to think the next thing, so he did not. He cleared the screen, with the second line still on it, and the Archive forgot him as he took his hand off the reader, and he stood up out of the child's chair with his knees stiff from it and his shoulder throbbing and walked back up the long blue aisle past the twentieth niche, the last of them, without looking at it, and out, and through the Temple at its evening hush, past a pair of initiates in sand-coloured tunics who went quiet when he passed and started up again when he had, and up, and along, and he was outside the door of the quarters with his hand on the plate before he knew that he had been walking anywhere in particular.

He took his hand off the plate.

He went instead to the end of the corridor, where there was a window and a ledge and nobody, and sat on the ledge with his back to the city, and took the datapad out from against his ribs, and opened the list, and did not add it.

He would not. It was not a thing that was different; it was a hole in a library, and a name, and a date, and a dozen possible reasons he had not been able to think of yet, and a man who had raised him twice and whom he had killed once, and who was in that room behind that door, reading, with the supper things not yet cleared, and who would look up when the door opened and say something dry and kind.

He sat with the stylus over the screen until the lamps along the corridor went down to their night level too.

He added it to the list.

*Kamino. Archive. KENOBI, O. Two years ago.*

He looked at it in his own ugly symbols, which nobody alive could read, and found that he had pressed too hard, and that the line, very slightly, went uphill.


## Chapter 7: The Dream the Temple Heard

Anakin went in, in the end, because the corridor lamps had gone down to their night level and a Padawan sitting on a ledge in the dark was a thing somebody would eventually ask about.

The common room had been tidied after all. The supper things were gone from the table, a cup stood rinsed and upside down on the drainer, the reader lay face-down on the arm of the chair where Obi-Wan had put it when he gave up waiting, and the light under his door was out. Anakin stood in the middle of the room with the datapad flat against his ribs under his tunic and listened to the quarters the way he had once listened to the bridge of a ship, for the sound under the sound, and heard nothing but the air system and, a long way off, a transport going over.

He did not take the list out again. He went into his own room and shut the door and sat on the bed to get his boots off, and lay down on top of the blanket in his tunic, because getting under it would have been a decision to sleep and he had not made that decision.

He had not slept properly in eleven days. He had learned in the first week how much sleep a fifteen-year-old body believed it was owed, and how loudly it complained when it was not paid, and he had been paying it in instalments: an hour here, two there, on his back with the shield held level in both hands like a tray of water, going down only as far as he could go and still hold the tray. He had done this before. In the suit, sleep had been a medical event, a thing that happened to the body on a schedule while the mind walked its corridors with the lights off, and he had got good at letting the body go and keeping the rest. He was good at it. He had also been forty-five and sealed inside a machine that did his breathing for him. Now he was fifteen and growing, and his shins ached at night, actually ached, the long bones complaining from the inside the way they had the first time he had been this age, and the body wanted the whole night and did not care what he was holding.

He lay and held it. The bond, thinned to a boy's sulk, a crack of light under a door. The surface, the fifteen-year-old's surface, speeders and grievances and Mirren Dask's hair. Under that the wall. Under the wall, himself.

He thought about the line on the list going uphill.
