He thought about *Kamino* in his own ugly cipher and *KENOBI, O.* beneath it and the two-year-old date, and the eleventh niche in the Archives with its bust, and the man asleep through the wall who had knocked on this door at supper and gone away. He thought about Monument Plaza. He thought, with great clarity, *I will not sleep. I will rest the body and I will not sleep,* and he felt his hands, which were a boy's hands and tired, and the tray in them, and the water in the tray, perfectly still.

The body, which had been promised eleven days' arrears, took the whole night.

* * *

*The Processional Way was wet. It had rained in the afternoon, the thin engineered rain the city made for itself and switched off, and the great stair held it in the shallow places where a thousand years of feet had walked the stone hollow, and the boots went through it.*

*He could hear the boots. That was what he would keep, after: not the sight of them, which was only white, rank on rank behind him with the blue of the 501st gone grey in the dusk, but the sound. The single, many-footed sound of men who had been bred to step together and had never in their lives stepped any other way. Behind him. Not beside him. He had not told them to come beside him and they would not have thought of it.*

*He walked up the centre of the stair as he had walked up it ten thousand times, and it took exactly as long as it had always taken, and he knew the count of the steps, and the hilt in his hand was warm from his hand and nothing else. He was not angry. He had been angry on the landing platform and in the gunship, and the anger had gone somewhere below him on the way over, like a thing dropped from a height that you do not hear land, and what it left was clear. He could see very well. He could see the lamps coming on along the façade, and the two Temple Guard on the upper landing turning towards the sound, the masks catching the light and the pikes not yet lit, and he could see that the one on the left was going to be the first to understand and the one on the right was going to be the first to die, because the one on the right was going to hesitate. He was right about both.*

*Jurokk met him at the gate. The Gate Master had come out onto the top step as he came out for every returning company, and he had a datapad in one hand because there was always a manifest, always somebody to sign in, and he looked past Anakin at the white ranks coming up through the wet, and then at Anakin, and his face did the thing faces did, that he had watched faces do all that afternoon: it went to the person it knew and looked for him there.*

*"Skywalker," Jurokk said. "What's happened? Is it the Chancellor? Is he—"*

*He did not answer. There was nothing to say that would have been true, and he had stopped saying things that were not true some hours before, on his knees, in a red room. The blade came on. It was blue. It was the same blue it had always been, and nobody had told him that, that it would still be blue. Jurokk had time to look down at it.*

*Then the gate, and the boots on the stone of the entrance hall, loud now, under a roof, and blaster fire behind him at something in a side passage, three shots and done, and ahead and to the left the sound of a lightsaber coming on and then the sound of it stopping. He gave orders. He gave them in the voice he had used at Christophsis and Umbara and Ringo Vinda and the men took them the way they had always taken them, and that was the part of it the dreaming part of him could not bear and the dreamed part did not notice at all: that nothing was different. Fives had been right. He had stood over Fives on the floor of a warehouse with the man's blood on the duracrete and said nothing, and here were the men, the same men, good men, doing it.*

*The Room of a Thousand Fountains was dark because somebody had cut the lights, and full of the sound of water, which did not stop for anything. He went in alone. He had sent the company round by the galleries because the room was Drallig's, and he knew where Drallig would be, and he was there, by the long pool under the central fall, with a dozen of the older initiates behind him and two Padawans and a Knight he did not know, and the green blade already lit and held low, in the guard he had taught every one of them in that room.*

*"Anakin," Drallig said. Not Skywalker. He had the right to the name; he had given the boy most of what he knew. "Whatever this is. Stop here. Stop here and we will—"*

*He did not stop. He never stopped; that was what Drallig had said of him at eleven and at fourteen and at nineteen, with a different weight each time: the boy does not stop. Drallig was the best blade in the Temple and old and had never in his life fought anyone who did not, at some level, want to live, and he had only one hand on his hilt by the third exchange and one working arm by the fifth. The fall came down behind them. The spray was on his face and he could taste it. He killed the Knight who came in on his flank without looking at him and then he finished it, and Drallig went down backwards into the shallow end of the pool and the water took him and moved him a little, and the initiates behind him did not run, because he had taught them not to.*

*He was wet to the knees, after. He noticed that on the stairs. The Council tower stairs, the long spiral he had climbed to be told no, and no, and not yet, and wait, and he was climbing them now, and the boots were not behind him, because he had left the men at the foot of the tower and had not said why. He did not know why. The door at the top had been shut from inside and he put his hand to it and it opened, because every door in the Temple opened to him. That had never been the problem.*

*The lights in the Council chamber were on. He saw the twelve chairs and the windows black behind them and the city beyond doing what the city did at night, and then he saw that the chairs were not empty, that the room was not empty, that in the circle of floor between the chairs, where the petitioner stood, where he had stood at nine years old while they told his master no, there were children. Eight of them. Nine. Bear Clan, by the size, by the tunic hems that had been let down and let down again and were still not long enough. They had hidden. Somebody had told them go up, go to the top, the Council will be there, and the Council had not been there, and they had hidden behind the chairs and under them, and the first of them, a boy, came out from behind Master Windu's chair with his face white and glad.*

*Glad. That was the thing the dream kept, every time. The boy was glad to see him.*

*The boy said his name. The old name, the one with Master in front of it.*

*He heard his own blade come on, in the quiet, and the small faces turned to the sound, all of them at once, the way the fountains room had turned, and the light on them was blue.*

* * *

He woke because the Temple was screaming.

Not the Temple. He understood that in the first second, lying rigid on top of the blanket with his hands full of nothing: stone could not scream. It was the thing that lived in the stone, the Force as it ran through five thousand sleeping Jedi, and it was ringing like a struck pan, and the ring was made of children.

He could not have said how many. Dozens. The dormitory levels were below him and to the east and to the west and they were all awake at once, in the way a hive is awake, and the awakeness was crying: not one child, a weather of children, high thin voices he could hear through the Force and one he could hear through the actual floor, a girl, close, in the quarters below. He felt the Hawkbat dormitory, which was below and east, the way you feel a room full of people through a wall. He felt Bear Clan, the little ones, who had not understood what they dreamed and were the loudest. He felt clan-masters coming awake and lights coming on and somewhere a Knight on night duty running, and he felt, high up and far off in the dark, a small old attention turn towards him like a lamp lit in a window on the other side of a city.

Yoda had sat up.

He did not know how he knew it was sitting up. He knew. Eight hundred years of stillness and then that: a small body in a dark room pushing itself upright, and the attention coming out of it and across the Temple, not fast, not searching yet, only open, the way an old man opens a door at night to see what the noise was.

Anakin put the shield up. It was like throwing a blanket on a fire that had already got into the roof. He put it up anyway, hard, all of it, the wall and the bond with it, slammed, and the crying did not stop because the crying was not his any more; it belonged to the children now, they would be crying for an hour, and he lay there and listened to it and understood, in a clean cold way that had nothing of the dream's coldness in it, that he had done this.

Twice. The first night, eleven days ago, waking with the hole in him where the Emperor had been, he had done it without knowing: one unshielded instant, the bell of it, before he slammed the shield down. It had woken Ahsoka and Yoda, and no one had cried. He had put that down to the arrival, to the violence of it, to whatever had brought him. It had not been the arrival. It was him. He had come back thirty years and carried this in with him, in his body, in the place where a man keeps the worst thing he has done, and tonight he had taken his hands off it for six hours and it had gone through the Temple like gas through a ship.

He had killed those children once. Tonight he had shown them.

The door opened.

He had not heard the knock; perhaps there had not been one. Obi-Wan stood in the doorway in a sleep tunic, hair flat on one side, the common-room light behind him, and did not come in and did not say anything, and looked at him. Anakin lay where he was. He had not moved since he woke. He realised his hands were in fists on the blanket and opened them.

"The younglings are awake," Obi-Wan said. His voice was quiet and very level. "All of them, I think."

"I heard."

"Did you dream?"

"No." It came out in the right voice, the boy's voice, rough with sleep. He did not know how; something in him had done it before the rest of him was in the room. "Something woke me. I don't know what. Is it— what is it?"

Obi-Wan did not answer. The bond was shut, Anakin knew, shut as hard as the first night, and the man in the door could feel it shut, could feel the exact shape of a door slammed, and said, "You have closed it again," in the same quiet voice, not a question.

"Sorry. I— it was loud. I didn't think."

"No."

Then Obi-Wan did a thing that the Obi-Wan he remembered would not have done. He came in. He came across the small room and sat on the end of the bed, with the mattress tipping under his weight, and put one hand on Anakin's shin through the blanket, the way one would with a child who had been sick, and left it there. He did not look at Anakin's face. He looked at the wall.

"They will ask in the morning," he said.

"Who will?"

"Everyone." He did not take his hand away. The girl in the quarters below had stopped crying; another, further off, had not. "Go back to sleep if you can, my friend. If you can't, don't lie here. Come and sit up with me. I'll make tea."

He got up and went out, and left the door open, and Anakin heard him in the common room, heard the kettle, heard the reader picked up and put down again. The man he remembered would have stood in the doorway. He would have said *Try to sleep* and gone back to bed, because he had been a good master and a good master did not sit on a Padawan's bed at the third hour with his hand on the boy's leg. This one sat. This one had been sitting, in one way or another, for eleven days, and Anakin lay and looked at the open door and the slice of light, and could not have said whether the thing in his chest was love or alarm, and did not go out, and did not sleep.

* * *

The summons came at the first hour, by the quarters' panel, in the flat voice the Temple's systems used for everything from fire to laundry. *Padawan Skywalker to the Halls of Healing at the second hour. Master Kenobi is informed.*

Master Kenobi was informed. Master Kenobi had been up since the third hour of the night with the lights on, and had made tea twice and drunk one of them, and when Anakin came out of his room dressed with his braid retied he was already in his boots.

"You don't have to come."

"No," Obi-Wan agreed, and came.

The Halls of Healing were on the north side, three storeys down, and the corridors on the way were wrong. Not loud; the Temple was never loud. But there were people in them who should not have been, at that hour: a clan-master with a sleeping four-year-old over her shoulder, going somewhere; two Knights talking at a junction who stopped when Obi-Wan and Anakin passed and started again after; a pair of Hawkbat initiates in sand-coloured tunics, a Rodian girl and the loud human boy, Tam, being walked somewhere by an older Padawan, the boy not loud at all. The Rodian girl looked at Anakin as she passed and then down. He had taught her a Shii-Cho guard eleven days ago and corrected her elbow. He did not know what she had seen.

The Halls were pale and warm and smelled of bacta and something green. There was a bench outside the chief healer's room and a Padawan on it, a Zabrak girl of twelve or so with a blanket round her shoulders, who was taken in as they arrived and brought out again ten minutes later, red-eyed and steadier, by a young human healer who put her hand on the girl's back and sent her off down the corridor. Then the healer looked at the panel, and at them, and said, "Padawan Skywalker. Master Che will see you now. Master Kenobi, she asked that you come in as well."

Vokara Che's room was small and had a window. She was standing at it when they came in, and turned: a Twi'lek, blue-skinned, her lekku wrapped in the pale cloth the healers wore, older than Obi-Wan by twenty years at least and looking, this morning, every one of them. She had been up all night. It was in the set of her shoulders and the way she held her hands, loosely, in front of her, the way people did who had been touching other people's pain for hours and had not yet had time to put it down. Her eyes were kind. That was the first thing and the worst thing. Anakin had expected a Council face and got a healer's.

"Sit down, Padawan," she said. "Master Kenobi. Thank you for coming. Sit, please. I'll be as quick as I can; there are others."

They sat. She did not sit. She leaned against the edge of the table with the window behind her and looked at Anakin, not hard, not for long, the way she would have looked at a sprain.

"I'll tell you what I know and then I'll ask you what you know, and I'd like it to go in that order. Last night, a little before the third hour, thirty-one younglings in this Temple, every one of them under the age of twelve, woke from the same dream. Not a similar dream. The same one. I have spoken to them all, with the clan-masters, and they describe it in the words they have, which are children's words, but it is one dream." She counted on her fingers, lightly, the way she might have counted symptoms. "A stair, going up, wet. Boots; a great many boots. A gate. Water falling in the dark. A room with chairs in a circle. And a blue light, at the end, in the faces of children. Several of them say someone they knew was in it. None of them can tell me who. Children's dreams are like that; a face in a dream is whoever you were thinking of when you woke." She paused. "Four of the older initiates say that they were the ones holding the blade. They are very frightened by that. I have had to be careful with them."

Anakin said nothing. There was nothing a boy would have said. Beside him Obi-Wan was perfectly still, the stillness of a man holding a cup very full.

"It is the second time in eleven days," Vokara Che said. "The first was not so clear, or we were not listening. This time we were. I was awake, and so was Master Yoda, and so were half the healers on this floor, and a dream that goes through a building has a centre, Padawan, as a sound does. You can stand in a corridor and tell which way the bell is." She looked at him. "It was on your level. It was not far from your quarters. I am not here to accuse you of anything. I don't think there is anything to accuse a boy of in a dream; I have had my own. But I would like to ask you: did you dream last night?"

He had decided, on the way down, what the boy would say. "Yes."

"A bad one?"

"Yes, Master."

"Can you tell me what it was?"

"I—" He let it catch. He let his hands do the thing a boy's hands did, in his lap, and looked at them. "It was Tatooine. It's always Tatooine. I'm sorry. I didn't know it— I didn't know it would go anywhere."

"No one does," she said. "That's not a thing anyone is taught, because it is not a thing that is supposed to happen." She was quiet a moment. "I'd like to walk it with you. Do you know what I mean by that?"

"Yes."

"Tell me, so that I'm sure you do."

"You come in," he said. "With my leave. I show you the dream. You look at what I show you. You don't go anywhere I don't take you. I can stop it whenever I want." He had been taught this at twelve by a healer in this room and had never once consented to it, and he knew the words, and he knew, with the part of him that had been a Sith Lord, what they were worth. Not nothing. That was the thing. The practice was gentle and the practice was honest; she would do exactly what she said. A good person was going to come into his head and keep her word, and he was going to lie to her with it.

"That's it exactly," said Vokara Che. "Master Kenobi may stay or go, as you like. I'd ask him to stay."

"Stay," Anakin said, and did not look at Obi-Wan, and Obi-Wan did not move.

"Then when you're ready."

He had perhaps four seconds. He had known since the summons that he would have them and had not wasted them on the walk down, because a thing like this could not be made in a corridor; it had to be made in the room, out of what was in the room, with the real dream still hot behind it so that the seams would not show. He shut his eyes, which was permitted, which a boy would do, and built it.

The rule was the one he had learned from the best liar in the galaxy, who had been his master for twenty-three years, and it was this: do not lie from nothing. Take a true thing and turn it. So. Tatooine, which was true. The heat, which was true, the specific heat of the slave quarter at Mos Espa at the fourth hour with the second sun not yet down, that pressed on the back of the neck like a hand. Sand, which was true, in under the door no matter what you stuffed in the crack, so that the floor of his mother's room had a soft grey drift along one wall that she swept every evening and that was there every morning. He put those down first, floor and air, because a dream needed a floor to stand on and a healer would check the floor.

Then the things the children had dreamed, because she would be looking for them, and if she found their shadows here she would stop looking. A stair: the stair down into the quarter from the street, cut in the rock, that his feet had known in the dark. Wet: the vaporator at the end of the row that leaked, that the whole row had queued at with cups because the leak was free water and Watto's water was not. Boots: not troopers, a Gamorrean's, Gardulla's overseer's, the sound of them on the rock stair when he was three and had not been Watto's yet. Water in the dark; a gate; a blue light: he gave her the blue of a pod engine lit at night in the Boonta pit, and let it fall on faces, and made the faces the faces of the other slave children watching from the wall.

And then, because a lie needed a wound to stop at, or the walker kept walking, he put his mother's hands in it.

He did not have to make those. They were there, they had always been there, under everything, under the suit. The cracked knuckles. The way she pushed his hair off his forehead with the back of her wrist because her fingers were dirty from the shop. He put them in the dream, in the heat, with the sand, and he put, at the edge of the dream where a child would not look straight at it, a slaver's whip: not on him, that would have been too much, that would have been melodrama, and Obi-Wan had read his file; on someone else, down the row, the sound of it, the two-part sound, and his mother's hands going still on his hair while they both listened.

It was a good dream. It was the worst dream a boy his age could have had and it was all true, every piece of it, and none of it was the thing that had rung through the Temple, and it took him four seconds, and when he was done he laid it down over the Processional Way like a cloth over a table and opened his eyes and said, "I'm ready."

"Thank you," said Vokara Che, and came in.

He had been walked by Sidious a hundred times, and had learned what that was: a hand that took the chin and turned the face to the light. This was not that. This was a hand on a railing. She came in the way a healer came into a sickroom, with the door, slowly, giving him time to see her, warm and blue and tired and very careful, and she stood at the edge of what he had made and waited for him to show her where to step.

He showed her the floor. She stood on it. She felt the heat, he felt her feel it, and the sand under the door, and she went where he led, down the rock stair, past the vaporator with its wet dark patch, into the room. She did not hurry. She touched nothing he did not hold out to her. When she came to the whip she stopped, and he felt her stop, felt the real grief in it, a grown woman's grief for a child three years old on a stair in the dark, and he hated himself cleanly and completely and held the dream steady. When she came to his mother's hands she stopped for a long time.

*I'm sorry,* she said, in the dream, not in words. *I'm sorry. This is enough. You've shown me enough.*

And then, because she was good at her work, and because the practice was honest and a healer who had sat up all night with crying children did not stop at the first wound she found, she looked down.

He had known she would. It was why he had built the floor. She looked down through the floor of the dream as a healer looked through skin, and under it there was the other thing: not the Processional Way, which he had put away; not the dream at all. The wall. He felt her find it. He felt her put her hand on it, flat, the way a person puts a hand on a wall in the dark to know what the room is, and he felt the hand go still.

She did not push. She did not try the door. She stood with her palm on it for the space of two of his breaths, and then she took her hand away, and came back up through the floor, and out through the door she had come in by, and was standing against the edge of her table with the window behind her and her hands loose in front of her and her face not quite the same.

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"Thank you, Padawan," she said. She said it to him. Then she turned to Obi-Wan, and she did not lower her voice or move away or ask Anakin to wait outside, because she was an honest woman and he was in the room, and she said it in front of him.

"There is a dream. It is a bad one and an old one, and it is a child's; Tatooine. I would not have asked anyone to show it to me and I am sorry I had to ask him." She stopped. "And beneath it there is a wall, old and scarred, as if built over years. I have not seen one like it in a Padawan. I have not seen one like it in many Knights. I did not try it, and I would not, and I don't think I could."

Obi-Wan said, in his courteous voice, "He has had reasons to build one. You'll know some of them, Master. He was a slave. He has spent a morning every ten days for six years with a man who—" He stopped himself, visibly, the way he stopped himself about the Chancellor in company. "He has had reasons."

"Perhaps," said Vokara Che. She looked at Anakin again, and he made himself a boy under it, thin and tired and ashamed. "Who taught you to shield, Padawan?"

"Master Kenobi."

"No," she said, quite gently. "He didn't." And to Obi-Wan: "I will have to tell Master Yoda what I found. You understand."

"Of course."

"Go and eat something, Skywalker. You look as though you haven't." She was already moving to the door, already, in her mind, with the next child. "And if it comes again, come here. Come at the third hour, come at any hour. Do you understand me? Don't lie in the dark with it. Nobody should."

He said that he understood.

* * *

Windu was in the corridor.

He had not been there when they went in. He was there now, standing at the junction with his hands behind his back as though he had been passing and had stopped to look out of a window, which was not a thing Mace Windu did. He was in his Council robes. Anakin felt Obi-Wan check beside him, half a step, and recover.

"Master Kenobi," Windu said. "Master Che will want you. She said so, as I came by." It was true; the healer's door had opened again behind them and the young human healer was looking out. "I'll walk your Padawan to the refectory. Unless you object."

There was nothing to object to. Obi-Wan looked at Anakin, one look, and went back.

Windu did not walk anywhere. He waited until the door had shut and then he looked at Anakin, down, because he was a tall man and Anakin was not yet, and his face was the face it had always been, that gave nothing and took nothing and had been right about him, in the end, and had never once liked him.

"Something has come into this Temple, Padawan," Windu said. "If you know what it is, now is the time."

Clipped, direct, not unkind. He had never been cruel. Anakin had told himself for thirty years that he had been, and he had not; he had been a man who looked at a boy and saw a danger and said so, to the boy's face, which was more than most of them had done.

*The window. The red room, and the glass gone out of it into the night, and the old man on his back on the sill with the lightning climbing his own arms, and Windu's blade above him, and Windu's face turned to Anakin saying* he's too dangerous to be left alive, *and his own blade coming up, and the hand. The purple blade spinning away. Windu's hand going with it, into the dark, and the rest of him after, the robes opening in the wind like a thing let go, and then nothing, the city, the wind.*

"I don't know what it is, Master," Anakin said. "I had a nightmare. I'm sorry it woke them. I didn't know it could."

Windu looked at him for a long moment. Anakin looked back and let his eyes go hot and wet, because he was fifteen and had been up all night and it was easy, it was easier than anything, and he felt the old thing come up in him under the boy's face: the grudge, the specific and unjust grudge, thirty years old, that said *you never once gave me the benefit of a doubt,* and that knew, even as it said it, that the man had been right not to. He had been right. Anakin had thrown him out of a window. He would, if he closed his eyes and let himself, throw him again. He hated him for it and it was not the man's fault and he hated him anyway, standing in a pale corridor in a boy's body with his eyes wet, and he lied to his face, and the lie went in, and Windu had no way to know.

"Then that is what I will tell the Council," Windu said. He said it the way he would have signed a form. "Go and eat, Skywalker. Your master will find you."

He went. His boots were very quiet on the Temple floor and the sound of them went away down the corridor, and Anakin stood where he was until it had.

Obi-Wan came out five minutes later. He did not say what Vokara Che had wanted him for. They walked to the refectory, which was a long way, three storeys up and across half the Temple, and the corridors were still wrong, still full of the night: a clan-master, two Knights, a Bear Clan boy of six being carried, asleep, past a window with the morning in it. Obi-Wan did not say anything for the whole of the first corridor, or the second. At the stair he put his hand briefly on Anakin's shoulder, as he had in the plaza, and took it off.

He had been asked, since the third hour, by everyone who had a right to ask, and had lied in answer to every one of them. The one who had the most right to ask was walking beside him. He had been there in the room when the healer said *a wall, old and scarred, as if built over years,* and he had heard her say *he didn't,* and he had not looked at his Padawan when she said it, and he had not, in two corridors and a stair, asked what the wall was.

Anakin waited for it all the way to the refectory, and it did not come, and he did not know, sitting down across from the man with a tray he could not eat from, which of them that was a mercy for.


## Chapter 8: The Night the Temple Screamed

Ahsoka was inside it before she knew she was asleep.

That was the first wrong thing. Her own dreams came to her as dreams, soft at the edges, so that even under them some part of her knew they would end. This one had no edges. There was a stair, and she was on it, and she knew the stair. She knew the height of its risers because she had once been smaller than they were. She knew the band of paler stone at the turn where the mortar had been repointed in the wrong colour, and the way the light from the high windows fell across the eleventh step and not the twelfth. She knew the stair. She would not say whose stair it was.

Then there was fire. Not the clean fire of a brazier or a forge. Fire in a place that had never been meant to burn, licking up the inside of a hall taller than ten of her, so that the great hanging lamps turned and swung in the heat and the shadows swung with them. Smoke lay along the ceiling like a second ceiling. Beneath it, in lines, in step, came the white. Rows of it, shoulder to shoulder, the colour of a thing scoured clean, and the sound its boots made on the floor was a sound she had known in her sleep for seven years and had never once let herself hear while awake.

She did not name it. In the dream she turned her face from it as a person turns from a lamp that is too bright, and she would not give it a word.

At the end of the hall was a blue light, and at the end of the blue light was a boy's face.

He was looking up. The blade was a hand's breadth from him, lighting him from beneath, so that his eyes and the hollows of his cheeks were the colour of the blade and the rest of him was dark. He was not afraid yet. That was the worst of it. He was asking something. His mouth was open on a question and the question had a name in it, and she knew the name, and the dream ended on it the way a cable ends when it is cut.

* * *

She woke on the floor between the bunks.

She did not remember falling. She had been in the lower bunk nearest the door, as she had been for three years, and now she was lying on her side on the cold tile between her bunk and Kessa's with her cheek against the leg of the frame and her hands fisted under her chin. The dormitory was dark. The dormitory was full of noise.

Every child in Hawkbat Clan was crying.

She lay still and let herself hear it, because she needed to hear it to know where she was. Twenty children. Tam Dorreth, who was eleven and loud in all things, was loud in this; he was sitting up and sobbing in great wet gulps and saying *the boots, the boots.* And above her, a hand's reach away, Kessa Ruun was crying the way Kessa did everything, carefully and without wanting to be a bother, into the crook of her own elbow.

Ahsoka did not cry.

She got up. Her legs were a child's legs and they did not quite hold her the first time, so she put a hand on the bunk frame and tried again, and then she was standing in the dark in a tunic that came to her knees with her lekku cold against her neck and her heart going like something trying to get out. She walked to the refresher at the end of the dormitory. She did not run. She closed the door and put the light on low and knelt at the basin, and she was sick, in silence, with her hands flat on the cold rim and her breath counted between, the way a grown person is sick when there are others on the far side of a door.

When it was done she rinsed her mouth. Then she went out and climbed into Kessa's bunk and held her.

Kessa turned into her at once, the way she would have turned into anyone. She was a Mirialan girl of ten with a fringe of dark hair and the diamond marks of her people on her cheeks, and she was shaking so hard that her teeth clicked. "There was a stair," she said into Ahsoka's shoulder. "Soka. There was a stair and the lights were all. And there was water. There was water going somewhere and I couldn't."

"I know," Ahsoka said.

"Did you have it?"

"Yes."

"Did you have the chairs? There were chairs in a circle and nobody was—"

"Yes," she said. "Hush. It's gone now. It's gone."

She held the girl and looked over her head at the dark room and the children in it, and she thought: *a circle of chairs.* They had been given the stair and the gate and the water and the blue light and the chairs. They had been given the house of it. They had not been given the hall. They had not been given the white, or the boy's face with the question in it. That had been hers. She did not know what that meant and she did not want to, and the not-wanting was so strong that it felt like a hand on her mouth.

The corridor door opened and the lights came up to half, and Master Imari Sen came in with her sleeves pushed back and her grey hair down, which none of them had ever seen, and behind her two healers in their pale tunics, and behind them, carried and led and stumbling, the smallest children of Bear Clan.

* * *

They were brought to the common room because the healers wanted the clans where they could be counted. Hawkbat sat on the floor in two uneven rows with blankets round their shoulders. Bear were put on the benches, where their feet did not reach the floor, and a healer went along them with a datapad and a light and asked each one the same three questions in the same gentle voice. *Did you dream? Was there a stair? Was there a blue light?*

Thirty-one, across every clan in the Temple. Ahsoka heard the number because she was listening for it. The healer said it low to Master Sen at the door, and Master Sen put her hand over her mouth, and the healer said something else that had *four of the older ones* in it and *holding it*, and then looked at the children and stopped.

Ahsoka was ten years old and sitting cross-legged on a floor with a blanket over her head like a hood, and she looked at the healer's hands, not the healer's face, and she thought about the four older ones and what it was they had been holding. She stopped thinking about it. She had got good at that. It was like putting a cup down.

A small weight arrived against her side.

Caleb Dume was seven. He was a Bear Clan boy with hair that would not lie flat and a way of looking at things slightly too long for comfort, and for a year he had followed her about the Temple the way a tooka follows a person who once fed it. He had a blanket too, and under it he had found her sleeve, and he had closed his whole fist on the cloth of it as a child holds a handrail on a ship, and he did not say anything at all.

"Hello, Caleb," she said.

He looked up at her. His eyes were green and open and perfectly whole, and they went over her face, checking it, and then he put his forehead against her arm and kept it there. He had not been crying. He had the particular pale stillness of a child who has gone past it.

She did not take her sleeve back. She found that she had shifted, without deciding to, so that her shoulder was square to his shoulder and her arm was where his hand could find it without looking, as if he could not have found it with his eyes; and she felt herself do that and did not stop it, and it was another thing she did not think about.

The children stopped crying one by one, the way rain stops. The healers took four away to the Halls, Tam among them, still saying *the boots*. The rest were told to sleep where they were. Ahsoka sat with Kessa's head in her lap and Caleb's fist in her sleeve and did not sleep, and watched the window at the end of the room go from black to grey.

* * *

Master Plo came at the second hour of the morning.

He was very large and the door was not, and he ducked through it in the way she had loved since before she could remember loving anything, and the clan, which had been pretending to be asleep, stopped pretending. He went along the rows. He did not say much. He put his gloved hand on each head in turn, Hawkbat and Bear alike, and he said each name, and the children went quiet under his hand as they always did, as if the mask and the breather and the great gentle bulk of him were a thing that had been put in the galaxy for that purpose.

He came to her last. She had known he would.

"Little 'Soka."

"Master Plo."

He sat on the end of the bench, which creaked, and the Bear Clan children on it slid gently toward him. Caleb had been collected an hour before by a clan-master with a tired face; her sleeve was still creased where his hand had been. Kessa was asleep against her at last, properly, with her mouth open. Plo looked at the sleeping girl and then at Ahsoka, and she could feel him looking through the lenses, and she made her face a child's face and kept it there.

"You did not go to the Halls," he said.

"I wasn't crying."

"No," he said. "You were not." He let that sit for a moment. "The healers tell me thirty-one younglings in the Temple shared it. Most of your clan, and the Bear children over four among them. They tell me it was one dream, in all of them, with the same rooms in it."

"The same rooms," she agreed.

"Did you have the same rooms?"

"Yes, Master."

He waited. He was very good at waiting. She had learned a good deal of what she knew about waiting from him and she used it now against him, which was a thing she had never once let herself feel badly about, and did.

"Master Plo," she said. "Can I ask you something? Not about me."

"You may ask me anything, little one. Whether I can answer it is a different matter."

She looked at her hands. They were small and the knuckles were skinned from the training floor and they lay in her lap like somebody else's. "If a youngling had a dream," she said. "Not like this one. One of her own. And it was true. Not true like a thing that might happen, that the Force shows you and then it changes. True like—" She stopped and chose the word the way one chooses a foothold. "True like a thing that *had* happened. Somewhere. And she knew it had. Would the Council believe her?"

She felt him go still. It was not a thing she could have seen; the mask gave nothing and the hands did not move. It was in the Force, where he lived to her as a warmth the size of a room, and the warmth did not go cold but it stopped, for a breath, the way a person stops on a stair.

"Would they believe her," he repeated.

"Or would they say she was ten."

"Ah." He turned his head, and she saw the window in his lenses, grey. "You ask a Master's question, and I will give you a Master's answer, and you will not like it, and I am sorry for that in advance." The breather hissed. "The Council does not count years, Ahsoka. The Force does not count them either, and the Council tries, with varying success, to listen to the Force. If a child of ten came before them and said *I have seen a thing that is true*, they would not laugh at her. They would listen. They would be kind."

"But."

"But," he said, "a dream that is true and a dream that is only very much wished for, or very much feared, feel the same from inside. I have had both. There is no way to tell them apart from the inside of one's own head. That is what Masters are for. That is why a youngling has a Master, and a Master has a Council, and the Council has the Force, and even the Force"—he tipped his head—"is sometimes in motion. The Council would believe that she believed it. That is not nothing. It is also not what you asked."

"No," she said.

"No." He was quiet. "You asked me something like this once, at five, in the Fountains. I have reminded you of it before."

"I don't remember," she said.

It was the one lie she told him that morning, and she had told it before, and it went in exactly as it had the first time. She watched it go in. She was not sure that it had.

He looked at her for a long time.

She had been looked at by him for seven years, and she knew most of his looks, and this was not one of them. It was not suspicion. It was something slower, something that went round her like a man walking round a sculpture he has been told is one thing and is beginning to think may be another. It lasted long enough that Kessa stirred against her and settled, and Ahsoka kept her face where it was, and then it was over, and he put his hand on her head as he had on all the others.

"Sleep, little 'Soka," he said. "If you can. If you cannot, come and find me, and we will not sleep together, which is a thing I am quite good at."

She said *yes, Master.* He went out, ducking. She did not sleep.

* * *

The Gathering was in ten days.

She had known it was coming for a month, the way all the clan had known it. Master Yoda would take a group to Ilum aboard the *Crucible*: initiates of about ten and over who were ready, and readiness was a thing the clan-mother drafted and Master Yoda approved and nobody else discussed. Ahsoka was ten by four months. She was good; she knew exactly how good she was, which was far too good, and she had been shaving it for three years, letting Tam take her blade in demonstrations, falling where a child would fall. She had thought she would go to Ilum when it was her turn, and had not much cared when that was.

She cared now. She had cared since the moment, two days after the dream, when Master Sen said in the refectory, in the carrying voice she used for things that were already decided, that Master Yoda had asked for a senior Padawan to accompany the group, as was sometimes done, and that it would be Master Kenobi's Padawan, who already assisted in the initiates' classes and knew the children.

Ahsoka had kept her spoon moving. The soup was the thin green soup that came on the third day of every tenday and she ate all of it.

Then she set about it.

She did it the way she did everything now, which was in pieces too small to be seen. In Drallig's hall, that tenday, she held her saber the right way round for the whole of the first form and reversed it only at the end, when a girl of ten who was tired and showing off might, because a girl who never did anything wrong was a girl somebody would eventually look at.

On the third day she found Master Plo in the lower gardens, where he walked when the Council had been long, and she fell into step beside him with the hem of her tunic catching on the gravel and said, in the voice of a child who has been working up to something all the way down the stairs, "Master Plo, am I ready for Ilum?"

He walked a few steps. "Do you think you are?"

"I don't know," she said, which was the right answer and also, she found, true. "Kessa's on the list. She'll be frightened in the caves. She's frightened of the dark on the stair to the dormitory." She looked up at him, which she had to do, which she always had to do. "I thought if I was there."

"That is a kind reason."

"It's not the only one."

"No," he said. "I did not suppose it was." But he did not ask what the other was, and she had been counting on that, and was, in the part of her that was not counting anything, a little ashamed. "Master Sen drafts the list. I may say a word to her. I will say only that you have asked, and that I think you are able. Whether able is the same as ready she will decide."

"Thank you, Master."

"Do not thank me yet," he said. "The caves are very cold."

She said a word to Master Sen herself the same evening, carefully, about Kessa, about the dark on the stair. The clan-mother listened with her hands in her sleeves and her grey head on one side, and said that the list was the list, and that it was not a thing children lobbied for, and that she would think about it. Her name was on it the next morning, sixth of six: *Tano, A., Hawkbat.* Ahsoka read it on the board outside the refectory with Kessa jumping up and down beside her and did not jump, and let Kessa hug her, and looked at the name on the board as if it belonged to somebody small.

In the corridor beyond the refectory a Pau'an Temple Guard went by in his mask and yellow robes, unhurried, and Ahsoka stepped back against the wall before she knew she had moved, her shoulders gone up around her neck. She could not have said what she was afraid of, and she was ashamed of it, and she made her face smooth again before he had passed. Kessa, still bouncing, saw nothing, and neither did the Guard.

* * *

The clan went to the Room of a Thousand Fountains on the fifteenth day, all of them together, because the healers had said water and green things and because Master Sen did not argue with healers.

It was too large to be a room. The waterfalls at the far end made a sound that got into the bone, and the air was wet and warm and smelled of soil and moss. The paths went in loops and over small bridges, and the clan went along them in a straggle, and she walked in the middle with Kessa's hand in hers and the bright noise of the water filling the place where thought would have been.

She felt him before she saw him. She had been feeling him for fifteen days, the hard-shielded thing in the Temple that she had fixed, rightly or wrongly, on the Padawan with the braid; it was there at the back of her mind the way a tooth is there when it is going bad, and she had got used to carrying it and never once stopped knowing where it was. Now it was ahead of her, and to the left, and close.

They were on the bridge over the second pool. Master Kenobi and his Padawan, standing, not walking, the Master with his hands behind his back and his beard and his brown robe and the boy beside him in the sand-coloured tunic that fitted him badly because he was growing too fast for it, with his hands in his sleeves and his face turned to the water. They were not talking. They had the look of people who had been talking and had stopped.

Her clan went past them on the lower path, below the bridge, in a straggle of ten-year-olds, and she went with it, and she looked up because she could not have done anything else.

The Padawan did not look down. He was looking at the water with the particular blankness she had seen on him in Drallig's hall, the face of a boy who was somewhere else and keeping it off his face. She saw his hand, the right one, go once to his left wrist and close there and open, and she filed that, as she filed everything about him, on the list that was not written anywhere.

Master Kenobi looked down.

He looked down at the line of children and his eyes went along it as a man's eyes go along a shelf, and they came to her, and they stopped.

It was not long. It was a beat too long. It was the length of time it takes a person to recognise a face in a crowd and then remember that they could not possibly, and in the whole of that length his face did not change at all, which was itself a thing. He was a Jedi Master and she was a youngling of ten with wet moss on the hem of her tunic and a Mirialan girl's hand sweating in hers, and he looked at her as if she were a word he had been trying all week to remember, and she looked back.

She looked away first.

She did not decide to. Her eyes simply went to the path, to the gravel and the moss and her own small boots, and she found that her hand had tightened on Kessa's hard enough that Kessa said *ow, Soka*, and she said *sorry*, and the bridge was behind them, and the sound of the waterfalls came up and took everything.

She did not look back.

* * *

That night she went to the dojo.

It was the fifth night and so it was permitted, by the rules she had made for herself and kept as if somebody else had made them. The third-level dojo was small and square and badly lit; the fault ticket on its lighting was three years old and the strips along one wall came on at half and the strips along the other did not come on at all, so that the room was always divided into a lit half and a dim one, and she worked in the dim.

She used the two short wooden rods the youngest children used for footwork, because they were the right length for her wrists, which the sabers were not, and because wood made no sound and no light. She held them reversed.

She worked for an hour. Two blades, the body low, the left rod guarding the line the right one opened. She did not think about the stair or the hall or the white. She did not think about the boy on the bridge with his hand on his wrist, or the Master with his long look. She moved until her arms shook and her tunic was dark at the back and her breath was the loudest thing in the room, and then she stopped and stood in the middle of the floor with the rods hanging from her hands and let the dim half of the dojo be quiet round her.

Then she looked up at the gallery.

She did not know why. She had looked up at it on the first night, eleven nights ago, when something that might have been nothing had sat in the dark up there and watched her, and she had not been able to prove it and had told herself so. She had not looked up since. She looked now, and there was nothing there, only the rail and the dark and the shut door at the gallery's end.

She went up anyway.

The stair to the gallery was steep and made for grown legs and she went up it on her hands as much as her feet, with the rods pushed through her belt. The gallery was a narrow walk along two sides of the room with a wooden rail at hip height, a grown person's hip, her chest. Nobody used it. The dust on the rail was the thick soft kind that comes to things nobody uses, and she could see it in the half-light from below, an even grey along the whole length of the wood, like a skin.

Except where it was not.

Near the corner, where the gallery turned and a person sitting on the floor could put their back to one wall and look down at the dim half of the room, the dust on the rail was gone in a patch the width of two hands. Not wiped. Worn, as a thing is worn when somebody rests their forearms on it and leans and looks and does not move for a long time. The wood under it was dark with old oil and showed through. Below, on the boards of the gallery floor, the dust was flattened in a shape she looked at for some time before she let herself call it what it was, which was the shape of someone who had sat there with their knees up.

She put out her hand, and did not touch it, and took her hand back.

It had not been there eleven nights ago. She would have sworn to that. She did not know for how long, or how many nights, or whether it was one person or several; a Knight checking a fault ticket, a Guard on a round, a child from another clan come to watch the strange Togruta girl who held her sticks the wrong way round. She did not know whether it was him. She did not know. She stood in the dark on a gallery with the rail at the height of her chest and the dust of it on her sleeve and she made herself say it in her head, in the plain words, which was the discipline: *I do not know who sat here.*

Below her, the lit half of the dojo hummed in its strips. The dim half was empty. She had left the rods on the floor down there, the two of them, lying crossed where she had let them fall, and from above they looked very small.


## Chapter 9: The Count on the Holonet

Master Vos-Kerrick kept the politics room dark for the holonet and so the first thing Anakin saw of Count Dooku, after twenty-three years and a death, was the light of him on the faces of children.

There were fourteen Padawans in the room, on benches built for adults that most of them had grown into. Anakin had not. He sat with his knees high and his hands under his thighs, which was how he kept them still, and watched the blue wash of the projector move across a row of profiles: a Zabrak girl with her horns cropped for the dojo, two human boys who sat together in every class, Mirren Dask, a year older than he was, whom he had told the Chancellor he was in love with and who did not know it, and at the end of the bench, long legs crossed at the ankle, Orrin Bellas.

The Master stood beside the projector with her arms folded in her sleeves. Tahlin Vos-Kerrick was Chalactan, tall and spare, with the small gold marks of her people set into the skin above her eyebrows so that she seemed always about to raise them. She had a way of letting the holonet speak for a long time before she did, as if the Senate were a witness she had called and intended to let hang itself.

"This was recorded four days ago," she said, "at the Serenno Institute of Letters, at the opening of a gallery which the Count has paid for. You will notice he does not mention that. Watch him. Listen for what he does not say."

The feed resolved.

He was standing at a lectern of pale wood with a wall of books behind him, and he was wearing black, and he was seventy-six years old. Anakin had not expected the age. The Dooku he carried in him was a man of the war, straight-backed and cold and quick, a curved hilt in a gloved hand, and this one was the same man with the years not yet pressed out of him by what was coming; the hair whiter, the face a little looser at the jaw, the hands at rest on the lectern as though he had never needed them for anything but turning pages. His voice came into the dark room low and unhurried, every consonant placed, and Anakin felt the boys on the next bench stop fidgeting.

"I was asked, in preparing these remarks, to say something uplifting," the Count said. "I was told that an opening is a hopeful occasion. I agree. And I thought a long while about what a man might say, honestly, that would be both hopeful and true, and I found that the only hopeful thing I could say honestly was this: that we are still permitted to notice."

A pause, exact, like a rest in music.

"We are permitted to notice that there are worlds in this Republic where a child will be born tonight and will die before she has a name, of something a bacta tank on Coruscant would cure between breakfast and lunch, and that the vote to fund the tank for her world has been on the Senate's calendar for eleven years. We are permitted to notice that it has been deferred eleven times, and that each deferral was procedural, and that each procedure was lawful, and that the law was written by the people who benefit from the delay. We are permitted to notice that no one in that chamber is a villain. I have sat with them. They are courteous people. They are fond of their children. That is the whole horror of it. A Republic that was run by wicked men could be saved by replacing them. A Republic that is run by decent men who have learned, over a thousand years, that decency is a thing you do with your face while your hands do what the committee requires, cannot be saved by anyone. It can only be noticed. And then, perhaps, it can be left."

Somebody on the far bench breathed out through their teeth.

"I do not say this to you as a Jedi," Dooku said, and the dry smile in it made Mirren Dask laugh once, under her breath. "I have not been one for some years. I say it as a citizen of Serenno, which is far from here, and which has learned what it means to be far. Distance in this Republic is a kind of poverty. The Core does not hate the Rim; it has simply never had to think about it. And the only argument the Rim has ever been permitted to make is the argument of patience. Wait. The vote will come. The tank will come. Be patient, and be grateful, and be far away."

He looked up, and for a moment the recording gave Anakin the eyes, dark and level and, in this light, almost kind.

"I would like to say something hopeful. I will say instead something true. Patience is not a virtue when it is the only thing you are allowed. It is a sentence. And I have come to believe that the most dangerous man in the galaxy is not the one who tells you that the Senate is corrupt; you know that already; you have always known it. The most dangerous man is the one who tells you that it is not yet time to say so."

The feed held on him for a moment, standing in his black among the books, and then Vos-Kerrick killed it and brought the lights up a little, enough to see faces by, not enough to make anyone feel seen.

"Well," she said. "Now you have been seduced. Tell me how it was done."

It was Bellas who spoke first, because Bellas always did. He uncrossed his ankles and leaned forward with his elbows on his knees and the long braid swinging, and he said that it was done by being right. "He didn't say anything false, Master. Every word of it is in the record. The medical appropriations for the Rim have been deferred since before any of us were born. You've taught us that yourself."

"I have. Go on."

"So where is the seduction? He noticed something and he said it. If that's seduction, the whole of the Archives is a brothel."

Two of the boys laughed. Vos-Kerrick did not. "You have confused the content of an argument with its purpose. Padawan Dask."

Mirren Dask had her chin on her fist. "He didn't propose anything," she said slowly. "He said the Senate couldn't be fixed and then he stopped. He said it could be left. He didn't say where you would go."

"Better. Where would you go?"

"I don't know. That's what bothers me. He made me want to go somewhere and he didn't say where, and I think that's on purpose. He wants you to fill it in yourself. Then it's your idea."

"And a man of seventy-six who was trained in this building to persuade does nothing by accident." Vos-Kerrick unfolded her arms. "He is not lying to you. He is telling you the truth in such an order that you arrive, by yourself, at a conclusion he has not had to state. First the dying child, so that you are angry. Then the courteous Senators, so that your anger has nowhere lawful to go. Then patience as a sentence, so that waiting feels like cowardice. And then he stops. He is very good. The Order lost him, and you should ask yourselves, as he certainly has, why."

The Zabrak girl said that none of that made him wrong, and Bellas said that it made him right, actually, and Dask said that it made him somebody who had decided being right was enough, and then the room was arguing, voices rising the way children's do when they have been given leave, and Vos-Kerrick let them, and watched them, and once, briefly, watched Anakin.

He had said nothing. He sat with his hands under his thighs and his face arranged, and heard the Count's voice go on in the room without the Count, in Bellas's mouth and Dask's, each of them carrying a piece of it like children carrying a dead bird they had found, and he thought: *yes. That is how it was done to all of us.* He had heard Dooku speak many times and never once heard what it was for. He had been in a ship's observation deck with a man on his knees before him and two blades crossed at the man's throat, blue and red, the red one his, both hands full, and the old voice from the chair behind him, patient, avuncular, a hand on the shoulder, saying, *Do it*, and he had done it, and the head had gone one way and the body the other, and the thing he had felt was not horror but relief, a boy's relief at being told.

That was seven years from now. It was in another life. He had been twenty-two, and it had been the first thing, the first of all the things.

He realised Vos-Kerrick had said his name.

"Padawan Skywalker. You have been very quiet, which in my experience is not your custom. What did you hear?"

He looked at her and let the boy answer. "I heard a man with a lot of books, Master."

"That is an observation, not an answer."

"Sorry. I mean." He shifted on the bench and scratched the back of his neck under the braid, which was a thing a fifteen-year-old did. "He's never had to wait for anything. He's a count. He says patience is a sentence, and he's right, it is, I've done some, but he's never done any, so when he says it, it's, I don't know. It's borrowed." He shrugged. "He talks like somebody who has already decided what he's going to do and wants us to think of it first."

The room went quiet in the particular way of a room in which a child has said something adult by accident.

"Yes," said Vos-Kerrick, after a moment. "That is the seduction. Thank you." And she went on to the Amidala bill, and the disclosure vote, and the question of whether a Senator from a world that had been occupied had standing to lecture Senators from worlds that had not, and Anakin took his hands out from under his legs and found they were steady, and did not look to the end of the bench, where Bellas was looking at him.

* * *

Drallig's hall was on the fourth level, with the tall windows, and at mid-afternoon the light came down through them in bars and lay on the floor like something that had been dropped. The advanced class was ten Padawans and the Battlemaster, and he worked them without an assistant because he said that at this level he wanted to see every mistake himself.

"Pairs," Drallig said. "Bellas, Skywalker. You've unfinished business, I think. Go to the far end where you won't hit anybody."

Bellas grinned.

Anakin had lost to him eight days ago, in this hall, after a one-handed parry he had not meant to make and that Drallig had watched as a man watches a bird fly into a room. He had thrown that bout the moment he understood what he had done, badly, a boy's throw, and walked home with the welt. Since then he had been careful: in the way he walked and stood and held the hilt with two hands on it, like a boy who had been told to, and he was tired of it in his body in a way that had nothing to do with the body being fifteen. He walked to the far end of the hall with his training blade humming at low power in his fist and thought about three days. The *Crucible* would go in three days, with Master Yoda aboard and six children and one Padawan to carry things, and the Padawan would be him; he assumed his master had arranged it; it had Obi-Wan's shape, and Obi-Wan had not said why. The sixth child on the list was a Togruta girl who held her saber backwards and had looked at his hand instead of his face.

He put it away. He had got good at putting things away. He turned and saluted and Bellas saluted and Drallig said "Begin."

Bellas was seventeen and had the reach. He was nearly a head taller and his wrists had stopped growing, and he fought Form Five the way Drallig taught it, with both hands and a lot of hip, big strokes and a quick recovery, a strong boy's form. He was good. He was the best in the class and he knew it and he had been Drallig's favourite for three years, and he came in high and fast and Anakin went back before him with the blade up and gave ground the way a boy a head shorter gives ground, two-handed, elbows in, and let the second stroke beat his guard down and took the sting on the forearm and felt it, the hot thin line of it, and stepped out.

"Point," said Drallig. "Again."

They went again. He gave him the second bout at the fourth exchange, a half-turn too slow on a cross-parry that any Padawan in the hall could have made and that he made late, so that the blade came past his ear and touched his collar, and he hissed and dropped his guard and Bellas stood back, breathing, pleased.

"Point. Skywalker, you're slow today. Were you up all night again?"

"No, Master."

"Then wake up. Again."

Bellas came close, resetting, and spoke under the hum of the blades, in the voice boys used for things they did not want the Master to hear. "It's all right, slave boy. Nobody's keeping score."

Anakin saluted.

"That's the thing, though," Bellas said, pleasantly, as if they were discussing the weather, as if the words had no weight at all. "Nobody is keeping score. Not Drallig, not the Council. You know why Kenobi took you? Everybody knows. Jinn made him promise, on the floor, with a hole through him. That's all. He didn't choose you. He's been keeping a promise for six years and he's too polite to say so, and you think it's—"

*The hall is on fire and Drallig is on his knees in the doorway and the blue blade is in him and the old man's face is only puzzled.*

"Begin," said Drallig.

He did not decide. That was the thing he would say to himself later, on the roof, with the city burning its ordinary lights below him: that he had not decided. Bellas came in high, as he had both times, the big two-handed stroke with the hip in it, and the body that Anakin lived in now was fifteen, as he himself had been fifteen once, thirty years ago, but the body he had lived in for the twenty-three years before that had been taller and heavier and had never once in all that time needed both hands, and it was that body that moved. The left hand left the hilt. The right came up and over, straight down, with the shoulder behind it and nothing else, no flourish, no step, a stroke the way a door is a stroke, and it met Bellas's blade a third of the way down and took it out of his hands as if the boy had been holding a stick, and the training saber turned once in the air and went the length of the hall, the whole length, and skidded under the far windows and lay there humming in the bars of light.

Bellas's hands were still up and empty. His mouth was open.

The blade stopped a finger's breadth from the hollow of his throat and stayed there, one-handed, without a tremor in it, close enough that the skin under it went pink.

The hall was silent. It was the silence Anakin knew from the other hall, with the other floor; not the silence of nobody speaking but of ten people who had all at once stopped breathing. Somewhere down the room a blade switched off and nobody turned to see whose.

He looked at the skin going pink under his blade and thought, quite clearly, in a man's voice: *you have done it again.* And then, because the body was fifteen and quick to flush and quick to tire, his arm began to shake, and he took the blade away and thumbed it off and stood in front of a seventeen-year-old with his chest going and his face hot, a boy who had just done something and did not know what.

Drallig had crossed the hall. He had not run. He stood a little to Anakin's left, where he could see both of them, and his face was not angry; it was the same face as eight days ago, the face of a man watching a bird in a room, and he looked at Anakin's right hand, which was still closed on the hilt, and then at the empty place at his left side where the other hand had not been.

"Where did you learn that?"

The hall waited.

"I don't know, Master."

It was the truth, as far as it went. He had not learned it. He had become it, over years, with one arm that was his and one that was not, and then none, and nobody had ever called it a stroke; it had only been the way his body went when something was in front of it. He had never heard a hall go quiet for it, because the halls he had used it in had been full of blaster fire and dying. He looked at Drallig and the Battlemaster looked back and neither of them said the thing in the air, which was that no one in this building could have taught it, and that the Battlemaster would have known.

"Bellas," Drallig said, not turning. "Fetch your saber. Then you will tell me, in front of the class, what you said to him."

Bellas went red to the hair. "Master—"

"I heard the first part. I will hear the rest from you. Go."

The boy went down the length of the hall under the windows and the class watched him go, and Anakin stood where he was and became aware, slowly, as a man coming up out of water becomes aware of air, that the doorway at the near end of the hall was not empty.

Obi-Wan stood in it. He was in his brown robe with his hands in his sleeves and he was perfectly still, and there was no way to know how long he had been there, whether he had come for the end of the class and seen the whole of it, or had come down the corridor at the sound of a blade hitting the floor; and Anakin, who had kept the bond thin for eighteen days, thin as a thread, a boy's sulk and nothing else, felt along that thread what his master was feeling.

It was not surprise.

He did not know what it was. He stood in the bars of light with the hilt in his hand and reached for it, carefully, the way one feels for a stair in the dark, and it was there, steady and old and entirely contained, and it was not surprise, and that was the whole of what he could say, and it frightened him more than the silence of the hall had done.

Obi-Wan's face gave nothing. He inclined his head a fraction to Drallig, who inclined his back, two masters agreeing on something across a room full of children; and then Bellas was back with his saber and the Battlemaster turned his attention on him like a lamp.

"Say it."

Bellas said it. He said it to the floor, and left out *slave boy*, and Drallig made him put it back in, and then said in a voice the whole hall could hear that Padawan Bellas would spend the next tenday with the Bear Clan, holding a training saber for six-year-olds, and would address every one of them by name. The class did not laugh, because nobody wanted to be next. Then he turned to Anakin.

"And you. You lost two bouts you had no business losing and won a third you had no business winning, and I don't know which worries me more. You will come to this hall every morning before first bell until the *Crucible* leaves, and you will show me that stroke, slowly, as many times as it takes, until one of us understands it. That is not a punishment. Don't look at me as if it were."

"Yes, Master."

"Dismissed. All of you. Go and think about your mouths."

They went. Obi-Wan stood aside from the door to let them pass and the Padawans went by him with their eyes down, children past an adult, and when the hall was empty but for the three of them he came in a few steps, no more, and looked at the far windows where the saber had lain.

"The whole length," he said.

"Yes," said Drallig.

"Hm." He did not look at Anakin. "I came to walk him back. The Council wants me at the sixth hour and I thought we might eat first."

"Take him."

In the corridor, in the first flight of stairs down, with the tall windows gone and the walls close, Obi-Wan said, "You stopped."

"Yes."

"Good."

That was all, for three levels. Then, at the turn above the refectory, with a knot of initiates going past them with trays, small children carrying things too big for them, his master said in the same voice, a voice deciding nothing and asking nothing, "He was wrong, you know. Bellas. About the promise."

"I know."

"I don't think you do," said Obi-Wan, "but we'll leave it," and they went down into the noise.

* * *

The roof of the Temple was not a place Padawans were supposed to go, which was why there was a way up to it that Padawans had been using for a thousand years.

He went up at the second hour after midnight, when the quarters were dark and his master's side of the bond had the slow weight of sleep, and climbed the maintenance ladder in the Tranquillity Spire's shadow with his hands going cold on the rungs, and came out through the hatch onto the flat of the ziggurat's top tier, where the stone was wide and bare and the night air came across it from the city like a thing with a smell. The city burned below him in all its ordinary lights, red lanes one way and white the other, the Senate dome a dark curve against the glow. Anakin stood and breathed. It was still new. The air went in and out and nothing clicked, and after twenty-three years of hearing every breath announced he had still not got used to the silence of his own lungs.

He sat down with his back to the base of the spire and his knees up and looked at his right hand.

He had not decided. He kept turning it, trying to find a place in the afternoon where he could have put his hand and stopped it, and there was none; there was Bellas talking and the hall on fire and then the blade a finger from a boy's throat. In between there was nothing. Not anger. He had been angry as Vader and knew its shape, the hot clean narrowing of it; this had not been that. His body had done it the way the chest did the breathing. Of all the lies he had told in eighteen days, *I don't know* had been the truest.

And his master in the door. Thin as a thread, and what came down it was not surprise.

It was cold on the roof. He put his hands in his sleeves, like Obi-Wan, and let the shield down a fraction, not the wall, never that, but the outer part that kept the sulk in place, because there was no one up here to feel it, and because he was so tired. The body wanted sleep the way a child wants it, totally and at once, and the mind would not let it.

That was when he saw the bird.

It was on the parapet, three metres off, where the stone dropped away, and he did not know how long it had been there. It had not flown in; he would have heard it. It was simply there, as if it had been there when he came up and he had not had the eyes for it. It was the size of a small cat, and it sat very upright with its wings folded, and it was green. Not the green of anything on Coruscant. The green of leaves in a world that had weather, with a paler breast and a crest of feathers like a crown someone had been careless with, and a round flat face with a hooked beak and two round eyes, owl-like, enormous, that were looking at him.

Not at the city. Not at the lights. At him.

He did not move. His hand had gone, on its own, to the hilt at his belt, and he made it stop and come back, and the bird watched the hand go and come back as if it had understood the whole of that and had an opinion.

He had seen a bird like it. He could not have said where. Twenty-three years in the suit had taken the catalogue of small living things out of him and left only the catalogue of deaths, and he reached into the first for the bird and found only a feeling, the way one feels the shape of a dream at noon: a sky that was wrong, a light that was not the sun, a woman in green; and then, from much later and much worse, a dark wall of stone and a doorway falling and something flying out of it, up, into a black sky, while he walked away. He did not let himself go there.

"What are you?" he said. His voice was a boy's, thin in the wind.

The bird turned its head, the whole head, smoothly, through a quarter turn and back, and did not fly. He waited for it to fly. Birds flew; it was what they were for; a Padawan on a roof at night was a thing birds flew from. This one blinked, slowly, its lids coming up from below, and settled its feathers, and went on looking at him with the vast round patient attention of something that had been given a task and did not intend to leave until it was done.

He felt it then. Not from the bird; from behind it, or through it, he could not have said; the way one feels, in a dark room, that the dark has a shape. He had been looked at before. He had been looked at by Sidious every tenth day for six years, in a red room with a window, and he knew that attention better than he knew his own hands, the warmth of it and the patience and the gentleness of a man counting his property. This was not that. This had no warmth in it and no cold. It had no wanting. It was the attention of something that had been watching for a very long time and had not, in all that watching, learned to be surprised, and it was on him now, through two round eyes on a parapet, as plainly as the city light was on the stone.

*Someone is watching me*, he thought. *Not Palpatine. Something older.*

He sat with that. Below him the hall where he had nearly killed a boy was dark, and somewhere in the clan dorms six children who would leave for Ilum in three days were asleep, or five of them were, and in two small rooms off a common room his master lay with the thread of the bond slack and whatever had come down it that afternoon put away wherever Obi-Wan put things, and in the Senate District a man who had walked in a boy's mind for six years was not asleep. All of them he had been counting since the night he woke: who knew, who did not, who had looked at him too long. He had made a list of them in a dead man's cipher and believed, because it was the only belief he could afford, that the list could be finished.

The bird had not flown.

He understood, with the cold coming up through the stone, that the list had been counting the wrong things. None of them, not the old man in the red room, not his master, not a ten-year-old girl with her saber the wrong way round, had put him here. None of them had the reach. Something had taken a dead man out of a fire on a forest moon and put him in a boy's narrow body in a bed in the Temple and had not said why, and whatever it was, it had been on this roof before him, and it had not been surprised to see him, and it was not finished.

He looked at the convor and the convor looked at him, and neither of them moved, and the city burned beneath them, and he thought, for the first time since he had opened his eyes in the dark eighteen nights ago, not *who else is here*, but *who sent him*.


# Part Two: The Watchers



## Chapter 10: The Archive Ghost

Before first bell, in a hall with the lights at half, Anakin Skywalker showed the battlemaster the stroke that had nearly killed a boy.

He showed it three times, because Drallig asked three times. The first was slow, as Drallig wanted it, the training saber coming up over the right shoulder and down in the single economical line that did not belong to any form Drallig taught, one hand on the hilt and the other hand empty and open at his side, as if it were holding something that was not there. The second was at speed. The third Drallig stopped halfway with his own blade and held there, the two humming rods crossed at head height, and looked at the boy's wrist, and then at the boy's face, and said nothing at all, which was worse than the questions had been.

"Again tomorrow," Drallig said, when he let him go. "Before the bell. Until the *Crucible* leaves."

"Yes, Master."

"You are tired."

"I didn't sleep well, Master."

"No," Drallig said. "I imagine not."

He went out through the hall with his arm aching to the shoulder and his tunic stuck to his back, because a boy's body sweated when a man's did not, and because he had been awake until the third hour on the roof with a bird that had not flown. The corridors were still dim. Initiates were not up. There was only one thing in him this morning, and it had been there since the night he went down to the courier archive and found a line in a log that said his master had looked for a planet that did not exist.

He had told himself for ten days that he would leave it. Under the line on the list, in the dead man's cipher, he had written *not yet*. He had meant it.

He washed his face in the refectory refresher, so that he would look like a boy who had washed his face, and went to the Archives.

* * *

The Archives at the first hour were as he remembered them from both of his lives, which was the trouble with the Archives. The light came down blue from the high windows and lay along the stacks like water in a channel. The air had the dry, kept smell of old flimsi and older stone.
