Along the central aisle the bronze busts of the Lost Twenty stood on their plinths, looking at nothing, and the twentieth of them, the newest, had a long patrician face and a beard that had been cast in metal six years ago and had not changed since, and Anakin did not look at it as he passed.

Jocasta Nu was at her desk. She was always at her desk; he had never in either life come into the Archives and not found her there, with her white hair pinned and her hands folded and her eyes already on the door, as if the door were a reference she had filed and expected to be consulted.

"Padawan Skywalker," she said. "That is twice in a tenday. Your master's needs are extensive."

"Yes, Master Nu."

"And at this hour."

"I have a penance with Master Drallig before the bell," he said. "I thought I would come after. Before class."

"So I had heard." Her eyes went over him once, neutral as a scanner. "Half the Temple had heard by supper. What may I give you?"

The courtesy of librarians, he thought. He had forgotten it. It was the precise opposite of the way Palpatine gave a thing: you were told exactly what you might have and given all of it, and told exactly what you might not and given none, and the line was never pretended to be anywhere other than where it was.

"My master's training materials," he said. "The access record. He has me working through the Soresu commentaries he assigned and I wanted to see which he'd pulled himself, so I don't repeat him."

"That is a sensible thing to want." She was already reaching for a stylus. "A Padawan may see the access record for his own master's assigned training materials, as those are in part his. The general query log he may not see. It belongs to the Order, not to any one Jedi in it, and certainly not to any one Jedi's apprentice." She said it without weight, as a fact about the building, like the height of the windows. "If Master Kenobi wishes to know what he himself has consulted, he is welcome to come and ask me, and I shall tell him, because I remember."

"Yes, Master Nu."

"Terminal nine. I have opened it for you." She paused. "Padawan."

"Master?"

"You were here on the tenth night after the eighth hour, checking something for your master, you told my assistant. I mention it only so that you know it is written down." Her mouth moved, not quite a smile. "Everything is written down. That is what the Archives are. Nothing that was ever filed here has been lost, in a thousand years, and I will not have it begin with a Padawan who has not slept."

He thanked her. He went to terminal nine. He sat down in a chair that was slightly too large for him, in front of a screen whose top edge he had to lift his chin to see, and he put his two flesh hands on the input surface, and for a moment, before he had decided anything, they were still.

*Nothing that was ever filed here has been lost.*

She had said it with pride, and she was wrong. There was a planet missing from her Archives, cut out six years ago by a man with a Council member's codes, so cleanly that the index had closed over the wound without a scar, and the woman at the desk did not know, and would not know for four years.

He pulled up the Soresu commentaries. He looked at them for the length of time a boy would look at them. Then he went down.

* * *

The Archive's index was old. It had been old when he was nine, and old when his master had stood at this desk in another life asking about a planet, and very old in the year the Temple changed hands and someone was needed who understood how the Jedi had kept their records, so that the records could be used to find the Jedi.

He had been that someone. There had been a season after the Temple fell when the index was the most useful thing in the building: every Knight ever assigned a mission, every initiate ever found on a world the Order had visited, all of it filed and kept, because the Jedi had believed memory was a virtue. He had walked the architecture of it in the dark with the respirator going and the stacks dead around him, and learned which partitions the droids had built and which maintenance accounts had been made in the second century of the building and never retired, because librarians did not retire things.

His hands knew it. That was what frightened him. He had not decided; he had only sat down with the Soresu commentaries open and let his hands do what they had done before, and they had gone, in the way a man's feet go to a door in a house he grew up in, down past the public index to the catalogue layer, and through the catalogue layer by a gap the droids had left when the Temple was young, and into a maintenance account that had the plain, unguarded name of a librarian dead nine hundred years. It took four minutes. It made no sound. The screen above his chin went on showing the Soresu commentaries to anyone who walked past, and beneath them, in a window the size of his palm, the general query log of the Jedi Archives opened to him like a hand.

He looked at his own hands on the surface, narrow and knuckly and a boy's. Anyone watching would see a fifteen-year-old frowning at a text. The competence was thirty-five years old and had been learned in a building full of the dead, and it was in a child's fingers, and the fingers were not even cold.

*The stacks dark and the red light coming through the high windows, and a woman with white hair standing in the aisle with her blade lit, and her voice, not frightened, telling him he was not permitted.*

He breathed. It was still a surprise, to breathe. He put the memory down where he put things and went into the log.

KENOBI, O. He knew the line already. He read the whole record this time: two years ago, in the cold third of 28 BBY, from terminal four, the one nearest the desk and nearest Jocasta Nu, which was either carelessness or a kind of honesty. String: *Kamino*. Duration before abandonment: forty minutes. Result: null. The index noted, with the flatness of a thing that did not know it had been cut, that no world of that name was recorded in the Archives of the Jedi Order.

Forty minutes. Obi-Wan had sat at the terminal nearest the desk for forty minutes looking for a planet that was not there, and then, he supposed, got up and asked her, and been told it did not exist.

Anakin scrolled. He meant only to see what came after. What came after was four days later.

KENOBI, O. Terminal four. String: *Sifo-Dyas*. Result: filled.

He did not move for some time.

The record Obi-Wan had been given was the public one. Master Sifo-Dyas, of the High Council; a seer, by reputation, with visions that troubled the Council; lost in 32 BBY on a diplomatic errand, presumed dead; a memorial entry three lines long. Obi-Wan had read it for eleven minutes. Then nothing else that week.

The boy whose body he wore had known that name in the way a boy knew the names of the dead. It had been said in corridors the year he came to the Temple, in the voice adults used for a thing that was over: Master Sifo-Dyas had gone out and not come back. He was not among the Lost Twenty, because he had not left; he was only lost. To a boy, that was all it was. A Master who vanished.

The man inside the boy knew the rest. He knew that Sifo-Dyas had gone to Kamino and ordered an army in the Order's name, and that his friend Dooku had learned of it, and had the Pykes of Oba Diah bring the ship down, and had used Sifo-Dyas's own codes to cut the planet from this room. He knew what the army was for, because he had led it up the Temple stair. And he knew that in the year his master had typed those two names into terminal four, the number of living beings who could have put them side by side was perhaps five, and that none of them was Obi-Wan Kenobi, aged twenty-nine, Jedi Knight.

He tried anyway.

Obi-Wan had been researching Dooku. That was a sane thing for a man to do in 28 BBY: his master's master had left the Order and was writing pamphlets, and Sifo-Dyas had been Dooku's friend, their names were in the same histories, one led to the other. That nearly held. And Kamino—a word from Dex, perhaps, a Besalisk's story over a counter, looked up out of idleness and found absent and forgotten. That held too, on its own.

What did not hold was the four days. He looked at the two dates until the numbers lost their meaning and became only shapes, and the shapes said what they had said the first time: a man had looked for a planet that had been erased, and when he did not find it, he had looked for the man whose codes had erased it, and he had done both in a single week, two years ago, at the terminal nearest the librarian's desk, as if he had nothing to hide.

Or as if he had learned, somewhere, that the best place to hide a thing was in the open.

*Who taught you that*, Anakin thought, and did not know, for a moment, whether he was asking his master or himself.

* * *

He should have closed the window then. He had more than he had come for. Jocasta Nu's assistant went past the end of the aisle with a cart of flimsi and did not look at him, and a sane man would have shut the maintenance account and gone to Vos-Kerrick's class and thought about it there.

He did not close it. He could not have said why, except that it was the same thing that had taken him to the courier archive on the tenth night and to the roof on the nineteenth: the sense that there was a second line in the log, if he only scrolled far enough.

The initiate files were in another partition. He had used them once to find out which children had been sent to which worlds for safekeeping, and the memory of that came up in him so fast that he had to put both hands flat on the surface and wait for it to go by.

TANO, Ahsoka. Togruta. Found on Shili by Master Plo Koon in 33 BBY, aged three, in a village whose name the file gave and he did not read; brought to the Temple the same month; Bear Clan, then Hawkbat. The clan notes were the sort of thing clan notes were. *Intense. Does not cry when the others cry. Prefers to watch. Grip faults persistent; corrected; recurs.* A note from Drallig's assistant in the year she was seven, about the saber, in a tone of bafflement. A note from Master Sen, two years ago, that she was *older than her years, and knows it, and would prefer we did not.*

And between them, filed from the Halls of Healing, under a healer's initials he did not recognise, dated 31 BBY.

*Initiate Tano, aged five, brought by clan-mother. Persistent night terrors, some weeks' duration; wakes without crying out; will not describe content. Physically well. Asked this healer, unprompted, "whether a dream can be a memory." Did not pursue it when answered. Rest and observation advised. No recurrence.*

He read it twice. The second time he read it under his breath, the way a boy might sound out a difficult word, and the sound of it in his own fifteen-year-old voice was so wrong that he stopped halfway.

*Whether a dream can be a memory.*

The floor of the Archives was stone and had been stone for a thousand years and it did not move. He was aware of this. He was aware of his heart, which was a boy's heart and went fast on nothing, going fast now, and of his hands, which had gone cold after all. He was aware that a healer had written *no recurrence* because the child had never said it again, and that a child who never said a thing again had either forgotten it or learned not to.

She was ten. She had been five. Children asked things. He had asked Qui-Gon, at nine, in the belly of a ship, whether the stars were the same ones from the other side, and it had been a boy's question. Children had night terrors. Children were brought to the Temple at three from mothers, and dreamed of them, and could not say what they dreamed because they did not have the words yet, and a quick child might ask whether the thing she saw at night had happened to her, because it felt like it, and a kind healer would say no and the child would nod and not ask again. That held entirely. There was nothing in it.

He had put a training saber into her hand in a hall nineteen days ago and she had turned it round, and she had looked at his hand and not at his face, and he had told himself she was a youngling with a grip fault, and he told himself so now, and the healer's note sat in its window the size of his palm and said *whether a dream can be a memory* in a voice he could not place and could not stop hearing.

He closed it. He closed the partition and the maintenance account and the gap the droids had left, in reverse order, carefully, the way a man leaves a house, and when the screen above his chin showed only Soresu, he sat for a while longer with his hands in his lap, so that if anyone looked they would see a boy who had finished his reading and was tired.

On his way out Jocasta Nu looked up from her desk.

"Did you find what your master needed?"

"Yes, Master Nu," Anakin said. "Thank you."

"Good." She returned to her stylus. "Sleep, Padawan. The commentaries will keep. We keep everything."

* * *

He did not go to Vos-Kerrick's class. He went up instead, to a maintenance landing on the east side of the Tranquillity Spire that nobody used, with a window the width of his shoulders, and sat on the sill with his boots against the far wall and took out the datapad.

He made a second page. He had the habit from another life, and had hated it then too: laying a thing out in lines so that it could be looked at without being felt. Four lines. He wrote them in the cipher and they looked like a maintenance schedule, and he read them back and every one of them was hateful.

*One. Obi-Wan.* The man who had searched for Kamino and then for the one name that explained it, who said *my friend* and touched his shoulder and sat on the edge of his bed at night and had never once let the Chancellor keep him for a whole day. If that man had come back, from wherever, then he had spent four years raising a boy he knew would burn, and had looked at that boy every morning across a teapot with the lava bank behind his eyes, and had been kind. Anakin could not bear it. He would rather have had the Obi-Wan who was only Obi-Wan. He would rather, he found, have been the only one.

*Two. The child.* A ten-year-old with her blade the wrong way round, who had asked at five whether a dream could be a memory. If the child had come back, then she had come from somewhere he had sent her, because there was nowhere she could have come from where he had not been the thing in the dark; and she had lived seven years in a body that could not reach the shelves, among children, telling nobody. He did not know which was worse: that she might have seen him through the cracked mask and come back to this Temple to find him waiting with a training saber, or that she might simply be a girl who had bad dreams at five, and he was building a cathedral on her.

*Three. Both.* He did not write anything under that. He looked at the word for a long time.

*Four. Neither.* Twenty-three years in the suit, and a memory that had been through a fire. Perhaps Obi-Wan had always been this warm and he had not noticed, being a boy and then a monster. Perhaps the healer's note was a healer's note. Perhaps the bell he had heard on the first night was his own skull ringing, and the bird on the roof was a bird, and he was alone in a child's body in a building that would burn, and mad. He had thought that the kindest of the four when he wrote it. Reading it back, it was the one that frightened him most, because it was the only one in which nobody was coming.

He put the datapad away. Below the window the Processional Way was filling with the morning, and the small sand-coloured shapes of a clan were being walked to a class, two by two, and he watched them until he could not tell which clan it was and then stopped.

He had to know. He could not ask. The thing was to find a question that could be asked without being one.

Something small, he thought. Something safe. Something a boy might say.

* * *

The Chancellor's standing audience fell that afternoon, as it fell every tenth day, and Anakin went, as he had gone on the tenth. Palpatine was pleasant; he talked of the Outer Rim conference and the Senate's tempers and the price of patience, and somewhere between the second cup and the third he tried the wall again, with a lighter hand than before, a thumb laid against a door to learn whether it was still a door. It held. Palpatine only smiled, pleased rather than troubled, like a man who finds the lock he has been trying has been oiled, and Anakin carried that smile back across the Senate District and liked it less than he would have liked anger.

They ate in the quarters that night, because Obi-Wan had been in the Council tower again until the seventh hour and came back with the look he had when he had been asked things, and said he would rather not sit in the refectory and be looked at, and did Anakin mind. Anakin did not mind. He brought the trays up himself from the kitchens, and set the common room's one table, and when Obi-Wan came out of his room with his hair damp and his belt off he had the tea going, which made Obi-Wan stop in the doorway.

"Well," Obi-Wan said. "I shall have to arrange for you to be in disgrace more often."

"It's only tea, Master."

"It is only tea, which you have made unasked, on the day after I watched you put a blade a finger from a boy's throat." He sat. "Sit down; you look like something Drallig has been using to sand a floor."

"He makes me do it slowly," Anakin said. "Three times. He doesn't say anything."

"He said a good deal to me." Obi-Wan poured. He did it as he had always done it, in both lives, with the care of a man for whom the pouring was the point. "None of it you need to hear tonight. Eat."

They ate. It was ordinary: the two of them at the small table with the city going violet in the window, the way Obi-Wan put his fork down to say a thing and picked it up when he had said it, the warmth that came down the thinned bond like heat from a wall with a fire on the other side. It had been like this once. Anakin had been too young to know it was precious and then too old to remember it had been real. He sat in it now, across from a man who might be a stranger wearing his master's face, and ate stew, and waited, and the moment came, as moments did, in the ordinary place.

"Vos-Kerrick had us on the Outer Rim conference," he said. Vos-Kerrick had, three days ago; it was true enough to be checked. "The neutrals. She said the Duchess of Mandalore has refused to send anyone at all, not even an observer. Half the class thought that was cowardice."

He said it to the stew, with a boy's lazy, half-interested scorn, the voice of someone trying on an opinion from class to see if it fit, and he let the name go past as if it were any name, a title out of a politics lesson, the kind of thing a fifteen-year-old repeated without having thought about it.

Obi-Wan did not stop eating. He did not look up quickly or slowly; he looked up in the time it took to look up. He finished the mouthful. He reached for his tea.

"Who told you about Satine?" he said.

Mildly. Entirely mildly. The way he might have said, *who told you Drallig was in a temper*. There was interest in it, and a very small amount of something that might have been amusement, and nothing else, and the bond carried the same low warmth it had carried through the stew, and Anakin sat with his spoon in his hand and could not read it.

He could not read it. He had been reading men's faces for forty years, across tables and the decks of ships and through a lens, and Obi-Wan's face in the violet light told him only that he had once known a woman called Satine and was faintly surprised to hear her title in his Padawan's mouth. It was the face of a man of thirty-one who had spent a year on Mandalore at twenty and did not talk about it. It was also, exactly, the face of a man of fifty-seven who had lost her, and had heard his Padawan say her name in another life, and had decided in the time it took to finish a mouthful to give nothing away. Both faces were the same face. That had been the whole problem since the first morning.

"Master Vos-Kerrick," Anakin said. "In class. Why? Do you know her?"

"I did," Obi-Wan said. "A long time ago, on a mission with Qui-Gon, when I was a few years older than you are. She was not a coward then. I doubt she has become one." He drank. "You may tell half the class so, if you like, with my compliments, and the other half that neutrality is a great deal harder work than war. Though I would not say so in front of Vos-Kerrick. She has opinions."

"Yes, Master."

"Eat your supper."

He ate his supper. The conversation went elsewhere, to the *Crucible*, to Huyang, who Obi-Wan said would like Anakin and never admit it, to whether Yoda wanted Anakin useful on Ilum or merely out of the Temple for a tenday, which Obi-Wan did not answer. The city went from violet to black. Obi-Wan cleared the trays and would not let him help, and said, "Early, tomorrow. Drallig," and went to his room, and the bond went slack and easy, the way it did when he slept, or wished to seem to.

Anakin sat at the empty table with the cold teapot.

He had not learned anything. The test had been small and safe and had told him what a small, safe test told, which was nothing, and he had known it would, and what he was sitting with now was not the nothing. It was that for the length of a mouthful of stew, with a name in the air between them and his master's eyes coming up to meet his at exactly the ordinary speed, he had felt his pulse go in his throat the way it had gone in a cockpit once, in the half-second before a turn, and he had liked it.

He had liked it. He had sat across from the man who taught him to hold a cup and played a card against him and waited to see if the man would play one back, and whether or not Obi-Wan had, it had been a game, two players and a table, the oldest game he knew, and some part of him that had been a boy long before it was anything else had leaned into it with its whole weight and thought, *again*. He had been something that did not play, for a long time. And here was the pleasure, undiminished, exactly where he had left it at fifteen, in a body that was fifteen, and he could not tell any longer which of them was enjoying the lie.

The shame came after, as it always had, slower and heavier and more honest.

He went to his room and did not light the lamp. Two days, he thought, lying on his back with his hands on his chest, where the respirator plate had been. Two days, and then the *Crucible*, and a world of ice, and six small figures going down into a cave at first light, the last of them a girl who had asked a healer at five whether a dream could be a memory, and Yoda watching him, and his master left behind in the Temple with a name Anakin had put into his mouth at supper, and nothing on his face at all.

*Who told you about Satine.*

He would be asked that again, he thought, in some other form, by someone. He would have to decide, before Ilum, whether he was going to go on enjoying it.


## Chapter 11: The Chancellor Takes Tea

The message came by the ordinary channel and said the ordinary things, and Obi-Wan Kenobi read it twice, standing in the common room with one boot on, because of the single word in it that was not ordinary. The Supreme Chancellor would be grateful for an hour of Master Kenobi's time at the fourth hour, in his office, on a matter of no urgency. He would be grateful if Master Kenobi came alone.

Anakin was already gone. He went to Drallig's hall before first bell now, every morning, to show the battlemaster a stroke that the battlemaster had not taught him, and he came back with his tunic dark at the collar and said nothing about it, and Obi-Wan asked nothing, which was the arrangement they had arrived at without ever discussing it. The teapot on the table was warm. The boy had made it and left it for him. Obi-Wan put his hand flat on the side of it and stood for a moment like that, and then he put his other boot on.

He walked. It was the day before the *Crucible* left and the Temple was full of the particular noise of children being made ready for something: lists read out in corridors, a Rodian girl crying in a stairwell because her boots had been taken away to be re-soled, Master Sen's voice saying *Hawkbat, with me* in the tone of a woman who had said it ten thousand times. Six of them were going. He had read the list the way he read everything that crossed the Council's antechamber, quickly and entirely, and he could have recited it, and he did not, because the sixth name on it was one he found he did not like to say even inside his own head. Anakin was to go as escort. Master Yoda had asked for him by name. Obi-Wan had been in the room when he did it and had said *Of course* with his whole face.

The Senate District at the fourth hour was all glass and the kind of light that showed every mark on a robe. He crossed Monument Plaza by the long diagonal, past the bench where he had sat every tenth day for six years waiting for a boy to come down the steps, and did not look at it.

* * *

The outer office was cool and grey and contained, as it always did, the Umbaran woman, who looked up at him with her pale eyes and said nothing, and two Senate Guards in blue who did not look up at all. The inner door opened without anyone touching it.

"Master Kenobi. How very good of you."

The Chancellor came round the desk to meet him. He was a man of middle height in dark, excellent cloth, with a face that had been kindly for so long it had set that way, and he took Obi-Wan's hand in both of his and held it a moment longer than a handshake needed. The office was red. The window behind the desk was the width of the room, and Coruscant lay in it from edge to edge, with the Temple a small brown shape far off to the left like something left on a shelf.

"Chancellor."

"Sit, sit. I have had them bring tea. I know the Order's position on most pleasures but I have never known a Jedi refuse tea, and I have known a good many Jedi." He poured it himself, from a pot that was too fine for an office, into cups that were finer.
Obi-Wan sat. The chair was good and had been placed, he noticed, a little nearer the desk than a visitor's chair usually was, so that the man behind the desk did not have to lean to speak softly. The cup was put in front of him. Steam came off it. He looked at it and found that his hand was in his lap and intended to stay there.

"You'll forgive the word *alone*," the Chancellor said, settling. "I did not want you to think the boy was in any trouble. He is not. I simply find that when a master and a Padawan sit before me together, I learn a great deal about the master and nothing about the boy, and when the boy sits alone, he tells me what he thinks I want to hear, and when the master sits alone"—a smile—"I may at last ask a plain question and have a plain answer. I hope."

"I will do my best, Chancellor."

"I am sure you will." He drank. "He seemed withdrawn, when I saw him last. I say *seemed*; I am an old man who reads faces for a living and I may read too much into a boy's. But he was quieter than I have known him. In six years I have not known him quiet. He has always been so open with me."

"He is fifteen," Obi-Wan said. "Boys that age go in and out like the tide. One learns not to set a clock by them."

"Just so. Just so. And he had a bad night, I understand. The night before the last audience. Or was it the night after?" The Chancellor frowned at his own memory and let the frown go. "The Temple's healers were kept busy, I'm told. Thirty-one children with one dream between them. It must have been very distressing for the little ones."

Obi-Wan let his face do what a Jedi Master's face did when a politician repeated Temple gossip, which was nothing at all, and said, "You are remarkably well informed about our nurseries, Chancellor."

"I hear things." The Chancellor spread a hand, deprecating. "It is most of what the office is, Master Kenobi. People bring me things to see what my face does. It is a sieve, your Temple, like every great house." A pause, perfectly placed. "I only wondered whether the boy had been among the ones who dreamed."

"The healers have been thorough."

"I'm sure." The old face was all concern. "And then there was the business with the senior Padawan. Bellas, is it? A bout that ended with a blade at a throat. I hear it with sympathy, I assure you. I was a boy once. I was called things. One does not always choose what the hand does."

"A training saber," Obi-Wan said. "A boy's temper, and a provocation I would not have tolerated from a Padawan of mine. He has been set a penance."

"By Battlemaster Drallig. Yes." The Chancellor turned his cup a quarter-turn on its saucer, and looked at it, and then up. "Forgive me. But a boy who can put a blade a finger's width from an older boy's throat and stop it there, at fifteen—I confess my first thought was not *penance*. My first thought was, is he being given enough to do?"

"He is given a great deal to do."

"Classes. Yes." He leant forward, and the chair that had been set a little too near the desk became exactly the right distance. "Master Kenobi, I will come to it. In some weeks the Naboo delegation will mark the anniversary of the battle. There will be a reception; the Queen sends people, the Senator attends, I attend. The boy was at that battle. He was nine years old and he was there, and he did a thing that is still spoken of on my homeworld in tones I will not embarrass you by repeating. It would please the Naboo to see him. It would please me a great deal. I should like him to accompany me. For the evening, nothing more. I would see him home myself."

The tea had stopped steaming. Obi-Wan looked at the man across the desk and felt, as he always felt in this room, the thing he did not have a name for and would not look for one: a cold that was not in the air, a weight on the back of the neck like a hand that had not yet been laid there. He had sat in this office perhaps forty times. He had never once picked up the cup.

"He'll be on Ilum," he said.

"Ah."

"The Gathering leaves tomorrow. Master Yoda has asked for him as escort. It is a considerable honour for a Padawan, and the first time it has been asked of him, and I don't think I could, in conscience, take him off the *Crucible* for a reception."

"Nor would I ask it. The Gathering takes, what, a week?"

"The crossing, the caves, and then the children build their sabers, which is not a thing that can be hurried, and then the crossing home. And Master Yoda," Obi-Wan said, "is not a man one hurries either."

"No." The Chancellor smiled. It was a very good smile; it reached the eyes and stayed there. "I said *some weeks*, Master Kenobi. I did not say next week. I have a Senate to keep in order and the Naboo are not people who hold a reception without a month of argument about the flowers. He will be home from Ilum, and rested, and—I should think—rather pleased with himself. And I should like to put him in a room with people who will tell him so."

"I'll put it to the Council."

"Of course." The smile did not change. The space after the word did. "The Council."

It was a small silence and it lasted exactly as long as it needed to. In it he could hear the air system and, far off, the speeder lanes, and under both the Chancellor deciding not to say something, and then deciding to say something else.

"You have never once let me have him for a whole day," the Chancellor said, quite gently. "In six years. You know, I think, that I have noticed."

"I'm sure you have, Chancellor."

"I find I rather admire it." He sat back. "You have refused me forty times with the most beautiful manners I have ever seen, and I have never once been able to be angry about it, and I have tried. I only wonder, as I wondered about the penance, whether it is the right thing for *him*. A boy that gifted. A boy that—" he searched for the word, and found it, and laid it on the desk between them like a card—"*alone*."

"He is not alone."

"No. He has you." The Chancellor looked at the untouched cup and then at the man. "Your tea, Master Kenobi. It will be quite cold."

"I'm afraid I've had rather a lot already this morning."

"Then I shall not press you." He rose; the hour was done, to the minute, and he had not once looked at the chrono. He came round the desk again and laid his hand on Obi-Wan's shoulder, lightly, the way a man does who is used to his hand being welcome. "Give him my regards. Tell him the Chancellor was asking after him. It pleases him to be asked after; he has told me so. And come and see me when you are back from—no. You are not going. I keep forgetting. You are staying behind."

"I am staying behind."

"Then come and see me while he is gone," the Chancellor said, "and we shall worry about him together."

* * *

In the plaza the light had moved round and the bench was in shadow. He did not look back at the window. He was quite sure that the man was standing at it, a dark shape against red, watching a Jedi cross a square with his hands in his sleeves; he had never seen the Chancellor at that window after an audience, not once, and he was quite sure, and he did not ask himself how, because the question had a bottom to it and he did not want to see what was lying there.

His hand was not steady. He put it in his sleeve, where it was nobody's business, and took the long way back.

* * *

The Archives in the early afternoon were a church. The light came down through the high lamps onto the long blue aisles and the busts of the dead, and a dozen initiates sat at terminals with the stillness of children who had been told that this was the one room in the Temple in which they would be killed for running. Obi-Wan found Madame Nu where she was always found, which was wherever one happened to be looking for her.

"Master Kenobi."

"Madame Nu. I'm not interrupting."

"You are, but it is a thing I have been meaning to put down." She set the datapad on a lectern and folded her hands over it. She was a small, upright woman with white hair pulled back hard and a face that had looked at eight hundred years of catalogue and found most of it wanting. "What do you need?"

"Nothing very much. A question about the logs." He said it lightly, in the voice one used for a thing one had been meaning to ask for weeks and had kept forgetting. "I wondered who had been reading them lately. The access logs. The record of who asks the Archive for what. I had a reason to think they'd been looked at."

Jocasta Nu regarded him for a moment with an expression he could not quite read.

"You are the second Kenobi to come to me about the Archive's records in a tenday," she said.

"The second."

"I'm aware he is a Skywalker. He is a Kenobi in every way that matters to a librarian. He asks for a thing by asking for the thing next to it." She unfolded her hands and folded them the other way. "Your Padawan came to me yesterday and asked, very politely, for exactly one thing: his master's Soresu training-access record. It was a proper request and I gave it to him. And I told him, which he had not asked, that the general query log was not for Padawans. Initiates and Padawans do not read who-read-what; they would do nothing else. I cannot tell you why I said it. He thanked me and went away."

"Did he."

"That same morning the general query log was opened from terminal nine, for most of an hour, under a maintenance account belonging to a librarian who has been dead nine hundred years." She let that stand. "The Archive is old, Master Kenobi, and it was built by people who assumed everyone in it would be honest, which is a charming assumption and a poor one. But it traces. It is slow, and it is ugly, and it took less than a day, and it points at terminal nine, and at the hour, and at the only person in this building I had told that log was closed. He found a way. I imagine he would say he was told no, and not told *how* no."

Obi-Wan said, "I'm sorry."

"Don't be. I have logged it. Reporting is for people with more time than I have, and I have not yet decided whether this is a thing for Master Yoda or a thing for you, and so I am telling you, because you are his master, and because you asked, and because I have a feeling you would rather hear it from me than from Master Windu." A pause. "What was he looking for?"

"I don't know."

"No." She picked the datapad up again. "I should tell you, since you asked so lightly, that the unfilled queries for the last five years are not a long document. Most are initiates misspelling things. The rest, one could read in a minute." She looked at him over the pad, and the look was not unkind. "Including yours, Master Kenobi. The one, at least: Kamino is there, unfilled. The other was filled, and so it is not on that list. He will have read the one. I thought you should know that before you decided what to say to him."

"Thank you, Madame Nu."

"Thank me by teaching him to ask properly. I should hate to have to change the locks on a building this size."

* * *

He walked out through the hall of busts without looking at the one he did not look at, and down the long stair, and found that he had stopped on the landing with his hand on the rail and the children's voices coming up from below like water.

Why would Anakin look for a planet that does not exist?

The sentence arrived whole and he turned it over and could not make it mean one thing. There was no such planet. There was no such entry. Two years ago he had sat at terminal four and typed a name into the Archive and watched it give him back nothing, for forty minutes, the way it gave nothing for a misspelling, and then he had gone away and come back four days later and typed another name, a dead Jedi's, and read what there was. He had never been able to say, even to himself, which of the two sentences he was telling himself when he thought about that afternoon: that the place was not there, or that the place was not *there*. He was not able to say it now. The boy had read the log. The boy had read a name that was nothing, tagged with his master's initials, and the boy had not come to him, and had sat across a supper table instead and said another name, a woman's, and watched his face.

Obi-Wan took his hand off the rail. He did not know what the boy was looking for. He was sure of that. It was the only thing he had said to Madame Nu that had been entirely true, and he found he was holding it the way one held a railing, and he let go of it, and went down.

* * *

CoCo Town at the eighth hour smelt of fry-oil and hot ferrocrete and the faint sweetness that came off the recycling stacks when the wind was wrong. The diner sat on its corner like a thing that had been there before the corner and intended to outlast it, all chrome and red seats and a window that had not been clean in Obi-Wan's lifetime. The droid at the counter swivelled her head at him and shouted into the kitchen before he had reached a stool.

"Obi-Wan!"

Dexter Jettster came out of the back wiping two of his hands on an apron and holding the other two wide, and Obi-Wan allowed himself to be embraced by something the size of a speeder with the smell of a griddle on it. The Besalisk held him off and looked at him and said, "You're thin," which he had said at every meeting for nine years, and steered him to the corner booth and lowered himself into the far side of it with the sound of a building settling.

"Tea," Dex told the droid, without asking. "The real stuff, not the sweepings. And pie. He'll say no. Bring it anyway." He folded his upper arms on the table and his lower ones under it. "So. How's the kid?"

"Growing."

"They do that. Still flying everything he can get his hands on?"

"Everything with a key in it, and several things without."

Dex's small bright eyes went over him in the way they had, the way of a man who had bought and sold things on forty worlds and could price a face. "You didn't come for pie."

"I came for the pie."

"You came for the pie *and*." The tea arrived. Obi-Wan held the cup. It was hot and thick and tasted of the bottom of a pot, and he drank it, and Dex watched him drink it and waited.

"Two years ago," Obi-Wan said, "I asked you about the Kaminoans."

Dex's face did not change, exactly. It arranged itself. "You did."

"I wondered whether anything had changed."

"Nothing's changed." He said it without hesitation and without heat, as a man says a thing he has already checked. "I told you then and I'll tell you now, and it's the same telling. Tall. Pale. Long necks, like something that's been stretched. Eyes like a fish that's been left out. Cloners, and the best there are, if you can find 'em and if you can pay, and most can't do either. They're out past the Rishi Maze, south of everything, so far off the charts the charts don't bother. And nobody—nobody, Obi-Wan—has seen one in this part of the galaxy in decades. I asked around after you asked me. Quiet. People who'd know. Not a whisper." He turned his tea cup with one thick finger. "Same as before. Word for word, near enough."

It was. Obi-Wan had known it would be; he had known the shape of the sentence before Dex got to it, *past the Rishi Maze*, and had felt his own mouth want to make the words a half-beat ahead, and had kept it still. He had not come for the answer. He had the answer. He had come to hear a man who was not a Jedi and not a ghost say the thing out loud in a room with grease on the windows, and to see whether it was still true that nobody was looking, and it was. Nobody was looking. Nobody but a boy with a dead librarian's codes and his master's initials in front of him.

"Thank you, Dex."

"Don't thank me, tell me." The Besalisk leant in, and the booth creaked. "Two years ago you asked, and I told you, and you went. Fine. But you've come back with the same question. Not *what else have you heard*. The same question, like you're checking I've not changed my story." He did not look offended. He looked worried, which was worse. "Why does a Jedi keep asking about a people nobody's seen?"

"A loose thread," Obi-Wan said. "I dislike loose threads. It is a failing in a man of my training; Qui-Gon used to say so."

"Two years is a long time to pull on one."

"I am a patient man."

"You're a lot of things." Dex sat back. "And you came in here looking like a man who's been to a funeral. You want to tell me whose, I'm here. You don't, fine. Eat the pie."

Obi-Wan ate the pie. When he stood to go, Dex took his hand in one of his and his elbow in another, which was as near as a Besalisk came to saying something gently.

"Bring the kid next time. Before he's taller than you."

"That ship," said Obi-Wan, "has sailed."

* * *

The quarters were dark when he came in and there was a light under Anakin's door, and then, as he stood in the common room with his cloak half off, there was not. He waited. He felt for the bond at the back of his mind the way one feels for a tooth with the tongue, and it was where it had been for a tenday: thin, slack, a thread where a rope had been, letting through the faint grey of a boy pretending to sleep or sleeping. He stood with his hand on the back of a chair and did not go to the door. He had sat on the boy's bed once in the night, ten days ago, and had been seen doing it.

The Chancellor was asking after you, he thought at the door. It pleases you to be asked after. You have told him so.

He went into his own room and did not open the drawer. There was a list in the drawer, in a hand he had taught himself so that it would not be his, and he had not added to it in a month, and he did not add to it now. He lay down in his clothes. One day. A ship, and a cold world, and six children with their boots re-soled walking into a cave at sunrise, the sixth of them a girl who held her saber the wrong way round and who, across the Room of a Thousand Fountains, had looked away first, and he had felt, absurdly, that he had lost. And Anakin at Yoda's shoulder. And Obi-Wan here, in a Temple full of people hunting a shadow, with an invitation to take tea.

He slept, eventually, and the dream came, the one that came every few months and never changed.

*A ridge of pale rock, and the sun over it so white there was no colour anywhere. Below, a long way below, a farm: a ring of tall machines standing in the sand like the spars of a wreck, a low dome with a door in it, a pit of a courtyard sunk into the ground against the heat. He was on the ridge. He was always on the ridge. A boy came up out of the courtyard with something in his hand, a tool, a toy, it was too far to see; a small boy in grey with hair the sun had bleached to nothing, and he stood in the glare and shaded his eyes and looked at the horizon, not at the ridge, never at the ridge, as if he was waiting for something to come over it. A woman's voice called from the dome. The boy did not go in at once. Obi-Wan did not go down. There was a rule, and the rule was that he did not go down, and he had made the rule himself for reasons he could not, in the dream, remember, only their shape, like a wall under his hand in the dark. The wind came across the sand and moved the boy's hair and brought no sound up the ridge at all. The boy went in. The dome's door closed. The machines in the sand ticked in the heat, one after another, a sound like a clock that had been set to the wrong day.*

He woke. The room was black and the Temple was silent in the way it was only silent in the hour before the first bell, and his face was wet, and he lay and let it be wet and did not touch it. On the table by the bed was the pad he kept for the things that came to him at night, and the stylus beside it, and his hand went out towards them in the dark and stopped.

There were things he wrote down, and there were things he did not. He had kept the two columns for four years with great care. He put his hand back on his chest and lay in the dark and listened to the boy breathing through the wall, and did not write it down.


## Chapter 12: Interlude: The Door

The Works kept no time, which was its virtue. Nothing in the district had been switched on in forty years; no shift had been called, no clock wound, and the only sounds that reached the room where Sidious stood were the settling of old iron and, a long way down, water finding its way across a foundry floor poured before he was born. He came here when he wanted to think without the Senate humming in the walls. He had been thinking, on and off, for eleven days, and he had enjoyed every one of them.

The boy's mind had been a garden. That was the word he had used for it privately, six years ago, and he had never found a better. A small walled plot, hot and bright, with the mother in the centre of it like a well. He had gone in every tenth day since the boy was nine. He had learned the paths. He had planted things, with care, and pulled up what Kenobi planted, and watched the Jedi's little lessons wither in that heat because the ground was wrong for them. A grievance against a Council that would not trust him. A fear of being sent away. The speeder he was not allowed. The master who waited in the plaza. The mother. Always the mother, and always the way back to her, which ran through this office and nowhere else. It had been slow, pleasurable, uncomplicated work, and he had been very good at it.

Eleven days ago the boy had sat in the chair across from him, and Sidious had gone in as he always went in, with the kindly question and the hand on the shoulder, and had found the garden, and the mother, and the speeder, and a girl's name he had not heard before, and beneath all of it, where the ground should have been, a wall. It had been shut three weeks, he judged, and he had been the last to know.

He had stood in front of it a long moment, in the part of the audience where he was saying something about the Senate's gratitude. It was not a Jedi's wall. He knew Jedi shielding: a clean room, a closed door, serenity laid over the mind like a cloth, and all of it learned from a book. This was old. It had been built in courses over years and it had been struck, hard, more than once, and mended where it was struck, and the mending was rougher than the building. He had put his hand to it, so to speak, and felt it hold, and felt it not care that he was there.

He had pressed nothing. He had smiled, and asked after the boy's letters home, and let the audience run its hour. And at the end of it, standing, the boy had said, *Thank you, my Master,* and corrected himself a breath later to *Chancellor*, flushing to the ears the way fifteen does; and the cadence of it had gone through Sidious like warm wine. Not the words. The cadence. It was how an apprentice said the word. It was how one had said it to him once, kneeling, with horns bowed almost to the floor.

He had gone back to his office and sat with the window at his back and had recognised, with something very close to delight, that he had been surprised.

It had been years.

He did not waste a moment on the impossible. The wall was there; it had been built by someone; that someone had either taught the boy or was the boy's borrowed hand. He had taught that discipline, in its whole form, to two people in his life. One of them had fallen in two pieces down a reactor shaft on Naboo six years ago, and Kenobi had been knighted for it. The other was on Serenno writing pamphlets.

He stood in the dark of the Works and considered Tyranus.

It was not out of character. Dooku was a man of reasons, and a man of reasons could always find one more. He had been Qui-Gon Jinn's master; the boy had been Qui-Gon Jinn's find; it would not be beyond him to think of the child as an inheritance left in the wrong hands. He had the knowledge. He had the pride. And an apprentice who took a pupil of his own, quietly, to present later as a gift, was a thing that had happened before among the Sith, and had usually ended with the gift being used on the giver.

Against it: Dooku had never met the boy. Sidious would have known. There was very little about his apprentice he did not know, including several things Dooku believed were his own. And the wall was old. That was the detail that did not fit any shape he tried it in. Six years of pamphlets would not build it. Six years of anything would not. It had the look of a thing built in a place where it was struck every day.

He let that go for the present, as one sets a piece aside on a board.

At yesterday's audience he had tried the wall again with a lighter hand, the touch of a man testing a latch he does not mean to lift, and it had held, and had not cared then either. That pleased him more than the surprise had; a thing that held twice was a thing that had been made to.

The rest he had assembled the way he assembled everything, from the Temple's own leakage. The Jedi believed themselves sealed. They were a city of ten thousand with a public gate, and a Knight came to the Senate Office Building every tenday to carry mission requests, a pleasant man called Aben Tuur who talked, over caf, as men talk who have never been listened to properly. From him Sidious knew that thirty-one children under twelve had woken one night crying from the same dream, and that the Council had a word for it now and the word was *shadow*. From the Padawans' politics instructor, by way of a senator from Chalacta and Mas Amedda, he knew that Kenobi's boy had put the Battlemaster's best pupil on the floor with a single stroke nobody had taught him, and was doing penance for it each morning before first bell, in front of Drallig, who had said nothing the first morning and then asked him to do it again.

And from Kenobi himself, sitting in that same chair with his hands folded and his courtesy on like armour, he knew that the boy's master had been asked for one evening, the anniversary reception, and had said, *I'll put it to the Council,* which in six years of this had meant no every time.

Sidious found he was smiling, alone, in the dark, and allowed it.

A struck wall. A dream the whole nursery shared. A stroke the Battlemaster had never seen. *My Master,* in that cadence. Something had come into the Temple, and the Jedi were hunting it, and looking in every direction except at the one boy they had never liked. The Order might well do his work for him before the year was out; it had a long history of that. But he did not intend to wait for it.

He woke the projector.

The figure that formed in the air was seated, which was Dooku's way of reminding him that on Serenno it was not yet dawn, and rose. The robe was dark; the beard was precise; the hands were folded at the waist in the manner he had carried out of the Order with him along with the posture and the contempt.

"Master."

"Tyranus. When were you last on Coruscant?"

A pause of exactly the right length. "The spring session. The Senate committee on Outer Rim commerce; I sat in the public seats as a private citizen. You were in the gallery."

"I was. And since?"

"Serenno. Twice to Raxus on the Institute's business. Nowhere else."

"Have you touched Kenobi's Padawan?"

The pause this time was not the right length, and that was itself information. "I have never seen the boy."

"That is not what I asked."

"No, Master." The old man's chin came up a fraction. "I have not. I would not. He is Qui-Gon's; I have always understood he was yours."

Sidious watched the blue light of the face and the stillness under it, and read it, and believed it almost entirely. The remainder he put with the other piece he had set aside, and did not look at it yet.

"Something has," he said. "The boy's mind is shut. It is shut in a manner you would recognise, and that I would recognise, and that I did not teach him. I wish to know who did. You will come to Coruscant."

"For what occasion?"

"The anniversary of Naboo. The Chancellor receives the heroes of the battle, as he does every year, and this year the Jedi will send Master Kenobi and his Padawan. I asked Kenobi for the boy alone, knowing he will insist on coming too, and I want him there, in the room, where he can be watched. The invitation is in a form that cannot be refused twice. A Count who has made such a name for himself denouncing the Senate's corruption can surely attend a reception in honour of the Senate's one honest victory. It will be remarked on. Let it be remarked on. You will be my guest."

"You want me to look at him."

"I want you to look at him. Nothing more. You will not touch what I have not told you to touch." He let that sit. "Kenobi has kept the boy from me for six years with a wall of small courtesies, and I have let him, because a boy who is kept from a thing wants it. He has now put it to the Council whether the child may be spared for an evening. The Council will tell him what I tell it. In the meantime Yoda is taking him to Ilum tomorrow, with the initiates, for the Gathering."

"Yoda," Dooku said, and nothing else, and a great deal.

"Just so. Yoda is not a fool, whatever else he is. He will stand the boy on the ice and watch him, and he will see something, and he will not know what he has seen, and he will say nothing about it to anyone, because that is what Yoda does. That gives us some weeks." Sidious inclined his head. "Go back to bed, Tyranus. Write something stirring about the Rim."

The figure bowed, and folded, and was gone, and the dark of the Works came back in.

There was the Senate to think of, and he let it come, because it was restful. The Naboo girl's bill was a nuisance of the kind he found almost endearing: a reform of Outer Rim franchise law, drafted in Amidala's apartment with Organa holding the stylus and Mothma holding the numbers, requiring the great trade houses to disclose their holdings in the systems they shipped from and placing the disputes in a tribunal the Rim would sit on. It was well made. It would go to committee, and in committee it would acquire friends, and the friends would improve it until it did nothing, and the girl would vote for it anyway because it had her name on it, and he would thank her for it in public. He had done this before. She did not yet understand that she was being taught.

The term was the thing. The law had its arithmetic, and the arithmetic was nearly done with him, and there was no crisis yet of the size that made a Senate forget its own rules. There would be. The Rim was dry tinder; it only wanted a man of standing to say aloud what everyone on Raxus and Sullust and Eriadu already muttered, and say it in good sentences, and be seen to be sincere. The pamphlets were doing that. The Institute was doing that. He had read Dooku's last address with real pleasure, the way one reads a letter one has dictated and finds improved in the copying. Another year, perhaps two, and the Rim would be unhappy in an organised way, and the Senate would be frightened in a disorganised one, and the Chancellor would be asked to stay.

And the reception was a stage, which was all a reception was for: the Naboo girl at his elbow as the hero of the hour, the Jedi in their brown with the boy among them, Dooku in black at the far end drawing every eye that was not on the Chancellor. He would stand in the middle of it with a glass he did not drink from and let them all look at each other.

He turned the projector off at the wall and stood in the full dark, and the thought he had set aside came back, because he had known it would.

Two people. He had taught that discipline to two people. One of them was on Serenno, and had just told him the truth. The other had gone down a shaft on Naboo in two pieces, and Sidious had felt him go, the way one feels a lamp go out in another room. He had not gone to look. There had been a great deal to do that week.

He left it there. He was a patient man, and there were doors it was more useful to leave shut and know the whereabouts of than to open; and the boy would leave for Ilum tomorrow and stand on the ice soon after, with Yoda watching, and that was enough to be going on with.

* * *

On Serenno it was still an hour short of light, and Dooku did not go back to bed.

He went out instead onto the upper terrace, where the house looked down three tiers of garden to the valley and the valley looked up at nothing, and stood with his hands behind him in the cold. The house was the old house; his family had built it when the Republic was young and had not improved it since, and he had come back to it at seventy with his lightsaber and his name and had changed nothing but the locks. There were nineteen people in it who kept it running. He saw perhaps two of them in a week. The lamps were lit before he rose and the terrace was swept before he walked on it, and he had come to think of this as the proper condition of a house: that it should work, and that one should not have to watch it working.

Qui-Gon's boy.

He turned the phrase over; he had used it without deciding to. He had never seen the child. He knew what everyone knew: that Qui-Gon had found a slave on Tatooine and brought him to the Council like a man bringing a sacred relic down from a mountain, and the Council had refused him, and then Qui-Gon had died, and the Council had taken the boy anyway because the dead are harder to refuse. He knew Kenobi had him. He had met Kenobi four times in his life and thought him exactly what Qui-Gon would have made of a clever, anxious, obedient young man: a good Jedi, which was to say a wasted one. And he had read the Council's own phrase for the boy in the minutes that someone had seen fit to leak to the Institute: *the chosen one*, with no capitals, as if it were an embarrassment, which to them it was.

He had never gone to see him. It had been a decision, taken once, and not revisited: the boy was the Master's, and the Master's things were not touched, and in any case he had not wanted to stand in front of a child and see Qui-Gon's judgement looking back at him out of a stranger's face. He had told Sidious the truth. He had told it in the voice he had used on the Council for decades, and he had felt the Master weigh it even through a projector.

Almost believed. He was seventy-six years old and had been almost believed by two institutions now.

He let himself think about the first one for as long as it took the sky over the eastern ridge to go from black to slate. Qui-Gon dead on Naboo, in a generator room, run through by a thing the Council had not believed existed until it was standing in front of them, and the Council in their circle afterwards with their hands in their sleeves, saying *grieve* and *trust* and *the Force* as if the words were a wall one could stand behind. Twelve chairs and not one of them had gone to Naboo. He had gone. He had stood in the hangar where they had burned Qui-Gon and had understood that the Order was a body kept alive after the mind had left it, and that it would go on moving, and taking children, and sending them out to die for Senate committees, for as long as it was permitted to. He had gone back and said so, for a year, and been listened to with great courtesy, and then he had stopped saying it and begun doing something about it.

Yaddle he did not think about. That was a room in the house he kept locked. It had been necessary; he had been told so by the only person whose word on necessity he accepted; and he had done it himself, which was the most he could say.

Light came down into the valley in a long slant and found the river.

He had obeyed. He had obeyed for six years, and he had never been able to decide whether obedience was the thing he had left the Order to escape or the thing he had left it to find. He had wanted a master who was worth it. He had found one; he was still, most days, certain of that. And he was proud, which Sidious knew and used, and which Dooku knew Sidious used and had decided, at seventy, was an acceptable price for being used by a competent man rather than ignored by twelve incompetent ones. It was only lately, on this terrace before dawn, that he had begun to notice that a price could be acceptable and still be paid.

The Chancellor's reception. He saw it entire: the rotunda done up in Naboo's colours, the Queen's party in their paint, the Senator in something too plain for the occasion, the Jedi standing in a group the way Jedi stood at receptions, as if waiting for a shuttle. And himself walking in, a Count, a critic of all they stood for, and every head turning, and the Chancellor's warm old hand coming out to take his. It would be reported. The Master wanted it reported.

And a boy. Somewhere in the room, in brown, with a braid behind his ear, fifteen years old, with a wall in his mind that the Master could not get over and had not built, and that Dooku had been sent to look at as one sends a man to look at a horse.

He found that he wanted to see it. That was the unlovely truth. A Sith wall in a Jedi child, raised in a Temple full of people trained to notice exactly that and failing to; Kenobi, of all people, standing in front of it with his courtesies; Yoda about to take it to Ilum and stand it on the ice. He had spent seventy years in the Order and six outside it and he had never seen a thing he could not account for, and he had just been told, by the one person he believed, that there was one.

He did not like the errand. He did not like being sent, or being almost believed, or being told to look and not touch, as if he were a man who touched things. He would go. He would stand in the Chancellor's rotunda in black and let the Senate stare, and he would find the boy, and he would look.

The first of the house came out onto the lower terrace with a broom, saw him, and went in again without a sound. Dooku stayed where he was until the sun was fully on the river, and then he went in to write something stirring about the Rim.


## Chapter 13: Ilum

The *Crucible* sat on the hangar floor like something the Temple had forgotten it owned, and Anakin Skywalker stood under its wing at the fifth hour with six children shouting around his knees and tried to remember whether it had looked this old the first time.

It had. He had been ten and it had been the oldest thing he had ever seen that still flew, and he had wanted to take the engines apart before they had cleared the atmosphere. Huyang had not let him. Huyang stood now at the foot of the ramp with his hands folded in front of his copper belly, the way he had stood at the foot of the ramp for a thousand years, and looked at the six younglings of Hawkbat Clan with the patient dismay of a droid who had seen ten thousand of them and expected every one to be sick on the way.

"Boots," Huyang said. "All of you. Show me."

Six pairs of boots were shown. Tam Dorreth's were on the wrong feet, which was a thing an eleven-year-old boy could do while discussing, at the top of his voice, whether the crystals were alive. Brannick Sel had his head-tails bundled inside a scarf because someone had told him Ilum would freeze them off; the Nautolan boy's eyes were enormous with the injustice of weather. Wix, the Ithorian, said something through both sides of his mouth that Huyang answered in Ithorese without looking round. Ilyo, the Rodian girl, had already got her kit bag open on the floor and was counting what was in it with a green finger. Kessa Ruun stood with her hands in her sleeves the way Master Sen stood, because Kessa did everything the way someone else did it, and beside her, a head shorter, the Tano child stood with her hands at her sides and looked at the ship.

Not at him. At the ship. Anakin had been counting, since the night of the bell, how often the girl looked at him, and he had noticed that she looked at him most when she had decided he was not looking at her. He let her have the ship.

Knight Sabo Thane came across the floor with a datapad and the expression of a man who had wanted this launch gone by the fourth hour. "Padawan Skywalker. Master Yoda's aboard. Master Kenobi's at the stair."

Anakin turned. Obi-Wan was at the top of the hangar stair with his hands behind his back, in the brown robe, as if he had come down for something else and happened to be there. He came down slowly. He looked at the children, each one, the way he looked at a room before he went into it, and then he looked at Anakin, and the bond, thin as Anakin had kept it, carried a thing across that Anakin could not have put a word to and did not try.

"They let you off the penance, then," Obi-Wan said.

"Master Drallig said he'd seen enough of the stroke to last him."

"I imagine he had." Obi-Wan's eyes went past him to the ramp, to the small shapes on it, and stayed a moment longer on the smallest. "Watch the ice at the door. It is quicker than it looks."

"I've been."

"You have." A pause, in which the older man seemed to weigh something and put it down again. "Bring them all home, my friend."

It was the sort of thing a master said at a hangar. It was not the way he said it. Anakin had two lives' worth of Obi-Wan's voice in him, and he knew the sound of a sentence that had been edited on the way out. He waited. Obi-Wan did not go on. He put a hand on Anakin's shoulder, briefly, the way he had taken to doing, and turned and went back up the stair without looking round, and Anakin stood with his shoulder warm and watched him go and thought: *you are afraid of something on that ship, and it is not me.*

"Padawan," Huyang said. "If you have finished being seen off."

* * *

The *Crucible* left the atmosphere at the sixth hour and the children were sick, as Huyang had foreseen, in the order he had foreseen, Tam first and loudest. Anakin held the boy's forehead over the refresher basin and felt, through the hull, the ancient ship find the lane and go to lightspeed, and the cabin tilted a degree and righted and the stars went to lines in the port.

"Better out than in," Tam said, wiping his mouth. "Master Sen says."

"Master Sen's right."

"Did you get sick? When you went?"

"No," Anakin said, which was true; he had been a boy who had flown pod-racers, and the *Crucible* had seemed to him then like being carried about in a cupboard. He wiped Tam's chin with the boy's own sleeve and sent him back up the passage, where Ilyo was explaining to Brannick in a carrying whisper that if you were sick in hyperspace it never came down.

The ship had one long cabin with the forge in the middle of it, cold now, and benches along the sides, and a round port at the back where the children sat in a heap to watch the lines go by. Anakin walked the length of it twice because his legs wanted walking and because the body he had was fifteen and had not learned to sit still. On the second pass he stopped by the forge. The housing clamps were the same. The gimbal was the same. Someone had rewired the heat stage, badly, perhaps a century ago.

"You wanted to open that," Huyang said behind him, "the first time. You stood where you are standing and you said the heat stage was inefficient. You were ten."

"It is inefficient."

"It is older than your Order's charter, young man, and it has made more sabers than your Order has had Knights." The droid's head tilted; the lenses adjusted. "I remember your crystal. Blue. You cut two housings for it because the first was not good enough, and you would not let me help you with the second, and you carried the first about in your pocket for a tenday afterwards as if it were a dead pet."

He had forgotten the first housing. He stood and let the memory come up and found it whole: the weight of it, the burr he had not been able to file off, the ache of having made a thing badly. He had made a great many things since. He had made a saber in the dark on Mustafar with his teeth clenched and the housing black and no one to help him, and he had not kept the first of those either.

"You remember them all?" he said.

"I remember the ones who came back." Huyang turned to the children. "Sit, all of you. Sit. Master Yoda will speak, and you will listen, or I will have the six of you polishing the forge until Ilum."

Yoda had been in the forward seat with his eyes shut since the hangar. He opened them now and came down the cabin on his stick, and the six younglings arranged themselves on the deck in front of the forge in a row, by height, which put the Tano girl on the end, and Anakin took the bench behind them and folded his long hands and made himself small.

The lesson was the one Anakin remembered. The crystal was not found; it called. One did not go into the caves to look for a thing but to be found by it. The caves would show each child what it needed to see, and each child must see it and go on, and the crystal was at the end of the going on, not at the end of the seeing. Yoda said all this in fewer words than Anakin would have used, and the children listened with their mouths open, and Tam asked whether a crystal could call the wrong person and Yoda said, "Hmm. The wrong person, is there?" and Tam thought about it so hard his face went red.

"Afraid, are you?" Yoda asked them, generally.

Five said no. The Tano child said nothing. Kessa, beside her, put a hand on her arm, and the girl let it stay there, and Anakin saw her let it, and saw the care in the letting, and wrote it in the list in his head under the long column of things that were different.

"Good," Yoda said, to the five. "Lie to me, you do. Good lies, for children." The ears went down. "In the ice, no lies. What you fear, the ice will show. What you are, the ice will show. Choose, then, what to carry out." He let it sit. "Rest now. Two days, Ilum."

The children went to their bunks in a mob and Anakin stayed where he was and found that the old eyes had come round to him and were not moving on.

He had expected it. He had been expecting it since the Council put Kenobi's Padawan on the manifest, and he had built against it: a surface Yoda could walk on without falling through, a boy of fifteen who was pleased to be going and anxious to do it well and thinking, underneath, about a girl in a politics class. He laid it out now and sat behind it and waited.

Yoda did not walk on it. He only looked. The look went on long enough that Anakin heard the engines change pitch in it, and his own pulse, and a child laughing through the bunk-room wall. It was not a probe. He would have felt a probe; he had felt Sidious's, every tenth day, like a hand on the back of the neck. This was a very old person looking at a young one, and it was worse.

"Changed, you are," Yoda said. "Grown."

"I'm fifteen, Master. It's what we do."

"Hmm." The stick shifted. "Grown, I said. Not taller." The ears lifted a fraction. "Ten, you were, when I took you to the ice. Angry. Angry at the ship, at the cold, at the crystal for being slow. A boy who thought the galaxy a thing to be fixed." A pause. "Not angry now."

"I'm older."

"Older." Yoda said the word as if he were turning it over for a hallmark. "Grieved, you have. For whom, I do not ask." The old eyes held him. "But careful, be. A man who has grieved, the ice knows. More than a boy, it will ask of you."

Then he turned, and went up the cabin on his stick, and Anakin sat behind his false surface with his heart going and realised that none of what Yoda had said had been for the Council. Some of it had been for the Order, perhaps; the long look had been the Order's. But the last had been the old creature's own, and it had been kind, and it had gone in under the surface without touching it, the way a knife goes in under a nail.

* * *

Ilum came up white in the port on the second morning and the children fell silent for the first time since Coruscant.

Anakin had seen it from the dark side of the ship the last time, as Vader, in the first year of the Empire, when it had cut the planet open with mining lasers and the sky had been full of ore barges. He had stood on a bridge and felt the crystals scream and had not cared. He looked at it now with a boy's eyes, whole, blue-white, a ring of weather at the pole, and felt the thing in his chest that the body did at such moments, which was to go hot and tight and fill his eyes. He blinked it clear. Brannick had his face flat against the port.

Huyang put them down in the valley below the temple as the sun was coming over the ridge. The cold hit as the ramp went down, a cold like a struck bell, and six children stopped as one on the lip of the ramp and then Tam went down it with a shout and the rest followed. Anakin went last with Yoda. His breath smoked. He had forgotten the feeling of cold on a face. Twenty-three years without weather, and now this, which went in at the nose and hurt.

The temple was cut into the mountain, a face of carved ice the height of a Coruscant tower, and across its door hung the waterfall, frozen hard, a sheet of grey ice thick as a wall. The children stood in front of it in a row in their sand-coloured tunics and looked up and up. Wix said something, and Ilyo said, "He says it's bigger than he thought," and Wix said something else, and she said, "He says that's all he's going to say about it."

Yoda went up the steps. He put his stick against the floor of the vestibule and shut his eyes, and the children shut theirs because he had, and Anakin, who knew what came next, kept his open.

The sun came over the ridge. It struck the great ice lens above the door and came through it as a bar of light and lay on the frozen waterfall like a hand, and the waterfall began to run. It began at the top, a thread of water, and the thread became a sheet and the sheet came down with a noise like a crowd, and behind it the door stood open and black and breathing cold.

Anakin, whose eyes were open, saw it then: on the carved stone above the falling water, where nothing alive had any business on Ilum, sat a convor, green-feathered and round-eyed, its feathers unruffled by a wind that was flaying his face. It was the bird from the Temple roof. It was not looking at the door or the children or the light; it was looking at him. He blinked against the glare off the ice, and when he looked again there was only bare stone, and Yoda, beside him, had not so much as turned his head.

"Oh," Kessa said, very small.

"Go," said Yoda. "Before the sun sets, return. Alone, each of you. Alone, the crystal calls."

Tam went first, because Tam went first into everything. Brannick went with his scarf over his mouth. Ilyo went with her bag, until Huyang said, "The bag," and she came back and left it. Wix went in and was gone like a shadow. Kessa took one step and stopped and looked back, not at the ship or at Yoda but at the girl beside her, and the Tano child said, "Go on," in a voice that was a child's voice, light and kind, and Kessa went.

The girl stood on the threshold. She was ten and small for ten and the dark in the door was taller than the Temple. Anakin stood ten paces behind her with his hands in his sleeves and saw her shoulders rise and fall once, and then the small body squared itself, and there was nothing of a child in the way it did it. She went in without looking round.

He let out a breath he had not known he was holding. Yoda, beside him, said nothing at all.

* * *

They waited in the vestibule, where the cold was only terrible and not fatal. Huyang went back to the ship to wake the forge. Yoda sat on the ice with his stick across his knees and his eyes shut, and Anakin stood, because sitting on ice was a thing the body objected to, and watched the door, and counted the hours by the bar of sun across the floor.

He knew what the caves did. He had gone in at ten, furious, and they had shown him his mother in the slave quarters with her hands in a bowl of water, saying his name, and he had walked through her as if she were smoke because Qui-Gon had been dead a year and he had already learned what walking through things cost. He had come out with the blue crystal in his fist and Yoda had looked at him then much as Yoda had looked at him on the ship. He had not thought about it for thirty-five years. He thought about it now, and about six children in the dark being shown what they feared, and found that his hands had gone to fists in his sleeves and made them open.

Tam came out at the third hour, which Anakin had bet himself he would, because Tam was a boy who ran at a thing and got it over. He came out of the dark at a run and skidded on the vestibule ice and went down on his backside and held up his fist and shouted, "Blue!" and then, "Master, it talked to me, it actually talked, it said my name," and Yoda opened one eye and said, "Your name, it said? Or your name, you heard?" and Tam sat on the ice with his crystal and his mouth open. Anakin pulled him up by the collar. The boy's hands were white with cold and the crystal in them was the size of a thumbnail and lit like a coal.

"One," Anakin said, to no one.

Wix came out at the fourth, walking, with both mouths shut, and would say nothing at all except, through Huyang's translator on the comm, that it had been green and that he had seen his clan-mother. He sat down beside Yoda and put his long head in his hands and Yoda put one small hand on his back and left it there. Two.

Ilyo came out at the fifth with a cut on her palm where she had held the crystal too hard, and the crystal was blue, and she showed Anakin the cut before the crystal, because it was the cut she was proud of. He wrapped it with a strip of his own sleeve.
