She told him, while he wrapped it, that the ice had shown her a room with no door and that she had sat down in it until there was one, and she said it in the tone of a child explaining a sum, and he looked at her small green face and thought that she would have made a good soldier, and then thought what that meant, and finished the wrapping. Three.

Brannick came out at the sixth and he was crying, openly, the way Nautolan children cried, with the whole face, and he had lost his scarf, and his head-tails were stiff with frost, and he had a green crystal in his hand and would not let Anakin see it until Anakin had promised not to touch it. "It showed me the ocean," Brannick said. "It showed me home and I couldn't get in." He had been brought to the Temple at two. He had no home that he could remember. Anakin crouched on the ice in front of the boy so that he had to look up to him only a little and said, "But you came out," and Brannick nodded, with his whole face, and Anakin held the boy's frozen head-tails between his hands until they went soft. Four.

The bar of sun had crossed the floor and was climbing the far wall.

Kessa came out at the eighth hour. Anakin heard her before he saw her, small boots on ice, not running. She came into the light with a blue crystal in her hand and her face set in Master Sen's expression, the one that gave nothing away, and he saw how hard she was holding it there. She came straight to him. She did not show him the crystal. She said, "Is Ahsoka back?"

"Not yet."

The expression broke and the child was under it, ten, Mirialan, frightened, with her friend's name in her mouth. "She went left. At the first split. I went right. I called to her and she didn't—" She stopped. "You're not supposed to call. Master Yoda said. I called anyway."

"That's all right."

"Is it?" Kessa looked at the door. "She's always back first. In everything. She's always back first and then she waits for me."

"I know," Anakin said, and heard himself say it, and did not know how he knew. Five.

The sun went down the mountain.

It went the way it went at this latitude, not gently but all at once, the bar of light shortening across the far wall and going red and then gone, and the cold came up through the floor as if the mountain had drawn breath. Huyang's voice on the comm said the forge was ready and that five was a good number and that he had had years when it was three. The waterfall over the door, which had run all day in a thin sheet, began to thicken. Anakin watched it. It went from water to glass at the edges first, and the glass spread inward, and the sound of it changed from a crowd to a whisper.

He looked at Yoda.

Yoda had not opened his eyes. He sat on the ice with his stick on his knees, and four children sat around him and one stood, and the old face was as still as the mountain.

"Master," Anakin said.

"Her path," Yoda said, "her own it is."

He said it without opening his eyes. He said it the way he had said everything on the ship, without weight, as a thing that was simply so. The waterfall whispered. Kessa made a sound. Anakin looked at the thickening ice across the door and at the dark behind it and at the old creature on the floor who had sat on this ice for eight hundred years and watched children not come back, and he understood that it was true, that it was the Order's truth and a kind one, that a crystal that called did not call twice and that a child the caves kept had been kept for a reason the Order would not question. He had believed it at ten. He had been taught it at ten.

He had also stood on a gantry over a reactor shaft, with the lightning still in his bones, and watched his son's face, and learned a different thing about what one did when a door was closing on someone.

The ice had reached the middle of the door. There was a gap the width of a man's shoulders and it was closing from both sides.

Anakin went up the steps. Kessa said, "Padawan—" and Tam said, "He's not allowed—" and Huyang on the comm said, "Skywalker," in the voice he had used for a thousand years on boys who were about to do something, and none of it was Yoda's voice, and Anakin waited for Yoda's voice at the top of the steps for one breath, with his hand on the ice. It was so cold it burned.

"Hmm," Yoda said behind him.

That was all. Anakin turned sideways and went through the gap into the dark, and the ice met behind him with a sound like a word.

* * *

The dark was not dark. He had forgotten that. The ice took what light there was and carried it, so that the walls had a glow in them like a thing seen through water, and the glow was enough to walk by if one did not mind what one walked into. He went forward with one hand on the wall. The cold was total. It went in through the tunic and the skin and sat in the long bones of a fifteen-year-old who had not yet grown into them, and the body wanted to stop and curl and sleep, and he told it no, and it went on.

He reached for her in the Force, and the caves took the reach and gave it back as his own echo.

He had expected that. He stood at the first split, where Kessa had gone right, and went left, and the ice closed in and opened out, and at the next turn he put his hand on the wall and the wall was warm.

He took the hand away. He knew. He went on anyway, because the only way through the caves was through, and at the next turn the walls were black glass and the glow in them was red, and the floor shook under him with the slow heave of something enormous and molten going by below, and the air stank of sulphur and burned cloth, and he was on the bank.

He stopped. He made himself stop and look. The river went by on his left, fat and slow and white at its heart, and the black sand under his boots was hot through the soles, and up the slope above him stood a man with a blue blade in a low guard and his face wet, and the man's mouth was open and the thing he was saying was not a sound yet.

Anakin looked at him for a long moment. Then he said, aloud, in a voice that cracked on the cold because the body was fifteen and the cold was real even if the fire was not, "I know. I was there," and walked up the slope, through the place where the man stood, and the heat went off him like a cloak dropped, and the walls were ice.

He went on. He was shaking. He let himself shake; it was a body's right.

She was at the next bend, and she was twenty.

Not the senator in the hangar with her hands at her throat. He had braced for that, and the caves, which were older than he was and had done this before, gave him the other one: Padmé at the lake, the first week, in the yellow dress, turning from the balustrade with the light on the water behind her and her mouth beginning to smile, before any of it. Before Geonosis. Before he had told her about the camp. Before he had been anything but a boy she had decided, against her own judgement, to love.

He stood and let it hurt. That was the trick of it; he had learned it on the bank. One did not refuse a thing by looking away. He looked at her face until he had the whole of it, the way it had been, and he said, not aloud this time, *you were right about me too, and I never got to tell you*, and she went on smiling, because she was ice and did not know, and he went through her and she was gone and the cold was in his teeth.

The bridge was last.

He came out of a passage into a cavern he could not see the top of, and across it ran a span of ice the width of a man's two feet, with no rail, over a drop that went down into blue and then into black, and the caves had made it, out of his memory, with a craftsman's care: the gantry over the reactor shaft on the second Death Star, the catwalk where he had stood with his master's lightning coming down on his son and made the last choice of his life. He knew the gantry's length. He knew the exact place on it, two-thirds across, where his legs had gone. The caves had put a crack in the ice there.

He laughed. It came out of him before he could stop it, a boy's laugh, high and strange in that place, and it went up into the dark and came back. "No," he said to the cavern. "That one I'd do again."

He walked across. He did not look down. At two-thirds the ice groaned under him and he felt the crack go and did not change his pace, and it held, because it had never been about whether it held, and he stepped off the far end onto solid ice, and the cavern behind him was a passage like any other, and the caves, he felt, had finished with him. There was a kind of silence in the ice that had not been there before. He had been examined, and the examiner had put down the pen.

He stood in it and breathed, and let the wall he kept round himself go thin, thinner than he had let it go since the night he woke, and reached.

She was close. She was very close, and she was not alone, and the thing that was with her was not of the caves.

* * *

He found her by a wall of ice.

The passage opened into a chamber no bigger than the Padawan quarters, and the far side of it was a single sheet of ice, blue-white, flat as a mirror and taller than the door at the temple, and in front of it stood a child.

She had her back to him. She was ten years old. The tunic hung on her the way it hung on all of them, a size made for the next year, and the short montrals and the lekku barely past her shoulders were white with frost, and the small body in front of the ice was shaking so hard he could see it from the doorway, a fine fast shaking like a wire in wind. Her right hand was at her side, in a fist. The fist was lit. Green light came out between her fingers, steady and clear, and lay on the ice in front of her, and in the ice there was a thing.

He saw it as she saw it. He did not mean to. He had let the wall go thin and she was ten paces off and what she was looking at came through the air between them like heat off a stove.

The ice was not showing her the caves. It was showing her a floor of black stone, and a dark in which a red light moved, and in the red light a shape: tall, black, cowled or helmed, he could not say which at this distance, with a red blade held low in one hand, and the blade's light climbing the black shape and finding no face in it. The shape was not moving. It was standing, as he was standing, and looking at the child, as he was, and the child was looking back at it and could not stop.

He knew what it was. He had known from the doorway, before he had the shape clear, the way one knows one's own hand in the dark. He had stood like that. He had held the blade like that, low, in the one hand, because the other had been for the choking. He had stood on a black floor and looked at a small figure in white and told her she would die.

The girl in front of him was ten and had never left the Temple and could not have seen that thing in any life she had led.

He did not think. He had done his thinking on the bridge. He went across the chamber in four strides and put a hand on each of her frozen shoulders, from behind, and reached into the child's mind to take her out of it, the way he had taken Luke's hand, the way one took a drowning thing by the hair—

—and met a wall.

Not the ice. A wall in her: smooth, cold, made. He went up it and it did not give. It was not a child's shield, the soft panicked clench the younglings made when Drallig touched their minds in class. It was built. It had layers. The outer was noise, engine-noise and hangar-noise and the chatter of a hundred people, laid over everything like a tarpaulin; under it a surface, plain and plausible, a girl's fears and a girl's wants, set out to be found; and under that the true wall, blank and hard and old, with the seams of it worked over and over until they did not show. He knew the seams. He knew the noise. He had chosen hangar-noise because a Padawan who had grown up in a hangar would find it easy to hold. He had built this, in the war, on a troop transport between Christophsis and the next place, in the dark, with a fourteen-year-old sitting cross-legged on a crate in front of him and her face screwed up, and he had taught it to her layer for layer, first the noise, then the surface, then the wall, over three nights, until she could hold it asleep.

He had taught it to one person in his life.

His hands were still on her shoulders. The shaking had stopped. The green light in her fist had not changed. In the ice, the black shape stood with its red blade, and the child had stopped looking at it, because the child had felt what had just touched her and what had just stopped, and he felt her feel it, through the wall he had made, which she was holding, now, with everything she had.

"Who taught you that?" he said.

She turned.

"You did."


## Chapter 14: The Long Night in the Ice

Ahsoka Tano had been ten years old for four months, and in that time she had learned nothing new about being ten; she had learned it all already, twice over. What she had not learned, in seven years, was how to stand in a cave with her back to a wall of ice and look up at a boy who had once been her master and once been the thing that killed him, and know which one she was looking at.

He had taken his hands off her shoulders. That was the first thing. He had taken them off as if the cloth had burned him and stepped back, and now there were three paces of blue-lit floor between them and she could see all of him: the tunic short in the wrist, the thin braid behind the ear, the mouth open a little on a breath that showed white, the eyes. Fifteen. He was taller than her by a head and a half and he had a lightsaber hilt in his right hand, not lit, the thumb resting on the switch the way a boy's thumb would rest if he had been taught to keep it there, and the way a man's would if he had used it every day of his life.

She had a crystal. It was in her fist, and the fist was in front of her at the height of her own chest, which was the height of his belt, and the green of it came through her fingers so that the small bones showed like a lamp held behind a leaf. It was the only thing in the chamber she owned.

"Say something," he said.

"I did."

Her voice came out of her the way it always did, high and light, a girl's instrument. She had hated it for seven years. She hated it now, because she wanted to sound like what she was, and what she was had died in a temple a great deal darker than this one, and the sound that came out of her was a child's.

He did not move. Behind him, in the ice where she had seen it, the black shape had gone; the wall was only a wall again, lit faintly from within as all the walls here were, and her own reflection stood in it, small, with the lekku barely to her shoulders and the hem of her tunic touching the tops of her boots. Her breath went up in front of her. The cold was in her feet already. On Ilum the cold came in through the soles first and then up the shins and then, if you let it, into the places where you kept things.

She held the wall. She held it the way he had taught her, on a transport in the dark, with the noise over the top and the plain girl's fears laid out beneath the noise for anyone to find, and the true thing under that. He had gone up it. She had felt him go up it and stop. He had recognised the seams; she had felt that too, a jolt down the whole length of him, like a man who has put his hand on his own handwriting in a stranger's house.

Which proved nothing. The thing in the mask had known the seams as well. The thing in the mask had known everything.

* * *

She had felt him come three weeks and two days ago, above and to the west, like a struck bell, and she had lain in the Hawkbat dormitory with Kessa breathing in the next bunk and her two hands pressed flat on her stomach to keep herself from sitting up, because a child who sat up in the dark at the fourth hour and said *he's here* would be in the Halls of Healing by first bell with a note in her file. She already had one. She had been five, and the dream had been of a hall and fire and white ranks and a boy's face, and she had asked a healer whether a dream could be a memory, and understood from the healer's face that she must never ask anything like that again.

So she had let the bell ring through her and not sat up.

Seven years. She had come awake on Shili at three years old in a body that could not run without falling, with her mother's voice in the next room saying a name she had not heard in thirty years, and she had understood, in about the time it took to fall down, what had happened and that there was no one in the galaxy to tell. Days later a Kel Dor Jedi had crouched in the grass in front of her and held out a hand, and she had taken it with a hand so small it closed round two of his fingers, and she had thought, *Master Plo*, and said nothing, and let him carry her.

The Temple had been worse. On Shili she had at least been able to be strange. In the Temple strangeness was recorded. She had learned to lose a bout to a boy she could have disarmed with her eyes shut, and to look as if losing it hurt; she had learned when children were expected to cry, and to do it then, and not when she wanted to; she had learned that when the other younglings wept at night she must not go to them. She had learned to be intense rather than strange. Intense was a word they had for children. It went in a file and nothing came of it.

She had a list. She had made it at seven, on grey flimsi stolen from the Archives, in Togruti, which no one in Hawkbat read, and she kept it under her mattress. Eight lines. Names, mostly, and one date, and one word that was not a name but a point on a compass. No one had seen it. On Shili a green bird had sat on the roof of her mother's house for four days and watched her learn to walk again, and she had told no one about that either.

And she had waited. For a boy with a braid to turn up in the initiates' saber class each tenday and correct her grip with a bored face, and for her to look at his hands and feel nothing, because there was nothing there yet to feel. For seven years there had been nothing. She had watched him at the back of a hundred rooms and he had been a boy and that was all, and she had been relieved, every time, and sick with it.

Three weeks ago the bell. And then he had walked into Drallig's hall on the ninth day and taken a stance she had not seen in twenty years, and she had held her training saber backwards in front of him on purpose, to see what his face did, and his face had done nothing at all, which was the most frightening thing it could have done.

She had been trying ever since to get close enough to be sure. She had not thought about what being sure would mean. She had not let herself. Being sure meant one of two things, and one of them was good and she did not believe in it, and the other was standing three paces away from her now with its thumb on a switch.

* * *

"I'm going to sit down," he said. "Over there. Against the wall. You can keep the width of the chamber if you want it."

"Put the saber on the floor."

He looked at the hilt as if he had forgotten it. Then he crouched, slowly, the way you crouched in front of an animal, and set the hilt on the ice and stood up, and the hilt lay there between them and did not roll. She had not expected that. She did not let her face show that she had not expected it.

"I can call it," he said. "You know that."

"I know that."

"So it doesn't help you."

"It helps me," she said, "to see if you'd do it."

He went to the far wall and sat down with his back to it and his knees up, and the tunic rode up his shins, and he pulled it down with a boy's unthinking tug and then stopped, mid-tug, and looked at his own hand doing it. She filed that.

Her feet had gone past aching into something else. She put the crystal in her left hand and the right hand under her arm, and after a while she sat too, because a soldier did not stand for nine hours to make a point. She sat cross-legged on the ice with the width of the chamber between them, and the blue light came up through the floor, and their breath went up, and nobody said anything for the length of time it took her to count to four hundred.

"Where did you come from?" he said.

"You first."

"No." He said it gently. "You've got a crystal and I've got nothing. You first. Then you'll know what I say doesn't come from what you said."

That was his voice. Not the pitch; the shape of the sentence. The way he would lay a thing down in front of you so that you had to pick it up. She had been fourteen and he had done that to her every day for three years and she had not once seen it coming.

She told him.

She told it the way she would have told it to a debrief: flat, in order, nothing in the voice. The temple, which was a different kind of temple from this one. The stairs that went down. Kanan, who was Caleb Dume, seven years old, Bear Clan, a boy who followed her about with a mouth full of questions; she had watched him have his sight taken, red through the eyes, by a man both of them had thought was dead. The thing at the top of the stairs. She told him what it had looked like, from below, with the ice of this chamber making the shape of it for her an hour ago as if it had been in the wall all along, waiting.

"It said, 'Anakin Skywalker was weak. I destroyed him.' I said I would avenge his death. It said revenge was not the Jedi way. I said I was no Jedi." She looked at him across the floor. "I cut the mask. I got in under the side of it and cut up and the mask broke, and there was an eye. It said my name. In your voice. Not the mask's. Yours." She heard her own small voice go thin and did not stop. "I said, 'I won't leave you. Not this time.' And it said, 'Then you will die.'"

He had not moved. His hands were on his knees. The knuckles had gone white, which she would not have known from a mask.

"And then the temple came down," she said, "and that's the last thing. That's where I'm from. A floor, and a red light coming through the floor, and the thing I'd said it to coming up the steps. I don't know what happened after. I don't think anything did." She stopped. "So you'll understand," she said, "if I ask what's wearing your face."

* * *

He told her Endor.

She had never heard the word. It was a moon, he said, green, forested, with a Death Star over it, and that was a word she did know and did not want to; a second one, he said. He told it as flatly as she had told hers, as if they had agreed on the register. An Emperor in a throne room. A boy, twenty-three, blond, with a green blade. Luke. His son. He said the name as if it had cost him something to keep it in his mouth so long. The boy had refused to kill him. The Emperor had put lightning into the boy and the boy had screamed, and he had stood and watched, and then he had not. He had picked the old man up and thrown him down a shaft. He had died on the floor of a hangar with his son's hands on the mask, with the mask off, with air on his face for the first time in twenty-three years, and the last thing he had seen was the boy's face, and the boy's face had been like Padmé's.

"And then I woke up in a bunk in the Temple," he said, "with two hands. A month ago, near enough. That's where I'm from."

She listened to it to the end. Then she said, "No."

"All right."

"That isn't a thing that happened. That's a thing you want to have happened." She heard the child in her voice and pressed on through it. "You know what I am. You know what I'd need to hear. You just said it to me in order, with the right names in it. It knew my name too. It said it in your voice."

"I know."

"Vader says what it needs to say."

"Yes," he said. "He did."

He did not insist. That was the thing she had not been ready for; she had been ready for him to insist. He sat with his back against the ice and his breath going up and let her not believe him, and after a while he said, "You've got the whole night. Ask me things."

* * *

"Mortis."

"The Father, the Son, the Daughter. A place that wasn't a place. You died." He said it without emphasis. "The Son touched you and you turned, and then you died. Obi-Wan and I carried you to the Daughter's altar. The Daughter was dying. She gave what was left of her into you through my hands, and you woke up and looked at me and said, 'Hey, Skyguy,' as if it had been an ordinary morning." He looked at the floor. "I've never told anyone that. There was no one to tell."

Her lips were cold. She made them move. "What did I say after?"

"You asked why I was crying. I said I wasn't."

She had asked him that. She remembered the altar and his face above her and the salt on it, and the lie.

"Rex."

"Captain. CT-7567. Blond, the hair cut short on the sides. Carried two pistols and a dead man's jaig eyes on his helmet. Said *sir* to me in a way that meant *you idiot* about half the time." A thin breath that might have been a laugh in someone warmer. "He'd have followed you into a sun. He did, more or less."

"What did he call me?"

"Commander. Kid, when he forgot."

"And you? What did you call me?"

"Snips." Only that. It went across the floor between them and lay down next to his saber.

She wanted to pick it up. She sat on her hands, which was a child's gesture, and she let it be one.

"Fives," she said.

He looked up at that. She had known he would; it was why she had kept it.

"You were gone," he said.

"I know I was gone. Tell me."

So he told her. A clone who had taken his own chip out of his head on Kamino, and gone to the Chancellor with what he had found, and been made to look mad there; who had been hunted across the undercity, and got a message to Rex, and Rex to him; who had been waiting in a warehouse on Level 1325 with Fox's men at the door, raving, in a way she would have recognised and he had not. Fives had said *the chips* and *the Chancellor* and *the nightmares*, and General Skywalker had told him to calm down, had believed none of it, and Fox had come through the door and shot him, and Fives had died with his head in Rex's lap and the last thing he said was that the nightmares were over. "That's how he died," he said. "I was two paces away. I thought he'd lost his mind. I told the Council he'd lost his mind. I told the Chancellor." He looked at his hands on his knees. "I told the Chancellor."

She had known that Fives was dead. Rex had told her, years after, in as few words as he could find, and she had not asked for more because his face had closed. She had not known the floor, or the lap, or the last words. It was the first thing he had given her that she had not already held. Everything else she had been able to lay against what she knew and check, and matching proved only that whatever sat across from her had Anakin's memories, which the thing on Malachor had also had. Fives she could not check. She sat on the ice with her hands under her and found that she had taken it, without deciding, while he was talking, the way you take a hand in the dark.

She did not tell him that.

"The chips," she said instead.

"Go on."

So she told him hers, because he had told her his. The Siege of Mandalore; Maul in a chair in the hold of a Venator, talking; the ship turning for Coruscant. Rex coming into the brig with his helmet on and his hands shaking, and reading her the order as if it were a thing in another man's mouth, and the pistol coming up, and *find him, find Fives*, forced out of him sideways through whatever the chip had left of him. The medical bay. Three droids and a scanner and her own two hands holding him down on the table while they cut the thing out of his head, and the whole ship trying to get in through the door. "It was a chip," she said. "In all of them. They didn't choose. Rex didn't choose. I've known for sixteen years that they didn't choose. I thought I was the only one who—"

"I know," he said.

She stopped.

"I knew," he said. "Not then. I found it out after. The whole of it: the order, the Kaminoans, where the chips were, who'd paid for them. I knew what had been in their heads when they turned round on the Temple steps and on every world. I knew it for twenty years." He did not look away from her while he said it. "I had the 501st under me on Mustafar. The same men. I knew they hadn't chosen and I used them anyway. Then I had the clones retired and the Empire took on men who'd volunteer. I knew about the chips the way I knew about everything. There's nothing you've carried for sixteen years that I didn't know, Ahsoka."

The cold had got into the places where she kept things.

She had thought it was hers. She had thought the chip was the one fact that she had and he had not, carried out of the war for the men who could not carry it themselves. And it had been in the mask the whole time. The thing at the top of the stairs had known the men who turned on her had had no choice, and had never said so, and had let the galaxy believe for twenty years that the clones had chosen it. Her list under the mattress had Rex's name on it. He would have been on no list of Vader's. He would have been retired.

"You're very quiet," he said.

"I'm thinking about what you knew."

"Yes," he said. "I thought you might."

* * *

The night went on. She got up once and walked the perimeter of the chamber to get the blood back into her feet, and he watched her and did not get up, and when she sat down again she sat, without choosing to, a little nearer. The saber lay where he had put it.

"Luke," she said. "You said Luke. Your son."

He did not answer at once.

"Padmé," she said. Her mouth did not want to make the name. "I knew. About you and her. Everyone with eyes knew. Rex knew." A breath. "She died. She died, I heard it on a transport two months after, from a man who'd read it on a screen. The war ended and the Senator from Naboo died, and no one said what of." The crystal was in her hand again. She did not remember moving it. "I didn't know she'd been carrying."

"Twins," he said.

Something happened in her chest.

"A boy and a girl. She didn't die on Coruscant. She died somewhere else. I found out where, years later. I went and stood in the room. I was told she died and the child with her, and I believed it for nineteen years, because he told me. Then a boy from Tatooine put a torpedo into the Death Star." He said the word as if it belonged to someone else. "Skywalker. Luke Skywalker. Obi-Wan had put him with Owen Lars on the farm. Obi-Wan sat in the desert for nineteen years and watched him from a ridge." A pause in which she could hear the cold. "And Leia. Bail Organa took the girl. Princess of Alderaan. I had her in a cell on the Death Star and I didn't know. I stood over her and I didn't know. She's got Padmé's face, Ahsoka. She stood in front of me in a cell and lied to me with Padmé's face, and I didn't—"

She did not hear the rest. She had put her hand over her mouth.

Padmé's children had lived.

She had carried the Senator for sixteen years as a closed door, a woman who had been kind to her at fourteen and had died with the Republic, and she had never once let herself think the word *child*, because if there had been one it would have died too, with everything else, in the first year. Dead was dead. And the whole time there had been a boy on a moisture farm learning to fly, and a girl in a palace learning to lie, and Obi-Wan on a ridge, and she had been Fulcrum, she had passed within a parsec of Alderaan a dozen times, and nobody had told her, and she had never known to ask.

Her eyes were full. She felt it happen with a kind of distant surprise, as if it were happening to a child a long way off, and then it was not far off at all; it was her, on the ice, ten years old, with a crystal in her fist, and the thing she had kept behind the wall for seven years had come out through the front of her face and she could not get it back in. She had cried on schedule for seven years, at the pitch and length a child of that age should, and watched herself do it from a long way back and felt nothing. This was the other thing. She had not done this since before Malachor; she could not remember when. It came out of her in the voice she hated and she could not stop it and she did not try.

He did not touch her. He sat where he was with his hands on his knees and let her, and that was right; that was the only right thing he could have done, and she hated that he knew it. The chamber took the sound and gave it back off the ice, small and high.

Then he got up. She heard him cross the floor, past the saber, and felt him sit down next to her, close, the cold off the tunic and the warmth under it, and his arm came round her shoulders, a boy's arm, thin at the wrist, and she turned her face into the sand-coloured cloth at his ribs and that was the end of her for a while. He smelled of the ship and of cold. He was fifteen years old and all bone. She was ten and she fitted under his arm the way she had never fitted at fourteen, and somewhere in the part of her that was still working she thought, *this is not forgiveness, this is only the cold*, and she let herself have it anyway, because the night was long and she had been alone for seven years and there was no one else in the galaxy who knew the names.

* * *

When she could speak she said, into his ribs, "Her face."

"Padmé's. And Luke's got mine. The old one. Before."

"You said the mask came off."

"Yes."

"I'm still not sure I believe that."

"I know."

She sat up. She wiped her face with the heel of her hand, both sides, the way a child did, and he looked away while she did it, which was the first gentle thing he had done all night that she could not have predicted. She was shaking. It was the cold; some of it was the cold.

"Rules," she said. Her voice came back hoarse and small and she made it work. "We need rules."

"Go on."

"Nobody knows. Not the Council, not Master Plo, not Master Kenobi. No one. Not for anything."

"Agreed."

"If they ask what happened in here, the door froze and we waited and I was frightened. I'll be frightened. I'm good at it."

"I've seen."

"You stay a Padawan who's good with a saber. I stay a youngling who's intense." She looked at him. "That stroke. In Drallig's hall. Stop doing that."

"I'm doing penance for it every morning."

"Do more."

The corner of his mouth went. It was not a smile. "Anything else?"

"Obi-Wan." She said the name and watched his face and saw it close, not a wall, only a door pulled to. "Is he back?"

"I don't know."

"You've been living with him for three weeks."

"He's different." Carefully. "He's warmer. He says *my friend* to me. He never did that; not at this age. He let me write to my mother. He won't leave me alone with the Chancellor. He looks at me as if he's counting." He turned his hands over. "It could be a different Obi-Wan. It could be my memory. I had twenty-three years in a suit to get him wrong in."

"He isn't back," she said. "I'd know."

"How?"

She opened her mouth and nothing was in it. It was true; she would know; she had known it with her whole body before he asked and had not the faintest idea why. Master Kenobi had looked at her once, across a hall, with his eyes older than his face, and she had felt him flinch from her, the way she flinched from the Pau'an on the Guard, and she had put it down to a child's imagination.

"I don't know how," she said. "I'd know."

"There's an Archive log."

He told her. Two years ago, 28 BBY, terminal four, a search under Kenobi's own name for a planet that did not exist in the records: forty minutes on it, a null return, no reason given. Four days later, from the same terminal, Sifo-Dyas, eleven minutes, public record only. He had found it by slicing a dead librarian's account; Jocasta Nu had found him finding it.

"Kamino," she said.

"Yes."

"He searched for Kamino. Two years ago."

"Forty minutes."

The Jedi did not know Kamino existed. She knew that; she had read the whole wretched business after the war in a prison transcript, Dooku, the erasure, Sifo-Dyas's codes; the entry had been gone from the Archives since before this Obi-Wan was knighted. No Knight of thirty-one could have had that name. Nobody in this Temple could have had it. Not until a tall man with a lightsaber dart walked into Dex's and asked.

She went quiet. She felt herself go, from the inside, like a room being switched off, and she did not come back out of it, and he let her stay.

"I don't know what it means," he said eventually.

"Neither do I."

"We could ask him."

"No."

"No," he agreed. "We couldn't."

* * *

Towards the end of the night he said, "We'll need a bond. A thin one. If we're going to do this."

"A Padawan bonded to an initiate would show."

"So thin it doesn't. You know how."

"You taught me how." She heard herself say it, and the ice gave it back. "Not now. After. On the ship. Not here. It may have to wait until the Temple, though; Yoda will be aboard."

"After," he said.

They sat. He had moved back to the far wall when she had stopped shaking, without being asked; she had let him go and not said anything, and he had understood that too. The saber lay where it had been all night. She had not touched it and neither had he.

There was a thing she wanted to say and she turned it over for an hour and did not. It was the name. He had given her hers and she had not given him his, and she knew what it would do to his face, and she was not ready to be the person who did that; the thing at the top of the stairs still stood in the ice behind her eyes with its red blade, and she still did not know what had got into a bunk in the Temple a month ago with two hands. She knew that it had known Fives's last words. She knew that it had let her cry. She knew that it had not insisted.

She had been sure, for sixteen years, that Anakin Skywalker was gone for good. She was less sure than she had been at sunset. That was all. It was a long way from the thing she could see he wanted, and she would not pretend it was nearer.

She said it once. Not aloud, at first; and then, because the light in the walls had begun to change, and because she was ten and cold and her face was stiff with salt and she would be a child again in an hour and might not have the nerve, aloud, very quietly, across the floor.

"Skyguy."

He put his head back against the ice and shut his eyes, and did not answer, and she did not need him to.

* * *

The door went the way it had come: not all at once. First the blue in the walls thinned to grey, and then the grey had a line of white in it, low down, where the ice met the floor, and then the sound, which she had forgotten, a long creaking tick like a hull in atmosphere, and the whole face of the door began to sweat. Water ran down it in sheets. The ice went to glass and the glass went to nothing, and the sun of Ilum came in low over the snow and hit her in the eyes, and she put her hand up and the green of the crystal went through her fingers again, brighter now, with the real light behind it.

She had not thought to look at it all night. She looked at it now: a shard the length of her smallest finger, green, with a flaw in it like a thread. Green. It had been green the last time too. She did not know what she had expected. She closed her hand on it.

He picked up his saber on the way to the door and hung it on his belt without looking at it, and that was a thing she filed as well.

They came out through the arch together, the Padawan a step ahead and the youngling behind him with her hand in her sleeve, which was how it had to look, and the cold outside was a different cold, with wind in it. The snow went off white to the horizon. Across the open ground in front of the temple the *Crucible* sat on its struts with its ramp down, and between the ship and the arch, in a line, in their sand-coloured coats, stood five children, and a tall bronze droid, and in front of them, small, in a hood, with both hands on his stick, Master Yoda.

Kessa was crying. Ahsoka saw that first, because it was the thing she had learned to see first. Tam Dorreth had his arm round her, which he had never done before and would be unbearable about later. Brannick was looking at the Padawan, not at her, and Ilyo was looking at the ground.

She did what she had learned to do. She let her face go, let the lip tremble, let the night and the cold come up through the small body and show, and it was not even hard, because there was so much in her this morning that was true that a little of it got out without her choosing which. She ran the last part, to Kessa, who grabbed her, and over Kessa's shoulder she watched Yoda watch the boy.

He had stopped a few paces short of the Master, hands at his sides. He looked, with the night on him, exactly fifteen: grey under the eyes, hair flattened where he had leaned against the ice, the wrists out of the tunic, a boy who had sat up all night in a cave with a frightened child. He had made that face. She had watched him build it, in the last minute before the door opened, the way you watched a man put on a coat.

Yoda's ears were down. He looked at the boy for a long time, in that way he had, which she had been afraid of at fourteen and was afraid of now in a different way, because she knew what it had been looking for in all the years after.

"Found her, you did," Yoda said.

The boy inclined his head. The braid swung. "She was in the deep chamber, Master. She'd lost the light. We waited for the door."

A pause. Snow came off the roof of the temple behind them in a soft fall and nobody turned to look at it.

Yoda's eyes moved. They came off the boy and crossed the open ground to where Ahsoka stood in Kessa's arms with her face wet and her hand in her sleeve closed on a green stone, and they rested on her, and she gave him the child, all of the child, every lesson of seven years, and felt him look through it the way you look through a window at weather.

"Found you, she did," he said.

He did not say it to her. He did not say it to the boy either. He said it, she thought, to the air between them, where the thin thing they had agreed to make on the ship did not yet exist, and he turned, and put his stick down in the snow, and went towards the ramp.

"Come," he said. "Cold, it is. Crystals, you have. Build, you will."

They went, the children in a straggle behind him and the droid behind the children, and the Padawan last, and when Ahsoka looked back from the top of the ramp he was standing in the snow with his hands in his sleeves, looking up at the sky over the ice, at the place where a bird might have been if there had been a bird.

Her crystal was green. She held it so hard it hurt, all the way up.


# Part Three: Two Against the Dark



## Chapter 15: Snips

Huyang built lightsabers the way other people told stories, and Anakin Skywalker, who had heard this one before, sat at the back of the *Crucible*'s workroom with his hands between his knees and let it be told.

"The crystal does not want to be hurried," the droid said. He had a hilt casing in one hand and Tam Dorreth's wrist in the other, turning the wrist so the boy could see the emitter from the side. "Nor do I. Nor, young man, do you, though you believe otherwise. Set it down. Now look at it."

Tam looked at it. He was eleven and loud and had been loud for three days of hyperspace, and the crystal on the bench in front of him, blue, the size of his smallest fingernail, shut him up the way nothing else had. The workroom smelt of hot metal and lathe oil and of children: six of them in sand-coloured tunics at six benches, sleeves pushed up, bent over parts laid out on cloth. Ilyo's green fingers were quicker than anyone's. Wix hummed through both his mouths and did not know he was doing it. Brannick had put his emitter in backwards and was pretending he had not. Kessa Ruun had her tongue between her teeth.

And at the sixth bench, nearest the door, a Togruta girl of ten with her lekku barely past her shoulders and her montrals too short to be anything but a child's was assembling a lightsaber slowly, and wrong, on purpose.

He had been watching her do it for an hour and it was the best thing he had seen in twenty-six years. She let the focusing lens slip once and said *oh* in a girl's voice; she asked Huyang a question she knew the answer to and listened with her head on one side. Every mistake was true-sized, one a child of ten who had never held a hilt would make, and she made it with such plain craft that he wanted to stand up and applaud, and could not, because he was Kenobi's Padawan escorting the Gathering and she was Tano sixth of six and they had agreed on the ice that in front of other people they had never met.

"Hands," Huyang said, and she held them out, and the droid turned them and looked at the palms as he had looked at every child's palms for a thousand years. "Yes. Small. They'll grow. A shorter grip than the boys, and the emitter at a steeper angle; you hold things close to the body. I remember a Togruta who held things close to the body. Two hundred years ago. Terrible temper."

"I don't have a temper," Ahsoka said.

"Everybody has a temper," said Huyang. "It is where one keeps it that matters. Fit the lens."

She fitted the lens.

Tam got his blade lit first, because Tam got everything first, and the blue of it went up through the workroom like a shout and he shouted with it, and Huyang said "*Off*, thank you, *off*," and the others laughed, and for a moment it was not a ship full of the future, it was six children with a new toy on the way home. Ilyo's blue. Wix's green, which he looked at from both sides and then closed, carefully, as if it were a book. Brannick's green, after the emitter had been turned the right way round by a droid who said nothing about it. Kessa's blue, held in two hands, her face rinsed with the light, and the Mirialan girl turned to the bench beside her the way she turned to that bench for everything, to be seen having done it.

Ahsoka looked up and smiled at her friend, and the smile was a child's and was also, Anakin thought, the kindest lie in the room.

Then she looked down and thumbed the stud.

He had known what colour it would be. Knowing did not help. The blade came up green in a room full of blue and green and he was on Christophsis with the dust in his teeth, on Teth with the stairs going up for ever, on Ryloth, on Felucia, on the deck of the *Resolute* with the hangar doors open and that colour going away from him into the black at the head of a boarding party. A thousand battlefields. He had never once seen it lit for the first time. He had turned up, that first day, to a Padawan who already had it.

She held it still and low, the way Huyang had told her, and did not look across the room, and the green light sat on the white of her face and did not shake.

"Well," said Huyang. "That will do nicely. Off."

She turned it off. Anakin found he had his hand round the edge of the bench hard enough to hurt, and let go.

* * *

Coruscant came up under them on the eighth day, grey and gold and endless, and the Temple hangar swallowed the *Crucible* with a hiss and a smell of coolant. Knight Sabo Thane stood on the deck with a datapad, checking the children off as they came down the ramp with their sabers on their belts and their chins up. Yoda went down first and did not look back. Master Sen was there with her hands in her sleeves to gather Hawkbat and march it away to be fed and put to bed, and the children went to her in a straggle, Tam loudest, Ahsoka in the middle with Kessa's arm through hers, looking at nothing.

And Obi-Wan was there.

He stood at the foot of the ramp in brown with his hands clasped and the look on his face that Anakin had learned to think of as the counting look: a man adding something up and arriving at the same sum twice and not liking it. It went to Anakin first. It went up him, boots to braid, and found him tired and thin and fifteen, and the bond, which Anakin had opened to a hair's breadth on the approach because closed would have been a confession, carried back something that was not relief, exactly, and not surprise, and was warm.

Then it went to the Tano child.

It stayed on her a beat longer than a man's look stays on a youngling he has no business with. Then it came back, and for a moment Obi-Wan's eyes rested on the strip of hangar floor between the Padawan at the foot of the ramp and the girl going away in the clan, as if there were something on the floor there. There was nothing on the floor there. Anakin had made very sure.

"Welcome home," Obi-Wan said. "You look appalling."

"Thank you, Master."

"Did it go well?"

"Everyone came back with a crystal."

"That," said Obi-Wan, "is not what I asked," and took him by the shoulder, and turned him towards the lift, and did not say anything else about it for three days.

* * *

The practical problem was this.

A Padawan of fifteen might speak to an initiate of ten in a corridor. He might say *mind the step*. He might, assisting Battlemaster Drallig in the Hawkbat hall every tenth day, correct her grip, once, in front of nineteen other children and the Battlemaster, and not again. He could not sit with her, or walk with her, or eat at a table she was at. The Temple was a city of eyes trained from infancy to notice what did not fit, and the Council was already hunting a shadow in the building and did not need another one. Obi-Wan had looked at the floor between them. That had been enough.

So they did it in the Room of a Thousand Fountains.

He had forgotten the size of it. He had been in it once, afterwards, as the other man, and the memory had overwritten the place so thoroughly that he walked into it on the second morning home and stopped in the doorway like a tourist. It was a storey and a half of green under a vault of glass, terraces and galleries and dripping stone, ferns taller than a man, a smell of wet earth and cold water, and the sound: not one sound but a thousand, falls and runnels and the slap of the big pools, so that two people could stand a body's length apart and not hear each other. Jedi came here to be alone together. It was the only room in the Temple where staring was bad manners.

The third waterfall, counting from the east door, came off a lip of black stone two storeys up and dropped into a pool the width of a speeder, and behind the curtain of it, where the rock had been cut back by a landscaper eight hundred years dead to let the moss grow, there was a ledge. Everything there was wet. A child could reach it by walking the rim of the pool and ducking, and so could a narrow boy of fifteen if he did not mind his sleeves, and behind the water the room went away entirely and all you could hear was the fall, and the light came through green.

He put the case there on the third day: a sealed sample box from the mechanics stores, waterproof, the size of a hand, with a strip of grey flimsi folded inside. He wedged it under the moss at the back of the ledge, walked out through the water, sat on a bench with his hair dripping and meditated for an hour for anyone watching.

On the fifth day the case had been opened and his flimsi had gone and another was in it.

He read it on the ledge with the water a hand's width from his face. It was three lines of Togruti script, neat and small, with the curling ascenders he had seen on a thousand transport manifests and never learned, and he looked at it for a long time, and then he laughed, alone, behind a waterfall, for the first time in twenty-six years in a body that did not hurt when it laughed.

Because his own note had been in cipher. He had written it without thinking, in the hand he had written everything in for twenty-three years, keyed to the serial of a respirator he had not yet been fitted with, and she had opened it and found a dead man's shorthand, and had answered in kind.

He wrote, in Basic: *I can't read that.*

She wrote: *I couldn't read yours. What did it say?*

*Hello. Also, don't use the east walk after sixth bell; Jocasta Nu goes that way to the Archive stair and logs everything she sees.*

*That was a lot of cipher for hello.*

*What did yours say?*

*Hello. Also, your hair is too long at the back and Drallig noticed.*

He put his hand up to the back of his head. It was.

They could not go on in Basic. A Temple Guard could read Basic; the clan-mother, if she found a strip of flimsi in a ten-year-old's boot, certainly could. They needed a third thing, and it took them most of a tenday to agree it, and the argument was the first they had had since Mandalore, and he would not have traded it for a ship.

*Mando'a*, she wrote. *I have enough.*

*I know eleven words of Mando'a and nine of them are things Rex said when he dropped things.*

*Aurebesh, shifted, then. We pick a number.*

*The number of ships you crashed with me on board.*

*Four.*

*Nine.*

*Felucia was a landing.*

*Felucia was a crater with a ship in it. Nine.*

*We'll split it.*

*You cannot split a shift. Six and a half is not a cipher. Are you fifteen?*

*Yes, actually.*

A pause in the exchange; a day in the world. Then:

*Six. Tano, sixth of six.*

He did not need it explained. He had read the Gathering list on Obi-Wan's pad the night it came through: *Tano, sixth of six.* She had been last on every list in this Temple for seven years and she had put it in his hand as a key, and he sat on the wet ledge and thought that if she had been fourteen and in front of him he would have ruffled the montrals, and that he was going to have to learn not to want to.

The signature was a separate argument.

*S.*, she signed, under the six.

*S.*, he signed back, and then, because he could see it coming: *That's going to be a problem.*

*You pick another letter, then. I was born first.*

*You were born second this time.*

*I was born* seven years *earlier this time, Skyguy, I was in this building when you were still being chased round a hangar by a Toydarian, and if you call me that other thing in writing I will put it in the clan-mother's hand myself and tell her you sent it.*

He read that one three times. Then he folded it into the lining of his belt, where he kept the things he could not afford to have found, and wrote back, in Aurebesh shifted six: *Understood, Snips.* And did not sign it at all.

* * *

They had promised each other the bond on the *Crucible*, and had not made it there: Yoda was aboard for all eight days, and with Yoda aboard there was no privacy to be had.

The bond they made on the nineteenth night after the *Crucible* came home, behind the water, at the second hour, when the Temple was as empty as it ever got.

He went first and lay on the ledge in the dark with the fall a hand from his face. It occurred to him that this was the quietest he had been in twenty-six years, and then that it was not: the suit had been quieter, the breath and the breath and nothing else, and this was better. His back was wet through. He was fifteen and lying on a ledge in a garden at two in the morning waiting for a girl, and if Obi-Wan found him here there was no explanation that did not end in the Halls of Healing.

She came along the rim of the pool without a sound and ducked the curtain and sat down beside him with her knees up and her back to the stone.

"You're sure," she said at last. The voice was a child's; it had no other voice to be. The thing underneath it was not.

"No."

"Good. Nor am I." He could see the pale shapes of the markings on her face and nothing else. "Thin. We said thin."

"Thread."

"If it goes the way it went before, I'll cut it. In front of Yoda if I have to."

"Cut it. I won't stop you."

"You couldn't," she said, which was not true and they both knew it, and then: "All right."

He had not done this deliberately since he was twenty-three. The first time it had made itself, the way a path makes itself across grass; he had woken one morning on Christophsis and known where she was without looking and been annoyed by it. This was the other thing, the thing Masters did with new Padawans in a quiet room, with their hands on a child's temples. He did not put his hands anywhere. He sat with his wet back against the stone and found the wall he had built across the front of his mind for a quarter of a century, the scarred one, the one Vokara Che had put her hand on and called old, and found a place in it where it was thin, and made it thinner.

And let her find it.

It was a shock to both of them. He felt hers go through her like a dropped plate. For him it was this: that the last time he had felt this presence beside him in the dark she had been seventeen and he had been a man, tall, two-handed, with the war round them both like weather, and the thing that came up the thread to him now was that presence, exactly that presence, the quick dry heat of her, a woman of thirty-three who had buried him and run a rebellion and walked into a Sith temple to find him and been told to die. And it was wearing a child. The body it was in was small and sat close to the stone with its knees up. He could feel the size of her. He could feel her feeling the size of him.

And her shock was the mirror. She had expected the boy. What met her was forty-five years old. It was twenty-three years in a machine. It was a reactor shaft, and a hand on a mask, and a son's eyes, which she had never seen and which came to her now not as a picture but as a weight, the weight a thing has when it is the last thing. And under all of it, so far down she had to go looking, the boy.

She pulled back. He felt the thread go taut and then slack and then settle at the width they had agreed, thin as a hair, a thing you could carry through a day of classes and a clan-mother's inspection and a Council member's look and nobody would see it unless they already knew.

"Oh," she said, in the dark.

"Yes."

"That's heavy. You're very heavy."

"I know."

"I'll get used to it."

"You don't have to."

"I know I don't have to," she said, and he felt her, down the thread, decide to, which was a thing he had not felt anyone decide about him in a long time.

They sat with it. The water fell.

"It's strange being shorter than you," she said after a while.

"You were always shorter than me."

"Not by this much. I used to come up to here." She put a hand up in the dark; he could not see where. "Now I come up to your belt. It's humiliating. I'm going to have to look up at you for four years. I'd got used to looking down at Caleb."

"Caleb is seven."

"Caleb is tall for seven. He's going to be enormous. He's going to be—" She did not finish it. He did not make her.

"You'll grow," he said.

"I know I'll grow. I've done it. That's what's humiliating; I know exactly how far." She drew her knees in. "And you. You used to have a voice."

"I have a voice."

"You have two. You had two in the hangar. You said *everyone came back with a crystal* and the *crystal* came out an octave up and Thane looked at you."

"It does that."

"It's going to do that at the Chancellor."

"It's done it at the Chancellor. He finds it charming." He heard her make a sound that was nearly the laugh he remembered, the one from the gunships, and it went into him under the ribs and hurt, because it was the war, exactly the war, two people in the dark being rude to each other while the sky fell; and the sky had fallen, and was going to fall again, and the people under it this time were a boy and a girl in a garden. "Say *crystal*," she said.

"No."

"Say it."

"*Crystal*," he said, and it broke clean in the middle, and she laughed properly, with her hand over her mouth, a child's laugh in a child's hand, and he lay back on the wet stone and let her.

* * *

She asked him in the same voice, when the laugh had finished. She had never in her life needed a run-up.

"What did you do in the Temple."

Not *tell me*. A flat question, the kind you ask about a route or a casualty count. He looked at the underside of the water, where the night-lamps came through it bent and green, and he told her.

He told her the order of it. The gunships in the dark and the Processional Way and the stairs; the sound a Temple makes when it is being killed, which is not loud. He told her the men behind him had been the 501st and that she had known most of them by name and he had known all of them and had not thought once about the names. He told her where Drallig had met him: here. In this room, under the glass, with the water going, two Padawans at his back and the blade up in the old guard, the one he had taught them both. He told her what he had done to the Padawans first so that Drallig would have to watch it. He told her how long the Battlemaster had lasted, which was longer than anyone else in the building, and that at the end the old man had looked at him not with hate but with a kind of professional grief, the look of a teacher at a stroke he had never taught, and that Anakin had used the stroke, about forty paces from where they were sitting.

He told her about the Council chamber.

He did not make it shorter. There had been eight of them, under the chairs and behind them, in the circle of twelve seats at the top of the tower with the city on fire through the windows, Bear Clan and a few from the clan above, the oldest perhaps nine, and they had come out when they saw the robe and the braid, because he had been Master Skywalker, because he had taught some of them to hold a hilt. One had come forward and asked him what they should do. He did not say the boy's words; he had said them once, to the ice, and would not spend them twice. He told her he had looked down at the boy and the boy had been looking up, and that he had lit the blade.

"All of them," she said.

"All of them."

"How long."

"Not long."

"You went room to room afterwards. With the men."

"With the men. Then Mustafar. You know the rest."

"I know the rest."

Silence, and water. He could feel her down the thread and made himself not reach: a woman in a child's body holding very still, the way you hold still on something that may not take your weight.

"Say the excuse," she said. "Whatever it is. Say it now so I don't have to wait for it."

"There isn't one."

"There's always one. *He had me. She was dying. I thought I could—*"

"I thought I could save her," Anakin said. "That's true. It isn't a reason. I stood in that room and I knew what I was doing and I did it because by then I wanted to, and I'd want you to put the blade through me if I ever stand in it again, and that's all there is. Don't look for the rest. I looked for twenty-three years. There isn't any."

She breathed out. It took a long time.

"All right," she said. "Here's mine. I'm not going to tell you it wasn't you. Everyone's going to want to tell you that, when they find out; Obi-Wan's going to want to tell you that, if he—" The smallest catch. "People are going to want to make it a different man. It wasn't. You've just told me, and I believe you, and it was you, and I'm not going to forgive you for it. I don't think it's mine. The people who could are in that room and they're nine." The child's voice did not change. "I'm going to be angry with you for the rest of my life, which is a long time, because I've done it twice."

"I hear it."

"And I'm going to work with you anyway."

He turned his head.

"Because I did it alone," she said. "Afterwards. I walked out of here and did sixteen years of it alone, and most of it was carrying things, and I am ten years old and there is one person in this building who knows what's coming and it's you, and it is not possible for me to care, tonight, that you're also the one who did it. I'll care tomorrow. I'll care in Drallig's hall when you correct my grip. But I'm not doing it alone again, and I'm not letting you do it alone either, because the last time you were alone you went to the Chancellor's office, and I'm not having that. So." She held out her hand in the dark, small, the palm up. "Terms."

He looked at the hand.

*Christophsis, and the dust. The shield coming down over the city like a bowl being lowered. A shuttle on the plaza and a girl on the ramp, fourteen, orange and white, a saber at her hip too big for her and a chin that had never once been put down, telling Obi-Wan that Master Yoda had sent her, telling Anakin she was the new Padawan learner, telling him, when he said he did not take Padawans, that he had one now, and standing there with the dust on her as if she had already decided how it would go.*

He took the hand. It was a child's hand and it gripped like a vice.

"Terms," he said.

* * *

They drew it up on flimsi, behind the water, with a stylus and the green light. Two columns, in Aurebesh shifted six, because neither of them wanted either of their own hands on it.

*What she knows that he does not.*

Rex. Alive after the chip, which she had told him in the cave; but alive long after that, a decade after, old and white-bearded on a dead world in a rusted walker, found by a boy, fighting again. Anakin wrote it down and did not look at it.

The Inquisitors. He had, as the other man, commanded them and loathed them and used them; he had known the Pau'an by his title and the way he said *Lord Vader* with the second word a fraction late. He had not known what she wrote next: *Temple Guard. Here. Now.* That the Grand Inquisitor had been one of the masked men with the yellow pikes who stood at the Council tower doors, and was standing at one of those doors tonight, and that she had been flinching from a particular Pau'an under a particular mask for seven years and could not prove it. He put the stylus down for a moment.

Kanan Jarrus. A name on a report, in his other life; a Jedi on Lothal, a nuisance; he had never seen the man's face. She wrote *Caleb Dume* under it, and he thought of the boy of seven in Bear Clan who followed her about with his hair in his eyes. *Blinded*, she wrote, *by Maul; you have that from the ice*, and went on. Ezra, under that: *a boy, Lothal*, and no more, and something went down the thread that she caught before it reached him.

Maul. Mandalore, the last week of the war, the siege, the capture, the thing he had told her about Anakin in a dark cell that she had refused to hear. Anakin had not known. He had been on Coruscant, and then in the Chancellor's office, and then here, and then on the lava bank, and nobody had thought to tell Vader what his Padawan had been doing that week.

*What he knows that she does not.*

Luke and Leia. She had them from the cave; he wrote them anyway, because a list was a list. Obi-Wan on Tatooine, under another name, watching a boy from a ridge for nineteen years. The Death Star, which she had never heard of, and what it had done to Alderaan, and that Obi-Wan had died on it, under his blade, with his own put up, on purpose; she went very still at that one and he let her. The Emperor. The shaft. She had half-believed it on Ilum and half-believed it now; she made a small mark beside it in Togruti that he could not read and did not ask about.

And Dooku. That on the bridge of a ship over Coruscant, with the Chancellor tied in a chair, he had taken the old man's hands off and crossed two blades at his neck, and the Chancellor had said *do it*, and he had done it, and had known, kneeling there in the smell, that the Chancellor had sold his own apprentice for the price of a nod.

"He'll do it again," she said. "To someone."

"He'll try."

They looked at the two columns.

It took him a moment to see it, and he felt her see it in the same moment: the shape the two lists made between them when you held them up to the light. The things she knew that he did not were about a war. The things he knew that she did not were about the end of it. And the things neither of them could write down, because they would not fit in a column and because to write them was to say them, were all about one man.

A Master who had searched the Archives for a planet nobody in this Temple had heard of and sat forty minutes at a terminal looking at nothing. A Master who was warm, who put a hand on a shoulder, who said *my friend* to a boy of fifteen as if he had been practising it. A Master who had let a Padawan write to his mother for three years against every custom of the Order and argued it before the Council and won. A Master who had waited in Monument Plaza for six years, every tenth day, so that the Chancellor should never once have the boy for a whole afternoon, and had never once stopped trying to end the audiences. A Master who had stood at the foot of a ramp and looked at a Togruta youngling he had no business looking at, a beat too long, and then at the floor between her and his Padawan, as if there were something written there.

"He looks at me," she said. "He's looked at me since I was four. I told you. I'd know."

"You said."

"I don't know what I'd know."

"No."

Neither of them said it. It sat behind the water with them like a third person, and they did not look at it, with great care, the way you do not look at a sleeping child you have just carried in.

"We test him," Ahsoka said.

"Later."

"Later. Not here. Not with a question; he'll answer a question. Something he'd have to *do*."

"I'll think."

"You'll think." She folded the flimsi in four and held it out, and he put it with the other in the lining of his belt, and felt her, down the thread, go back to being ten: the shield coming up over her like a hood, the child settling into the small body as if into a chair.

"Bed," she said. "Sen does the dorm at the fourth hour. And you have Drallig at first bell, and the Chancellor at the end of the tenday, and your hair is still too long at the back."

She ducked under the water and was gone. He lay on the ledge a while longer in the dark with the fall a hand from his face and the thread, thin as a hair, running out through the stone and the gardens and the dormitory walls to a bunk where a girl was climbing in quietly beside a sleeping Mirialan, and he did not pull on it, and it did not break.


## Chapter 16: Of All the Children

Yoda took tea the way he took everything, slowly and with his eyes on the person rather than the cup, and Obi-Wan had learned long ago that the only defence was to drink his own. The chamber was the small one under the Council floor where the old Master kept the hours he did not keep anywhere else. One lamp. A window the width of a hand. The *Crucible* had docked the evening before, and the Temple's stone still carried the sound of it, the way stone did: a tremor that was not quite a tremor, a cargo of children come home.

"Sit," Yoda said, though he was already sitting. "Tired, you look."

"I have been told."

"You have been told. You have not listened." The old Master set his cup down. "Of Ilum I will speak, and then you will tell me what you think, and then I will go and sleep, which is more than you will do. Hmm?"

"Go on."

"Six went in. Five came out before the sun went down. Your Padawan stood beside me at the door and counted them, as I counted them, and when the fifth was out he looked at the sky, and I told him to wait." Yoda's ears moved, a fraction. "Custom, it is. You know this. The door closes. The child inside learns what the cold has to teach. Dawn opens it, and the child comes out alone, with a crystal or without one, and knows what it is."

"I know the custom."

"He did not wait."

Obi-Wan looked at the tea.

"Said nothing, your Padawan. Gave me no argument. Did not ask. Walked in through the closing ice as if it were a curtain, and the door shut behind him, and for the length of a night I sat with Huyang and five children with crystals in their hands and a boy who was not where I had put him." The small clawed hand turned over on the table, empty. "At dawn the door thawed, and out he came, and she with him, the two of them through it together, she a pace behind."

"Together."

"The Tano child. Sixth of six. Small she is. Walked out on her own feet, she did, a pace behind him, and when the ice was under them both she went to the Ruun girl and wept into her shoulder as a child weeps, and he stood apart and looked at the sky again." Yoda watched him. "Her crystal, green. His face, grey. Said I to the both of them, and to neither, *Found you, she did.*"

Obi-Wan's hand had closed round the cup. He opened it. The tea had gone cold some time ago and he had not noticed, which was not like him, and he noticed that.

"Found you, she did," Yoda said again, gently, as if setting it down a second time in case it had rolled. "Not *found her, you did*. That I said also. But the other, I said twice. Hear it, do you?"

"I hear it."

"Hmm." The old Master's eyes did not move from his face. "It is a breach of custom. A report to the Council, I will make, and the Council will say what Councils say, and a mark it will be on the boy, and nothing more, because I will tell them nothing more. Nothing more, there is. A boy went after a child because he was afraid for her. Good, that is, in a boy. Dangerous, in a Jedi." A pause. "Which is he, your Padawan?"

"He is fifteen."

"So. An answer, that is not."

"It is the one I have."

Yoda drank. The window had gone from black to a dirty blue; the first bell was an hour off. "Watched him, I did, on the ice," he said at last. "For the Order, and for myself. More than a boy went into that cave. More than a boy came out, carrying more than he took in. What the ice showed them, I do not know. Ask them, I will not; tell me, they would not." He set the cup down a final time. "Ask him, will you?"

"I will ask him how he is," Obi-Wan said, "and he will say *fine, Master*, and I will believe about a quarter of it, which is the usual rate."

"Hmm." It was almost a laugh. "Go. Sleep. Lie to me in the Council at the sixth hour; lie well."

He went. On the stair he found that his hand was not quite steady on the rail, and he stood a moment in the dark of the tower and let it be unsteady, since no one was there to see, and then he put the hand in his sleeve and went down.

*Found you, she did.*

He did not explain to himself why the words had landed where they had. He had a list for things he did not explain, and he did not write them on it.

* * *

He began to see them after that. It was not that they had been invisible before; it was that he had not been looking, or had been looking in the way one looks at weather. Now he looked, and they were everywhere, and they were never together.

The Room of a Thousand Fountains, the third morning after the *Crucible* came home: Hawkbat Clan on the grass by the east door with their clan-mother, learning to sit still, twenty small sand-coloured backs in a crescent and Imari Sen with her hands in her sleeves. Across the water, on the bench under the ferns where he himself had sat at fifteen and been miserable, his Padawan with a datapad. Forty paces between them, a stream, a stand of blue reeds. The boy did not look up. The girl, seventh from the left, short montrals, lekku barely to her shoulders, did not look round. For the half hour he stood on the upper walk and pretended to read, neither of them looked, and the not-looking had an architecture to it. A child looks. A child looks at everything. A boy of fifteen with a datapad looks up when a youngling drops a cup, and a cup was dropped, by the Rodian girl, and twenty heads turned, and his Padawan's did not.

The corridor outside the initiates' refectory, a tenday later: the Tano child walking with the Ruun girl, the two of them in step, the Mirialan talking; Anakin coming the other way with Bellas and three of the politics class. They passed. Nothing. The girl's eyes stayed on her friend's face. The boy's stayed on Bellas, who was saying something about Serenno. Obi-Wan, at the far end of the corridor with Vos-Kerrick, saw the Togruta's left hand drop to her side as they passed and open, and close, once, the way a hand does when it has been told not to reach for something.

The hangar bay. The third-level stair. The archive walk after sixth bell, twice. He did not go looking for them, and he did not need to. In a Temple of two thousand souls his eye found two with no effort at all, the way one's tongue finds a broken tooth, and each time the same thing: the boy, the girl, the distance between them kept with a precision that nothing on the training floor had ever taught either of them, and never one word.

*Of all the children in the Temple*, he thought, on the archive walk, watching a small figure in sand-coloured tunic go up the east stair without turning her head, and did not finish the thought. *That one.*

He had been afraid of her since she was four. He did not know why. He had told himself a number of things about it over six years, and some of them had been plausible, and it was long past time he stopped telling himself things.

The Chancellor's tenth day fell, as it always fell, and he waited in Monument Plaza in the thin autumn light for the two hours it took, and the boy came out of the Senate Office Building with a face that told him nothing and said the office had been warm and the Chancellor had asked after Ilum. "He knew about the caves," Anakin said. "Not what happened. That I went in." "Everyone knows that, my friend. It was in the Council minutes by the ninth hour." "Yes," the boy said, and looked at the Senate dome, and said nothing else all the way home.

The anniversary reception was a good way off still. The Council had not refused it. When the invitation came he would ask them, once, informally, to decline it, and he expected to lose.

Jocasta Nu had told him, before the *Crucible* left, that someone using a dead librarian's maintenance account had read the general log from terminal nine, and that the trace, when she ran it, had come back to Padawan Skywalker; and she had asked him, in her dry way, whether she should take it to Master Yoda or leave it with him, and he had said leave it with me, and she had. He had not raised it. The boy knew, then, about the forty minutes two years ago at terminal four and the name he had typed and the nothing that had come back; and he had not raised that either. They were, he thought, two people standing on either side of a door each had locked, both holding keys, both waiting for the other to knock.

* * *

Plo Koon found him in the gardens under the Council tower, where the Kel Dor Master went in the evening to stand among the trees he could not smell. They had been friends a long time in the way of two people who had both been Padawans to difficult men. Plo did not sit. He stood with his hands behind his back and his mask turned to the dusk, and talked, for a while, about nothing, which was his courtesy.

"Little 'Soka," he said eventually, "came back from Ilum a different shape."

"They all do."

"Hm. They do." The breath in the mask was slow and even. "She was the last out of the cave. You will have heard."

"I heard."

"The last out, and the first to speak, on the ship. The others were building their sabers with the droid and she sat with hers in her lap and asked him whether a crystal could choose wrong. Huyang said no. She said, *Then why does it feel like mine already?*" Plo's mask tilted. "He had no answer. I have thought about it for a tenday and I have no answer."

"Crystals are strange."

"They are. She is stranger." A pause, the length of a breath. "I brought her from Shili myself, Obi-Wan. She was three years old and she did not cry when I lifted her. Her mother cried. The child looked at me as if she were checking something. I have never forgotten it. I told myself: she is a strong child, she is a calm child, the Force is with her early. Seven years I have told myself." He turned, slightly. "She is older than her years. I do not mean she is clever. Clever children are easy.
