I mean that when she is alone in a room and thinks no one is watching, she sits like someone who has buried people."

Obi-Wan looked at the trees.

He could have said: *I know.* He could have said: *I have watched her since she was four and I do not know why, and she frightens me, and your Padawan's master is afraid of a child of ten, and I have never told anyone.* He could have said: *She holds the saber backwards and your Padawan's master knows where he has seen that and will not look at it.* He could have said a number of things, and all of them were true, and none of them were his to say, and some of them he had not let himself think in words.

"Children are older than their years in this building," he said. "We take them from their mothers at three. What did we expect?"

"That is not an answer."

"It is the one I have, Plo."

The Kel Dor was quiet. Then he put his hand on Obi-Wan's shoulder, as if it were Obi-Wan who had been talking about a child he loved, and left it there a moment, and went back to his trees.

* * *

Some weeks later, near the fiftieth day, he set the boy to the drill called the closing circle, which was the fourth sequence of the Third Form, and which Obi-Wan had taught Anakin at twelve.

It was not difficult. That was the point of it. Soresu was not difficult; Soresu was tedious, and the closing circle was the most tedious thing in it, eleven parries in a ring that tightened a hand's breadth with every turn until the blade was moving in a space no wider than a man's chest, and then the circle opened again and you went round the other way. A remote, a blindfold, a count. It taught nothing except patience, and it could not be done at all by anyone who did not have patience, which was why he had made the boy do it every morning for a year, and why the boy had hated it, and why, at thirteen, in a temper, Anakin had done it blindfolded against two remotes while Obi-Wan ate breakfast and had not been touched once. He had the sequence in his bones. He could do it asleep; he had, Obi-Wan was reasonably sure, once done it asleep.

"Closing circle," Obi-Wan said, in the small dojo on the second level with the door shut. "Blind. One remote. I'll count."

Anakin looked at him. The boy had grown again; the practice tunic was short at the wrist and the braid had been re-plaited badly, by himself. There was a pause where there should not have been a pause, and then he said "Yes, Master," and tied the cloth over his eyes, and Obi-Wan sent the remote up and began to count.

The first ring was fine. The second was fine. On the third the boy's blade was a finger's width too far outside on the fifth parry, and the remote stung him on the forearm, and he hissed, and brought it back. On the fourth ring he was late on the ninth. On the fifth he dropped his guard on the left as the circle tightened, which was the one place a Padawan who had done this a thousand mornings could not drop it, because the body remembered that the left was where the circle bit; and the remote found his ribs, and his shoulder, and his ribs again, and the training saber's hum wandered up and down as his wrist went loose, and at the sixth ring he stopped, and pulled the cloth off, and stood breathing with his face flushed to the hairline, and said, "Sorry. I lost it. I'll go again."

Obi-Wan said nothing.

He had watched the whole thing with his arms folded, and he had watched it the way one watches a man forge a signature, not for the shape of the letters but for the pen. The misses were wrong. Not the fact of them; the places. A boy who was tired, or careless, or sulking, missed at the wide end of the ring where there was room to miss. This boy had missed at the narrow end, where there was none, where the sequence was so deep in the hands that a boy with a fever did it right; and between the misses, on the seventh and eighth parries of the fifth ring, when the circle was at its tightest and the remote was firing twice a second, the blade had been exactly, beautifully where it should be, a fraction early, with a kind of lazy economy that Obi-Wan had never taught him and had seen somewhere, and had put away.

He had failed it on purpose. He had failed it badly on purpose, and the badness was the confession, because a boy who had something to hide would hide it well, and this one had hidden it like a child putting a broken cup behind his back while still holding the handle.

"Anakin."

"Master."

"Put the saber down."

The boy put it down. He stood with his hands at his sides and the blindfold crushed in one of them, and he was fifteen, and narrow, and the flush was going out of his face and leaving it pale.

"You are hiding something from me," Obi-Wan said. "I would rather you lied to me well than badly."

There was a silence in the dojo that had the quality of a held breath. Anakin's eyes went to the floor, to the saber, to the door, to his own boots. His mouth opened and shut. Something went down the bond, thin as it had been for fifty days, a flicker that was not words, that was close to *please*, and was gone before Obi-Wan could be sure of it. The boy did not say *I'm not*, which was what a boy would say. He did not say *what do you mean*. He stood there with the blindfold in his fist and his braid over his collar and said nothing at all, with his whole body, the way a child says nothing when he has decided that nothing is the only thing he can afford.

Obi-Wan waited long enough to be certain that was all he was going to get. Then he unfolded his arms.

"All right," he said.

"Master—"

"All right. Pick that up. Put it on the rack." He went to the door and opened it and stood with his hand on the frame. "Change your tunic. Wash your face. Meet me in the hangar in a quarter of an hour."

* * *

Knight Sabo Thane had a speeder bay on the north lip of the hangar and a face that gave nothing away, which was why Obi-Wan liked him. The speeder was an old open two-seater the Temple kept for errands in the Senate District, dented along one flank, fast enough. Thane handed him the chit and did not ask, and Obi-Wan signed it and did not say.

Anakin came down the hangar in a clean tunic with his hair still wet and stopped three paces off, and looked at the speeder, and looked at him.

"Where are we going?"

"We are not going anywhere. You are." He held out the chit. "The day is yours. You are excused from Drallig at the first bell tomorrow, I have told him, and he said something I will not repeat, so you owe me for that. Take the speeder. Bring it back with fuel in it and the paint no worse than it is."

The boy did not take the chit. "Why?"

"Because you have not had a day since the ice, and before that you had not had one since I cannot remember, and a Master who is any use at all knows when to stop looking." He kept his hand out. "I am not going to ask where you go. I am not going to reach for you. If anyone asks me where my Padawan is I will say I gave him the day and I did not enquire, and they will think me careless, and that will be true." A beat. "Go on."

Anakin took the chit. His hand, taking it, was a boy's hand, big-knuckled, the nails bitten, and it was not quite steady, and he closed it round the thing and looked at it as if it were in a language he had once known.

"Master," he said. "I—"

"Don't."

The boy shut his mouth.

"Don't," Obi-Wan said, more gently. "Not today. If there is a thing to say, it will keep, and if it will not keep then you had better be sure of it, and you are not. Go and drive somewhere too fast. Do not go to the Works; it is full of people who will sell a Padawan's saber for the price of a drink. Do not go to the Senate District either, or if you do, do not tell me." He stepped back. "I mean it, my friend. Go."

He watched the speeder lift off the lip and drop into the lane, the boy's head bare and the braid lifting in the wind, and bank south, and go into the traffic, and be gone. He did not follow. He stood in the hangar mouth with his hands in his sleeves and the wind off the city on his face and did not reach down the bond, though it was there, thin and slack as a line let out; he let it stay slack. It was the one thing he could give. It was not nothing. It was a hand taken off a shoulder, and he had spent four years learning to keep the hand there, and it cost him more than he had expected to take it away.

He knew the boy would go somewhere. He knew that where he went would tell him something, if he looked. He chose not to look. It was, he thought, walking back up through the Temple with the afternoon going gold on the stone, the first thing he had chosen in some weeks rather than merely done.

In their quarters he made tea and let it go cold again. He took the night-pad out of the drawer where he kept it and sat with it on his knee. Two columns. *Written. Not written.* The first was long. The second was longer. He looked at the second for a while and did not add to it, and then, at the bottom, in a hand that was smaller than his usual, he wrote: *He failed the circle at the narrow end.* He looked at that. He drew a line under it and wrote nothing else.

The boy came back after the ninth bell. Obi-Wan heard the speeder in the hangar, far below, and the lift, and the boy's step in the corridor, quieter than it had been a year ago, and the door, and did not get up. "I'm back," Anakin said through the wall. "Fuel's in it. Paint's fine." "Good." "Master?" "Go to bed, Anakin." A pause. "Goodnight," the boy said, and the inner door closed, and after a while the bond settled into the shape it took when he slept, and Obi-Wan sat with the cold tea and the pad and the lamp, and did not ask himself where the speeder had been, and found that he knew, and did not look at that either.

* * *

He slept, eventually, and the desert came.

It always came the same way. There was no beginning to it. He was simply there, on the ridge, with the sand under his knees and the two suns going down and the wind that never stopped, and below him the farm: the low domes, the pale lip of the courtyard pit, the vaporators standing out across the flat like a row of thin patient men. He had watched this farm for a long time. He did not know, in the dream, how long. He knew the shapes of the domes the way he knew his own hands. He knew where the woman hung the washing and where the man parked the speeder and which of the vaporators was always failing; he knew the hour the boy came out to it with the tool bag, and he waited for that hour, every time, with his hand pressed flat on the sand as if he could feel the boy's step through it.

The boy came out. Small, at this distance. Fair-headed. He went to the failing vaporator and knelt and opened the panel, and the wind took his hair, and Obi-Wan on the ridge did not breathe.

He never turned round. That was the dream. That was all the dream had ever been: the ridge, the farm, the boy's back, the wind, and a feeling in Obi-Wan's chest of such weight that he woke from it, always, as if he had been dropped.

This time the boy turned round.

He stood up from the vaporator with the tool in his hand and he turned, slowly, as if someone had called him, and looked up the slope at the ridge, at the man kneeling there, at Obi-Wan; and across all that distance and that light the boy's face was clear, and young, and known, and it was—

He was awake.

He was sitting up in the narrow bed with the sheet in both fists and the room black and the lamp off and the Temple silent round him, and there was a name in his mouth. He could feel the shape of it. His lips had made the first of it and his breath had stopped on the second, and he sat there in the dark and held it, the whole name, behind his teeth, the way one holds a mouthful of water on a march.

He did not say it aloud.

Through the wall the boy was asleep. The bond lay thin and slack and untroubled, a line let out into still water. Nothing had come down it. Nothing had woken the younglings on the lower levels. Whatever had turned round on that ridge had turned round for him alone, and he sat with it in the black and the quiet with a name he did not say aloud and had not said aloud in a very long time, and his hands, when he opened them at last and laid them flat on the sheet, were not steady, and he let them not be, since there was no one there to see.


## Chapter 17: The Reception, Part One

Weeks after the Soresu drill, with the reception set for the fourth month since he had woken, the invitation came through the Council, which was how Anakin knew it could not be refused.

It arrived nine days before the reception, in the form Council business always took: a summons to the antechamber below the High Council Chamber, where Master Windu kept them standing. The Chancellor's office had written to the Order. The sixth anniversary of the Battle of Naboo would be marked by a reception in the Senate, and the Chancellor, mindful of the debt the Republic owed, had asked particularly that the heroes of that day be present: Master Kenobi, who had killed the Sith; his Padawan, who had been nine and had flown; and the Senator for Naboo, who had been Queen. Windu read it out in his clipped way without looking at either of them, and when he had finished he said, "Master Kenobi asked me, once and informally, to refuse it on the Order's behalf. I have overruled him. The Order accepts."

Anakin had not looked at Obi-Wan. He had looked at the floor between his boots, which were a size too small again, and had felt down the thin line of the bond something held very flat and very still, like a hand pressed on a tablecloth.

He knew why Obi-Wan had tried. There was the plain reason, the one anyone in the Temple could have given: Kenobi did not let the Chancellor have his Padawan for a whole day, had never let him, had sat in Monument Plaza through six years of tenth-day audiences like a man waiting outside a surgery. An evening in the Senate with the Chancellor as host was a whole day by any reckoning. And there was the other reason, the one only Anakin could have given, which was that Count Dooku of Serenno was to be the Chancellor's guest, and Obi-Wan, this Obi-Wan, the one who had searched the Archives for a planet that was not there, might know exactly what Count Dooku was.

Or might not. That was the trouble with the second reason. It explained everything and could be proven by nothing, and Anakin had lived with it long enough now to distrust how well it fitted.

"You'll wear the formal robes," Windu said. "Both of you. The Count will be there." He said the name the way the Council said it, with the faint ceremonial distaste reserved for the Lost Twenty. "He has written to say he attends to honour Qui-Gon Jinn's memory. You will be civil."

"We are always civil," Obi-Wan said.

"You are always something," said Windu, and dismissed them.

* * *

The night before the reception he went to the Room of a Thousand Fountains after the ninth bell, when the lamps dimmed to the blue the gardeners used for the night-flowering beds and the paths belonged to whoever was willing to get wet.

Third fall from the east door. The ledge ran behind the water where the rock had been cut to carry a pipe, and the moss on it was thick enough to hide a hand. He crouched with the spray on the back of his neck. He still noticed water, every time, the cold of it on skin, as if the body were reporting the fact to someone who had been away a long while and ought to be told.

Her note was there, in the sealed sample box under the moss, folded to the size of a thumbnail. He read it standing in the dark behind the fall, tilted to the blue light, the shifted Aurebesh unlocking in his head letter by letter without his needing to count any more. Six along. She had small, hard handwriting, the letters pressed deep, as if the stylus were a chisel and the flimsi had offended her.

*Reception tomorrow. I know. The whole clan knows. Kessa thinks you'll meet the Queen and wants to know if she's pretty. I said yes.*

*Three things.*

*One. The Count will touch you. Not with his hand. He can't help it, it's what he does; he did it to me once on a landing platform with four hundred people watching and nobody but me felt it. Let him find a boy. You know how.*

*Two. The Chancellor will be watching the Count watch you. Don't give him a show. Nothing he can use twice.*

*Three. Don't look at her like she's already dead.*

*She'll see it. She noticed me the first day we met and I was trying. Look at her like a Padawan who met a queen once and has been thinking about speeders ever since. You can do that. You did it for years.*

*If the Count offers you a drink, it's poisoned. (Joke.) (Mostly.)*

*S.*

He stood with it for a while, the water loud at his back. The thread between them lay where it always lay, east and down, faint as a hair across the eye: she was awake, she was in the dorm, she was waiting to feel him find it. He let her feel it. Then he took the stub of flimsi from his sleeve and wrote against the rock, six along, in a hand that was still, after everything, a boy's.

*Three for three. If he offers me a drink I'll hand it to the Chancellor. Sleep. S.*

He folded it to a thumbnail and set it in the sealed sample box under the moss, closed the lid, and pressed the moss down, and the thread east and down gave a small tug, like a line with something on it, and went still.

* * *

The formal robes came up from the quartermaster in the morning in a sealed bag with his name on it and smelt of the cedar they kept the stores in. Cream under-tunic. Tabards in a brown so dark it was nearly black. The outer robe heavier than the one he wore every day, with a weight in the hem to make it hang. He had been fitted for them at the start of the year and in the months since he had grown out of the sleeves, so that when he put his arms down the cuffs stopped a finger's width short of his wrists and showed the bone there. He stood in the common room in his socks and looked at his wrists and did not know what to do with them.

He moved the flimsi terms-list in shifted Aurebesh, and her latest notes, from the belt he wore every day to the lining of the formal one. Then he put the belt on and the lightsaber on the belt, and the weight of it on his left hip was the only thing about the whole outfit that felt as if it belonged to him.

Obi-Wan came out of his room in his own formals already dressed, beard trimmed, and stood and looked at him.

"Stand still," he said.

"I am still."

"You are vibrating." He came over. He took the Padawan braid between finger and thumb and ran it to the end and found the end-tie had slipped, and re-tied it, which took him longer than it should have because his hands were not quick at it. Anakin stood with his chin up and looked at the wall over Obi-Wan's shoulder. Then Obi-Wan straightened the collar of the outer robe, one side and the other, the way he had done a thousand times for a nine-year-old and a twelve-year-old and now did for a fifteen-year-old who was already nearly his height. The hand stayed a moment at the collar. It was not holding anything. It was simply there, flat against the cloth over Anakin's collarbone, as if it had been set down and forgotten.

"You will stay where I can see you," Obi-Wan said.

"Yes, Master."

"I mean it, Anakin."

"I know you do."

The hand came away. Obi-Wan stepped back and looked at the sleeves and his mouth did the thing at the corner it did when he had decided not to say something.

"The Count," he said, "was a great Jedi once. Qui-Gon loved him. That is all you need to know about him tonight." He said it to the sleeves, lightly, and then looked up, and his eyes were perfectly mild. "If he offers you his hand, take it. If he offers you his opinion, nod. If he offers you anything else, I shall be standing next to you."

"And if he offers me a drink?"

"Then you have had a long evening," Obi-Wan said, "and we are going home."

* * *

The Senate Guards at the upper doors wore the blue, the full ceremonial blue with the high helms, and stood like fence-posts. Anakin walked between them behind Obi-Wan with his hands in his too-short sleeves and let the boy's register come up in his face: a Padawan at his first real reception, a little dazzled, a little bored, looking at everything. It was the easiest thing in the world. He had been that boy. He had stood in rooms like this at fifteen and seventeen and nineteen and thought about speeders.

The reception was in the Dome Gallery, which ran round the upper rim of the Rotunda like the inside of a crown. On the outer side the windows gave on to the city at dusk, traffic in its lanes, the towers of 500 Republica, and far off across the haze the five spires of the Temple with the last light on them. On the inner side the wall was transparisteel from floor to ceiling, and through it one looked down into the Senate chamber itself, empty, immense, the thousand pods docked in their tiers and dark, like boats at a quay at night. Between the two walls the gallery curved away out of sight in both directions, and it was full.

Naboo's colours hung from the ceiling in long banners, the pale greens and golds of the lake country. There was a Gungan delegation near the windows, a dozen of them in their stiff ceremonial collars, towering over the Senate staff who were trying to bring them drinks; the Boss himself was there, enormous, his jowls shaking as he laughed at something. There were Naboo in the dress of the Royal House, there to represent Queen Réillata, who had sent her regrets and a long message. There were senators. He recognised them the way one recognises faces from a bulletin: the dark-haired man from Alderaan with the listening face, Organa, younger than Anakin had ever seen him; a young woman in white from Chandrila, Mothma, who was twenty and looked it, and whom he last remembered older by thirty years on a wanted notice with a price beneath her name. He looked away from her. He looked at the pods below, dark, docked, waiting.

Mas Amedda stood at the centre of it all with his horns and his staff and his look of a man who had been Vice Chair for six years and had not yet forgiven anyone for it. A step behind him, where there was always a step behind him, the Umbaran woman stood with her hands folded and her white face turned a little to one side, as if listening to something in the next room. Sly Moore.

"Stay close," Obi-Wan said, under the noise.

"I'm close."

They were given drinks they did not drink. They were introduced to the Naboo party, who were gracious and wanted to talk about the Queen, by which they meant the former Queen, by which they meant the Senator, who had not yet arrived; she had been detained in committee, they said, with the air of people who had been told this would be the case and did not approve. A heavyset Twi'lek senator with a jewelled headpiece, Taa of Ryloth, drifted past with two aides and said to no one in particular, as if continuing an argument begun elsewhere, "Franchise disclosure. A tribunal for the Rim. She has been in this building three years and she thinks a ledger is a weapon. I have seen weapons." The aides laughed. Obi-Wan said, "Senator," with a courtesy so exact it could have been measured with instruments, and Taa went on round the curve of the gallery trailing his laughter behind him.

Anakin found that he had been holding his glass hard enough to whiten his knuckles and made his hand let go.

"Yes," Obi-Wan said quietly, not looking at him. "Quite."

* * *

The Chancellor came to them. That was how it was done: the Chancellor came to the heroes of Naboo, and the room turned to watch him do it.

He came with Amedda at his left and the Umbaran at his right, in the plain dark robes of office that he had worn for six years and would wear for seven more, and his face was doing the thing it did, the kind face, the pleased grandfather. He took Obi-Wan's hand in both of his and said something about the Order's generosity in sparing them, and Obi-Wan said something about the Order's honour, and the two of them held the shape of it for the length of time the room required. Then Palpatine turned.

"And here he is," he said. "My boy."

The hand came down on Anakin's shoulder. He had known it would and had set himself for it, and it was still a thing to be stood through: the weight of it, the warmth through the robe, the thumb finding the collarbone where Obi-Wan's hand had been that morning. And under it, as there always was, the other touch. Not a hand. A pressure in the mind like the pressure of a man leaning on a railing, testing it, the way one tests a railing one has leant on before. It found the surface Anakin kept for it. A boy's surface: the too-short sleeves, which were an embarrassment; the Gungans, which were funny; a Padawan called Mirren Dask, who had not looked at him in politics class for a tenday and whose not-looking took up a great deal of room. Under that, the wall. The pressure touched the wall as it touched it every tenth day now, without surprise, almost with a kind of proprietary satisfaction, the way a man touches a fence he has decided is his.

He had stopped pressing it weeks ago. Sidious had found a locked door in his garden and tried it once, and then simply left it, and planted round it, and came back every ten days to admire it. A man who does that is not waiting for the door to open. He is waiting to be told who has the key.

"You've grown again," Palpatine said, with the kind face. "They'll have to let out those sleeves."

"Yes, sir."

"Six years. I remember a boy who had to be lifted into the cockpit." A squeeze of the shoulder, and the hand came away, and the touch under it came away with it, unhurried. "I shall embarrass you in a moment in front of everyone, I'm afraid. It's a Chancellor's privilege. Bear it as you bear everything, my boy."

He went to the centre of the gallery and Amedda struck the floor with his staff and the room went quiet.

The speech was short, which was the mark of the man. He spoke of Naboo. He spoke of a small world's courage and a great Republic's debt, of a people who had not waited for help and of the help that had come anyway, late and insufficient and welcome, and the Naboo in the room stood a little straighter and the Gungans rumbled. He spoke of the Jedi, and this was the part Anakin had been waiting for. He said that the Republic had been served on that day, as it was served on every day, by men and women who asked nothing and were given, too often, exactly that. He said that one of them stood in this room tonight, who had faced a thing in the Theed generator complex that the Republic had believed a thousand years dead, and had killed it, and had never once in six years asked to be thanked. The room looked at Obi-Wan. Obi-Wan inclined his head a quarter of an inch, which was what he had. And beside him, Palpatine said, a boy of nine, from a world the Republic did not reach, had got into a starfighter he had never flown, and flown it, and ended a battle, and was now fifteen and could not keep his sleeves long enough. The room laughed, warmly. The room looked at Anakin. He flushed on cue, and it was not even a performance; the body did it for him, the blood came up his neck into his face the way it had at nine and at fifteen, and he looked at his boots.

"To Naboo," the Chancellor said, and raised his glass, and the gallery said it after him, two hundred voices, a sound like surf.

Anakin raised his glass with the rest. Over the rim of it, across the width of the gallery and the length of the toast, he saw the man in black.

* * *

He had seen him already, the moment they came through the doors, and had put him where he put Mothma, in the part of the mind where things are seen and not looked at. Now the toast was done and the room was moving again, and the man in black was moving with it, towards them, without hurry.

Count Dooku of Serenno was seventy-six years old. He was tall, taller than Obi-Wan, and he carried the height the way old soldiers do, from the spine, as if it were something he had been issued and was responsible for. Silver hair swept back from the forehead. A silver beard cut close and precise. The black was not mourning black and not Senate black; it was a Count's black, a long high-collared coat of a cloth that drank the light, and a cloak clasped at the throat with a plain silver chain, and nothing else on him anywhere that shone. He had a face that had been handsome and had decided, at some point, to be formidable instead. The hands were long. He was, Anakin thought, holding his glass again too hard, exactly as he remembered him, which was the thing he had not been ready for: not that Dooku was here, but that he was *the same*, that the man who had taken his arm on Geonosis and the man who walked towards him now across a gallery full of senators were one man, wearing one face, and this one did not know him at all.

*The blue blade coming across and his own arm leaving him at the elbow, the white flash of it before the pain, the smell.*

He breathed. The thread east and down stirred, as if someone far off had turned over in bed.

Dooku reached them and stopped at the distance etiquette required, and bowed, from the neck, to Obi-Wan.

"Master Kenobi," he said. His voice was deep and unhurried and had the kind of accent that had been expensive for a thousand years. "My student's student." And his eyes moved, without the head moving, to Anakin. "And his."

"Count Dooku." Obi-Wan's bow was a fraction deeper, as was proper from the younger to the elder, from a Knight to a man who had sat on the Council before the Knight was born. "Qui-Gon spoke of you often, and always with respect."

"He spoke of me often and always in disagreement," Dooku said, "which from Qui-Gon was the same thing. You were there at the end."

"I was."

"And it was quick."

"It was quick."

"He would have preferred it otherwise," Dooku said. "He never did anything quickly in his life. I am glad to have found you, Master Kenobi. I had wondered what sort of a man he had made, and I see he made one."

Anakin listened to it the way one listens to a lock being turned. Obi-Wan's courtesy was flawless. Every line of it was the right length. The bow was right and the deference was right and the small dry warmth was right, exactly the warmth of a man who had loved Qui-Gon speaking to a man who had taught him, and under it, down the thin line of the bond, there was nothing. Flat. Still. A hand on a tablecloth. Anakin, who knew what this man had done to Obi-Wan in another life, who had seen Dooku stand over him on Geonosis and put the red blade through his shoulder and his thigh and leave him on the floor, could not tell whether the courtesy was flawless because Obi-Wan was courteous, or because it had to be. He could not tell. Here was the test he and Ahsoka had agreed on and never designed, handed to him in a crowded room, and he could not read it.

"And this is the boy," Dooku said.

He had turned. He stood now a pace from Anakin, looking down, and his hand came up from his side and moved towards Anakin's shoulder, and did not arrive. It stopped a finger's width from the cloth of the formal robe, open, as if to rest there, and hung. A courtesy, perhaps: the old-world gesture of a man who did not touch a Jedi without leave. Perhaps. The hand did not touch him.

What was under the hand touched him.

It was nothing like the Chancellor's. The Chancellor's touch was warm and heavy and everywhere, a man leaning on a railing. This was cold and narrow and exact. It came in under the sleeve of the mind like the point of a jeweller's glass, looking for the stone, and it had a flavour to it that Anakin knew in his wrists before he knew it in his head: the flavour of Makashi, of a blade held low and a wrist turning, of economy and contempt. A Sith's assessment. It found the surface, the boy's surface, the sleeves and the Gungans and Mirren Dask, and it did not stop at the surface the way Palpatine's did. It went through it with a kind of weary precision, as if the surface were a lie it had been told to expect, and came down on the wall.

And Anakin, who had let the Chancellor lean on that wall a dozen times, who had stood through it in the red office every tenth day with a boy's face on, felt the wrong thing come up.

*The red blade crossing his own, two blades, scissored at the throat of a man on his knees on a steel deck. The silver head bowed. The old eyes looking up, not at Anakin, at the chair behind him, with something in them that Anakin had not understood for twenty years and had understood, in the end, perfectly: the look of a man who has just learned what he was for. And from the chair, mild, patient, a hand on the shoulder: Do it.*

The hatred came up through him like water through a floor. Not Vader's, not the long cold thing he had carried in the suit; this was older and hotter and had a boy in it, a nineteen-year-old on his back in a hangar on Geonosis with the stump of his arm smoking and this man walking away. The arm. The kneeling. *Do it.* It went up the wall and over the top of it before he could put a hand to it, one clear flare of it, a lamp uncovered, aimed at the man in front of him and at nothing and no one else in the galaxy.

He clamped it. He clamped it so hard his jaw ached with it an hour later. He brought the wall up over it the way one brings a hand down over a lit match, and the surface back over the wall, the boy's surface, the sleeves and the speeders and the girl who did not look at him, all of it, in less time than it took to breathe in.

It was a second too late.

He saw it land. He saw it arrive in Dooku's face, and he had never seen this in Dooku's face in two lives, not on Geonosis, not on the deck of the *Invisible Hand*: something behind the eyes went very still, the way a man goes still who has put his foot on a stair that is not there. It was not fear. Dooku did not have fear in him the way other men did. It was a kind of recalculation. The eyes, which had been assessing, were now, for the length of a held breath, simply looking, at a fifteen-year-old in too-short sleeves, as though the boy had spoken to him in a language he had not heard since childhood and could not place.

The hand that had not touched him came away. The touch beneath it withdrew, not quickly, with care, as a man draws his hand back from something he has found warmer than it should be.

"Qui-Gon's last message to me, before Naboo, concerned a boy from Tatooine," Dooku said, in the same deep unhurried voice, "and I have wondered since what it was that he saw."

Anakin bowed. It was a Padawan's bow, slightly too deep, slightly too fast, a boy who has been spoken to by a great man and does not know where to put his hands. "Count," he said.

That was all. Dooku looked at him a moment longer. Then he turned the look on Obi-Wan, and it was a different look, and it said nothing at all, and he inclined his head and went on along the gallery with the crowd opening before him, and did not look back.

Anakin found that Obi-Wan's hand was on his elbow. He did not know when it had got there. It was holding him, not steadying him, the way one holds a thing that might go.

"Breathe," Obi-Wan said, barely moving his mouth.

He breathed.

And he looked, because he could not help looking, because Ahsoka's second rule was in his head in her chisel letters, across the width of the Dome Gallery to where the Chancellor stood with Amedda on his left and the Umbaran on his right and a glass of something gold in his hand that he had not drunk. Palpatine was not looking at the Gungans. He was not looking at the Naboo. He was looking at Dooku's back as it moved away through the crowd, and then, with no haste at all, at Anakin, and then at Dooku again, the way a man looks from one hand to the other when he has just felt something pass between them. The kind face was on. But under it there was a thing Anakin knew better than he knew his own name, because he had lived inside the mind that produced it for twenty-three years and had learned to read it the way a dog reads weather. Pleasure. Not the pleasure of a man who has had a question answered. The pleasure of a man who has heard his two instruments strike the same note, and did not know until this moment that they were tuned to each other.

*Don't give him a show. Nothing he can use twice.*

He had given him something. He did not yet know what.

* * *

Then the doors.

They were the great doors at the north end of the gallery, where the Naboo party had been standing all evening pretending not to watch them, and they opened inward on their silent hinges, and the chamberlain at the top of the steps struck his staff once, as Amedda had, and said in a voice trained to carry over surf, "The Senator for Naboo."

The room turned. Anakin turned with it.

She came through the doors with a man a pace behind her, a broad young Naboo in the dark uniform of the Royal Security Forces with a leather patch over one eye and a hand that did not leave the vicinity of his sidearm: Typho. She wore blue. Senatorial blue, the deep clear blue of the Naboo delegation, a long gown with a high collar and the sleeves cut close, and over it a short cloak of a darker blue clasped at one shoulder. Her hair was dressed the Naboo way, up and back, held with something that caught the light, and her face was bare of the Queen's paint and was the face he had known under it. She was twenty years old. She was twenty years old and it was six years since Theed and she stood at the top of the steps for a moment while the room looked at her, with her chin up and her hands folded at her waist, and she was not dead. She was not grey. She was not lying on a table on a dark rock at the edge of nothing with two children crying beside her and a word on her mouth that he had not been there to hear. She was twenty and she was alive and she was looking for someone in the room, and he knew who, because the Naboo were already moving to her, and because the Chancellor had turned his kind face towards the doors, and because he had been fifteen once and had stood exactly here and had known, with his whole body, that she was looking for him.

*Don't look at her like she's already dead.*

He looked at her. He could not have said, afterwards, how.


## Chapter 18: The Reception, Part Two

She did not come to him at once. She came down the steps into the Naboo, who closed around her the way water closes over a thing dropped into it, and Anakin stood where Obi-Wan's hand held him and watched her be a Senator for a while, because that was what she was, and because he could not yet have walked.

He knew the moves. He had watched her make them for three years of war from the edges of rooms like this one, in a Knight's robes, pretending to be bored. The hand to the elderly delegate from Theed, both of hers over his one. The half-turn so that the woman standing behind him was not left out of it. The listening, which was real, and which was the thing people could not forgive her for, because it meant they had been heard and could not pretend afterwards that they had not. She was twenty. She had done all of it at fourteen with the paint on, and now she did it with her face bare, and it was better, and it was worse, because he could see her doing it.

"Breathe," Obi-Wan said, very quietly, beside his ear. "You've stopped."

He breathed. The body took the air gratefully and he hated it a little for that.

Across the gallery the Chancellor had not moved from between Amedda and Moore, and his glass was still full, and his kind face was turned towards the Naboo with the expression of a man watching his own garden come into flower. He did not look at Anakin. He did not need to. Dooku was somewhere behind the pillars to the east with the Serenno party and Anakin did not look for him either, because looking for him would have been a thing to be seen.

Then she lifted her head over the Theed delegate's shoulder and found him.

He had known she would. He had stood here once and felt it as a thing happening to the front of his body, and it happened to the front of his body now: her eyes crossing the room, catching, stopping, and the small change in her face that was not a smile yet but was the place a smile came from. She said something to the delegate. She touched his arm. She came through the Naboo and out the other side with Typho a pace behind her like a shadow that had been issued a sidearm, and the gallery, which had been pretending not to watch her all evening, pretended harder.

He had perhaps nine seconds. He used them to put the fifteen-year-old on over himself like the formal tunic with the sleeves a finger short: the shoulders back and a little too far, the chin up, the hands not knowing where to be. He let the face flush. It was going to flush anyway.

*Don't look at her like she's already dead.*

She stopped in front of him. She looked up, because she had to now; he had six centimetres on her, which was absurd, which was wrong, which was the single stupidest thing in a galaxy that had been very stupid to him, and her eyes went up his face and found the braid behind his right ear and came back down, and she said, with a laugh under it that she did not let out, "Ani? My goodness, you've grown."

Here was where the boy had said something about her having grown too, and gone red, and made a mess of it, and she had been kind. He had the whole sentence in his mouth. It had been waiting there for thirty years.

He bowed. The Temple bow, hands at the belt, the exact depth a Padawan gives a member of the Senate, not a degree lower.

"Senator Amidala," he said. His voice held. "It's an honour to see you again."

* * *

Everything he did not say to her stood behind him in a line, and he could feel it there, the way you feel people in a corridor behind a door.

*You will be twenty-seven. It will be night, and far from here, and there will be a medical droid, and you will say my name to a man who is not me.*

"Master Kenobi," she said, turning, and Obi-Wan had let go of Anakin's elbow in the second before she reached them and was now a Jedi Master at a reception, dry and courteous, with his hands in his sleeves. "I was told you'd both be here. I didn't quite believe it. The Temple doesn't usually lend its people out for a party."

"The Chancellor was persuasive," Obi-Wan said, "and it is your anniversary, Senator, not a party. We were told so most firmly at the door."

She laughed then, properly, and it was the laugh, the one he had heard across a lake. Anakin stood inside the boy's body and let the laugh go through him and out the other side and did not move.

"You look well," she said to Obi-Wan. "You look exactly the same. Does the Order issue that, or is it practice?"

"Practice. A great deal of it, and tea." He inclined his head. "You, if I may say so, look like a Senator who has read every line of a very long bill and intends to make the committee read them too."

"Oh, they've read them. That's what they're frightened of." She said it lightly and glanced, without appearing to, towards the east pillars, where a heavy blue Twi'lek was laughing too loudly with a trade delegation. Then she turned back, and the glance she gave Anakin was the one she had given the boy in the Queen's ship on the way to Coruscant, when he had been cold and she had put the blanket round him: curious, kind, a little amused, a person deciding to take an interest. "And you. Padawan Skywalker. Six years. I kept expecting to hear about you. Every time a Jedi was in the news I looked for the name."

*The arena. The chains. Your shoulder laid open and you climbing the pillar anyway, and the look you gave me from the top of it, which was not fear.*

"The Temple doesn't put Padawans in the news, Senator."

"No, I gathered." She tilted her head. "I did try. Did you know? After Naboo, after the funeral, after you'd all gone, I wrote to you. To the Temple. I addressed it to Ani Skywalker, because I didn't know what else to call you, and I expect that's why it came back." She was smiling at herself. "A very polite note. Something about Padawan learners and private correspondence. I wrote again a year later, properly, Padawan Skywalker, care of Master Kenobi. I never heard. I assumed it had been lost, or that you'd been told not to, or that you'd forgotten. You were nine. It's allowed."

He had not known. He went looking for it and it was not there: not in the boy's memory, not in the man's, not in the twenty-three years of the suit when he had gone through every record of her he could reach, twice. Two letters. A year apart. The first stamped and returned by some clerk in the Temple's correspondence office who had done his job. The second to Master Kenobi, care of, and never heard of again.

He did not look at Obi-Wan. Obi-Wan did not look at him. The bond, thin as a thread, gave nothing at all, which was in itself a thing.

"The Temple is strict about letters," Anakin said.

"So I learned."

"It isn't personal. They don't want us—" he found the word a fifteen-year-old would use, "—pulled. Outward. By people."

"By people," she repeated, and he saw her hear it, and file it, and not know yet what it was.

*You in white on a terrace at Varykino with the lake behind you and the light coming off the water onto your throat, saying no to me, and meaning it, and the way you stood when you said it.*

"That's a shame," she said. "I'd have liked to know how you were."

"I was well, Senator. Thank you."

* * *

He saw the moment she registered it, because he had been watching for it, because he had decided, somewhere between her second sentence and her third, what he was going to do.

It was not a decision in the way the Council meant the word. It arrived whole. He was standing in a boy's body in front of the only person in the galaxy he had ever been entirely happy beside, and she was looking at him the way she had looked at him at the start, before the start, with the beginning of a fondness in her that was the fondness of a kind woman for a child she had once helped, and he saw the whole road from that look to a table on Polis Massa laid out in front of him like a landing approach, every beacon lit. It began here. It began with her liking him. Every version of her death he could construct began with her liking him, and then more than liking, and then the lake, and then the arena, and then a sunset on a terrace with two droids for witnesses, and then her belly under his hand on a balcony with the Senate dome going gold, and then a landing platform in a red light, and then her feet leaving the ground.

*Her hands at her throat. Her feet leaving the ground. Obi-Wan's voice from the ramp saying his name as if it were an order, and it had not been.*

There was no way to be loved by her and keep her alive. He had tried the first and it had been the thing that killed her. So: the other. Be a thing she could not love. Be the Temple. Be the boy in the news she would never read about, polite and closed and a little cold, the one who had been told attachment was forbidden and had believed it, the one she would mention to Typho in the speeder afterwards and then, not unkindly, forget. He could do it. He had been a wall for twenty-three years; he could be a smaller one for a quarter of an hour.

It would cost her a little. He saw that and did it anyway. A small hurt now, a puzzle, against the whole of the rest of her life. He had made worse trades for less.

So when she asked whether he still flew, with that bright interest in her face, the podrace in it, the boy at the controls of a Naboo fighter with his hands on the wrong switches, he said, "When the Order requires it, Senator. Master Kenobi prefers that I not fly for its own sake."

"That must be hard."

"It's the discipline."

"You were quite good at it, as I recall." Dry. She was trying him. "You blew up a droid control ship."

"The ship did most of that. I was nine." He gave her the Temple's small polite smile, the one Padawans gave visiting dignitaries who wanted to be told that the Jedi were charming. "We're taught not to make much of it."

She looked at him. It was a short look and it did two things: it took a small hurt, somewhere behind the eyes, a quick flinch like a finger near a flame, and then it put the chin up, which was her tell, which had always been her tell, and over the hurt the Senator laid a puzzle. He could see her turning him over. She had met a boy who could not stop talking. She had expected, perhaps, a youth who could not stop talking either, red to the ears, and she had come across the room ready to be kind to that. This was something else. This was a well-mannered door. She did not know what she had done to close it and she was a person who, when she did not know a thing, went and found out.

That was the risk. He had known it was the risk. He held the door.

"The Temple's made a Jedi of you, then," she said, and there was the smallest edge on it, not for him; for the Temple.

"Not yet, Senator. It's trying."

"And is it—" she hesitated, which she did not do, "—are you happy there, Ani?"

The name. He had not expected her to use it twice.

*A hangar on Naboo and a bundle of blankets and her hand on the boy's cheek, the paint still on, saying his name as if it were a thing worth keeping.*

"Padawans aren't meant to be happy or unhappy, Senator," he said. "We're meant to be useful." He heard his own voice say it, light and flat and fifteen, with the smallest crack on *useful* where the body betrayed him, and he thought: good. Let her hear that. Let her think the Temple did that to a child.

Beside him Obi-Wan had gone very still. Not the stillness of a Jedi in a reception; a different one, deeper in, the stillness of a man who has recognised something and does not yet want to have recognised it. Anakin felt it down the bond as a kind of pressure change, the way a cabin feels before the seal fails, and he did not let himself turn his head. Obi-Wan knew what Anakin was doing. Obi-Wan had watched this boy be in love with this woman across half a war and had said nothing until it was far too late, and now he was standing here listening to the boy put the love away in front of her, deliberately, sentence by sentence, with the Temple's own manners for a tool, and whatever he thought about it he kept behind his beard and his folded hands. But the bond did not lie as well as his face. It said, in the mood it gave, something very like grief.

"Useful," Padmé said. She turned the word over as she had turned *by people*. "That sounds like a thing someone told you."

"Everyone tells us that, Senator. It's the first thing we're told."

"Then I'm sorry." She said it simply. "I remember a boy who wanted to see all the stars. I'd hoped the Order would let him."

"It will, Senator. In its own time." And then, because the fifteen-year-old would have, because the door needed a handle on it or she would go on working at the hinges: "I am grateful you wrote. Even if I didn't get them. I'd have liked to have had them."

A true thing. It cost more than the lies had.

Her face eased, a little, and he saw her decide to let him be what he was; to let the puzzle lie for now; to be, since it was all he was giving her room for, kind. He had counted on that. He had counted on her being kind and it worked, and it was the worst thing he had ever done to her except the thing he had actually done.

* * *

They talked for a few minutes more, the three of them, the way people do at such things. She asked Obi-Wan about the Order's view of the Outer Rim and Obi-Wan said the Order's view was that it had no view and she said that was a view, and they both enjoyed that. She spoke, briefly and with the heat kept under, about the bill: the franchise disclosure, the Rim tribunal, the committee that had been very polite and was about to take the heart out of it with a scalpel. The Chancellor supported it, she said. The Chancellor had been generous with his time. She said this looking at nothing, and Anakin, who had lived inside the man she was speaking of, heard the thing she did not yet know was there under *generous*, and kept his face.

"He is always generous with his time," Obi-Wan said, in the tone of a man remarking on the weather, and she glanced at him, and the glance was a Senator's glance, and she put something away behind it.

*The holo. Blue and grainy, the box going through the streets of Theed with the flowers piled on it, her hands folded on her chest with the japor snippet between them, and the respirator counting while he watched it for the ninth time.*

And he saw she was about to go. The room was pulling at her; Organa had appeared at the edge of the Naboo with his heavy patient face, and a tall pale woman beside him whose face Anakin still could not see without the wanted notice behind it, and Typho had shifted his weight in the way of a security man who has been given a schedule and intends to keep to it. She would go, and he had been the Temple to her for a quarter of an hour, and it had worked, and the Chancellor would have her in his office next tenday and be generous with his time, and she would find the thing under it herself, in her own time, in four years, in a pod in the Senate with her hands over her mouth, when it was too late to use.

He had meant to give her nothing. He found there was one thing he could not keep.

"Senator," he said.

She turned back. She had already half gone; she came back to him with her eyebrows up, the Senator's attention given to a boy because the boy had asked for it and she was that kind of person.

"May I say something? It'll sound odd."

"I like odd. I spend my days with people who are careful."

He let the fifteen-year-old find the words, the way a boy repeats a thing he has heard a master say and half understood and wholly believed. He made it awkward. He made it come out too fast.

"Don't trust anyone who tells you the Senate needs a stronger hand. Anyone. However reasonable it sounds. However much they seem to be on your side. If they say it, or if they get someone else to say it for them, then that's the thing they want, and nothing else they say matters." He stopped. His voice had cracked on *matters* and he let it. "That's all. I thought someone ought to say it to you. I don't know why me."

There was a pause, and then she laughed.

It was not the lake laugh. It was a Senator's laugh, delighted and a little startled, a woman being told the time of day by a child and finding it was the right time. Behind her Typho, who had not changed his expression in the whole of the evening, allowed the one eye to go, briefly, to the Jedi boy and then back to the room.

"You sound like Captain Typho," she said. "Word for word. He says it to me in the speeder every morning, in case I've forgotten overnight."

"Then he's right, Senator."

"He's always right. That's why I don't listen to him." She was still smiling. "Thank you, Ani. I'll remember who said it."

And she put her hand on his arm.

It was nothing. It was the touch any person gives at the end of a conversation to a youth who has said a thing out of turn and meant it: light, quick, above the elbow where the sleeve ended a finger short, so that her fingers were on the cloth and the heel of her hand, for an instant, on skin. He felt it as if a door had been opened onto a furnace. He felt it in the places where there had been metal. The body stood and let it happen and did not move, not a hair, not a breath, and she took her hand away and nodded to Obi-Wan and said something about hoping the Order would lend them out again, and turned, and went across the gallery to Organa and Mothma with Typho at her shoulder, and was a Senator, and did not look back, and he watched her not look back until the Naboo closed over her again.

"Anakin," Obi-Wan said.

"There's a terrace," he said. "Where is it."

"Through the pillars. To the west. Anakin—"

He was already walking.

* * *

The terrace was a strip of stone along the outside of the dome, two storeys below the gallery, reached by a stair that nobody at a reception used because it was cold and had no servers on it. The stone was wet; it had rained earlier and the city was still steaming. A rail at hip height, old bronze, the long curve of it following the dome. Beyond the rail the drop, and then the Senate plaza a thousand metres down pricked with lights, and then Coruscant going out and up on every side in its lanes and towers to a sky the colour of a bruise, with the Temple's five spires standing against it far off to the south like something that had been left there.

He got to the rail. He got both hands on it. Then it was as though whatever had been holding him together from the inside, the thing he had been standing inside of for the last quarter hour, simply let go of the walls.

It came out through the hands first. They would not stop. He gripped the bronze until the knuckles went white and the hands shook anyway, not the fine tremor of a tired boy but a deep one, from the forearm, the kind that in the suit would have meant a servo failing. He looked at them. They were a boy's hands, long and unfinished, no burns, no scars, the nails bitten, and they were shaking as if they were very old. The breath went next. It came short, and then it would not come, and then it came in a kind of drag that he heard himself make and could not stop, and he thought, absurdly, that he had been breathing through a machine for twenty-three years and had never once been short of it, and that this was what it was for, this, to be used up by a thing like this. He bent over the rail. He put his forehead on the back of his hands on the cold bronze and his body shook from the shoulders down in complete silence, because the gallery was two storeys up and sound went up, and because he had learned a long time ago to do this without a sound. Not a sob. Nothing so useful. Only the body going, and going, with no noise, like a building settling.

*Her hand on the arm. The heel of it on the skin.*

*Her feet leaving the ground.*

*Luke's face above the mask with the light on it. "Tell your sister." The same eyes. Hers.*

She had touched him and gone and she was alive, and she would go home to the Senate apartments in a speeder with Typho telling her to take the long route, and sleep, and get up, and fight the committee, and lose, and go on fighting, and never once think about the boy at the reception who had been strange and cold and had said a thing about a stronger hand that sounded like her captain; and in that she would live. That was the shape of it. That was the whole of what he had done, and it was right, and it was the only thing he had ever done for her that was wholly for her, and it felt like the lava bank. It felt exactly like the lava bank. He had not known the body remembered that.

He stayed there a long time. The rain started again, lightly, and stopped. Below, the traffic went round and round the dome in its lanes, red one way and white the other, as it had always done, as it would do when she was dead and when he was dead and when the dome was a thing the Emperor addressed the galaxy from. His hands stopped first. Then the breath came back, in pieces, and he put them together. He straightened. He wiped his face on the too-short sleeve, which was a thing a boy did, and which he was.

He did not hear Obi-Wan come. He felt the bond stir, as a mood stirs, and then there was a man at the rail beside him, not close, an arm's length off, with his hands on the bronze as Anakin's were and his face turned to the city.

Obi-Wan said nothing. He did not say it for a long time. The traffic went round. A tower off to the east went dark, floor by floor, as someone put it to bed. Anakin's hands on the rail were nearly steady now and he kept them there to be sure of it. He thought that if Obi-Wan said *my friend* he would go over the rail, and Obi-Wan did not say it. He thought that if Obi-Wan touched him he would break again, and Obi-Wan did not touch him. He only stood, in a Master's dark robes with the sleeves that fitted, and looked out, and was there, and let the being there do what it did.

Then he said, quietly, to the city: "I know."

"You don't."

Anakin did not look at him when he said it. It came out in a man's register, low and flat and certain, nothing of fifteen in it, and he heard that and did not care.

Obi-Wan was silent a while. The rain came and went again, a breath of it.

"No," he said at last. "I suppose I don't."

He said it slowly, and gently, in a voice Anakin could not read. It might have been a man who had stood beside a weeping boy and admitted he did not know what the boy had lost. It might have been a man who knew exactly, who had buried her, who had stood on that ramp and said that name as if it were an order and watched it fail, and who was saying *I suppose I don't* the way you agree to a lie for someone else's sake, because they need the lie more than you need to be known. It could be read either way. Anakin, who had spent four months reading this man, could not tell which, and the not being able to tell was so like the whole of this year that he almost laughed, and did not, because the body would not have survived it.

They stood. Below them the Senate plaza lay with its lights on, empty and clean, and far off the Temple's spires held up the dark.

It was Obi-Wan who turned his head first, not to Anakin; along the rail, to the east, where the terrace followed the dome's curve out of the light of the gallery doors and into the shadow of the next pillar.

Anakin followed the look.

Count Dooku was standing at the far end of the terrace with a glass in his hand he had not drunk from, alone, very upright in his dark Serenno coat with the silver at the collar, perhaps forty metres away. He was not looking at the city. He was looking at Anakin, and had been, with the steady unembarrassed attention of a man studying an object he has been told the provenance of and does not believe; and when Anakin's eyes found him he did not look away, as a courtier would have, but went on looking a moment longer, as a swordsman does, and then inclined his head, a fraction, and did not come nearer.

And in the gallery doorway behind Dooku, where the light fell out onto the wet stone in a long yellow shape, the Chancellor stood with Sly Moore's pale face a step behind his shoulder, and his glass, still full, in his hand. He was not looking at Anakin at all. He was looking at Dooku's back, at the set of Dooku's shoulders, at the stillness of the old man's head as he watched the boy; and on the Chancellor's kind face was an expression Anakin had seen from the inside and had never, until now, seen from without: the look of a man who has set two instruments side by side and is listening, very carefully, for which of them is out of tune.


## Chapter 19: Interlude: The Count's Report

Count Dooku left the reception at the eleventh hour, which was the hour at which a man of his age and standing was expected to leave, and he had made a lifetime's practice of doing what was expected at the hour it was expected, so that the rest of what he did might go unremarked. He said his goodnights. He bowed to the Chancellor last, as a guest should, and the Chancellor took his hand in both of his and said how good it had been of him to come so far for Qui-Gon's memory, and smiled, and let him go.

The speeder was his own, driven by his own man from Serenno. The Senate dome fell away above and behind, lit from within like a lamp with its wick turned high, and Dooku sat with his hands folded on the head of his cane and looked at nothing, and for the first time in a number of years that he did not care to count, he was not entirely in command of what he was thinking.

He had touched the boy's mind. He had been told not to; he had done it anyway, lightly, for a breath, the way a connoisseur runs a thumb along the grain of a piece he has been told he may look at and not handle. His Master had wanted him to look, and one did not look at such a thing with the eyes.

He went through it again, in order, because order was what he had instead of comfort.

The surface first. A boy's surface, and a competent one: the tidy resentments of fifteen, a mother on a desert world, speeders and the smell of them, a girl in a politics class. All of it plausible. All of it a little too neatly set out, like a room arranged for a visitor, with the chairs turned to face the door. Every Padawan of talent learned to build one by twelve.

Then the wall.

He had known at once what it was, and the knowing had gone through him like cold water, because he recognised the grammar. Not the Jedi grammar, which was all stillness and release, a shield like the surface of a pond; this was built. It was laid in courses. It had been raised against a pressure and had held, and had been breached, and had been raised again over the breach, and he could feel the old lines under it the way one feels a mended bone through the skin. It was a discipline he had been taught in a derelict foundry by a man whose face he had not been permitted to see for the first year, and he had taken six years to learn it, at seventy, with half a century of the other grammar to unlearn. The boy was fifteen.

And then, for one second, through the wall, the thing itself.

He had expected fear, or the sullen, formless dislike that the young had for the old, or nothing. What came through was none of those. It was hatred, and it was aimed. It had his name on it. It was not the hatred of a boy for a famous man who has left his Order and written unkind things about it; he had met that often and it was a thin, borrowed thing, somebody's master's opinion wearing a Padawan's face. This was his own. It had the precision of a history: the hatred a man feels for someone who has taken something from him, and knows exactly what, and has had years to turn it over. There was a shape to it that he could almost see the edges of. Something lost. Something given up on one's knees. He did not know what. He had never, before this evening, been in the same room as Qui-Gon's boy.

And the boy's face had not moved.

That was the part he returned to in the speeder, with the lanes of light sliding across the window. He had been looking into the boy's face when it happened; he had chosen the moment so that he would be. A boy's face, narrow, growing, the braid behind the ear, the sleeves of the formal robe a finger too short at the wrist. The hatred had come through the wall with the force of a door kicked in, and the face above it had gone on being politely attentive to a Count's one remark about a dead man's last message, and then the door had been shut, not slammed, shut, and the boy had said *Count* and bowed the exact depth a Padawan bows to a visiting dignitary who is no longer a Jedi. Not a hair's depth more. Dooku had taught that bow to Qui-Gon, who had taught it to Kenobi, who had taught it to this.

He had not been so well handled in years. He did not like how it tasted.

* * *

He had watched the boy afterwards, from across the Dome Gallery, because his Master had asked him to look.

He had watched him with the Senator from Naboo. He had seen the girl cross the gallery in her blue and go to the boy with her security captain a step behind, and he had seen the boy receive her the way a Padawan receives a Senator, with courtesy and nothing in it, and he had seen her face do what a young woman's face does when she has expected warmth and been given manners. She had gone back across the gallery to Organa and the Chandrilan woman, and the boy had stood where she left him for the space of three breaths with his hands held still at his sides in the way of someone who has had to tell his hands to be still.

He had watched him with Kenobi on the terrace. That had been longer and quieter and harder to read. The boy had stood at the rail; the master had stood beside him, a half-pace away, which was closer than a Padawan stood and further than a son. At one point Kenobi had turned his head and said a short thing, and the boy had answered with two words, and whatever they were, Kenobi had looked for a moment at the plaza as if it had been taken away from him. There had been no hand on the shoulder. Dooku had noted that. Kenobi was a man who touched; he had touched the boy's elbow in the gallery, and his collar, and on the terrace he had not touched him at all, and the not touching had the look of a decision.

And the boy had found him watching, across forty metres of wet stone, and had not looked away. It was not a boy's look. It was the look of someone who has measured the distance between them, and the ground, and the light, and knows what he would do with it.

He had inclined his head. He had not gone nearer. His Master had said, *look, and do not touch,* and he had already disobeyed the second part once that evening.

* * *

The Works at night were a city that had died and not been told. Dooku's man set the speeder down on a loading apron where a foundry had been and did not ask where the Count was going, and Dooku walked the rest, with his cane sounding on the ferrocrete and the smell of old chemistry in the air, past the dead chimneys and the gantries that no one had taken down because taking them down would have cost more than leaving them.

His Master was waiting for him in person. He had expected the hologram; the hour was late, and the Chancellor had a Senate in the morning. But there was a shape in the dark at the far end of the long room, a hood and the pale hands folded in the sleeves, and the voice when it came was the other voice, the one the Senate did not hear.

"Well?"

Dooku knelt. The knee complained; he did not. "Master."

"You touched him."

"I did."

"I told you not to."

"You told me to look. I looked with the instrument that sees." He kept his head down. "You wished to know. I am telling you."

A silence. Then, out of the dark, not displeasure. Interest. "Tell me."

He told it as he had ordered it in the speeder: the surface, the wall, the second through the wall. He did not embellish. He described the grammar of the wall and his Master said nothing, which meant his Master had already found that much for himself. He described the hatred. He said that it had his name on it, and he said it without inflection, as if it were a line in an inventory.

"Yours," said Sidious.

"Mine. Not the Order's. Not a boy's. A man's, and old, and particular."

"You have never met him."

"I had never met him before this evening."

"And his face?"

"Did not change." Dooku lifted his head. "He shut it on me as one closes a door on a draught. Master, I know a fifteen-year-old's control. I trained three of them. This is not that."

The hood tilted, a degree.

"Then say what you came to say, Tyranus."

He had composed the sentence on the loading apron and he gave it whole, because it was the one true thing he had brought.

"The boy is not yours, and he is not mine. Someone has had him."

The pleasure that came out of the dark then was a thing he had felt only a few times in six years and never without a small cold movement at the back of his neck. It was the pleasure of a mind that has been bored for a very long while and has found, at last, a thing it cannot immediately account for. His Master's voice was soft and almost fond.

"Then we will find out who."

* * *

They did not sit; there was nothing in the room to sit on. His Master walked, slowly, a few paces along the dark, and back, with the sleeves folded, and spoke as he walked, and Dooku stood where he had risen and listened.

"Consider what we know. For six years I have walked in that boy as I walk in my own house. Four months ago the doors closed. The healer found a wall and called it old. You find a wall and call it built in the right grammar, and behind it a hatred for you with a history you did not give it." A pause, a turn. "I will not insult you by asking again whether it is yours."

"It is not."

"No. You have not the patience for a long seduction." Said mildly, as a fact about the weather. Dooku let it pass. "So. Someone has walked where I walked and left a wall behind them, and has done it so well that the boy's own master suspects and cannot name it, and the Council hunts a shadow through its own corridors and has not thought to look at the one child it watches most. It was not you. It was not I. Therefore it was a third, and I do not know a third."

Dooku said nothing. There had been, four months ago, in this room, a third possibility that his Master had looked at and not named, and he had not been invited to name it since.

"We will not find the hand by asking the boy," Sidious said. "The boy lies as well as you do. Better; you I can read. We will take something from him and watch where he looks." He stopped walking. "The master."

"Kenobi."

"Kenobi. The Jedi lose their masters, Tyranus. It is the one thing they do reliably. They send them out into places they do not understand with a Padawan at their heels, and the master dies, and the Padawan is given a cup of tea and a new name for the thing he feels. It is how the Order has made every Jedi worth having. It is how it made Kenobi." A beat, exactly placed. "It is how it made you."

Dooku did not move.

"Qui-Gon was lost to a mission the Council should never have sent him on," he said.

"Yes. And look what it gave us." The hood turned toward him fully. "Kenobi will be lost to a mission the Council should never send him on. The boy will be there, because the boy is always there. And we will see what the boy does when the thing he has been building his wall around is taken from him. If he weeps like a Padawan, I will know less than I know now, and I will have Kenobi out of the way, which I have wanted for four years. If he does something else—" the voice dropped, not for effect, Dooku thought, but because it was the part his Master actually cared about— "then I want it seen. Not reported. Seen. I want a recording, and I want it in a form the boy cannot deny and the Council cannot explain, and I want it in my hand before anyone else knows it exists."

"Fett," Dooku said.

"Fett records everything. It is the only thing about him I find endearing. He oversees; he does not lead. He is not to touch the boy. He is to see that others do, and to stand where the light is good." The pale hands turned once, open, as if showing that they held nothing. "The Pykes, I think. They owe you. And they have a lightsaber in a drawer they do not know what to do with, which makes them the sort of people who will take a Jedi hostage if they are told it is a negotiation."

"It will need to reach the Council through the proper channels."

"Everything reaches the Council through the proper channels. That is what the channels are for." A last turn in the dark, and the voice went, in one breath, from the one the Senate did not hear to the one it did, warm and a little tired. "Go and arrange it, Tyranus. Ask for the hero of Naboo by name. The Senate loves a hero, and the Council cannot refuse the Senate twice in one year."

Dooku bowed. It was the right depth. He had taught it.

* * *

The hologram to Kamino went out from a relay on Serenno that did not exist, through two others that did, and arrived in a white room Dooku had seen once and never wished to see again. Jango Fett stood in it in his armour with the helmet under his arm, which was a courtesy; it meant he was not recording.

"Count."

"Fett. Oba Diah, within two months. The Pykes will hold a Republic survey party and a Jedi will be sent to talk them out of it. You will not lead. You will oversee, and you will record, and what you record you will deliver to me and to no one else, uncut. There will be a second Jedi, a Padawan. You will not touch him. If the first Jedi dies in the course of it, that is the Pykes' affair and not yours."

Fett's face did nothing, which was what his face did. "Price."

"Name it."

He named it. It was high, and less than it should have been, because Fett knew who paid his retainer. He had conditions. He would choose his own ground. He would not go in under the Pykes' command; he would go in beside it. He wanted a clean route out and a ship that was his. He wanted it understood that if the Padawan turned out to be what Dooku was not telling him the Padawan might be, he reserved the right to leave.

"You will not need to leave," Dooku said. "You will need to stand still and watch."

"That's the part that costs." Fett shifted the helmet to the other arm. "I'll be off-world eleven days, twelve. The boy stays here. I want that in the ledger."

"The boy stays where he is."

"Then we're done." The hologram cut.

The Pykes were slower, because a syndicate was a committee with knives, and it took three intermediaries on three worlds before Lom Pyke's answer came back in the form of a question about mineral rights. There was, it seemed, a Republic survey team already on Oba Diah: eleven people under a woman named Deshan Orr, mapping a spice-bearing ridge the Pykes had understood to be theirs and the Republic had understood to be nobody's. Lom Pyke was happy to be aggrieved about that. He would, for a consideration and the quiet return of a certain small item he had kept since a certain Jedi had been dealt with on his world six years ago, make his grievance loud, and hold the eleven for their own safety, and ask the Republic to send someone sensible.

Someone sensible would be asked for by name. Senator Caius Verrane of Tyrrhus, whose shipyards the Count of Serenno had kept from going under two years before and who had never been allowed to forget it, would rise in committee and say that only the hero of Naboo could be trusted with eleven Republic lives, and the Chancellor would look grave and say the Council must decide, and the Council would decide what the Chancellor told it.

It took Dooku four days, from a room in 500 Republica with the shutters drawn, and at the end of them there was a sequence of events laid out ahead of a man he had met for the first time at that reception and a boy he had met once, and none of it bore his name.

* * *

On the last night he stood at the window with the shutters open, which he did not usually do, and looked across the lit distance to the five spires of the Temple holding up the dark.

He had been a Jedi for seventy years. He had told himself, when he left, that he was leaving a thing that had failed, and he still believed it, and it still cost him something each time he said it, and he had begun, in the last months, to keep a private account of the cost that he showed to no one. Tonight he added a line to it. He had stood in the dark and listened to his Master say *it is how it made you,* and had felt the old loss turn over in him like a body in a bed, and had said nothing, and his Master had known he would say nothing, and had said it anyway, to show that he could.

That was the usual cost. It was not what kept him at the window.

What kept him there was the second through the wall. He had reported it faithfully. But there was a thing his Master had not asked for, because his Master did not think in that direction, and so Dooku had not said it, and found now that it was the first thought in six years that was entirely his own.

It was this. Somebody had had the boy, and had taught him a wall, and had given him, somehow, a hatred for Count Dooku with the shape of a history in it: the shape of a thing taken and a man made to kneel. Dooku had no such history with the child. He had gone through every encounter of his life, in the speeder and since, and could not find it.
