And the thing that kept him at the window, that kept his hands still on the sill the way the boy's had been still at his sides, was not the question of who had done it.

It was that some part of him, old and careful and very tired, did not entirely wish to know.

Below, the lanes of the Senate District went on about their business, and the Temple's spires stood where they had stood for a thousand years, and somewhere in them a boy of fifteen was asleep, or was not, behind a wall that had been raised against a pressure and had held.


## Chapter 20: The Shape of the Trap

Ahsoka Tano had learned, in seven years, that the safest place to hide a question was inside a smaller one.

"Does the Council sit every day, Master Plo? Even on rest days?"

She had asked that one three tendays ago, with her boots not reaching the floor from the refectory bench and Kessa Ruun beside her chewing with her mouth open, and Master Plo had answered it the way he answered everything she asked him: fully, gently, as though a child's curiosity were worth the whole of his attention. Most days, he had said. And she had nodded and asked whether the chairs were comfortable, and he had laughed behind the mask, and the first question had gone into the ground like a seed.

Since then she had learned that if she asked him *what is the Council doing today* in a voice that wanted a story, he would give her the shape of one, with the names left in. He did not think of it as telling her anything. He thought of it as talking to little 'Soka, who had asked him at five whether a dream could be true and had never since asked him anything that mattered. She had spent seven years making sure that nothing she said to him mattered, so that now, when it did, he would not hear the difference.

She hated it more than she had expected to.

"Master Plo's here," Kessa said, and kicked her under the table, and Ahsoka looked up with a child's face already on.

He came down the refectory between the long benches with his hands folded into his sleeves, tall in the way all adults were tall to her now, the breath-mask hissing softly. Master Imari Sen, at the end of the table with her grey hair pinned and her own hands in her own sleeves, inclined her head to him; the clan-mother had been told, some weeks ago, to keep an eye on the Tano child, and had been keeping it, and Ahsoka had made sure there was nothing for it to find.

"Hawkbat Clan," Plo said. "Initiate Ruun. Initiate Tano." He sat on the end of the bench, and the children laughed because it tipped. "What are you learning this tenday?"

Kessa told him, at length. Ahsoka let her, and ate her porridge, and listened with half of herself to Kessa and with the other half to the thin, faint thing at the back of her mind that was not Kessa and not Plo.

It was always there now, since the nineteenth night, a thread no wider than a hair, running from her to a boy somewhere above her and to the west. It carried no words; it carried a mood, and this morning the mood was flat and careful, which meant he was in front of someone. She did not pull on it. A Padawan bonded to a youngling would be seen by any Master who happened to look, and so they kept it so faint that some mornings she had to be very still to be sure it was still there, and some mornings she was not sure, and that was worse than she had told him.

"And you, 'Soka?"

"I'm learning to lose at sabers," she said. "Tam says I hold it wrong."

"Do you?"

"Yes, Master."

"Then I expect you will keep doing it." The mask turned towards her and stayed a moment: the silence that meant he had decided, again, not to name what he saw. "The Council has a long day. I came to see your faces first."

"What's the Council doing?" Kessa said, because Kessa always said it, because Ahsoka had made sure, over three tendays, that Kessa always said it.

"Reports," said Master Plo. "Petitions. A great deal of sitting." He rose. "Eat your breakfast, Hawkbats."

That was all, that morning. Nothing had come through the drop for days except the one thing, and the one thing she had read eleven times.

* * *

It had been under the moss two nights after the reception, and she had carried it back inside her tunic and read it under the blanket with the glow-stick they were not supposed to have, moving the letters seven places back in her head.

*She called me Ani. I was cold to her and it worked and I do not want to talk about it. D. touched. The wall held. One second over the top before I shut it. He felt it. P. watched him feel it. Three for three. S.*

She had lain in the dark with the flimsi against her chest and had felt, for the second line, nothing she could use: a woman who had been kind to her and right about everything and had died for it had stood on a terrace and said *Ani* to a boy who had already killed her once, and he had been cold, and it had worked. She could not help him with that.

It was the next line that had kept her awake.

*One second over the top.* She knew what one second was. She had stood within arm's length of Count Dooku once, his curved hilt lit and his face entirely without expression, and had understood, the way one understands cold, that he missed nothing because he had never needed to. One second of Anakin's hatred over the wall was not a slip. It was a signature. Dooku had read it, and turned and reported it to the other one, and the other one had watched him read it and been pleased.

*Three for three,* he had written, as if it were a joke. She had made it a joke, in her own note, so that he could walk into that room. She had told him *don't look at her like she's already dead* and he had looked at her like a stranger instead, which was the same thing in better clothes, and now there were two old men in the city who knew something was wrong with Obi-Wan Kenobi's Padawan, and knew it in the only grammar they trusted.

She had put the note back in the lining of her belt with the Togruti list and had slept, in the end, because the body she wore was ten and did what it was told.

* * *

The next clan up had the Room of a Thousand Fountains the hour before Hawkbat did, and so Hawkbat always passed them coming out: twenty children of twelve and thirteen in tunics a size larger, walking in the straight quiet line the older clans were proud of, the one at the back always turning to shush whoever had laughed.

The one at the back was always Barriss Offee.

Ahsoka had never managed to arrange her life so that she did not see her. The Temple was too small: a floor apart in the dormitories, an hour apart in the fountain room, two benches apart in the refectory. A thin Mirialan girl with the black diamond tattoos still new across the bridge of her nose, hood up even indoors, earnest in the way the Temple produced earnestness, forever helping. If a younger child dropped a stylus Barriss picked it up. If a younger child cried, Barriss crouched and asked why, in a low voice, as if crying were a reasonable thing that could be discussed. She was, by every measure Ahsoka had, a good child. She would be a good healer, with careful hands.

Ahsoka went past her in the doorway and Barriss, catching her eye, smiled at her, the small polite smile the older clans gave the younger, and Ahsoka smiled back, because a child would, and went on into the green wet air with the smile stuck to her face like something she had walked into.

*A hangar, and the noise coming first, and then the heat; a corridor with the lights gone red; a cell with a bench; a circle of chairs and the faces in them deciding; a woman's hand on her shoulder on the Temple steps that she had stepped out from under, saying sorry, and meaning a different thing by it than they had heard.*

Twelve. The girl was twelve. She had never set a charge or lied under oath or put a saber in someone else's hand and watched them hang for it. She was a child who picked up styluses. And some part of Ahsoka that was not a child stood in the doorway every tenday and looked at her thin back and did the sum, and could not make it come out. If a thing has not happened, it is not a crime. If it has happened to you, it is not nothing. There was no column for a debt owed by a person who did not yet exist, and she could not decide, had not been able to decide in seven years, whether to hate a twelve-year-old, and the not deciding was its own small daily wound.

She found her place on the moss by the long pool and sat down to meditate with the others, and did not.

* * *

The water fight was Tam Dorreth's fault, as most things were.

Master Sen was called to the door by a Knight with a datapad, and in the forty seconds she was gone, Tam, who could not be still for forty seconds if the Order depended on it, flicked a handful of the long pool at Wix. The Ithorian boy made the double-throated honk that meant outrage and put both long hands in. Ilyo squealed. Brannick Sel, who was Nautolan and could not be made wetter, waded in up to his knees on principle and began using his tunic hem as a bucket.

Kessa grabbed Ahsoka by the wrist and pulled, laughing, and Ahsoka let herself be pulled.

She had meant to stand at the edge and get a little wet, as a careful child with a reputation for intensity would. But the pool was cold and Tam was shouting *Tano's on my side,* and the body she wore was ten years old and strong for its age and had not been allowed to do anything with its strength for a very long time. She found she had put a wave over Tam's head that made him splutter and sit down. She found, which was stranger, that she was laughing, out loud, a child's laugh, from somewhere she had not known was still there.

For perhaps a minute the Room of a Thousand Fountains was full of nothing but wet shrieking children, sand-coloured tunics gone dark to the chest, Ilyo's green head bobbing, Wix honking, Brannick standing in the middle like a small benevolent island, and the waterfalls going on overhead as they had for a thousand years.

Then she saw the Guard.

He was on the gallery above the east door, masked, the yellow pike upright, as still as the stone. Her body stopped before her mind did; the laugh died and her hands came out of the water, and for a moment she simply stood, hip-deep, and looked at him. Then the mind caught up and did its checking. Human height, not taller. Shoulders set wide. Not the one. The one stood on the Council tower doors now, tall and narrow and patient; this was some other Knight behind some other mask, watching younglings make fools of themselves in the sacred quiet.

It did not matter. Her hands were shaking under the water and she pressed them against her thighs until they stopped.

"Hawkbat Clan."

Master Sen had not raised her voice. The clan went silent in a single wet second, twenty children turning to the doorway with water running out of their boots, and Master Sen stood with her hands in her sleeves and looked at them for a long time.

"Out," she said. "All of you. On the moss. Sit."

They sat. The Guard on the gallery had not moved.

"This room," said Master Sen, "is where Masters of this Order have come to be quiet for longer than the Senate has existed." Ahsoka did not look at the third waterfall, or at the stretch of grass forty paces from it where she had been told a man she had never seen fight would one day be killed by a boy she had. "You have been given it an hour each day because we trust you. Initiate Dorreth."

"Yes, Master."

"Was it you?"

Tam, to his credit, said yes.

"Then you will clean the long pool filters this evening instead of supper. The rest of you will change, and come back, and sit this hour again, in silence, and the Guard will watch you do it. Initiate Tano."

Ahsoka looked up. Had to: she was sitting, the clan-mother was standing, and the distance was absurd.

"I am surprised at you," Master Sen said, not unkindly, and Ahsoka felt it go into her like a needle, because the woman meant *you are usually so careful,* and that was the whole of her disguise, and she had let it slip for a minute because a boy named Tam had wanted her on his side.

"Yes, Master," she said. "I'm sorry."

Barriss Offee's clan was passing along the gallery. Barriss saw twenty dripping children on the moss, and her face did something small and sympathetic, and she took the towel from her belt, the one the older clans carried, and leant over the rail and dropped it to the nearest child, who was Ahsoka.

It landed in her lap, warm, smelling of soap. She held it a moment and then, because a child would, used it.

* * *

They played Jedi and Sith on the grass terrace after the second meditation, with their tunics still damp, because it was the game the clan always played when they had been in trouble and needed to be heroes again.

The rules were a child's rules. The Jedi were everyone. The Sith was one, and stood at the top of the terrace steps with a broken-off bit of trellis for a blade, and the Jedi came up the steps and killed him, over and over, with sound effects, and when he had been killed enough he lay down and they stood on him and said something noble. Nobody ever wanted to be the Sith. Being the Sith meant losing, with twenty children watching.

"Tano," said Tam, who had forgiven her for the wave. "Tano can be the Sith. She holds it backwards anyway."

"That's not fair," Kessa said, loyally, and then, less loyally, "but she does."

"I don't want to," Ahsoka said, in the voice.

"You're always on my side," Tam said. "Be on nobody's side for once."

So she went up the steps with the trellis reversed in her small fist and turned at the top, and twenty children spread out below her with their sticks up and their faces lit, and she stood there, a girl of ten with her lekku barely past her shoulders and her boots full of water, and looked down at them.

She knew how to do this. That was the thing. She knew exactly how a Sith stood at the top of a flight of steps and looked down at the people coming to kill him. She had seen it from below: the mask and the long black and the red light making a line down the centre of it, the stillness that was not calm but something underneath calm, the patience of a thing that has already won. She could show them. She could drop the reversed stick into the grip her hand wanted and set her feet the way she had set them on a stone floor in a temple older than this one, and only she would know whose feet she was standing in.

She played it as a child would. She growled. She said *I am the dark side* in a stupid deep voice and Ilyo shrieked with delight and they came up the steps at her. She blocked a few, let a few through. Tam got her in the ribs and shouted *got you,* and Wix got her in the back, and then Kessa came in with her eyes screwed shut and hit her on the shoulder, and Ahsoka lay down on the grass and died.

They stood on her. Not hard. Children's boots and the smell of wet grass, the terrace lamps coming on against the dusk, and Tam with his foot on her stomach saying something noble about the light, which he had got from a holo.

She lay with her eyes open and looked past him at the first stars and thought about a floor that had tilted and a ceiling that had come down, and a hand she had not been able to hold onto, and a voice through the cracked place in a mask saying her name the way it had been said when she was fourteen. The children cheered. She lay there a moment longer than the game needed, and nobody noticed except the small boy sitting at the edge of the terrace with his knees drawn up, who was not in Hawkbat Clan and should not have been there at all.

* * *

He followed her down the stairs afterwards, the way he always did, a floor behind, pretending he was not.

Caleb Dume was seven and in Bear Clan and had attached himself to her some months ago for no reason she had ever found. He was a scrap of a boy with dark hair in his eyes and a habit of standing too close to things he wanted to look at, and he had already, at seven, the stubborn set of the jaw she remembered on a man leaning on the rail of a freighter with a mask over eyes that did not work, telling her it would be fine.

She did not slow down for him. It was the one thing she could do for him: not look.

The lip of the fourth step from the bottom was worn into a shallow dip that every child in the Temple learned the hard way. She heard him miss it, the catch of breath and the scuff, and turned, already moving, and had him before he hit the landing: a hand under one arm and a hand in the back of his tunic, his whole weight swinging against her, which was nothing, a seven-year-old's weight, and then he was standing on the step below her with his face very surprised.

She should have let go then.

She did not. She stood on the stairs in the last of the light with a boy of seven held against her by the shoulders, his hair against her chin, the small quick heart going under her hand, and went on holding him, and could not have said why except that he was warm and he was there and both of his eyes worked. She had seen him walk into a thing he could not see with his hand out. He had been very calm. She had hated him a little for it.

"You can let go," Caleb said into her tunic. "I'm not falling."

She let go.

He stood back and looked up at her with his head on one side, as if she were a problem in astrogation.

"Why are you crying?"

"I'm not," she said. She was not. She put her hand to her face to be sure and the hand came away dry. "Watch the fourth step. Everyone misses it."

"I know," said Caleb, with dignity, and went on down ahead of her, and missed it again.

* * *

Master Plo came back at the evening hour, which he did not always do, and found her on the bench outside the Hawkbat dorm with a datapad on her knees, the maths she was pretending to do.

"You are in disgrace, I hear," he said, and sat. "I gather there was a wave."

"Tam started it."

"Tam always starts it. You do not always finish it." He let the mask hiss a while. Then, because he was tired, and because she had taught him over three tendays that it was what the child asked, he said, before she could: "You will want to know what the Council did today."

She made her face want to know. It was not hard. "What did it do?"

"Sat," he said. "Mostly. There is a thing on Oba Diah. Do you know where that is?"

"No, Master." She did.

"Nor should you. It is a bad place in a bad stretch of space, run by people called the Pyke Syndicate, who are not very nice." His voice had the mild, telling-a-story cadence she had spent seven years earning. "A Republic survey team went there to map a mineral ridge, eleven of them, with permits, and the Pykes have decided the permits were not sufficient and are holding them against a fee. The Senate does not pay fees. So the Council is sending someone to negotiate."

The bond at the back of her mind was faint and flat. He was somewhere above her, with his Master, perhaps, who was being told this now in some other voice.

"Who?" she said.

"Master Kenobi. He is good at it. The request came through the Chancellor's office this morning and a Senator asked for him by name, which is not usual, but he is the hero of Naboo and Senators like a name they have heard." Plo turned the mask towards her, and the goggles gave her her own small face back. "It is a routine thing, 'Soka. A tenday there and back. He will be home before Tam has finished the filters."

She said something a child would say, something about whether Master Kenobi was frightened of the Pykes, and he laughed, and said that Master Kenobi was not frightened of anything so far as anyone had been able to determine, and put his hand briefly on her montrals, and went.

She sat with the datapad on her knees and looked at nothing.

She knew what the Pykes were.

She knew them from Mandalore, the domes under the sun and the helmets in the streets, where the thing in the shadows had worn the Pykes as one of its three hands, with Black Sun and Death Watch for the other two, and had taken a planet with them. She had stood in a throne room full of their lanky grey-faced soldiers with a man in white armour at her shoulder whose name she said to herself now, deliberately, *Rex,* because no rule in this skin forbade her to think it. The Pykes had been bought that time. They were always bought. And she knew them from after, from the years when she had been a voice on a hidden channel and not a Jedi at all, when the Syndicate's reputation came to her over and over on other people's reports: they did not take hostages for fees. They took hostages for clients. A Pyke did not hold eleven Republic surveyors for the Senate's money; the Senate had no money a Pyke could not get easier elsewhere. A Pyke held eleven people because somebody had paid him to make sure that a Jedi came.

Which Jedi did not need asking. A Senator had asked for him by name. Through the Chancellor's office. Twelve days after an old man in a Count's coat had put his fingers into the mind of that Jedi's Padawan and found something there that frightened him.

She had, she realised, stopped breathing, and made herself start.

Isolate the master. Hold him on the far side of the map with people who could not be reasoned with, and the boy was alone in the Temple with a thin bond and a tenth-day audience and nobody between him and the office with the red walls. Obi-Wan would not see it, because he had been given the shape of a thing he did well, and because he had already decided, for reasons of his own that she could not read, to be the one who went where the Chancellor's requests pointed rather than let the boy go. Plo thought it was routine. He had used the word, and she had sat beside him with her boots not touching the ground and said nothing, because she was ten.

The thread at the back of her mind was as faint as it had ever been. She could not tell whether he knew. She could not have told him down it if she tried; it was mood, not words, and she would not have dared put this mood into it with Masters on every floor.

So she went to bed and lay in it, and counted the breaths of nineteen sleeping children, and when the count was right she got up.

* * *

The east door of the Room of a Thousand Fountains was never locked, because nothing in the Temple was locked against its own, which had been, once, the whole trouble. She went along the dark gallery with her boots in her hand and counted the waterfalls by their sound. The third. Down the wet steps, along the ledge where the spray made the air cold enough to hurt, to the place where the moss was thicker than it should be, and under the moss the box.

She had written the flimsi on the bench with the maths in front of her, shifting each letter seven back as she went, so that if Master Sen had looked over her shoulder she would have seen a child's scrawl in an alphabet that did not quite make sense.

Unfolded, by the glow-stick, it was two lines.

*Oba Diah. Pykes. It's a trap. Who asked for him by name?*

*S.*

She put it in the box and the box under the moss and crouched there a moment with the water going past a hand's breadth away, loud enough to cover anything, loud enough that a man could die forty paces from here with the whole Temple awake and not be heard.

Then she went back along the ledge and into the dorm and lay down beside Kessa, who did not wake, and put her hand flat on her chest where the thread was, faint, faint, a boy's mood somewhere above her in the dark, and could not tell, and would not know until the next night and the next count whether the box would be empty or full.

She lay awake and waited for it, and the Temple, which was not on fire, went on being quiet around her.


## Chapter 21: Not This Time

The box was full. Anakin knew it by the weight of the lid before he had it open.

He crouched on the ledge behind the third waterfall with the spray coming through his tunic and read the two lines by a glow-stick held in his fist so that only a thumb's width of light showed. The alphabet was seven back. He had stopped needing to shift it after the second note; the cipher had begun to look like plain text and the plain text like cipher, which was a thing that happened to a man who had spent twenty-three years reading encrypted traffic in the dark.

*Oba Diah. Pykes. It's a trap. Who asked for him by name?*

*S.*

He read it twice. Then he held the glow-stick to the corner of the flimsi until it caught, let it burn down to his fingers, and dropped the last of it into the water.

She was quick. He had known since the evening before and had spent the whole of today trying to decide what could be put in a box for a ten-year-old, and she had worked it out from a clan-mother's gossip and a mission roster she had no business seeing, and had got to the right question before he had.

Who asked for him by name.

He did not know. That was the truth, and he disliked it. The request had come down through the Chancellor's office, as a third of the Council's missions did, stamped and courteous: a senator with constituents on a survey team, a mineral ridge on Oba Diah, eleven Republic citizens held by the Pyke Syndicate pending the settlement of a fee. The senator's name had not been on the flimsi Obi-Wan had left on the table, and Obi-Wan had not said it, and Anakin had not asked, because asking was a thing a boy did not do and he had been careful for five months to be a boy.

It did not matter whose name was on it. He knew whose hand.

He knew Oba Diah. Not from this life. He had sat in the cockpit of a shuttle with Obi-Wan beside him, both of them grown, both of them tired, the war two years old, and listened to his master tell it in the dry voice he kept for the things that frightened him: a Master called Sifo-Dyas, lost before the war; a message from the Rishi Maze; a Pyke who had kept a dead man's lightsaber in a box for twelve years because he was more afraid of the client than of the Jedi. And the client's name, which the Pyke had given up in the end, in that other life, with the client himself standing in the room. *Tyranus.*

So. The Pykes still had the saber. The Count still had the Pykes. And a Jedi Master was being sent to the one place in the galaxy where the Sith had a body buried, at the request of a Chancellor who had every reason to want that Master alone and far from Coruscant for ten days.

And Obi-Wan, if Obi-Wan was what Anakin had spent five months half-believing and half-refusing to believe, knew every word of it. Knew the Pyke's name. Knew what was in the box. And was packing anyway.

He had the stylus out and the flimsi on his knee before he had decided what to write.

*Don't know the name. Chancellor's office carried it; a senator with surveyors. I know the place. He told me about it once, somewhere else. If he is what we think, he knows it too. He's going anyway. Can't stop him without telling him how I know.*

He looked at it. A boy's hand, because he had made himself write like a boy for five months and now could not do otherwise without thinking about it. He added a line.

*Stay away from the hangar.*

*S.*

He put the flimsi in the box and the box under the moss and went back along the ledge, and in the dark gallery he stood a moment with the water loud behind him and the thread at the back of his mind as thin as he could hold it, and tried to feel what his master was feeling two floors above. He felt what he had felt for months: a door that was neither closed nor open, and nothing beyond it he could name.

* * *

He asked the next evening, because waiting had stopped being patience and become something else.

Obi-Wan was packing. He packed the way he did everything, with a list, and the list was on the table beside the tea, and the tea was going cold because he had been reading the list instead of drinking it. Two tunics, folded. The second comlink. The small medical kit he had carried since before Naboo. The common room was the size it had always been, two doors off it and a window on the city, and it had never felt small to Anakin until this body, which had grown four centimetres since the spring and did not yet know where to put its elbows.

He stood in the door of his room with his shoulder against the frame, because his hands wanted to do something and he had learned not to let them.

"Why did you look for Kamino in the Archives?"

Obi-Wan folded the second tunic. He did it properly, sleeves in, the way he had once spent a year trying to teach a nine-year-old to do it and given up. He did not look up.

"Who told you I did?"

"The logs."

A pause the length of a breath. Then Obi-Wan set the tunic in the case and sat down across the table from the cold tea and looked at him, and his face was the face Anakin had known for thirty-six years: courteous, attentive, giving nothing away and not pretending otherwise.

"Sit down, my friend. It is not a short story, and you have taken to looming."

He sat. The chair was too low for him now, or he was too long for it.

"Sifo-Dyas," Obi-Wan said. "You will not know the name. There is no reason you should. He was a Master, on the Council for a time, and a friend of Qui-Gon's from when they were both initiates. And a friend of Dooku's, which is the only reason I know anything about him at all." He turned the cup a quarter-turn on the table and left it. "I met the Count four times while Qui-Gon lived. On one of them, in the corridor outside the Archives, he asked Qui-Gon whether he had heard from Sifo-Dyas lately. Qui-Gon said no. The Count did not care for the answer. I was twenty-three and I thought it was nothing, the way one does."

"And after Naboo."

"After Naboo." Obi-Wan's mouth moved, not quite a smile. "You will remember I was not a great deal of use to anyone for a while. Somebody had to go through my master's effects, because the Archives wanted his notes and nobody else would do it. In a treatise on the living Force, folded into the pages where no one would ever look, there was a flimsi in Qui-Gon's hand. It said Sifo-Dyas had come to him with a dream he did not trust. A war. He had asked Qui-Gon whether a man should act on a vision, and Qui-Gon had written down the question and not the answer, which was very like him." He stopped. "Clipped to it was a transit docket. A freight house on Ord Mantell, passage for one, outbound toward the Rishi Maze. The Rishi Maze, Anakin. There is nothing out there. I assumed Sifo-Dyas had left it with him the way a man leaves a thing with a friend when he is going somewhere he does not expect to come back from."

"And there was a word on it."

"There was a word on it I did not know. A destination, or a company, or a captain's joke; the docket did not say. I went down to the Archives and put the word into a terminal, and the Archives had never heard of it. Not a system, not a station, not a ship. Nothing. I sat there for a long while thinking that could not be right, and then I thought: it is a misspelling, or a smuggler's name for a rock, and I have a Padawan who has set fire to the refectory." A quarter-turn of the cup. "Four days later it occurred to me to look up the man instead of the place. The Order's word for him was *lost*, which is what the Order says about a Master it would rather not discuss. I read eleven minutes of nothing and closed the terminal and let it go. I have not thought about it from that day to this." He looked at Anakin. "That is the story. It is a dull one. I am sorry it is not better."

It was not dull. That was the trouble with it.

Anakin sat with his too-long hands on his knees and took it apart the way he had taken apart intercepted transmissions on the bridge of a ship that did not yet exist, and every piece fitted. Kamino first, forty minutes, null: a man looking up a word. Sifo-Dyas four days later, eleven minutes: a man looking up a name, finding nothing, going away. The Count asking in a corridor; the Count *had* asked, somewhere in the other life Obi-Wan had told him that too, a passing thing, a corridor. Qui-Gon's habit of writing down the question and not the answer. Ord Mantell. The Rishi Maze. The refectory, which was true, and which Anakin had forgotten until this moment: the smell of burnt sugar and a Master's hand on the back of his neck. Even the way it was told was right: the dryness, the apology at the end, the thing Obi-Wan did with a cup when he was deciding how much of himself to spend.

He could not tell.

He had lived inside Sidious's mind for twenty-three years and could hear a lie in that voice from across a room. He had been lied to by Dooku, by Tarkin, by every governor in the Outer Rim, and had known it every time. He sat across a table from the man who had raised him and could not tell whether he had just been told the truth or the best lie he had ever heard, and understood with a cold, clear feeling under the breastbone that the two were not different from the inside. A man who had come back would tell exactly that story. So would a man who had not.

Obi-Wan watched him work it through and let him.

He did not ask how a Padawan came to have two-year-old Archive logs. He did not ask whose terminal, or whose account, or what the Chief Librarian would say. He did not ask the one question any Master in the Temple would have asked first, and the not-asking sat in the room between them like a third chair, and Anakin looked at it and knew that Obi-Wan knew he was looking.

"All right," Anakin said, and heard his own voice come out of a boy's chest, light, a little sullen, exactly right. "It was just strange. Seeing your name on it."

"I expect it was." Obi-Wan got up and went back to the case. "While you are sitting down. The Council has given me Oba Diah."

"I know. When do we leave?"

"I leave the day after tomorrow, on the morning courier. You stay."

Anakin did not move. His hands did not move. It took everything.

"Say that again."

"You stay. It is a negotiation with a crime syndicate over eleven surveyors and a fee the Republic will not admit to paying. A tenday there and back. A Padawan is not needed for a negotiation, and the Council agrees, and before you say it, yes, I asked them to." The medical kit went into the case. "You have the Drallig class on the fourth day and Master Vos-Kerrick's seminar, and you have the audience, which I am told the Chancellor would be very disappointed to miss."

"You've never left me."

"No."

"Six years. Carnelion. The *Ventris* thing. The time I had a fever and you carried me onto the ship because you said you'd rather have me sick in a corridor than well on Coruscant without you."

"I remember what I said."

"Then why?"

Obi-Wan closed the case. He did it gently, the latches one at a time, and stood with his hand flat on the lid and did not turn round, and the back of his neck above the collar was the back of a man of thirty-one, not grey yet, and Anakin looked at it and saw, as he had on the first morning and every morning since, how young it was.

"Because it is a negotiation," Obi-Wan said, "and you negotiate with your hands."

It was a good answer. It was the answer Obi-Wan would have given at thirty-one in the other life, dry and affectionate and final. It was not the reason, and they both knew it was not, and the reason stood in the room beside the third chair and neither of them looked at it.

Anakin could have said *it is a trap*. He could have said *the Pykes have a dead Master's saber in a box, and the man who paid for the death is a friend of yours, and you are walking into it with two tunics and a comlink*. Every sentence of it was true and not one of them could be said by a fifteen-year-old who had never left the Core without his master, and he sat there with the truth in his mouth like a stone and swallowed it, and across the room his master stood with his hand on a closed case and swallowed, Anakin was almost sure, the same stone.

"Fine," he said.

"Anakin."

"I said fine." He stood up, too fast, caught his hip on the table; the cold tea went over and ran across the list. Neither of them moved to stop it. "Ten days. I'll try not to burn anything down."

He went into his room and shut the door, which he had not done in anger since he was eleven, and stood with his back against it in the dark and breathed, and the breath came, as it always did now, without a machine, and he hated that he noticed.

* * *

The anger was old. He knew exactly how old.

He lay on the narrow bed with his boots on and let it come, because fighting it would spill, and a spill tonight would have Masters at the door. It came up through the body like heat through a deck plate, and it was not fifteen years old. It had a voice, and the voice was deep and slow and came through a grille.

*The Council left me behind.*

They had. A hundred times. They had put him on a Council seat and refused him the rank, and sent him to guard a senator while the war was decided elsewhere, and in the end they had sent Obi-Wan to Utapau and kept the boy at home like a dog they did not trust off the lead, and the boy had gone to the opera instead, and the Chancellor had been waiting, as the Chancellor was always waiting, with a story about a Sith lord who could save people from dying.

*Obi-Wan left me.*

He had. On a black bank above a river of fire, with three limbs gone and the rest of him burning, he had stood with Anakin's saber in his hand and said the thing about brothers and walked away, and the walking away had taken twenty-three years to stop hearing. Vader had hated him for it with a hatred that had an architecture, floors and stairs and rooms, and Anakin had lived in every room of it.

He lay on the bed and felt all of it, the whole building, go up in a chest that was not big enough to hold it. A forty-five-year-old grudge in a boy's ribs. His hands shook; he let them. He knew it was borrowed. He knew it was lifted whole out of another life and laid over this one like a stencil, that this Obi-Wan had never walked away from anything, that this Council had voted exactly as any Council would vote on a tenday's negotiation with a Padawan on the roster. He knew the Chancellor's office had carried the request precisely so that the master would be ten days gone when the tenth day came and the boy sat alone in the red room with the wide window.

He knew it, and felt it anyway, and the feeling did not care what he knew.

After a while, when his hands had stopped, he took out the datapad and opened the list in the cipher keyed to a serial number no one in this galaxy would ever stamp on anything, and under *Things that are different* he wrote, in a hand that was not a boy's because there was no one to see it:

*He is leaving me. He has never left me. He is doing it on purpose and he will not say why, and I cannot say why I know, and we sat in a room and lied to each other for love and both of us knew. If he is who I think he is, he is walking into Oba Diah with his eyes open. If he is not, I have let him go blind.*

*I do not know which is worse.*

He closed the pad. Through the wall, faint and steady, the bond held its door neither open nor shut, and after a long time he slept, shielded, and no child in the Temple woke.

* * *

The morning courier left from the north bay at the seventh hour, and the north bay at the seventh hour was Knight Sabo Thane's, the way a ship was its captain's.

Thane was a big, unhurried human with a hangar officer's eye and a hangar officer's manners, which meant he looked at every person who came through his doors as a potential fire. He had looked at Anakin that way since the speeder day and had never quite stopped. He was at the foot of the courier's ramp with a manifest when they came in, and he nodded to Obi-Wan and gave Anakin the look and went back to his manifest, and the maintenance crew around the courier went on doing what maintenance crews did, which was to be loud and slow and in the way.

The courier was a long-nosed Temple ship with Republic markings, a six-seater with a hold and no guns, the kind of thing that carried diplomats and letters and, once every three months or so, a Padawan's envelope to Tatooine. Its engines were already warm. The bay smelled of coolant and hot durasteel and the city coming in through the open doors, and the light out there was the flat grey light of a Coruscant morning before the traffic had properly woken.

Anakin had carried the case. He had insisted, in a boy's voice, with a boy's shrug, and Obi-Wan had let him, and the case was heavier than two tunics and a comlink and a medical kit had any right to be, and he did not ask what else was in it.

They stopped at the ramp.

He had thought all night about what he would say and had got nowhere. Everything true was unsayable and everything sayable was small. He stood on the deck plates in the coolant smell with his master's case in his hand and his Padawan braid against his neck and could not find a single sentence.

Obi-Wan found one for him.

"You have not asked me what I am carrying," he said. "And I have not asked you where you got the logs. I think we may call that even, for now." He took the case. He did not look at it; he looked at Anakin, and the look was the one from the first morning, the counting look, the one that went over a face as if checking it against a list. "Whatever you are hiding, I would still have you at my back. But not this time."

The deck did not move. Anakin felt it move.

*Not this time.*

He had heard the words before. He had heard them in a voice that was not Obi-Wan's, in a place that was not a hangar, through the ruined left lens of a mask with a crack running down it, so that the world came in halved and bright on one side and dark on the other, and on the bright side a woman's face, white-marked, blue-eyed, older than he had ever seen it and still so young. The temple going down around them in slabs. The Force screaming. Her voice saying she would not leave him. Saying it would not be like the last time; saying those three words, exactly those, as if she had chosen them to cut. And the thing he had answered her with, the sentence he would have given the rest of his hands to unsay, that he had said in a voice that was not his and had meant every word of.

*She had been wrong. She had left him. He had made her.*

The hangar came back. Coolant, grey light, Thane's manifest, a maintenance droid whining somewhere under the courier's belly. It had been less than a second. Obi-Wan was still looking at him, and if anything had crossed Anakin's face in that second, Obi-Wan's gave no sign of having seen it, which was itself a sign, or was nothing, and he could not tell, he could not tell, he would never be able to tell.

"I'm not hiding anything," he said. The lie came out in the boy's register, thin and sulky and perfect, and he hated it.

"Of course you are not." Obi-Wan put his free hand on the back of Anakin's neck, the way he had on the night of the refectory, the way he had on a hundred ramps, a hand on a boy's neck, warm, brief. "Ten days. Do your forms. Go to your seminar. Eat something that is not from a machine. If the Chancellor offers you a seat on the Council, decline politely and send me a message." A pause. "I will come back, my friend."

"You'd better," Anakin said, and did not know which of them had said it in which life.

Then the hand was gone and Obi-Wan was up the ramp with the case, and Thane was signing the manifest and stepping back, and the ramp came up with a hydraulic sigh that Anakin had heard ten thousand times and had never, until this body, heard with ears that were his own.

The courier lifted. It did it badly, the way Temple couriers always did, a lurch and a correction, somebody's Padawan at the controls who had learned on simulators. Anakin's hands twitched to fix it. It cleared the bay doors, found its lift, and went up into the grey, into the first thin stream of the morning lanes, a long-nosed shape turning north over the Senate District with its running lights still on, and then a smaller shape, and then one of a thousand.

He stood in the hangar and watched the place where it had been.

He had watched ships leave before. He had watched them from the bridge of a star destroyer with his hands behind his back; he had watched a freighter go up from a landing pad on a cloud city with a boy's blood on its ramp; he had watched the last transport off a burning Temple with a saber in his hand and no one left to use it on. He had never, in any life, stood on a hangar deck with two flesh hands and a braid behind his ear and watched Obi-Wan Kenobi go somewhere without him. It was the first time. It had taken forty-five years and two lives to arrive, and now that it was here it felt like nothing so much as being nine, on a landing platform, watching a tall man with a ponytail walk away towards a city with a boy's whole future in his pocket.

Sabo Thane cleared his throat, which was Thane for *you are standing in my hangar*.

Anakin did not move yet.

The maintenance crew had gone back to their noise. Someone was running a diagnostic on a shuttle two bays over, a steady electronic chirp. Someone had dropped a hydrospanner. And twenty metres off, at the edge of the crew's tool cart, with a flimsi permission slip in one small fist that she was holding up at an angle so that anyone who wanted to could read the crew chief's signature on it, a ten-year-old Togruta in a sand-coloured tunic with a brown belt and lekku barely past her shoulders was looking very carefully at a coil of fuel line, and not at him, and not at the sky where the ship had been, and not at him.

He had told her to stay away.

He stood with his boy's hands empty at his sides and the city coming in grey through the doors and did not look at her either.


## Chapter 22: Oba Diah

Oba Diah had no sky to speak of; it had a lid. Obi-Wan Kenobi watched it come up through the courier's forward port, a world the colour of wet slate under a cloud deck that never broke, and the spires rising out of it like something that had been poured and then left to set. They were not towers in any sense a Coruscanti would have recognised. They were too narrow and too many, black, with lights in them at irregular heights, and from the low approach they looked less built than grown, the way a mineral grows in a dark place where nobody has thought to stop it.

The pilot, a Temple courier who had said perhaps forty words in five days, said, "There's our beacon," and Obi-Wan said, "So there is," and went aft to get his cloak.

He had been calm for five days. Calm was a discipline and he was good at it; he had been good at it for a long time, longer than anyone aboard this ship could have guessed, and it had held through the jump out of Coruscant and the dull stretch past Ord Mantell and the last two days in which he had read the survey team's file four times and his own briefing twice and had not opened the case under his bunk, which was heavier than it ought to have been and which he had not needed to bring and had brought. Calm held. It held now, with the spires coming up and his hands steady on the clasp of the cloak. What it did not do was reach his body. His body had known since the Council chamber, since the words *mineral rights* and *Pyke* had been said in the same breath by Master Windu reading from a flimsi, and it had been telling him in the small ways bodies have (a jaw that would not quite loosen, a sleep that came late and left early) that this was a place he should not go.

He was going. Eleven people were in a room at the top of one of those spires, and the Council had agreed that Padawan Skywalker would remain at the Temple at his master's own request, and a senator from Tyrrhus whose name Obi-Wan had had to look up had asked for Master Kenobi by name, and all of that could be thought about later, in order, with tea. Now there was a landing pad coming up out of the dark, lit along its edges in a cold green, and a line of tall figures standing on it with their hands folded in front of them, and he had a job.

"Stay with the ship," he told the pilot. "If I am not back in six hours, you will not come looking for me. You will go up and make the jump and tell the Council exactly that."

The pilot looked at him. "Six hours."

"Six," said Obi-Wan, "is generous," and went down the ramp into the smell of the place, which was burnt and metallic and faintly sweet, like a refinery that also made candles.

* * *

The Pykes were tall. He had known that, and it was still a thing to stand in front of: a row of them a head and a half above him, narrow all the way up, their long skulls hooded and their faces hidden behind masks that were half breathing apparatus and half ornament, lensed, dark, with filigree worked into the metal at the temples as if someone had decided that if they must be hidden they would at least be hidden expensively. They did not fidget. Their hands were folded. When the one at the centre stepped forward, the others did not watch him do it; they watched Obi-Wan.

"Master Kenobi." The voice came through the mask flattened and precise, a courteous voice, a voice that had practised courtesy as other people practise the blade. "Lom Pyke. You are welcome on Oba Diah. The Syndicate regrets the circumstances of your visit and is honoured by the person of it. You will take some refreshment before we begin."

It was not a question and Obi-Wan did not treat it as one. He bowed, the exact depth that a Jedi gives a head of state he does not recognise as one, and said, "You are kind. I would rather see the survey team first, and take refreshment with them, if the Syndicate can spare it."

A pause. Very slight. The lenses did not change.

"Of course," said Lom Pyke. "They are comfortable. You will see that they are comfortable. This way."

The Pykes. Of course it would be the Pykes.

He did not let the thought go any further than that, because there was nowhere further for it to go that he could afford to visit, and because the courtesy was too perfect. That was the thing his body had been waiting to tell him. He had been received in a great many places by a great many people who did not want him there, and there was a texture to that, a slight roughness in the welcome where the resentment showed through, and here there was none. The welcome was smooth all the way down. It had been rehearsed by someone who had been told what a Jedi expected and had decided to give him exactly that, and the only people who did that were people who already knew how the afternoon would end.

He walked beside Lom Pyke into the spire with his hands in his sleeves and his face pleasant and made the only decision there was to make, which was to go on.

* * *

They had put the survey team in a room near the top. He counted the levels in the lift by the way the pressure changed in his ears and made it forty or more; the door opened on a chamber that was round and high and ringed, two storeys up, by a gantry of black metal from which cargo lines hung down into the empty air like roots. There were windows, narrow and tall, and through them the other spires stood in their cloud, and the light that came in was the colour of an old bruise. Down on the floor, which was broad and bare and had drains in it, somebody had set out eleven chairs, a long table, water, bread, fruit in a bowl. It looked like the setting for a meeting that had not been allowed to begin.

Eleven people were in the room. Ten of them were sitting, in the way of people who have been told to sit, and one was standing, a woman in a surveyor's field jacket with the Republic's seal half torn off the shoulder and iron-coloured hair pulled back hard from a lined face, and she looked at him as he came in and did not look relieved. That was to her credit. He thought he would have liked her under other circumstances.

"Deshan Orr," she said, before he could speak. "You're the Jedi."

"Obi-Wan Kenobi. I am sorry it has taken this long."

"Nineteen days." She said it flat. "Nobody's been hurt. They've fed us. They took our comms and our instruments and Varl's inhaler, and when I asked for the inhaler back they gave it back. I want that on your record. They've been polite." Her eyes went past him to the tall shapes at the door and came back. "I don't know what the polite is for."

"Nor do I, entirely," said Obi-Wan, which was true as far as it went. "Tell me about the ridge."

She told him, in the precise bored voice of a professional who has told the same story to too many people who did not listen. A mineral ridge forty kilometres east, surveyed under a Republic warrant for a consortium on Tyrrhus; a dispute over a boundary drawn forty years ago on a chart that nobody could now produce; a Pyke claim to a seam that ran under the Republic concession and came up on the other side of it; her team taken off the ridge at dawn by very tall people with very good manners and brought here. "It's a land dispute," she said. "That's all it is. I've seen a dozen. Nobody takes eleven hostages over a land dispute."

"No," said Obi-Wan, "they don't," and he put his hand on the back of the nearest chair and looked, for one second, at the gantry over her head, and then he turned to Lom Pyke and said, with every appearance of a man who had just arrived at a reasonable position, "Shall we begin?"

* * *

He had been called many things, and some of them he had deserved, and one of them he had earned in rooms like this one. He had never thought much of it as a gift. It was a craft, like the blade, and he had learned it the way he had learned the blade, from a master who was better at it than he would ever be, by watching and by being thrown on his back, and the whole of it came down to a few things that could not be taught to anyone who was not already listening. You did not argue. You found what the other man wanted and what he was afraid of and you set them side by side on the table and let him look. You said less than he did. You let him win in front of his people, because a man who has been seen to win can afford to give something away. And you never once let him see that you knew the game was rigged, because the moment he knew you knew, the game ended, and the people in the room with you were no longer worth anything to anyone.

So he sat down at the long table across from Lom Pyke, with the eleven behind him and the tall masked guards along the wall, and he began.

He began with the Pykes' grievance, not the Republic's. He had read the forty-year-old boundary question on the way out and he laid it out now from the Syndicate's side, better than Lom Pyke's own people had evidently laid it out to Lom Pyke, with the dates and the chart and the name of the Republic surveyor who had drawn the line and the name of the mining house on Tyrrhus that had paid him, and he watched the lenses go very still as the Pyke understood that the Jedi had come to do his work for him. "Your claim is not frivolous," Obi-Wan said. "I would go so far as to say it is better documented than theirs. Which is why I am puzzled, and I say this with respect, that the Syndicate has chosen to make its case in a form that no tribunal in the Republic can hear."

"A tribunal." Lom Pyke let the word sit. "The Republic has no tribunal for the Rim. The Republic has a Senate, and the Senate has a great many friends on Tyrrhus."

"It has fewer than it had last year," said Obi-Wan. "There is a bill. Three senators, two of them young and one of them stubborn, and it has survived its second reading, which no one expected. Outer Rim franchise disclosure, and a tribunal with standing to hear exactly this. If it passes, and I think it will, the first cases it hears will set the manner of all the others. The Syndicate could be one of those cases. It could be seen to be." He paused. "Or it could be the reason the bill's opponents point to when they say the Rim cannot be trusted with a tribunal. I confess that if I were the mining house on Tyrrhus, I would want very much for you to keep these people another month."

It was a good stroke and he saw it land, not in the mask, which gave nothing, but in the guards along the wall, two of whom looked at each other. He did not follow it up. He let the silence do that. He poured himself water from the jug on the table and drank some and set the cup down.

"You propose," said Lom Pyke at last, "that we release the team and trust the Senate."

"I propose that you release the team into my custody, and that I carry the Syndicate's claim to Coruscant under my own seal and put it before the committee in person, and that I say there, which will be true, that the Pykes treated Republic citizens with a courtesy the Republic has not always shown the Pykes. You lose nothing you have. You gain a witness nobody can buy." He let a little dryness in. "I am told I am tiresomely difficult to buy."

Somebody behind him, one of the eleven, made a sound that was nearly a laugh and swallowed it.

Lom Pyke inclined his long head, a gesture that in another species would have been a smile and here was something more careful. "You have a reputation, Master Kenobi."

"It precedes me," Obi-Wan agreed. "I have never been able to make it wait."

They went on. It took most of an hour. The Pyke wanted the survey instruments and their data; Obi-Wan gave him the data and kept the instruments, and gave him the data again in a form he could show his own people. The Pyke wanted a written undertaking that the ridge would not be worked until the boundary was heard; Obi-Wan wrote it, on the Syndicate's own flimsi, in his own hand, and signed it with the full form of his name, and the Pyke read it twice. The Pyke wanted the team to leave by the Syndicate's own transport, under escort, as a courtesy. Obi-Wan declined the courtesy so gently that it was a minute before anybody understood he had declined it, and then he offered another in its place: the escort could walk them to the Temple courier, and he would say so in his report, with names.

And all the while his body went on telling him. It was the same message it had been sending since the Council chamber, only louder here, under this lid of a sky, in this round room with its drains in the floor and its cargo lines hanging from the gantry; it was in the way the guards did not shift their weight, and in the fruit in the bowl that nobody had touched in nineteen days, and in the way Lom Pyke gave ground, when he gave it, at exactly the speed a man gives ground when it costs him nothing because the ground is not where the fight will be. There had been something in this room once. He did not know how he knew that, either. It had been here and it had been taken away quite recently, and the room still had the shape of it.

He negotiated beautifully. He knew it while he did it, as a man knows when the blade is moving well, and he took no pleasure in it whatsoever, because he was negotiating for the floor of a trap and the only question left was whether he could get eleven people across it before it closed.

It was while he was writing the second undertaking, the one about the escort, that he looked up at the gantry.

* * *

A man was standing on it.

He had not been there when they came in. He had not come by the lift. He stood at the rail two storeys up with the cargo lines hanging past him and the bruised light from the windows on his shoulders, and he was in armour: Mandalorian, a full suit of it, plates of silver-blue over a dark flight suit, a jetpack on his back with the nozzles cold, twin holsters at the hips and the pistols in them. The helmet was the old shape, the T of the visor black and blank. He had his hands on the rail. He was not doing anything. He was watching the room below with the stillness of a man who has been paid to watch and would not have moved if the spire had caught fire, and just under the left edge of the visor, where the helmet's optics sat, a small lens turned with a soft mechanical patience and a light beside it burned a steady red.

He was recording.

Obi-Wan knew him.

That was the whole of it, and it was impossible, and it went through him like cold water. He knew the armour. He knew the way the man held his shoulders, slightly forward, as if he were always about to step off something. He knew the helmet, not as a type but as an object, the way one knows a face, with a scratch along the left cheek-plate that he could not see from here and was sure of. He had never in his life stood in a room with this man; he would have sworn to it before the Council with his hand on his own saber. And a name came up in him, whole, two short syllables and then one, and he did not think it. He put it down. He put it down the way he had learned to put things down, under the calm, in the place where the case under his bunk was, and the queries he had made in the Archives two years ago, and the Tano child's eyes across a dojo; and he finished writing the undertaking and signed it and slid it across the table, and he said to Lom Pyke, "You have a guest."

"An observer," said Lom Pyke. "A courtesy to a client. He will not interfere."

"No," said Obi-Wan. "I don't suppose he will."

He stood up. It was the first unplanned thing he had done since the ramp. He stood and turned his back on the Pyke, which was a breach, and looked at Deshan Orr, who had sat at the end of the table for an hour with her hands flat in front of her and her eyes on his face, and he said, quietly and clearly, "When I say, you will take them to the lift. Not before. Not the door. The lift."

She did not ask what he meant. She said, "Yes."

He turned round again. And it was in the turning, with his back to the eleven and his face to the Pyke, that he heard the doors on the far side of the round room begin to open: not the lift, which was behind him, but the wide doors the cargo lines had been for, the doors he had seen when he came in and placed in his mind and hoped very much not to think about again. They slid apart on the sound of a motor that had wanted oil for some time. Beyond them, in a double rank, with their long heads bowed forward on their thin necks and their rifles held across their narrow bodies, stood battle droids.

Trade Federation stock. He saw it in the same instant he saw them, the tan plating and the backpack transmitters and, on the nearest, the stencilled hull-code of a Federation landing craft that nobody had troubled to scrub off; and he noted it, with the part of him that was always noting, as a thing to be thought about later: that somebody had sold the Pykes an army, and the Pykes did not buy armies. The Pykes bought people. Somebody had given them this.

"Roger roger," said the first droid, in the flat bright voice he had last heard a long time ago and, it seemed to him, in another life, and raised its rifle.

"Now," said Obi-Wan, and his blade was in his hand.

* * *

Soresu is not a form for winning.

Qui-Gon had told him that, when he first turned to it, with the mild contempt of a man who fought to end things. It is a form for staying alive, his master had said, and for keeping other people alive, and it will never give you anything but that; and Obi-Wan had said, Then it will give me everything I need, and had been, he thought, in all the years since, more right than he knew.

He did not go to meet the droids. He went to the place between the droids and the lift and stopped there, and the first volley came in across the width of the round room and he took it.

The blade moved. That was all he could have said about it afterwards; the blade moved and he stood behind it and let it. The form was a circle, close to the body, economical, a wall built one stroke at a time out of nothing but the next stroke, and he put the wall between the eleven and the fire and held it. Bolts came in red and went out red, into the ceiling, into the floor, into the droids themselves, which went down with the small clatter they made, and more stepped forward over them. The Pyke guards along the wall had unfolded their hands and there were rifles in them. He had known there would be. They fired from the flank, from two sides, high, and he turned the circle to take that too, and behind him he heard Orr's voice, hard and flat and wonderfully loud, counting her people into the lift by name.

He held.

The thing about Soresu that Qui-Gon had never liked was that it had no end built into it. Other forms were a sentence with a full stop; this one was a sentence that went on, and the only way it finished was when the other side ran out of things to say. The droids did not run out. They came through the cargo doors in their double rank and he cut them down at the edge of the circle and they kept coming, and the Pykes kept firing from the walls, patient, unhurried, not trying very hard, and he understood (as he had understood everything today, late, and with his body before his mind) that they were not trying to kill him. They were trying to keep him here. The eleven were in the lift now, nearly all of them. He heard the doors try to close and heard someone hold them, and he heard Orr say a name, Varl, and say it again, and he moved, four steps back and to the left, to put the blade between the lift doors and the one droid that had found an angle on them, and that was the moment the circle opened.

He felt the gap before he saw it. His left side, low, the one place the blade was not, because the blade was where the lift was. A Pyke on the wall fired into it.

The bolt went through him under the ribs. He felt it as heat first, not pain, a heat so complete it was almost clean, and then the pain came in behind it and took his breath and his footing in the same instant, and he went to one knee on the bare floor with his blade up and found that he could not feel his left hand. And under the pain, far off, along the thin bond he had stopped expecting anything of, there came a first faint tug, as of a thread touched in another room, and was gone.

The lift doors closed. He heard them. Eleven; he had counted them in by Orr's voice and he had the number. That was done. That was the thing that was done.

He got up. He did not know afterwards how. There was blood on the floor between the drains, dark on the black, and the droids were coming on, and he went backwards, up, because up was where the stair to the gantry was, and the gantry ran round the room above the fire, and from the gantry a man could get to the windows, and the windows were narrow and tall and gave on to the cloud and the dark. He was thinking very clearly. He had never thought more clearly. He went up the stair with his right hand holding the blade and his left, as the feeling crept back into it, pressed to his side, and the blade still moved, slower, taking what came, and he came out on to the black metal of the gantry with the cargo lines hanging past him and found, with no surprise at all, the man in the silver-blue armour standing at the rail ten metres along, watching him, his hands at his sides, the small red light under his visor burning steadily.

He did not fight. He had not been paid to. Obi-Wan looked at him across the ten metres, with the fire coming up from below and the shouting of Pykes and the clatter of droids, and there was nothing to say to him that would not have been a confession, and so he said nothing.

The helmet did not move.

A bolt took him in the shoulder from below. He felt the gantry rail against the small of his back. He felt the blade go out, not switched off but gone, out of fingers that had stopped answering, and he heard it fall and strike the floor of the round room far down with a small bright sound. And then the rail was not against his back any more, because he had gone over it, and he was in the air with the cargo lines going past him, and the floor was coming up.

It came up slowly. He had time.

He had time to think of a desert, of all things, a desert he had stood at the edge of once for three days and knew in every grain, and a farm at the edge of it, white domes sunk in the sand, and a ridge above the farm where a man could sit with the wind in his beard and watch a boy walk out to the vaporators at dusk, and never once go down. He had time to see the boy's face turn up. He had time to know he had been wrong about something, wrong for a long time, wrong about the only thing that mattered, and that it was too late, as it had always been too late, to say so to anyone who could hear it.

He said it anyway. He said it out loud, with the floor coming up and the dark coming in at the edges, to a boy who was not in the room, who was in a hangar on Coruscant with his hands empty at his sides, who was fifteen years old and had never in this life been given a reason to be told.

"I'm sorry, Anakin. For all of it."

Then the floor, though something took the worst of the speed out of it at the last, so that only a rib went.

Then nothing.

And in the nothing, where there should have been nothing at all, there was the bond. It was thin. It had been thin for months, a thread where there had been a rope, a door held one hand's width ajar by someone who would not say why; it had been so thin he had stopped expecting anything to come down it but the faint weather of a boy's moods. It was there now, in the dark, a single filament running out from him towards somewhere he could not see. And someone at the other end of it had taken hold.

They were pulling. They were pulling on it as if they could haul him up it hand over hand, as if it were a line and he were a man gone over a rail, and they were pulling hard enough to hurt.


## Chapter 23: The Theft

The bond tore open while Anakin Skywalker had a seven-year-old's wrist in his hand.

It was the sixth morning since the north-bay courier had gone, and Drallig had Bear Clan in the small hall, fourteen of them in sand-coloured tunics with training sabers too long for their arms, and Anakin was walking the line the way he had walked it every tenday for months, correcting grips.

Two days ago, at the tenth-day audience, with Obi-Wan's place empty beside him, the Chancellor had pressed so gently: how hard it must be for a boy, alone in the Temple with his master gone, and how readily the Council leaves him behind when it has weightier things to weigh. Anakin had given him what he wanted to see, a boy's worry, a sleeve twisted in the fingers, and kept the wall up behind it, every course of it. He had walked out under the old man's kind regard and had not quite stopped feeling it since.

The child in front of him was a Nautolan girl with her thumb wrapped over her fingers. He had just turned her hand so the thumb lay along the hilt, and said *there, like that*, in the voice he used for children, when something went into his left side below the ribs, one hard blow of pain, and the hall went white.

He was on one knee. He did not remember going down. The girl was looking at him with her saber still up in the corrected grip, and the floor of the hall was very close and very cold, and the pain was not his. He knew that at once, the way one knows a voice in the next room. It had come down the bond, the thin held-ajar thread he had kept for five months between himself and the man who had left on the courier, like a blade down a wire, and behind it the presence at the other end had gone out.

Not gone. Going. A lamp with the fuel running low.

*No.*

He took hold of it. There was nothing to take hold of; a bond is a direction one's attention leans. He leaned the whole weight of what he was, every year of it, the years nobody in the hall could see, down the thread towards the dark it ran into, and he pulled. He pulled the way one hauls a rope with a man overboard on the end of it. He felt the thread take the weight and hold, and he felt, far off, something at the other end answer, faint and bewildered, as a man answers who is being hauled up out of sleep by the collar.

"Skywalker."

Drallig. Boots beside him. The battlemaster's shadow across the mat, and fourteen children gone absolutely silent, which children of seven never are.

"Cramp," Anakin said. His voice came out a boy's: high, cracked at the edge. Good. "Sorry, Master. Stood up too fast. I'm—" He put a hand flat on the mat and pushed and got up, and the hall tilted and righted. The Nautolan girl had lowered her saber. "I'm fine."

Drallig looked at him: at his face, which Anakin could feel had gone the colour of the mat, and at his left hand, which had gone to his own side under the ribs and was pressed there as if to keep something in.

*A hall of water and green, a tall man with grey in his hair turning on a path with his blade already lit, the look on his face not fear but recognition, and the stroke that took him—*

"Go to the Halls," Drallig said. "If you are going to be sick, do it there."

"Yes, Master."

He walked. He did not run; running would be remembered. He walked out of the small hall past the rack of training sabers and past the Temple Guard at the gallery stair, whose mask turned to follow him, and into the corridor, and some minutes after the first the pain came again, a second pulse, higher, in the left shoulder, and with it the long drop of a body going down onto stone, and he put his back to the wall and stood there with the thread in his hands, pulling, pulling, because the presence had dimmed again and he could not tell any more whether he was holding it or only holding the place it had been.

* * *

She found him through the other bond first.

It was so faint he almost did not feel it: a touch at the back of the mind, the thinnest line they had made together nineteen nights after the *Crucible* came home and kept at a whisper since, because a Padawan bonded to a youngling was a thing the Council would see from across the Temple. It did not carry words. It carried her, for a second: a small hard attention, like a hand laid flat on his chest, and a direction. The fountains. Then it was gone, or she had let it go, because even now she would not risk it being seen.

He went down. He went down the service stair and through the east door of the Room of a Thousand Fountains, counting waterfalls because he could not think and the count was something to do, one, two, and behind the third there was the ledge, and the moss, and the box under the moss that held their notes, and standing on the ledge with her boots in the spray was a girl of ten in a sand-coloured tunic with her lekku dark with wet.

She had come from class. She had the chalk of the mechanics room on her sleeve. She was small; she was always smaller than he expected; she had to look up at him and she did, with her blue eyes gone very steady, and he saw that she had felt it too, not the pain, the pain was his, but what the pain had done to him, and that she had already understood it.

"He's hit," Anakin said. "Side and shoulder. He fell. He's—I can feel him, he's there, but he's going, Ahsoka, he's—"

"Go," she said.

A child's voice. A child's pitch, a child's size, the water coming off the ledge behind her in a sheet; and in the word, under it, the thing he had heard in that voice on a hundred bridges and in the Kalevala sky over Mandalore, the flat certainty of a commander who has looked at the board and sees one move. She was not asking him what he thought. She had been thinking since the moment she felt him go down, and she had finished.

"I can't get a ship."

"Yes, you can." She stepped off the ledge. The spray had made her markings stand out white on white. "I'll get you the ship. You get him. Walk with me and don't talk unless I talk to you first. You're a Padawan who's been sent for. That's your whole face. Can you do that?"

"Yes."

"Then do it."

She went first. He went after her, a boy following a youngling through the green, with his master dying at the end of a thread in his chest and a ten-year-old girl laying out the sequence in a murmur ahead of him as they walked: the guard, the hangar officer, the log; what she would say and what he would not; where he would stand.

He had seen her do this before. He had seen her do it with a battalion.

* * *

The Temple Guard at the hangar level stood where it always stood, at the turn of the corridor where the lifts let out, with its pike grounded. Anakin had never once seen it move, and it moved now, a quarter-turn, because a youngling of ten was out of class on a hangar corridor at midmorning with a Padawan behind her.

"Initiate."

"Master Plo sent me, Guard." She did not stop walking; she slowed, which was better. "I was in mechanics. He came to the door and sent me to find Padawan Skywalker and bring him to the north bay, because Master Kenobi's courier's still out and he wants the other one checked before it's rostered. I'm meant to go straight back after." A breath, a child's breath, slightly too fast. "He said to say it was his word if anyone asked."

The mask considered her. It considered Anakin, who stood with his hands at his sides and his face arranged into the face of a boy who had been sent for and did not know why and was slightly anxious about it, which was not difficult, because his left side was on fire and the thread in his chest had gone quiet for the last forty paces.

"Go on, then."

"Thank you, Guard."

They went on. He waited until the turn.

"Plo will say he didn't."

"Plo will say he didn't in about four hours, when someone thinks to ask him, and by then you're gone and it's my problem, and I've been a problem since I was five. Don't look at the guard. Look at the floor. You're a Padawan who's been sent for."

Knight Sabo Thane was in the north bay with a datapad and a crew chief and the second courier, the sister of the one that had taken Obi-Wan, a Temple shuttle with the port repulsor cowling off and a droid under it. Thane was a big quiet man with a scar through one eyebrow who had lent a fifteen-year-old his own two-seater and asked no questions, and Anakin found now that he could not look at him.

Ahsoka could.

"Knight Thane. Master Plo sent me down." The straight face. It was not a blank face; a blank face is a lie on its own. It was the face of a child delivering a message she only half understood and was proud to have been given. "He wants the courier flown before it's rostered, because of the coil, and he wants Padawan Skywalker to fly it, because he's rated on the type and the knights on the roster are all in Council this morning. He says it's on the log already. He says he's sorry it's short notice."

Thane looked at the datapad. Anakin, looking at the floor, saw the pause, and in the pause he saw what she had done, because he had done it himself a thousand times in another body: the log had already been sliced. Somewhere between the mechanics room and the fountains she had put her small hands on a crew chief's terminal she had been visiting for a tenday on a permission slip, and entered a maintenance flight, port repulsor test, post-repair, pilot to be assigned, under the crew chief's own code, dated this morning. The lie fitted the record because the record had been built to fit the lie. She had not come to him until the paper was done.

"Huh," said Thane. "He didn't comm me."

"He's in Council, Knight Thane."

"He is." Thane scratched the scar with the stylus. "Skywalker. You rated on this?"

"Yes, Master." The boy's register. "Master Kenobi had me do the type conversion last year."

Which was true, as it happened. She had chosen a lie with a true floor under it.

"Six hours," Thane said. "Up through the Temple corridor, out to the test box over the Works, run the coil through its range, bring it back. No lanes. No stunts. If Plo Koon wants it done today he can have it done today, but if that coil throws a fault I want it on the pad in ten minutes, not in Monument Plaza. Clear?"

"Yes, Master."

"Chief, button it up."
