The crew chief went to put the cowling on. Thane went to sign the pad. Ahsoka stood with her hands folded in front of her the way initiates were taught to stand before a knight, looking up, small and wet-lekku'd and ten, and Anakin stood behind her looking at the floor of the north bay and understood two things, one after the other.

She was better at this than he remembered. He had taught her to lie to Separatist commanders over a comm and she had been good. But this had not come from him. This had come from seven years of being a child in a building full of people trained to feel a lie from across a room, from crying on schedule and losing on purpose and asking a healer whether a dream could be a memory and learning from the healer's face never to ask again. She had made herself an instrument for exactly this, in a body of ten, and the instrument was flawless.

And it was horrible to watch. That was the second thing. A child of ten standing in front of a decent man and lying to him with a face that would never be caught, and doing it for him, because she had been a child for seven years in order to be ready for this and this was what ready looked like.

He wanted to put his hand on her head. He did not.

"Padawan." Thane held out the pad. "Thumb."

He gave it his thumb.

"Go on, Initiate. Back to class. Tell Master Plo it's done."

"Yes, Knight Thane."

She went. She did not look back at Anakin, because a youngling would not; and down the faint line she had made between them, as she crossed the bay, there came one thing, hard and brief, the shape of a hand on his chest, pushing. Then the line was gone, and she was through the door, and he was climbing into a courier shuttle under the eyes of a man he had just robbed.

* * *

The seat was too big.

It had been set for a knight: the back a hand's breadth too far from the yoke, the pan a hand's breadth too low for a boy's narrow backside to reach the pedals square. He pulled it forward on the rail with a jerk that made the crew chief look up, and sat, and the harness came down over his shoulders set for a chest that was not his, and he cinched it, and it was still loose, and he cinched it again. His hands on the yoke were a boy's hands. Thin wrists; the right one with the knob of bone that had not finished growing. He looked at them for a half-second as if they belonged to someone else, which they did, and then he stopped looking, because the hands knew what they were doing and the boy did not need to.

The courier came up off the pad and out through the Temple's air corridor into the morning.

He flew it the way the other hands flew. On speeder day he had flown Thane's two-seater like a talented child, with too much of everything, and it had been a joy. This was the thing under the joy. The shuttle went up through the Temple corridor at exactly the speed the corridor allowed, and at the top, where the lanes began and Thane's test box lay off to the east over the Works, it did not turn east. It went up, through the gaps between the lanes, through three stacked levels of morning traffic in a line so clean that nobody saw it until it was gone, and out of the top of the city into the dark with the harness biting his shoulders and the thread in his chest so faint he had to hold his breath to feel it.

The navicomputer asked where he was going.

He told it: Oba Diah, Kessel sector. It began to chew. It would take ninety seconds; it was a Temple courier with a Temple courier's brain. He did not have ninety seconds. He had the route in his head already, because he had flown it, not in this body, not from this end, but he had flown it, the Kessel approaches and the Pyke lanes and the dust-shoals off Oba Diah's third moon, in a ship with a black hull and a reason to go to Pyke space that he did not let himself look at now; and the solution was there the way a word is there, and he put it in by hand, the exit vector and the mass-shadow margins and the three corrections, and the navicomputer was still chewing when he pulled the lever. The Temple's filed route to Oba Diah took a courier five days, because it ran the cleared lanes, past every checkpoint and customs beacon where a Jedi ship was expected to be seen and logged; his took hours, because it ran through the gaps nobody had filed, where nobody flying meant to be seen at all.

The stars went to lines. The lines went to the blue tunnel. The courier settled into the long hum.

Then there was nothing to do.

He sat in the too-big seat with his hands on a yoke that had nothing to steer, and in his chest was the thread, and it was so thin. It had been thin for months because he had made it thin, because closed would have been a confession and open a worse one. Now it was all there was. He held it. Sometimes it pulsed, faint, a man's presence turning over in the dark, and sometimes for a minute or five it did not, and in those minutes he sat absolutely still, not breathing, and the terror was simple and total and had one shape: that it would stop. That he would feel it stop, in a borrowed ship with a boy's hands on the controls, and know, and still have four hours to fly.

He had been the other thing.

That was what came, in the fourth hour. He had come down out of the dark onto a world whose name he could not now remember and found a Jedi bleeding in a stairwell, a woman who had held off three Inquisitors for a day and a night and had nothing left, and he had stood over her with the respirator going and done what he had come to do. He had been the dark at the end of someone's thread, on a hundred worlds, for twenty-three years, and every one of those someones had had a Padawan somewhere, or a master, or a child, who felt it go.

He sat with that. It did not resolve anything.

Then he made the vow, and he made it in a man's voice in his head, without ceremony, because it was not a thing that needed ceremony. *Nobody has him. Not the Pykes. Not the Count. Not the old man. Not the Council, afterwards, with their questions. Not me. Whatever I was, I am going to be it for this, and then we will see.*

The thread pulsed. He let out his breath.

* * *

Oba Diah came up out of the tunnel black.

The spires of the Pyke city stood up out of the night in a cluster on their mesa like a handful of needles pushed through cloth, lit along their edges in a cold violet that did not reach the ground, and the courier came down through the upper air with its lights off and its transponder saying what Ahsoka had made it say, a maintenance flight, a nothing, and nobody challenges a nothing. He found the tower from the bond. The spires were a hundred and the tower was one of them, but the thread ran to it as a plumb-line runs to the ground, and he brought the shuttle down on the pad at the tower's foot with the thread going straight up through the floor of the cockpit into the dark above him.

The pad was empty. There was no Temple ship on it. The six hours had run out, then, and the pilot had done as he was told; Anakin filed that and did not look at it.

He undid the harness. He stood up out of the too-big seat and his legs were a boy's legs and they shook once, and then they stopped shaking, because the other thing had come up through them and taken them, and he went down the ramp with his saber in his right hand, unlit, and walked across the pad to the door.

There were two Pykes on the door.

They were taller than him by a head and a half, narrow bodies and small heads inside breathing masks and long grey hands on long rifles, and they had time to see a boy in a Jedi tunic crossing the pad, and one of them had time to raise the rifle. The blade came on. He did not deflect the shot. He went under it and through the man with one stroke, upward, no turn of the wrist, no second movement; and the second Pyke had got his rifle up and the stroke that took the rifle took the hands with it, and the Pyke went down screaming behind his mask and Anakin stepped over him and put his left hand on the door, and the door, which was locked and barred and ten centimetres of plasteel, went inward off its track and down the corridor beyond like a sheet of flimsi in a wind.

The cargo lift at the end of the corridor went up the inside of the tower through forty levels with a sound like a long breath, and he stood in it with the blade down at his side and the thread going up and up and then, at the fortieth level, sideways.

The doors opened on the round room.

He had it in a glance, because the other thing took rooms in a glance: the drains in the floor; the narrow windows; the gantry running round the wall at two storeys with its cargo lines hanging; the stair up to it; six droids on the gantry with their heads swinging round at the sound of the lift, Trade Federation skeletons with their hull-codes unscrubbed, plain as a manifest, the same numbers Obi-Wan must have read from this gantry; four Pykes on the floor with rifles; one more Pyke standing apart by the far window in a better coat, not Lom, Lom was not in the room, Lom had gone the way a man goes when he has paid for a thing and does not want to watch it done. And blood. Blood on the floor under the gantry in a smear that went from the centre of the room across the drains to the foot of the stair, and under the stair, in the wedge of dark where the treads met the floor, a shape.

The thread went to the shape. It pulsed, once.

He went across the room.

He did not go round. There was a Pyke between him and the stair, and the Pyke fired, twice, and the blade took the first bolt back into the Pyke's chest and the second into the window behind him and he did not slow down, because there was no need to slow down, because the stroke that came next was already in the arm. It was a single stroke, overhand, one-handed, the whole weight of the shoulder behind it and nothing in the wrist: a heavy, falling, economical thing with no beauty in it. It had been made in a body that could not turn fast and did not need to, and it came out of a boy's narrow frame as if the frame did not matter, and the Pyke's rifle and the Pyke's arm and a good deal of the Pyke went to the floor in pieces.

The droids opened up from the gantry.

He walked through it. He took the stair in four strides and came up onto the gantry among them and did not spin or turn or do anything a Padawan would have done; he went down the line of them the way a man goes down a row of something that needs cutting, and each stroke was the same stroke, down, through, and each droid came apart at the waist with its top half talking and its bottom half standing, and the top halves went over the rail one after another onto the floor of the round room.

Below him a Pyke had got to the foot of the stair with a rifle, between him and the shape in the dark.

Anakin looked at him.

He did not raise his hand. His left hand was at his side where it had been, with the fingers curled a little, as a man's fingers curl when he is holding something that is not there. The Pyke's rifle came up and then stopped coming up. The Pyke's long throat, inside the collar of the mask, drew in. His boots left the floor. He went up, a hand's breadth, two, with his heels kicking and the rifle falling out of his hands and clattering down into the drain, and the small grey hands went to the collar and found nothing there to pull at; and Anakin stood on the gantry and felt, under the fingers of a hand that was not there, the long cartilage of a Pyke's windpipe and the narrow bones either side of it, and knew exactly how much more it would take, and did not take it. He let go when the rifle was in the drain. The Pyke fell and lay with his legs moving and his breath coming in a sound that was wrong, and did not get up, and Anakin had already stopped looking at him.

The two who were left broke. One got to the lift. The other ran at him, which was brave and pointless, and Anakin put him into the curved wall of the room with a gesture so small that it was less a gesture than a glance, and the Pyke hit the wall at the height of a man's head and came down it and lay still.

Then the room was quiet, and in the quiet he looked up.

There was a gallery above the gantry. It was not a gallery, it was a maintenance walk, a strip of grating under the roof where the cargo lines ran to their drums, and standing on it with one hand on a line and the other hand loose at his side was a man in Mandalorian armour, silver-blue, with the T of the visor pointed straight down at him. He was not armed. That is, his pistols were in their holsters; he had not drawn; he had not moved while six droids and five Pykes came apart under him. On the side of the helmet above the ear a small light burned red.

Recording.

Anakin knew the helmet, by a route that did not pass through thought: the rain on Kamino running off that silver-blue; the arena at Geonosis; a boy with the same face holding that helmet in both hands and looking into it. And the helmet knew something; the visor did not move, but the man's weight shifted on the grating, the slight settling of someone who has just revised a price.

He could reach him. Even up there. He could feel the line the man's hand was on and the drum it ran to and the way the grating hung from the roof.

Under the stair the thread pulsed, and was fainter than it had been.

He let him go. He turned his back on Jango Fett, which was a thing he would not have done in any other body, and went down the stair, and behind him the jetpack lit with a cough and a roar and the roof vent banged and the roar went up and away and was gone, taking the red light with it into the Oba Diah night. He did not look. He was already on his knees in the blood under the stair.

* * *

Obi-Wan was on his right side with his back to the treads and his knees drawn up, the way a man lies who has crawled somewhere and has nothing left for arranging himself. The robe was gone. The tunic was black with blood from the left armpit to the belt. There was a burn through the left shoulder from front to back that had cauterised itself the way blaster wounds do, and a second one lower, under the ribs on the left side, that had not, that had gone through muscle and out and was bleeding, slow and dark, into the drain.

Anakin put two fingers to his throat.

There. Thready, fast, a pulse like something trying to get out. Alive. The eyes were half open and did not focus; the beard was wet; the lips moved and made nothing. Alive.

"I've got you," Anakin said. He heard his own voice say it and it was not a boy's voice and he did not care. "I've got you. Don't move. Don't help."

On the floor three metres away, in a smear of its own, was a lightsaber. He knew the hilt. He had seen it last in another life, lit, on the bank of a lava river, in the hand of a man who had just taken his legs; he reached, and it came across the floor to his hand, and he clipped it to his belt beside his own without looking at it, and put his hands under Obi-Wan's shoulder and hip to lift him.

He could not lift him.

He had known he could not, and had not let himself know it, and now it was simply a fact: he was fifteen, and narrow, and the man under the stair was thirty-one and heavy with muscle and heavier with blood, and when Anakin got his arms under him and heaved, the man came up a hand's width and his own knees went out and they both went down into the drain, and Obi-Wan made a sound that was the worst sound he had heard in this life or the other one.

"All right," Anakin said. "All right. The other way, then."

He did it the other way.

He had lifted men before with the thing he was, by the throat and by the chest and off the floors of ships, to break them, and he had never once lifted a man to carry him, and he found that it was the same and not the same. He knelt in the blood with his hands open over the body under the stair, and the Force came up through him and under Obi-Wan the way water comes up under a boat, and the body rose. It rose flat, as it lay, on its side with its knees up, a hand's breadth off the floor, then two, with the blood running off the tunic hem and pattering onto the drain grille beneath. He stood. He kept one hand on Obi-Wan's chest, not for the Force, the Force did not need his hand, but because he needed to feel the chest go up and down. He walked backwards. He walked backwards across the round room with his master floating beside him at the height of his own hip like a boat on a tow-line, and at the lift door Obi-Wan's boots struck the frame and swung, and Anakin turned him with a thought, the way one turns a sheet of flimsi over, and brought him in.

It was absurd. He knew it was absurd. A boy with a braid behind his ear, walking backwards into a cargo lift with his left hand out flat, and a bearded man in a soaked tunic hanging in the air beside him with his head lolling and his blood dripping onto the lift floor at intervals, tap, tap. In any other life it would have been the thing one tells afterwards. He did not think about any other life at all.

On the lower level the loading bay doors were open and there were people in it. Deshan Orr had all eleven of her surveyors packed into a cargo lift that had jammed on a crate, its door stuck a hand's breadth short of open, and a Pyke's rifle in her hands that she had nowhere to point. Anakin freed it in passing, the crate out of the track and the door off its stop with a glance, and did not break step; he said, "The Pyke's ship's on the lower pad; take it; go now," and Orr looked at the boy and at the man in the air beside him and lowered the rifle and said nothing at all, which was the right thing to say. Eleven faces saw a boy and a floating man, and nothing else in the world.

* * *

The courier's medkit was under the co-pilot's seat where Temple couriers kept them. He had it open before Obi-Wan was down.

He put him on the deck behind the seats because there was nowhere else, on his right side as he had lain under the stair, with the lifted leg dropping last and the whole weight of him settling onto the plating with a sound Anakin felt in his own back. Then his hands went to work, and he watched them.

They were a boy's hands. Small; the right wrist with its knob of bone. They did not shake. They cut the tunic from hem to armpit with the kit's shears and peeled it back off the wound under the ribs and looked, and what they knew, they knew from a place the boy had never been. He knew what a blaster bolt did going through a body because maintenance droids had explained it to him, in detail, in a chamber with the air hissing, about his own; he knew the smell of cauterised muscle, which was the smell of the chamber; he knew that a wound that bled slow and dark would stop with pressure and one that bled bright would not; that the shoulder was the lesser wound because the bolt had sealed it, and that the side was the one that would kill, not from the hole but from the slow draining, a man emptying himself an hour at a time into a drain on a Pyke tower. The hands tore the bacta patch and laid it over the exit wound and pressed. They laid the second over the entry. They put the field dressing round the whole of the ribs, over both, and pulled it tight enough that Obi-Wan's breath caught, and tied it, and put the heel of one hand flat on the dressing and leaned on it with a boy's slight weight, and held.

He looked up from the hands and Obi-Wan was looking at him.

The eyes had come back. Not far; they were the eyes of a man a long way under water looking up at the light. But they were on him, on his face, and they had been on him for some time; and Anakin understood, with the heel of his hand on his master's ribs, that they had been open in the round room. That the man under the stair had seen the stair, and the gantry, and the stroke, and the Pyke go up by the throat without a hand on him. That he had seen all of it. That he was seeing it now.

Obi-Wan's lips moved. Nothing came.

"Don't," Anakin said. "Don't talk. We're going."

He went forward and did not sit; there was no time for the seat. He stood at the yoke with one knee on the pan and took the courier up off the pad with the ramp still closing and the Pykes' violet spires going past the ports and the thread no longer a thread but a man on the deck three metres behind him, breathing, and he put the exit vector in by hand again, Coruscant, the Temple, the Halls, and the stars went to lines and the lines went to blue, and he let go of the yoke and went back and got down on his knees on the deck.

The dressing was holding. The dark had stopped coming through it. He put his hand on it anyway, flat, and the chest under it went up, and down, and up, and the eyes stayed on him, and he did not think about anything; he was not thinking; it came out of him in a man's voice, the one he used when he was alone, low and quiet and worn, without any decision at all.

"Stay with me, Ben."


# Part Four: Three



## Chapter 24: Ben

The word was still in the cabin when Obi-Wan came up.

He came up slowly, the way a man comes up out of bacta: the body first, reporting from a long way off that it was cold, that it was lying on a metal deck, that there was a weight on its ribs and a wrongness under the weight where something had gone in and something had gone out; then the eyes, which had been open for some time without him; then the hearing, which brought him the drone of a courier's drive in hyperspace and a boy's breathing and nothing else. And then the word, which had not gone anywhere. It had been said, in a man's voice, a low worn voice that did not belong in that throat, and it had stayed where it was said, about a metre above his face, and he lay under it and let it do what it did.

No one alive knew that name.

He had been so careful. That was the thing he found himself thinking, with a Pyke's blaster bolt through his side and his Padawan's hand flat on the dressing: *I was so careful.* Four years. He had never once let it into the bond. He had never written it, not even in the second column of the night-pad, the one for things not written. He had chosen it himself, on a ridge, in a wind, with a dead man's child a kilometre below him walking out to a vaporator with a hydrospanner too big for his hands, and he had kept it the way a man keeps the one thing he has left, and he had taken it with him into a corridor on a battle station and let it go there with everything else. Ben. The name was a hut with a door that stuck. It was a bantha-wool blanket and a kettle and the Jundland Wastes going blue at evening. It was nineteen years.

*The hut. Sand in the seams of everything. A Tusken's cry across the ridges at night, and the krayt-dragon roar he had learnt to make to send the scavengers off. The ridge above the Lars farm, a shelf of rock with a view of the domes and the vaporators, and the boy, five, seven, ten, fifteen, walking out at dawn with Owen's hat on and Owen's walk, Shmi's eyes, Anakin's hands. Owen, who had met him at the edge of the farm with a blaster rifle and told him to keep away, and had been right to. The Inquisitors, in their black and their masks, asking at Anchorhead and Mos Eisley for a man with a beard; and he had let the beard go grey and his name go to Ben and the saber stay in a chest under the floor. The girl, come out of a droid in blue light with her mother's mouth and her father's spine: Help me, Obi-Wan Kenobi. Nine years before that, a dark world, a thing in black with its helmet cracked across one eye and the eye in the crack his Padawan's eye, and the voice saying what it said, and himself saying what he said back, and leaving. The corridor. The red blade and the black mask and the breathing like a bellows in a forge. His own blade coming up to guard, and the boy at the far end of the hangar by the ship, and the choice, which was not a choice: the blade raised and held still and let fall. The heat across the throat. The light.*

*And then waking. A bed. A ceiling. The Temple's hum. A body with no ache in it, hands with no spots on them, a beard that came off brown on his fingers when he put his hand to it. Twenty-seven. And through the wall, in the second small room, asleep, eleven years old and sprawled across the cot like a thing dropped, with the thin braid across his face and his mouth open, the boy. His boy. Still his.*

He had got up that first morning and made tea, because he could not think of anything else to do with his hands, and when the boy had come out rubbing his eyes and said *Master, you're up early*, he had said *Yes* and poured him a cup, and he had not said anything else for four years.

Four years. The lists. The letters to a woman on Tatooine, carried by a courier, argued for in front of a Council that had looked at him as though he had suggested the boy keep a pet. The Chancellor: more than a hundred audiences, as many afternoons in Monument Plaza, as many times watching the boy come out of that red room with his face a little smoother than it went in, and never once a way to stop it that did not announce what he knew. The Archives at night, and Kamino, which was not there. The night-pad with its two columns. The list of the dead, which he kept in a place the boy would never look, and which had forty-one names on it now and the places, and which began with a Temple and ended with a corridor.

And none of that was what the word meant. He lay under it and understood that. The word did not mean that someone had found him out. The word meant that someone else had been there.

He looked up.

The boy was looking down. Fifteen. Sandy hair cut short, the braid behind the right ear, no scar, a face that had not finished yet. A boy's hand, narrow, two flesh hands, one of them flat on the dressing over Obi-Wan's ribs with the heel pressed in. The eyes were not a boy's. He had known that for five months. He had known it the night the bond went opaque, like a door closing, and had not let himself think the word, and had thought it anyway every day since in the place under thinking where he kept the other things. He thought it now.

He had not used his own voice in four years. He had used Master Kenobi's, which was dry and courteous and said *my friend*; he had built it the way he had built everything, carefully, out of what he remembered. It did not come. What came was older and quieter and had lived in a hut.

"Where did you hear it?"

The boy did not pretend not to understand him. That was the first mercy, and Obi-Wan would think later that it was the one that had undone him: that there was no pause for the lie. The hand on the dressing did not move. The boy's face did something that a boy's face is not built to do, a thing he had seen it do once across a bank of lava and never since.

"From my son," Anakin said.

* * *

The drive hummed. The blue went by the ports.

Obi-Wan found that he had closed his eyes, and opened them.

My son. There was only one person in the galaxy that could mean, and he had watched that person walk out to a vaporator at dawn for nineteen years, and the person had called him Ben, and had not known there was anything else to call him; had sat in the hut with the saber in his hands and said *My father didn't fight in the wars*, and he, Obi-Wan, had said what he said and had been ashamed of it for the rest of his life, which was three days. Luke. Luke had said the name, and the thing with the cracked helmet had heard it, and the thing with the cracked helmet was kneeling on the deck of a Temple courier with its hand on his ribs, and had a boy's hands, and had said *my son* as though it were allowed.

"Luke," he said.

"Yes."

"He said my name to you."

"He said it on Bespin." The boy's voice went somewhere and came back. "He was hanging from the underside of the city, and he said it. I heard it through the Force. I'd taken his hand off a minute before."

Obi-Wan tried to sit up.

He got as far as the elbow. The dressing pulled and the side went white and the cabin tipped, and the boy's other hand came round the back of his shoulders and held him there, not up, not down, just there, the way you hold a thing that has decided to fall and is still deciding; and he hung in it with the sweat coming out on his forehead and his breath going short, and he said, through it, "Say that again."

"I'll say all of it," Anakin said. "You have to lie down."

"I will not lie down."

"Then you'll fall down. There's a bulkhead. I'm going to put you against it. Don't help."

He was put against it. It took the boy some time, because the boy was slight and Obi-Wan was not, and because Obi-Wan helped. When it was done he was sitting with his back to the aft bulkhead and his legs out along the deck and his left arm in a sling made of a seat harness, and the boy was crouched in front of him with his elbows on his knees looking at the dressing and not at his face, and Obi-Wan could see, from this angle, the yoke, and the navcomp, and the blue, and the whole small cabin that there were only two of them in.

"How long," he said.

"Eleven hours. Ten now. The safe lane, not mine; I'm not racing a hole in you."

"And you're going to tell me."

"I'm going to fly. I've set it, but she's old and she wanders, and I'm not going to drop you out in the middle of a lane." Anakin got up. He was a head shorter than the bulkhead door. He stood in it a moment. "And I'm going to tell you. You've been asking for five months. You just never used the words."

"No," said Obi-Wan. "Neither did you."

* * *

He flew it from the pilot's seat with his back to Obi-Wan, and he could not keep his back to him; that was what Obi-Wan saw first and went on seeing, as the thing was told. The boy would say a sentence to the forward port and then turn round in the seat, the whole narrow body coming round with one arm over the back of it, to see what the sentence had done; and then turn back and fly; and then turn round again. A man in his forties turning in a pilot's seat with a fifteen-year-old's lack of economy. Obi-Wan had watched him do it in a speeder at twelve. He had never thought he would see it again and be glad.

"You know what I was."

"I know what you were."

"Say it. I want to know you can."

"Vader," Obi-Wan said. His side hurt. "Darth Vader. The Emperor's — " He stopped. "I was going to say *hand*. I don't know that I ever found a word for it. I had nineteen years and I never found the word."

"There isn't one." The boy turned back to the port. "Twenty-three years. I know you know most of it. The Temple. The Purge. The Inquisitors; I ran them. Kanan — Caleb; I don't know if you knew him, he was seven when — no. Not now. I'll tell you what you don't know, and you'll tell me, and we'll see where we come out."

"Agreed."

"You were on the ridge."

"I was on the ridge."

"Then you know about the Death Star, because you died on it." The boy's hands were steady on the yoke. "You don't know he blew it up. Within the day. He flew a snubfighter down a trench with me behind him, and I had him dead in the sights, and something hit my wing and I spun out, and he put a torpedo down a port the width of this cabin and the thing went up with a million people on it, and I thought: *the Force is strong in this one*, and I didn't know whose he was. I hadn't thought about Tatooine in nineteen years. I'd had it burnt out of me." He turned round. "I went back there, after. I found the hut, the chest under the floor, the blanket. I found the name on a water-ration docket at Anchorhead: *Ben Kenobi*. I stood in your house."

Obi-Wan said nothing. The hut had had a stuck door. He could not for his life remember whether he had closed it.

"I found out what he was three years later, from the Emperor, who'd had it from the records of a birth at Polis Massa. He let me find it in him and watched me find it. Then I went and got the boy." He turned back. "Bespin," he said, and went no further: it was a thing he would not describe, and Obi-Wan did not ask him to.

Obi-Wan found that he had put his good hand over his mouth. He took it away.

"Go on."

"A year. Then the second station, over Endor. He came to me. Walked into my hands on a forest moon and let me take him up to the Emperor, because he thought there was good in me, because he'd got it into his head from somewhere." The boy's voice was quite flat. "Sidious put us in a room and made us fight. He wanted the boy to kill me and take the suit. He nearly had it. Luke took my hand off, the right one, the one that was already a machine, and stood over me with the blade up, and looked at it. At the hand. At the wires. And he threw the saber away and said he was a Jedi, like his father before him." Turn, port, turn. "So Sidious did what he did to people who disappointed him. Lightning. I'd seen it. I'd felt it, once or twice, when he was teaching. He put it into the boy and the boy went down and screamed and he kept putting it in, and I stood there. I stood there, Obi-Wan. I stood and watched him burn my son and I did the sums. How long it would take. Whether I could still use the arm. I stood and did sums."

"And then."

"And then I picked him up. Sidious. I picked him up by the middle like a sack of grain, and the lightning went into the suit, into the whole of the suit, and I walked him to the edge of the shaft and put him over. He screamed all the way down. I didn't know a thing could scream that long."

The courier wandered. The boy corrected it with one hand without looking.

"The suit was gone. The lightning had done it. I had about ten minutes, I thought; it was less. Luke got me as far as a landing ramp. There was a ship burning out over the moon, the whole fleet was coming apart, and I asked him to take the mask off." He said this to the port. "He said I'd die. I said nothing could stop that now. And he took it off. He had to undo the clamps at the back; I couldn't lift my arms. And then it was off and there was air on my face, real air, for the first time in twenty-three years, and it hurt, it hurt like — and I looked at him. With my eyes. And he was — " The boy stopped. He stopped flying. The yoke sat in his hands and he did not correct the wander. "He looked like her. I'd never seen it. I'd fought him twice and I'd never seen him. I said some things. I told him to tell his sister something."

Obi-Wan thought he could guess what he had said. He did not know how he could guess. He had heard the boy say something like it once, to a dead man's image in a dark room, when he had thought no one was listening, and he had not let himself know what he had heard; it came back now and sat down beside the word *Ben* and would not go.

"You told him he was right," Obi-Wan said. "About his father. That there was something in you to be right about."

"I'm not going to say it again. I said it once. Once was enough." The boy's shoulders went down. "He said I'd be with him. I was. And then I wasn't. There was a light, and it was — it wasn't bad. I'd thought it would be bad." He turned round again, all the way, and looked at Obi-Wan with those eyes in that face, and said, in the boy's own register, as if it had slipped out of him: "I thought you'd be there. In it. I thought you'd be there and you weren't, and then I woke up in my bed with my own hands, and you were in the next room making tea."

* * *

It was Obi-Wan's turn.

He had thought, when the word came, that he would find it hard. He found that he had been telling it for four years to no one, in the second column, in the dark, and that it was all there and in order and only wanted a listener, and that the listener had to be the one person who had the right not to hear it.

"You know I was on the ridge," he said. "You don't know what it was."

"Tell me what it was."

"It was nineteen years." His side ached. He found a way to sit that let him breathe. "I took him to Owen and Beru when he was three days old and Owen took him from my hands and told me to go. I went. I found a hut in the Wastes that a hermit had died in, and I buried the hermit, and I kept his name off the door and put mine on it, the one you heard. I had nothing to do, Anakin. Do you understand that? I had been Obi-Wan Kenobi, High Council, General, I had held a system in one hand and a treaty in the other, and I had nothing to do. So I went up the ridge every morning. There was a shelf of rock. You could see the domes and the vaporators and the sand-crawler road. And I watched your son learn to walk."

The boy had turned round. He did not turn back.

"I watched him learn to walk and to fall and to stand at a vaporator with a hydrospanner in both hands because one was not enough. I watched Owen teach him to drive the landspeeder. I watched him fight with Owen in the yard at nine and at twelve and at fifteen, the same fight, you and Qui-Gon's fight, *I want to go and you cannot go*. I watched Beru put a loaf on a rock at the edge of the farm once a tenday and never once look up at where I was. Nineteen years she did that. I never thanked her. There was no way to thank her that did not put the boy at risk, and I had decided, you see, that nothing was worth that."

"The Inquisitors came."

"Twice. Once early on, and once in the tenth year, the hunt that came nearest to finding me. They asked in Mos Eisley and in Anchorhead for a man with a beard, and I let the beard go white and I let my name go to Ben, and I put the saber in a chest under the floor and did not take it out for a decade. I bought water from a Jawa and I fixed vaporators for farmers who did not like me and I made a noise at night that Tuskens were afraid of. That is what your master did with his late thirties, his forties, his fifties, Anakin, the second time round. He hid from you."

"I know. I ran them. I'd have — " The boy stopped. "I'd have killed you. If they'd found you. I'd have come myself."

"Yes." Obi-Wan looked at him. "That is what I believed you were. I want to tell you that now, so that we do not have to come back to it. I believed that the thing I left on that bank had got up, and that nothing of you was in it. I had evidence. I had Padmé's face in the medcentre. I had a Temple with the children in it. I had a dark world, ten years in, where I went to get Leia out of a cell, and the thing in black was waiting for me, and its helmet had a crack across the eye, and the eye in the crack was yours. I said your name to it. I said, *Anakin*, and it said, *Anakin is gone. I am what remains.* And I said, *Then my friend is truly dead. Goodbye, Darth.* And I went. I took her and I went and I did not look back, because I had looked back once and it had cost me what it cost me." He drew a breath that hurt. "And then nine years later I walked into a hangar on a moon-sized gun and you were at the end of the corridor, and I put the blade up, and I thought, *the boy is at the ship, he is nearly aboard*, and I let it fall. I let you have it. Not because I had forgiven you. Because the boy was nearly aboard, and the one thing I had learnt in nineteen years was what I would spend to buy him three seconds."

The boy's hands had left the yoke. They were in his lap, two flesh hands, turned up.

"You said something. Before. When the blade came down. I've thought about it for — I've thought about it."

"I said what I believed. I believed it." Obi-Wan held his eye. "I was wrong. You have just told me I was wrong. I am not going to pretend that is easy."

"No."

"Good. Then we understand each other."

The blue went by. The courier drifted and the boy did not correct it, and after a moment Obi-Wan said, "The ship," and the boy turned and put her back on the line and said, over his shoulder, "The four years."

"Yes."

"Why you didn't tell anyone. I've had it from the other side, you know. I've had five months of watching you not tell anyone."

"I watched the Council fail," Obi-Wan said. "That is the whole of it. I watched them fail at Naboo and at Geonosis and at Kamino and at the end, and I watched them fail you most particularly, because they never once in thirteen years decided to trust you, and you knew it, and a man who is not trusted goes where he is. I woke up with four years' lead and a boy of eleven in the next room, and I asked myself what a Council that knew would do with that boy, and the answer was: watch him. Like a bomb. And a watched boy becomes the bomb. So I told no one. I told Yoda nothing. I told Qui-Gon, if he was listening, and he was not. I decided, on the first morning, over the tea, that the only thing I had was you, and that I would raise you differently, and that the differences would have to be small, because large ones would be noticed."

"The letters."

"The letters. You were nine and you wrote to your mother every tenday in your head, I could feel you doing it, and the Order's answer was that attachment is forbidden. So I went to the Council and argued that a wound in the open heals, and they let me have it because they thought it a harmless eccentricity in a new master, and your mother wrote back, and you stopped dreaming about her four nights in five. I counted."

"The Chancellor."

"I have never once got you out of that room." Obi-Wan's voice was flat. "More than a hundred times. I stood in Monument Plaza more than a hundred times and could not think of a single reason to give the Council that did not begin with *the Chancellor is a Sith Lord*, and I had no proof, and I will not have any for four years, and in four years it will be a war. So I waited for you in the plaza and bought you a sweet on the way home and watched the thing he had done to your face wear off, and I did that for four years. If you are looking for the worst of it, that was the worst of it."

"The Archives. Kamino."

"A hopeless thing, done because I could not do the other. I searched for a planet that had been cut from the records by the man who cut it, because I thought if I could find the clones, I could — I don't know what I thought. Stop them. Warn them. Buy them." He closed his eyes briefly. "I know they turn. I know they turn on an order and I do not know how an order makes a man do that to a man he has fought beside for three years, and I have thought about it for twenty, and I still do not. I put it down on the list under *not understood*."

"The list," the boy said. He was turned round again. "You have a list."

"I have a list." Obi-Wan did not say where. "Every Jedi who dies and where. Forty-one so far; it is not finished; I write them as they come back to me. It begins with a Temple."

"I know what it begins with."

"I know you do."

"Is my name on it?"

"No," said Obi-Wan, and found that this was true, and that he had never once asked himself why. "No. You're not on it. I don't know why you're not on it."

* * *

Then there was a long time when neither of them said anything, and the courier drove on through the blue, and Obi-Wan sat against the bulkhead with the sling across his chest and listened to his own blood going round, slow and insufficient, and watched a fifteen-year-old fly a ship with his back to him. He had thought there would be more. There was more; there was twenty years of more; but the shape of it had been said, and what was left was the one thing under the shape, the thing they had never said to each other, because one of them had been in a mask and the other on a ridge and there had been no occasion.

He knew the boy was thinking of it, because the boy had stopped turning round.

"Anakin."

"I'm here."

"We have ten hours. I would rather say it now than carry it in."

The boy's hands came off the yoke. He set the course lock. He turned the seat all the way round, so that he was facing aft with his back to the stars, a boy in a pilot's chair with his feet not quite flat on the deck, and he looked at Obi-Wan down the length of the small cabin, and for a moment his face was the face on the bank, the one that had been burning.

"You left me to burn."

The drive hummed. Obi-Wan did not look away. He had not looked away on the bank either; that was a thing he could say for himself, and he had said it, in the night, in the second column, more than once. He had watched. He had watched it take the hair and the face and then he had picked up the saber and gone.

"Yes."

The boy's hands closed. Opened.

"I hated you for twenty years."

It had come across the lava. It had come across the lava in a voice that was already going, and he had heard it with the saber in his hand and Padmé on the ground behind him, and he had carried it up the ridge every morning for nineteen years and set it on the rock beside him and looked at the domes. He did not need the boy to tell him. He told him anyway, because the boy had the right to hear it heard.

"I know. I heard you."

A long silence. The blue went by. Somewhere in the aft bulkhead a coolant line ticked as it cooled, and the sound was very loud.

"I'm sorry," Anakin said.

Obi-Wan had thought, in the hut, on the ridge, in the corridor, that if the boy ever said it he would know what to do with it. He had rehearsed what he would do. He found that he had nothing. There was a thing he had said to him across the river, with the lava going by and the boy's face coming apart, and it was true, and it was the truest thing he had ever said, and he did not say it now; it was not a thing that could be said twice; the boy had heard it. What he had was two words, and he gave them.

"So am I."

That was all. He had thought there would be more. There was not more. The boy sat in the chair and he sat against the bulkhead and nothing in the cabin had changed: the wound was where it was; the Temple had still burnt; the children; Padmé; the ridge; the corridor; Luke hanging under a city calling a name. None of it was mended. He did not know that any of it could be. But the thing had been said, both halves of it, in the right order, with the right silence between, and he felt, sitting there with his side on fire and his Padawan's eyes on him, that something had been set down on a table between them that had been carried, separately, in two directions, for a very long time; and that neither of them was going to pick it up again alone.

The boy turned the chair back to the port.

"There's someone else," he said, after a while, to the stars. "At the Temple. You need to meet them. Not now. When you can stand."

"Someone else," Obi-Wan said, and the Tano child's blue eyes came up in him out of nowhere and he put them down again. "I see. Yes. I rather thought there might be."

"You're not going to ask."

"I have ten hours and one lung, my friend. I am going to ask you everything. I am simply going to do it in order."

The boy laughed. It was a boy's laugh, high and surprised, and it stopped at once, as if he had heard it and not known whose it was.

* * *

Coruscant came up at the end of it the way it always did, a hard white line and then the whole glittering disc, the lanes thick as veins, the Temple's five spires a dark shape against the dawn side; and the courier dropped out of the blue with a lurch that went through Obi-Wan's side like a second bolt, and he made a sound, and the boy was out of the chair and across the cabin and on his knees with his hand on the dressing before the stars had finished becoming stars.

"It's holding."

"I know it is holding. I can feel it holding. Fly the ship."

"She's on approach. Temple beacon. Thane's crew chief will have us down in nine minutes." The boy did not get up. "They'll want to know."

"Yes."

"How I got there. Who sent me. Where the ship came from. Why you've got a Pyke bolt through you on a routine escort and eleven surveyors telling anyone who'll listen about a torn door and the dead on the pad and a boy walking out with a man floating beside him. And a Mandalorian saw all of it, Obi-Wan. He had a helmet on. They record."

"I saw him."

"Then you know where that goes."

"I know where it goes." Obi-Wan looked past the boy's shoulder at the Temple coming up in the port, the Processional Way, the gate, the thousand windows with the children behind them, and he thought of the Council in its circle, and of Plo Koon's long mask, and of Master Yoda who said little and the right thing, and of all that he had kept from them for four years, and of how much easier it had been to keep it when he had been the only one keeping it.

"We will tell them the truth," he said, "as far as it goes. The Pykes held the surveyors. The Mandalorian was theirs. You came after me because I opened a private channel and summoned you when the negotiation turned, within two hours of my landing, and if the Council asks why I did not call them instead we shall have to see what can be done about his memory. The droids were old. I was lucky. You were a boy who did a brave and foolish thing, and I will be properly cross about it in front of them." He shifted, and the side bit, and he let it. "Everything else, we will lie about. The name. The fight. What you are. What I am. The son. Each of us has been lying to that Council alone for years, Anakin, and done it rather well, and now there are two of us, and the lies will have to match."

The boy's hand was still on the dressing. The Temple filled the port. Below them the hangar doors were opening, and there would be a hangar officer at the foot of the ramp with a face like a slapped tally-sheet, and a stretcher, and healers, and questions, and behind the healers, somewhere, small, in a sand-coloured tunic, a child with blue eyes who had sent this boy a ship.

"Then we had better," said Anakin, "agree on what we did with the six hours."


## Chapter 25: Three

The hangar officer was waiting at the foot of the ramp with a stretcher, two healers, and the face Obi-Wan had promised, and Anakin Skywalker came down behind the stretcher with his master's lightsaber still clipped beside his own and found that of everything he had braced for in the last nine minutes, the thing he had not braced for was being fifteen in front of a man he had robbed.

Sabo Thane said nothing. He looked at the boy, then at Obi-Wan going past on the stretcher with the dressing on his side gone the colour of weak tea, and put away whatever he had been going to say. Men like Thane kept things.

The Halls of Healing smelled of bacta and warm stone. Vokara Che met them at the inner door with her lekku bound back and her sleeves already rolled, and she did not greet anyone; she put two fingers on Obi-Wan's throat and two on the dressing and said, to the room, "Tank. Now. Full immersion."

"After the Council," said Obi-Wan from the stretcher.

"The Council can come here."

"The Council does not come anywhere, Vokara, that is rather the point of them." He had his eyes shut. His face had gone the grey of old mortar under the beard, and he was speaking in the voice he used for customs officials and the Chancellor's aides, light and courteous and immovable. "A patch. Something for the pain that will leave me able to count. Three hours. Then you may pickle me for as long as you like."

Che looked at him for a long moment, and then she looked at Anakin, standing by the door with his hands at his sides and his master's saber on his hip, and Anakin felt the look go over him the way her hands had gone through his dream months ago, slow and professional, finding the shape of a thing without needing its name.

"Days," she said. "You understand me. The bolt took part of the lung and the shoulder is held together with my goodwill. You will be in that tank for days, Master Kenobi, and you will be weak for a month after, and if you stand in front of the Council for three hours this morning you are doing it on stubbornness and a stim and I will note that in your file."

"Note it," said Obi-Wan. "I have a very full file."

* * *

The High Council Chamber had not changed. Anakin had known that it would not, and he had still walked in through the tall doors expecting something: a mark on the floor, a scorch on the sill. There was nothing. Round room, twelve chairs, the sky all the way around through the tall windows, Coruscant in its morning haze, and the light lying across the floor in the same long bars it had lain in when he had come through those doors with the 501st at his back and found eight children behind the chairs.

He did not look at the chairs. He stood at the centre with his hands behind him and his feet together and his shoulders in the shape a Padawan's shoulders were supposed to make, and he let his Force presence down into the floor as far as it would go, and he waited to be asked questions.

Not all the chairs were filled. Yoda was there, small in the big chair, his hands folded over the head of the stick. Windu was there. Plo Koon was there with his mask catching the light. Ki-Adi-Mundi, Shaak Ti, two others by hologram, flickering. Obi-Wan had been given a seat, which was unheard of; Yoda had said, "Sit, you will," in a tone that was not a kindness, and Obi-Wan had sat, upright, arm strapped across his chest, and had somehow made it look like a courtesy he was extending to the Council.

"Four matters," said Windu. He did not stand. He had a datapad on his knee and did not look at it. "A courier taken from the north bay without authorisation. A Padawan absent from the Temple on a mission this Council did not assign. A Mandalorian in full armour present at what was reported to us as a routine escort. A Pyke comm intercept, out of Oba Diah, in which someone is telling anyone with a comm that a boy walked through a droid garrison like a door." He let that sit. "I'd like to take them in order."

"Then I will save the Council time," said Obi-Wan, "by taking them all at once. The order was mine."

It was very quiet in the chamber. Anakin kept his eyes on a point on the floor a metre in front of Master Windu's boots and listened to his master lie.

He had never heard it before. In two lives he had heard Obi-Wan deflect, evade, understate, and tell the truth in ways that left the listener believing the opposite; he had never heard him simply say a false thing to the Council's face and go on saying it. The voice did not change. The grey face and the strapped arm did the rest.

"The negotiation turned on the second day. Lom Pyke had decided that the surveyors were worth more as a lever than as a fee, and that I was worth more as a corpse than as an envoy, and the droids on the gantry were not his, Masters, they were Federation landing-craft stock with their hull-codes unscrubbed, which I will come to. I had the courier pilot under a six-hour order and the six hours were nearly gone. I opened a private channel to my Padawan."

"On what equipment," said Windu.

"My own. A comm I keep for the purpose, which I will surrender to the Council if it wishes to see it. I told him where I was and what I expected to happen, and I told him to take whatever ship he could get and to come, and that I would answer for the ship. He told the Temple Guard and Knight Thane that Master Plo had sent him; I used Master Plo's name on the channel without his leave, and I shall answer to Master Plo for it. He did as he was told. It is a thing he does more often than his reputation suggests." The ghost of the eyebrow. "I was shot before he arrived. I have a fairly clear memory of the floor, and a less clear one of the boy, and I am told I owe him my life. The Mandalorian was the Pykes' observer, there as a courtesy to their client, and he left when the odds turned. The surveyors are alive because my Padawan freed them and told them to take the Pyke ship, which was sensible. If the Council has an objection to the order I gave, the objection is to me."

"A Padawan on a comm order from a wounded master," said Windu, "does not, in my experience, clear a garrison of droids."

"Then your experience of my Padawan is incomplete, Master Windu," said Obi-Wan, "which I would say is the general condition. The droids were old. Their codes were a decade stale, their targeting was poor, and he had the Force with him and a good deal of adolescent outrage. I have seen him do worse to a training remote."

Somebody — Mundi, Anakin thought — made a sound that was nearly a laugh and was not permitted to be. Windu did not turn his head.

"The Pyke intercept has him using a stroke nobody on that gantry had seen."

"The Pykes are extortionists, Master Windu, and frightened ones. I would not take their word on lightsaber forms. I would take it on bribes."

It went on. Windu asked about the Federation hull-codes and Obi-Wan gave him the codes from memory, which was true and which bought three minutes of real concern around the circle, because a Federation garrison on loan to the Pykes was a thing worth being concerned about and was not a boy. Windu asked who had requested Master Kenobi by name for a routine escort and Obi-Wan said, "Senator Verrane of Tyrrhus, through the Chancellor's office," and Anakin filed the name where it would stay. Windu asked about the six hours and Obi-Wan said the pilot had obeyed his order, as he should have, and would the Council like to reprimand a civilian for punctuality.

And through all of it Yoda looked at Anakin.

Not at Obi-Wan. At the boy. The old eyes rested on him the way they had rested on him on Ilum across the ice, patient, unblinking, interested, as if the boy were a stone in a stream and Yoda were watching to see which way the water went round him. Anakin kept his presence in the floor. He kept his face in the shape of a Padawan who has done a brave stupid thing and is now being talked about in the third person. He kept his hands still. He thought about speeders. He thought about Mirren Dask, who did not exist, and about his mother's handwriting, and about the sting of a training saber on the wrist, and he laid those things over the top of himself like a cloth over a cage, and Yoda looked at the cloth, and said nothing at all.

"The hangar log," said Plo Koon.

Anakin's stomach went cold and he did not let it reach his face.

Plo's voice came filtered through the mask, deep and unhurried. "It records a repulsor test on the courier under a crew chief's authorisation, entered some forty minutes before the ship left the bay. A test on a vessel which then made a hyperspace jump to Oba Diah." A pause, in which the mask turned very slightly, not toward Anakin, toward the windows. "I note it. I have no question."

Nobody else had a question either. Windu looked at Plo for exactly one second, and Anakin saw him decide not to ask the Kel Dor why not, and saw him file it.

"Lucky, you were," said Yoda, at last, to Obi-Wan, and then to the boy, "Luck, a poor teacher is. Hm."

"Yes, Master."

"Reprimand noted," said Windu. "Both of you. The Padawan is confined to the Temple until Master Kenobi is out of the tank, and the courier's cost comes out of Master Kenobi's stipend for the next five years, which I understand is the first expense he's incurred in ten." He stood. The others began to stand. He looked at Anakin, directly, for the first time, and said in the clipped voice, "I was wrong about you once this year, Skywalker. I'd like to know which way."

*A window. A window and purple light, and an old man's face turned up with its ruin already in it, and his own hand on his own hilt as he chose.*

"Master," said Anakin, and bowed.

He had hated Mace Windu for twenty-three years. He had carried it in the suit like a stone in a boot: the distrust, the appraising look in this very room, the way Windu had said *the boy* as if the word were a verdict. He had told himself it was unjust. He had told himself that a man who was trusted does not fall, and that Windu's doubt had been the first stone out of the wall.

And Windu was right. Windu had been right then, for the wrong reasons, and he was right now for reasons he could not name, and he was standing in a room where eight children had died behind the chairs looking at the thing that had killed them, and he knew it was wrong and could not prove it and would not stop trying. Anakin thought that if he were in Windu's place he would have had the boy in a cell by now. He found, bowing, that he could not hate him. It was a strange thing to lose in the Council chamber, and he had no time to look at it.

* * *

Vokara Che put Obi-Wan back in the tank the moment the Council rose, and Anakin and Ahsoka waited two days, which was exactly as long as she would allow. On the second night, at the eleventh hour, she took him out again, swearing in Ryl, because he had asked her to and because, she said, a man who could argue through a healing trance could not be healed by it and she would rather lose a few hours of bacta than the whole week's.

"You have until the third hour. Then he is back in and I am sealing the lid."

"Vokara."

"Walk slowly. Do not bend. Do not lift. If you fall, Padawan Skywalker, you will carry him back here and you will explain to me in person what you were doing in the dark with a man who has no business being out of a tank."

"Yes, Master Che."

They walked slowly. Obi-Wan was in a healer's grey robe with the arm strapped under it and he walked the way Anakin remembered walking in the first year of the suit, like a man carrying himself in a box, every step planned a step ahead. The corridors were empty. The Temple at night had its own breathing, the air-handlers and the far-off fountains and the hush of a thousand children asleep, and Anakin kept his presence low and thin and felt, faint as a thread, the other presence that had been waiting for this for two days with her hand on her chest.

"You have not told me who," said Obi-Wan, at the east door of the Room of a Thousand Fountains.

"No."

"You are going to make me find out by walking."

"You'll know before we get there."

The Room was dark and green and full of water. They went in by the east door and counted waterfalls, one, two, the path turning between ferns taller than the boy, the air wet on the face, and at the third the path went behind the water into the dripping space where the ledge was, where the moss was, where a waterproof sample box had lived under the moss for four months with two people's handwriting in it. Obi-Wan stopped before the fall. He stood very still with his good hand on the wet rock and his head slightly turned, the way he had stood in the hangar months ago when he had said there was something on that ship he was afraid of.

"Of course," he said, very quietly. "Of course it is you."

He went through the water. Anakin went after him.

She was sitting on her heels at the far end of the ledge with the spray on her montrals and her hands on her knees. Sand-coloured tunic. Brown belt. Lekku to her shoulders and no further, white markings bright in the dark, blue eyes. Small. Smaller than the box of moss beside her made her look. She had been crying at some point in the last hour and had done what she always did about it, which was to finish and wash her face. She got up when she saw him. She had to tip her head back.

"Master Kenobi."

"Ahsoka."

For a moment nobody said anything else, because there was nothing to be said that was the right size. Anakin stood with the water at his back and looked at the two of them. He had known both of them longer than anyone alive, and he had never seen them look at each other like this, because the last time they had looked at each other at all, she had been a girl of sixteen leaving the Temple by the front steps with the Council watching from the top and Obi-Wan among them, and the time after that she had been a blue flicker on a holoprojector in a Mandalorian hangar and he had been the same, a foot tall, telling her that Anakin was with the Chancellor and could not come.

A fifty-seven-year-old man in a thirty-one-year-old body, with a bolt through his lung and a beard that was not yet grey, looking down at a thirty-three-year-old woman in the body of a ten-year-old girl, who was looking up.

"You're shorter," said Ahsoka.

"I am precisely the height I was. You are smaller. You will forgive me if I do not bend; I have been expressly forbidden." His voice was not steady. He made it steady. "You will have to do the looking up, I am afraid."

"I'm ten. I look up at the furniture."

"And your beard's wrong," she said, after a moment, and her voice was not steady either. "I remembered it longer. Greyer."

"It is eleven years younger than you remember. So am I. So, for that matter, are you." He looked at her, and the look went on too long, the way his looks at the Tano child had always gone on too long, except that now he knew why, and she knew that he knew, and something in his face that had been held for four years let go. "I have been afraid of you since the first day I saw you in the initiates' corridor, child, and I never once knew what I was afraid of. I thought I was going mad. I made a note of it."

"I know," she said. "You looked at me like I'd taken something of yours."

"You had. I did not know what it was yet." He put his good hand flat on the rock, and lowered himself, slowly, badly, with his jaw tight, until he was sitting against the wall of the ledge with his legs out in front of him, and when Anakin moved to help he was waved off without a glance. "The last time I saw you, you were walking down the Temple steps. I was at the top. I did not go after you. I have been meaning to say that for some years."

"The last time I saw you, you were blue, and you told me he was busy."

"He was."

"I know what he was busy with."

"Yes," said Obi-Wan. "I rather think we all do."

Anakin had meant to say something at this point. He had thought about it in the tank's anteroom for two days, what the right thing would be, and he had decided on something short, and when he opened his mouth to say it, what came out was: "She was my Padawan," and on the last word his voice went up and cracked, the whole fifteen-year-old register of it, a boy's voice breaking on the one word he had wanted to say like a man.

Ahsoka put her face in her hands. Obi-Wan closed his eyes.

"I'm aware," he said. "I was there."

"I'm going to kill you both."

"You have already killed me, my friend, and I did not care for it. Sit down. We have very little time and Master Che has threatened to seal the lid."

* * *

The three lists came out of three pockets, and lay on the wet stone between them, and for a moment nobody touched them.

Anakin's was the datapad, scratched, its screen showing a column of characters that were not Aurebesh and were not any cipher the Archives held, because the key to them was the serial number of a respirator unit that would not be built for seven years. Ahsoka's was the grey flimsi, folded to the softness of cloth, eight lines of Togruti and the lines under them, and the mark beside the sixth that Anakin had never asked about. Obi-Wan's was a single sheet, folded twice, and he laid it face down on the rock and kept his hand on it.

"Not yet," he said. "You know what it is. Forty-one names and where each of them falls. I will show it to you when there is something to be done about it. Tonight there is not."

"Rex isn't on it," said Ahsoka.

"Rex is a clone, my dear. The list is of Jedi."

"Rex is alive," said Anakin. "On Kamino. He's six. He looks twelve."

Obi-Wan looked at him. Then he looked at the girl, and something in the look sharpened, because he had spent four years watching this child hold a training saber backwards and had never once thought to ask himself what she had seen that had taught her to hold her tongue.

"Tell me," he said, "what you know that I do not. Quickly. Start with the thing that will hurt most; it saves time."

"What do you think happened on Utapau," said Ahsoka.

"I know what happened on Utapau. The order came. They obeyed it. Cody handed me my lightsaber, which I had dropped, and said something about the next time, and ten minutes later his gunner put a shell into the ledge I was standing on." He said it in the customs-officer voice. "I have had nineteen years to think about it. They were soldiers; soldiers obey. I do not hold it against him. I find I cannot quite forgive him either, which is a failing in me and not in him."

"He didn't choose."

"Ahsoka—"

"He didn't *choose*." She had got up onto her knees to say it, which brought her face level with his. "They had chips. Here." She touched her own temple, under the montral. "Organic inhibitor chips, implanted at the embryo stage. The Kaminoans were told they were a safeguard against rogue Jedi, in case any of us turned, and they put one in every clone they grew. One order, one code, and the chip takes the man. Fives found it. A trooper of the 501st, in the third year of the war; he found it in his own head and he got as far as Coruscant to tell someone, and they shot him in a warehouse with Anakin standing over him. I had Rex's cut out of his skull on a Venator with a medical droid while the rest of his brothers were trying to kill us both. I watched him come back. He cried, Master Kenobi. He was crying when he came round, and he kept saying the names of the men who'd just tried to kill him, and he did not know for a moment whether he had done it or they had. Cody didn't choose. None of them chose. It was done to them before they were born."

Obi-Wan did not say anything.

He was already sitting; there was nowhere further down to go. But Anakin watched him go there anyway. He watched the straight back come off the rock wall and bow, slowly, around the strapped arm, as if something that had held his spine up for nineteen years had been quietly taken out of it, and watched the good hand come off the list on the stone and go to his face, and stay there.

"He handed me my lightsaber," said Obi-Wan, through his hand, after a long time.

"I know."

"I thought— he looked at me. Before. He looked at me and I have thought, every day since, that there was a thing in his face. A decision." The hand came down. The eyes were wet and the voice was very dry. "There was not. There was a man with a knife in his head."

"Yes."

"Thank you," said Obi-Wan, "for the order of business," and laughed, which hurt him, and stopped.

Then it was his turn. Kamino: the planet that was not in the Archives because Dooku had taken it out with a dead man's codes; Sifo-Dyas, who had ordered the army in the year Qui-Gon died and been killed for it the same year on Oba Diah, by the Pykes, for Dooku; the Pykes keeping the lightsaber as a trophy; and the tower room where Obi-Wan had sat across from Lom Pyke and felt, with the part of him that had spent twenty years learning to feel such things, that something had been taken out of that room very recently, something that had sat in it a long time. "I would wager the saber. I would wager it went to Dooku. I would wager Dooku did not want it for sentiment."

"And Verrane," said Anakin.

"Caius Verrane of Tyrrhus asked for me by name for a routine escort. Senators do not ask for Jedi by name unless someone has given them the name. It came through the Chancellor's office." He looked at Anakin. "You were to be alone with him on the fourth day I was gone."

"I was."

"Tell me about the wall."

Anakin told him. Not all of it; there was not time for all of it. He told him that the Chancellor had walked in his head once every ten days since he was nine, and that the night he had woken in this body the garden had closed and a wall had come down behind it, old and scarred, built over twenty-three years by a man who had learned shielding from the Sith themselves, and that Vokara Che had put her hands through his fake dream and found it and said so, and that the Chancellor had found it too, twenty-two days before he said a word, and had pressed nothing and smiled and said the boy looked tired. "He thinks somebody's had me. Someone who knows the discipline. I think he suspects Dooku. I think he's been watching Dooku to see if Dooku knows."

"He does not consider that you came back," said Obi-Wan.

"Nobody considers it," said Ahsoka. "We didn't."

"At the reception," said Anakin, "Dooku touched me. He went through the surface like it wasn't there, straight to the wall, and he put his hand on it, and I hated him. For one second. All of it, Geonosis and the arm and the shuttle and the kneeling, the whole thing, aimed at him, and he felt it, and he couldn't understand where it had come from, because he's never met me. And the Chancellor was ten metres away watching him feel it."

"I saw his face change," said Obi-Wan slowly. "Across the gallery. I thought it was the wine."

"And now the helmet."

Nobody needed that one told. They had all three seen the shape of it. A Mandalorian hunter on a gantry forty levels up, in a helmet that recorded everything, watching a fifteen-year-old Padawan walk through a garrison of droids with a single-handed overhand stroke that had never been taught in this Temple, and close his fist, and lift a Pyke off the floor by the throat without touching him. That recording had left Oba Diah before the courier had. It would be with Dooku by now, and everything Dooku had went to his master.

"So," said Ahsoka. She was sitting back on her heels, small, with the list in her lap. "They know. Not what. But they know he's wrong. Before tonight they thought somebody had got to him. Now they've seen him fight."

"And the Council," said Obi-Wan, "is hunting a shadow in the Temple. Thirty-one children woke screaming on the same night and the centre of it was his level, and Master Yoda has felt it, and Master Windu is looking for a stain, and the stain was standing in the middle of the chamber two days ago with its hands behind its back, and he looked straight at it and reprimanded it for stealing a ship."

"He's right not to trust me."

"He is entirely right. He is right about everything except what it is. Which is the Council's condition, my friend, and always was; I sat on it." Obi-Wan looked at the two lists on the stone and at his own, face down under his hand. "So. The Sith know the boy is wrong and are closing from one side. The Council knows something is wrong and is closing from the other. There are three of us who know what, and one of us is a child the Council will not hear, and one is a Padawan the Council will not trust, and one is a Master who has just lied to it, fluently, and been believed."

"With reservations."

"With reservations. Windu does not believe a word. He also cannot break it, and he knows that, and it will keep him up at night, which is good for him." He drew a breath, carefully, around the lung. "Plo Koon saw the hangar log and chose not to see it."

"I know," said Ahsoka, very quietly. "I'll have to tell him something."

"Tell him you were frightened for a boy. It is what Master Yoda told the Council about Ilum. It has the merit of being true."

Above them the fall went on falling, and the spray beaded on the datapad's screen, on the grey flimsi, on the back of Obi-Wan's hand where it lay over his dead. Beyond the water a Temple Guard's boots went by on the main path, and the three of them went still until they had gone, the way children go still.

Anakin looked at them. His master, grey and bowed against the rock, holding a list of the dead he would not yet show. His Padawan, ten years old, face washed, eyes very steady on him, as they had been through a cracked black mask the last time she had looked up at him from this near. The two people he had loved most and failed worst, who had both seen only the monster, and were both here, on a wet ledge behind a waterfall, lying to everyone they knew.

"So we're all lying to everyone," he said.

Obi-Wan opened his eyes.

"Yes," he said. "Try to do it well."


## Chapter 26: What Remains

The chair had been carried in from the common room and set beside the bed, and Obi-Wan was in it, and Anakin lay on his back under a blanket he was too warm for and looked at the ceiling, where the traffic lanes of Coruscant drew their slow lines of light across the plaster, and did not take the wall down. It was the night Vokara Che had let Obi-Wan out of the tank for good, a week after the Council had finished with them both.

"You should be in the tank," he said.

"I have been in the tank. The tank and I have an understanding. It let me out for a few hours at a time and I came back grey and apologised. Tonight it has let me go for good, and I intend to be insufferable about it." Obi-Wan's left arm was in the harness sling still, and the dressing over his side made a square under the tunic; he sat the way a man sits who has been told not to sit, upright and careful and a little pleased about it. He had been out of the tank six hours. He should not have been awake. "Go to sleep, Anakin."

"You don't know what's in there."

"I have some idea." A pause, dry. "I have had, for four years, a little room in my head with a door that would not open, and every night I have sat outside it. Tonight I would rather sit inside." He shifted the sling. "Whatever comes through the bond comes to me. It stops here. There will be no younglings crying on the levels below. I have done this before, for a Padawan with nightmares, when he was a boy, and he was never the wiser."

"That was a boy."

"Yes," Obi-Wan said. "It was."

Anakin's hands lay on the blanket, two of them, both flesh, narrow and too big for the wrists, the hands of fifteen. He had not slept unshielded since the night thirty-one children across the Temple had dreamed his stair and his boots and his circle of chairs, and the healers had come, and the Council had begun to hunt a shadow. Every night since, he had gone to sleep inside the wall, the old scarred thing that Vokara Che had read like a scar on a hand, and he had woken each morning with his jaw aching from holding it, and gone on. A man can live like that. He had lived like that for twenty-three years inside a worse thing than a wall.

The bond was open between them. It had been open since the ledge behind the third waterfall, when there had been nothing left to hide it for, and the openness was its own strangeness: Obi-Wan's presence at the back of his mind was not the thin, patient, worried thing it had been for five months, nor the vast closed grief it had been on the other side of a cracked helmet. It was warm. It was tired. It was waiting, as a man waits in a chair.

"If you look away," Anakin said, "I'll know."

"I shan't look away."

He let the wall down.

It was not like taking off armour. He knew what that was like, and it was nothing; the armour never came off. It was like the catch of the mask, the one at the back, the one only the droids and the tank-room could reach, giving under a hand that was not his own. The first thing that came through was the room: the chair, the light, the dressing, the tea gone cold on the shelf, a man's breath careful around a lung. The second thing was Obi-Wan, closer than a voice. And then he was fifteen and growing and had been awake for nineteen hours and the body, which was a boy's body and did what a boy's body does, took him down into the dark like a hand on the back of the neck.

* * *

*The table was cold through what was left of his back.*

*They had not put him under. He understood later that this had been decided, and by whom, and why; at the time he understood only that there was a ceiling very far up, and lights in it, and that the droids were working at the ends of him where the ends were, and that the sound coming out of his own throat was not a scream because there was not enough of his throat for a scream. The legs went on first. He felt them go on: the sockets seated over the bone, the pins driven, the nerves found and taken, each one lighting like a fuse. Then the arm. Then the thing that went down into his chest, the two thick tubes that found what the fire had left of his lungs and fixed themselves there, and from that moment he did not breathe.
