He was breathed.*

*He heard it before he understood it. A sound from inside his own chest, a mechanical draw, a long rising hiss and a drop, and then again, the same, and again, the same, and it was in him and it was not his, and it would never be his, and it would be the last sound in every room he ever stood in for the rest of his life. The droids lifted the mask. He saw the inside of it come down: black, moulded, the two lenses, the grille, a thing like the inside of a helmet made for a face that no longer existed. It came down over him and seated with a sound like a door, and the lenses lit, and the world went red.*

*A voice at the edge of the table said his new name. He asked for his wife. The voice told him what he had done to her.*

*He took the room apart. The droids went into the walls; the table bent under him; the ceiling lights went one after another in a line, and the voice stood in the middle of it, in the one still place, and watched him with something that was not pity and was not fear, that was, he understood later, possession. When he was done he hung in the straps and the sound in his chest went on, draw and drop, draw and drop, regular as a clock, and nothing he had done to the room had touched it.*

* * *

The chair did not move. Through the open bond, as clear as the lights on the ceiling, came the fact of a man sitting very still in a chair in the dark, with his good hand closed on the arm of it, and his eyes open, and his eyes on the bed.

* * *

*The tank at the castle was the only place he was ever out of the suit.*

*He had had it built over the river. Not near it: over it, the fortress rooted into the black rock of the bank with the lava a long way below, so that from the tank-room's single tall window, if he turned his head in the bacta, he could see the bend where the crust had a particular shape, where the slope ran down from the landing platform to the water, where a man had once lain and burned. He had had the window placed. He had stood with the architect and the plans and said: there. The architect had not asked why.*

*The bacta was warm and the breathing-tube ran down his throat and the mask hung on its arm beside the tank with its lenses dark, and he floated, and his skin, what was left of it, hurt. It never stopped hurting. The burns had never healed and would never heal; they had been sealed, which was different, and under the armour every day the sealed places itched and pulled and wept, and he could not reach them, and no one could, and the tank was the only hour in which anything touched him that was not metal. He did not use it for that. He used it for the window.*

*He would turn his head in the fluid and look down the slope and find the place and lie there and look at it. The lava went by, slow and red. He did not look away. He fed it. He went down there with his eyes every day and lay on the bank again and felt the heat come up through the ground and the fire reach his hair, and the man above him on the slope with his face wet, and he took that and put it into the thing he kept in his chest beside the machine, and the thing grew, and he got out of the tank and let the droids put the mask back on.*

* * *

Obi-Wan did not move. Something came along the bond, from the chair, and it was not pity, which Anakin would have known how to hate; it was a man watching a window be placed and understanding, with twenty years' grief, exactly where.

* * *

*The Grand Inquisitor had worn yellow once. He had stood at the Council tower doors with a pike and a mask, and said nothing, and let the Masters go by; the man who told Vader this told it as if it should matter to him. The Pau'an was tall and grey and had filed teeth and red lines on his face and a way of speaking that was almost courtly, almost Dooku's, and he hated the Jedi with the thoroughness of a man who had been one of their furniture. Vader disliked him and used him. He used all of them. They were a pack of damaged children with red blades, and when they failed he broke their arms in the training room and let the droids set them.*

*The moon had rain. He remembered the rain on the lenses, and the way it ran, and the sound it made on the armour. The Jedi was a woman of about forty, a Knight who had been a Knight for six years when the Order ended, and she had been there eleven years as a well-digger and a healer of animals, and a family had taken her in, and when the shuttle came down in their field she came out of the house first, so that they would not. He respected that. He remembered respecting it, in the half-second it was given room.*

*She had a saber. She lit it. She was good; she had kept up her forms in a barn, alone, for eleven years, and it showed in her feet. It took him eleven seconds. One overhand stroke, which she took, and which drove her a step back into the mud; a second, which she took; the third she could not take, because she had been trained to parry a Jedi and he was not one, and the blade went through her guard and through her and she sat down in the rain. The Grand Inquisitor, at his shoulder, made a small sound of appreciation, the sound a man makes at a clean cut of meat. The family stood in the doorway. He let them stand. He had no orders about them and no interest, and that, too, was efficiency.*

*That was what it felt like. Not rage. Rage was for the table. This was clean. This was a thing done fast and well with nothing wasted, and in the cleanness of it there was a kind of rest, because for eleven seconds there was only the work, and no bank, and no river, and no woman's throat under his hand. He went back to the shuttle in the rain. He slept, afterward, if what the suit allowed was sleep, and did not dream.*

* * *

In the chair, the man who had taught him that third stroke's parry, once, in a dojo when he was eleven and his wrists were too thin, sat with his eyes open and watched a woman sit down in the mud. Nothing came along the bond that Anakin could have named. He was asleep. He knew, the way the sleeping know, that Obi-Wan had not looked away.

* * *

*The Archives were lit blue.*

*They were always lit blue: the holobooks in their stacks, tier on tier, a cold undersea light that had been the colour of every evening of his boyhood that he had spent in here failing to be a scholar. He came in through the main doors with the 501st at his back, their armour marked in his blue, and the light fell on the white of them and on the black of him, and the archivist droids turned from their shelves and asked, in their mild voices, whether he required assistance.*

*The row of busts ran down the centre aisle. The Lost Twenty. Bronze faces of Jedi who had left the Order of their own will, which was the Order's way of saying it forgave them a little. Dooku's was at the end. He had killed Dooku days before, on his knees, with two blades, at the suggestion of a voice at his shoulder; he walked past the bronze of the old man's face and did not slow and felt nothing, and noted that he felt nothing, and was pleased.*

*A Padawan came down the stairs between the stacks. A Twi'lek boy, sixteen perhaps, green-skinned, lekku bound back for archive duty, with a datapad still in one hand and a Knight's saber in the other, a saber too heavy for him, that he had taken off a body somewhere in the lower halls on his way here, because the Temple was burning and someone had to hold the Archives. He saw the blue markings. He saw the black figure at the head of them. He had not been told. He came down three more steps with his face opening, and the saber lowering, because he knew that armour and he knew those markings and he thought help had come.*

*He said a name. Not the one the voice had given at the table; the other one. He said it the way a youngling says a Knight's name, with the title in front of it, and relief in it, and he was four steps from the bottom.*

*He was not relieved for long. It was one stroke, from above, with the whole weight of the arm, and the boy and the datapad and the borrowed saber went down the four steps together and lay at the bottom in the blue light, and the clone sergeant at his side said, Sir, and he said, Secure the vault, and went on, and the archivist droid behind him said, in its mild voice, that the stairs would need to be cleaned.*

* * *

The chair did not move. Anakin, asleep, felt the man in it hold himself in it, felt it in the back of his own mind like a hand gripping the arm of a chair until the knuckles show; and felt him stay. The light on the ceiling went on drawing its lines. Below the quarters, on all the levels of the Temple, children slept, and nothing reached them.

* * *

*The hunter said the name standing up.*

*He had come aboard with his report and his price, a man in green Mandalorian armour that was a dead man's armour, the son of the man who had worn it before him, who had worn it once on a tower on Oba Diah with a recorder running in the helmet; this son had been six years old then. He was not six now. He said that he had found the pilot. He said that the pilot had got away from him on Tatooine, which was a thing Vader did not forgive and did not, this once, hear, because the hunter went on, and said the name.*

*Skywalker.*

*He had not heard it said aloud in nineteen years. He had not said it. The voice at the table had taken it from him and he had let it go into the fire with everything else, and here it was in a hunter's mouth, attached to a boy who flew.*

*The room was a Star Destroyer's briefing bay with a viewport. He did not take the room apart; he was past that. He stood very still, and the sound in his chest went on, draw and drop, and inside him something he had kept twenty years in the dark put its hand on a wall that was not there any more. Because it meant she had lived. It meant she had lived long enough. It meant there had been a child, and someone had taken the child, and had hidden the child on the one world in the galaxy he would never go back to, and had known he would never go back to it, and had known exactly why. There was one man who could have done that. He saw the slope. He saw the wet face. He had thought the hate was finished with that man and found it was only beginning.*

*And beneath it, far down, where he did not go: a boy. Alive. Flying his ship. With his name.*

* * *

Something came along the bond from the chair. It was not a word; the bond had never carried words. It was the shape of a man on a ridge above a farm in the desert, watching a child walk between the vaporators, year after year, and never going down; and it was the shape of that man understanding, now, in a chair on Coruscant, what the name had done when it was said. He did not move. He did not look away. Anakin, asleep, felt him not look away.

* * *

*Bespin was orange. The freezing chamber, the steam, the pit where the carbonite came up; he had built it for the boy and tested it on the smuggler, and the smuggler had gone down into it with his eyes open. He remembered that. He had not expected to.*

*Then the boy, with the blue blade. His blade; the one Obi-Wan had picked up off the bank and carried for nineteen years, in the boy's hand now, and the boy using it badly, with heart, the way a boy uses a thing that is too heavy for him. He let it go on. He wanted to see. He drove the boy back through the chamber and out along the gantry and into the dark where the wind came up the shaft, and the boy fought, and the boy was fast, and the boy was not enough, and had never been going to be enough. He had known that since the first exchange. He drew it out because he could not stop looking.*

*Then he was tired of looking, and the blade went through the boy's wrist.*

*It was not a hard stroke. It did not need to be. The hand went away with the saber in it into the wind and the dark and the boy made the sound a boy makes and went back along the gantry to the end of it, to where there was nothing but a rail and the shaft, and held his wrist to his chest, and looked at him.*

*He told him. He had not planned to tell him. He had planned to bring him in whole and let the voice decide, and instead he stood on the gantry with the wind in the cloak and told the boy who his father was, in the voice the machine gave him, and watched the boy's face. Luke's face. He had not known the name until that year. Luke's face took it in, and he watched it go through the boy the way the voice at the table had watched the room come apart, and the boy said no, and said it again, and the boy's eyes were hers.*

*He held out his hand. The good one; the black one. He said what he could offer. The boy looked at the hand, and looked at him, and let go of the rail.*

*He went to the edge and looked down. The boy fell a long way. He was very small by the time the wind took him. Vader stood at the rail of the gantry with the cloak going out behind him and the sound in his chest, draw and drop, draw and drop, and looked down into the shaft where his son had chosen to go rather than be touched by him, and did not move, and did not move, and did not move.*

* * *

Obi-Wan put his hand on the bed.

It was the good hand, the right; it came down on the blanket near Anakin's shin, not on him, on the blanket, and it stayed there. It was the only time all night that the chair moved. It came through the bond as plainly as the ceiling, a man who had hidden that child, who had sat on a ridge above him for nineteen years and had died to get him off that world, watching him let go of a rail, and reaching out, with the one hand that worked, for the nearest thing he could reach.

* * *

*What the hate was for.*

*It was for the table. It was so that when the droids seated the legs he had something to be instead of what was being done to him. It was for the itch of the sealed skin under the armour that no hand could reach, the lungs that did not draw, the lenses that made every colour red, so that the body was a thing he hated rather than a thing he was. It was for the bank: for the slope and the heat coming up through the ground and the wet face above him and the words said down the slope, so that he could lie on the bank every day in the tank and not die of it. It was for her throat. It was for the sound her breath had made under his hand on the landing platform, and her eyes, and her falling; it was so that he never had to stand in that again, because the hate stood there for him, and told him it was Obi-Wan's doing, and he let it. It was for the stair, and the blue light, and the four steps, and the Council chamber, and the faces at the height of his belt. It was a floor. It was a floor laid over the lava, and he walked on it for twenty-three years and never once looked down, because looking down was the fire.*

*And what it bought.*

*Nothing. He laid it out, in the dark where the sleeping lay things out, and it was nothing. It had not brought her back. It had not brought the children back. It had not made the lungs draw. It had killed Jedi who would have died anyway and some who would not, and it had killed a boy on the stairs who thought help had come, and it had served a voice that had never once, in twenty-three years, said a true thing to him, and it had put him on a gantry over a shaft watching the only clean thing he had ever made choose the fall. It had bought the Emperor an instrument. It had bought the instrument a tank with a window. Everything else it had cost.*

*And at the end, in a corridor on a thing the size of a moon, with the lightning in him and the boy on the floor, it had not been the hate that moved. He had looked for it and it had not been there. Something else had been there, something very old, that had been under the floor the whole time, the whole twenty-three years, in the fire, not burned. He had picked the voice up. He had carried it to the edge. The boy had taken the mask off him and he had seen with his own eyes, and the eyes had been hers, and the hate had bought him nothing, and the nothing had been enough.*

* * *

The light on the ceiling had gone from the slow lanes of night to the thick grey wash of early morning, and Anakin lay under it, awake, and did not know for a moment that he was; and then he knew, because his jaw did not ache. He had slept. He had slept nine hours without the wall and the Temple below him had not cried out, and he was in his own narrow bed in a body that was fifteen and growing and had a cramp in one calf, and both his hands were on the blanket, and one of them was a finger's breadth from where another hand had been all night and was still.

He turned his head.

Obi-Wan was in the chair. He had not moved from it. The sling was where it had been; the good hand was on the blanket. He had not slept; it was in the grey of him, in the way the lines at the eyes had gone deep and stayed, in the mouth. He looked, in that light, like a man of sixty. He looked ten years older than the man who had sat down in the dark, and the man who had sat down had already been carrying four years and a tower on Oba Diah and a list of the dead. He looked like the man on the ridge. He looked like a man who had sat all night in a room with the door open and had not once gone out.

"I saw," Obi-Wan said.

His voice had very little in it. It was not the dry voice. It was not the voice that said *my friend*. It was a voice that had been used to hold a thing up all night and had nothing left over for shape.

"I know," said Anakin.

The traffic began outside, the first lanes of the day, and the light moved on the ceiling, and neither of them moved. Anakin lay and looked at his master's face, and his master's face did not do anything. It did not close. It did not arrange itself. He had seen that face arrange itself a thousand times in two lifetimes, for Councils and Chancellors and Senators and him, and it was not doing it now; it was only grey, and tired, and there, with its eyes open.

He had wanted two things, for five months, since he had woken in this bed. He had wanted to keep anyone from becoming what he had been. And he had wanted never again to be looked at the way he had been looked at from the top of the slope.

He had not meant to ask. He had meant to say something about tea, or the tank, or the hour. What came out came out in a voice he had not used since the braid had been new behind his ear, a boy's voice, fifteen and unsteady and thin at the top, and he heard it and could not take it back.

"Can you still look at me?"

Obi-Wan looked at him.


## Chapter 27: The Council Must Not Know

The eastern spire had four hundred and twelve steps, and Obi-Wan Kenobi had counted them once, at twenty-seven, on a morning when he had needed something to count.

He counted them again now because the alternative was to think about his left side, which had a hole in it that the bacta had closed and his body had not yet agreed to. The sling held the arm. Nothing held the rest. He had been out of the tank two days, and Vokara Che had given him a list of things he was not to do, and climbing the eastern spire to be questioned by a creature nine hundred years old had been left off it only because Che had not thought anyone would be so stupid.

Yoda had sent for him. Not the Council. Yoda.

He stopped on the hundred-and-fortieth step, where the stair turned and there was a slit of window, and let his breath come back. Below, the Temple roofs went away in grey terraces, and beyond them the city, and beyond the city, under the haze, the dome of the Senate. He had stood in this exact place four years ago. He had been counting then because the corridor was still in his hands.

It had been in his hands for days. The weight of the hilt; the thumb on the switch; the old body's ache in the shoulder from holding the blade up and level, the way the eyes behind the mask had been a dark he could not see into, the small, private smile he had allowed himself because he had understood, at the end, what he was for. And then the sweep of red, and nothing, and a ceiling.

The ceiling had been this one. The quarters below. The small room off the common room, a cot, a shelf, the window he had looked out of for thirteen years without seeing it. He had lain and looked at the ceiling and then he had looked at his hands, and they had been a young man's hands, no spots, no thickening at the knuckles, the little finger of the left one straight that had been crooked thirty years, and the hilt had still been in them. He could feel it. He could feel the hilt of a lightsaber he had let fall on a floor that did not yet exist.

He had got up. He had gone to the other door.

The boy had been asleep on his stomach with one arm hanging off the cot, the way he always slept. Eleven. The braid, two years old and still thin as a bootlace, lay across the pillow. Sandy hair cut short and badly, because he would not sit still for it. A child's hand on the floor, palm up, the fingers curled. A child's shoulder blades under the sleep-tunic. A child's breathing, open-mouthed, untroubled, with no machine in it.

Obi-Wan had gone into the refresher and shut the door and sat down on the floor with his back against it, and he had stayed there an hour, and he had not made a sound, because the boy would wake.

He began to climb again, and the other thing climbed with him, the thing he had been carrying up the stair since the first step. *Can you still look at me?* Two nights ago, the night Che had let him out of the tank for good, the boy had asked it in the dark of the common room, in a voice with no armour on it at all, and Obi-Wan had looked. That was all he had done, and all he had been able to do: he had looked at Anakin through the whole of that night's vigil, at the face that had been Vader's under the mask and was fifteen and wary and wanted an answer, and he had kept looking until the window greyed, so that the boy would have it in the only coin he had that did not need a lie. He had not slept since. He had lain on the cot with his eyes shut and the question going round, and had got up before the bell and dressed one-handed, and now he was climbing to a creature who saw further into a man than any scanner, with no sleep in him to hide behind.

* * *

He had decided on the stairs, that first morning, not to tell Yoda. He had been deciding it every day since, and the reasons had not changed, which was either the mark of a sound decision or of a man who had stopped listening.

The first reason was simple and he could have said it to the Council's face. He had watched them. He had sat in that circular room for three years and watched the twelve wisest people in the Order be wrong at every turn: wrong about the Sith, wrong about Dooku, wrong about the Chancellor, wrong about the army, wrong about the war, wrong about the boy, wrong about him. Not stupid. Never stupid. Wrong in the particular way of people who had been right for a thousand years and could not conceive of the shape of a thing they had never seen. He had been one of them. He had been wrong with them. He would not, this time, hand the one thing he knew to a room that would do with it what it did with everything, which was to deliberate, and watch, and wait for certainty, and arrive too late.

The second reason was the boy. A Council that knew would watch him. They would do it kindly; Yoda would do it with love. They would watch him the way one watches a charge that has been set and not yet gone off, and the boy would feel it, because the boy felt everything, and a watched boy becomes the bomb. That was how it had happened the first time. Not Palpatine alone. Palpatine, and a Council that had looked at a frightened child and seen a danger, and a child who had looked back and seen that he was not trusted, and had gone to find someone who would.

The third reason he had never said aloud, not even to himself, not until the ledge behind the third waterfall nine nights ago, with the lists on the stone and the boy's face turned towards him and the Tano child's small boots not reaching the floor.

They would have told him to give the boy up. For the boy's own sake; for the Order's. And he could not have done it. He had been told once to let go of everything he feared to lose and he had tried, by the gods he had tried, and what he had let go of had got up out of the lava and gone and killed everyone he had ever known.

He reached the top of the stair. His side was a hot wire. He stood a moment, so as not to arrive breathing like a man who had climbed four hundred and twelve steps with a hole in him, and then he went in.

* * *

Yoda's own room was small, and round, and had nothing in it but a cushion and a window and the old one on the cushion, and it was always warmer than the rest of the spire, as if nine hundred years of sitting had got into the stone.

"Sit, you will," said Yoda, without opening his eyes. "Before fall down, you do."

"I had been going to stand, Master. On principle."

"Hm. Principle. Heavy, principle is. Sit."

Obi-Wan sat. The floor was warm. He arranged the sling and did not let his face do anything while he did it. Yoda opened his eyes and looked at him, and it was the look that Obi-Wan had been looked at with since he was a boy of four in a sand-coloured tunic, and it had never once got easier.

They did not speak for a while. That was Yoda's way; he let a silence sit until it had said what it had come to say. Obi-Wan, who had learned a longer patience on a ridge above a farm, let it.

"Five months and a little," Yoda said. "A shadow, the Council has hunted. In the Temple. Since the night the younglings woke crying."

"Yes."

"Found it, we have not."

"No."

"Know what it is, you do."

It was not a question, and Obi-Wan did not answer it as one. He had shaped what he would say on the stairs, between the two-hundredth step and the three-hundredth, and he had shaped it the way one shapes a blade: to be true along its whole edge and to cut only where he chose.

"The boy had a vision, Master," he said. "Of the end of the Order. Of everything that is in this Temple, ended, and by whose hand. I believe it. I believe it is true. I will not say more than that, and I am asking you not to make me."

Yoda looked at him. The ears had gone down a fraction, which was the only tell the old one had ever had, and Obi-Wan had learned it at nine and had never told anyone.

Behind the eyes was a thing Obi-Wan had seen there once before, on the *Crucible*'s return, when Yoda had said four words about the Tano child to Plo Koon in his hearing, and had looked, saying them, as though he were looking at a stone dropped into water a very long way off and counting the rings. He was looking at Obi-Wan like that now.

"Believe him, you do," Yoda said at last.

"Yes."

"Lie to me, you do also."

"By what I do not say. Yes."

"Hm." The old one shifted on his cushion. "Nine hundred years, I have taught. Thousands. Asked me many things, they have. Asked me to be blind, none has." A pause. "Blind you ask me to be, Obi-Wan."

"For a while."

"For a while." Yoda's voice was very quiet. "To the Council, this I will not bring. Not yet. Hunt the shadow they will, and find it they will not, and say nothing I will, and when Master Windu looks at me, look away I will. Cost me, this does."

"I know."

"Know, you do not. Lied to the Council, I have never. Not in nine hundred years. Begin now, I do, for you. And cost you more, it does. Your oldest teacher, you have lied to. Let you, I have. Carry it, we both will."

Obi-Wan found that he could not speak for a moment.

"When the while ends," Yoda said, "know you will. Before I do. Tell me then, you will, or tell me not, and the Council I will tell either way. Agreed, this is?"

"Agreed."

"Hm." The ears came up. "Go now. Four hundred steps, down. Fall, do not. Explain to the healers, I would have to, and like that, I would not."

Obi-Wan stood. It took him longer than it should have. At the door he turned, because there was one more thing, and he did not know until he said it that he was going to.

"Master. The boy is not the shadow."

Yoda had closed his eyes again.

"Know that, I do," he said. "Afraid of what he has seen, he is. Afraid of him, you are not. Afraid for him. Different, that is. Different enough, perhaps."

Obi-Wan went down.

* * *

He did not go back to the quarters. He went to the Archives, because they were on the way, and because he was tired enough to want the one room in the Temple where no one looked at him.

He had come here in the second year, when the boy was thirteen and in Drallig's hall for the afternoon, and had sat at terminal four and typed *Kamino* and waited forty minutes for an answer that did not come. He had known it would not. He had typed it anyway, to see the gap with his own eyes, because if the gap had not been there then nothing he remembered could be trusted. It had been there. He had felt what he had come to call, privately, the relief of the damned.

Four days later, *Sifo-Dyas*. Eleven minutes. A Council seat, a death on Oba Diah, a lightsaber unrecovered. He had read it and gone to Dex's.

Dex had put a mug in front of him with one hand, wiped the counter with another, and said, before Obi-Wan had asked anything, "You look like a man who's about to ask me about something a long way off." And Obi-Wan had asked, and Dex had told him: tall, white, polite, cloners, the best there were, out past the Rishi Maze on a world that was on no chart in the Core because they paid to keep it off, and it would cost a courier and a season to get there and he'd want to go armed. "What's on Kamino that's got you drinking my coffee at eleven in the morning, Obi-Wan?"

"Nothing I can prove," he had said, which was true.

He had not gone. He had sat in this Archive the next week with the Padawan's training schedule on one side of the terminal and a list of courier costs on the other, and had understood that he could go to Kamino, alone, for a season, and leave the boy in the Temple with the Chancellor's audiences falling every tenth day, or he could stay. He had stayed. That was the choice, made in this room, that had made all the others. He had chosen the boy over the army.

The list of the dead he did not read here. He did not read it anywhere but at night, alone, at the table in the common room with the boy asleep. By day it lay face down in the drawer under his night-pad, and he did not open the drawer.

It had begun in the first week. He had lain awake on the third night with a name in his head that would not go, a name he had seen on a security recording in a place he would not name, and he had got up and written it on a flimsi so that it could be somewhere other than inside him. Then another, because the first had been lonely. Then he had understood what he was doing and had done it properly: a name, a place, what he knew of how. He had got to forty-one. He had stopped when the knowing stopped, because he would not guess at the dead. The Tano child's name was not on it. He had not known.

There was a place at the bottom of the list he had never written in. He had never written the boy's name. He had had the stylus on the flimsi, more than once.

* * *

The message had come that morning, before the spire. It was on the table in the common room when he got back, where he had left it, and the boy was standing over it, not touching it, with his hands behind his back as though it might go off.

"The seal's the Senate liaison office," Anakin said. "It's addressed to you."

"It is. Sit down. You are looming, and it does not suit a person who still has to grow into his feet."

The boy sat. Obi-Wan sat, slowly, across from him, and picked the flimsi up and read it aloud, because there was no way to tell him that was better than another.

"'The Office of the Senator for Naboo presents its compliments to Master Kenobi of the Jedi Order, and enquires after the health of Padawan Skywalker following the incident on Oba Diah, of which the HoloNet reports have been various and alarming. The Senator's committee on Outer Rim franchise disclosure will take evidence in the coming session on Syndicate conduct in the Oba Diah sector, and the Senator would be grateful for any account the Temple is in a position to share. With respect and regards, by the hand of the Senator's secretary.'" He put it down. "There is a stamp. It is a very good stamp."

He watched it land.

The boy's face did nothing for the length of a breath. Then it did several things, fast, and put them away, and what was left was a fifteen-year-old sitting very still with his hands flat on the table, and Obi-Wan, who had seen this face at every age it had had, including an age it had not had a face at all, saw the man under it go and stand at a window a long way off, and not turn round.

"She wrote to you," Anakin said.

"She wrote to the Temple. The Temple wrote to me. There is a department for it. I believe there is a droid." He let that sit, and then he said, more gently, "She has written before. I have the second letter. I have had it four years. I did not give it to you, because I did not know what it would do to you, and then I knew, and that was worse."

"I know you have it."

"Of course you do."

The boy looked at the flimsi. He did not pick it up either.

"What are you going to tell her?"

"That Padawan Skywalker is in excellent health, having lately been in the company of a master who is not, and that the Temple has no account to share with the committee at this time. Which is true, and which is what the Council will want said, and which I shall say in better prose than the liaison office is used to." He paused. "Unless you want to answer her yourself."

"No."

"All right."

"Not—" Anakin stopped. His jaw worked. He was fifteen, and he flushed easily, and he was flushing now, up the neck, the way the boy had in the first year when anyone spoke of Tatooine. "Not yet."

"All right," Obi-Wan said again, and left it, because he had learned when to, and he reached across the table with the hand that worked and turned the flimsi face down, the way he turned the other thing face down, and the boy watched him do it and understood, and neither of them said so.

* * *

Qui-Gon had never answered.

He had tried on the first anniversary of Naboo after he woke, in the Room of a Thousand Fountains, at the hour of the morning when the mist came off the lower pools and the light came through it sideways and the whole place looked like somewhere a dead man might consent to visit. He had sat, and opened himself, and asked. He had asked with courtesy, because his master had liked courtesy when he could get it, and then with grief, and then, in the second year, with the particular dry irritation that had got more out of Qui-Gon Jinn alive than any amount of reverence ever had. *You spoke to me on Tatooine. I was sixty and alone and you spoke to me. I am younger now and I have better questions.* Nothing. Water on stone. The third year, the fourth. He had gone on going. He had sat, this last time, with the boy fourteen and the Tano child a shape in the back of every room, and said nothing at all, and waited to see whether silence would do what words had not, and the silence had been answered with silence, which was at least consistent.

He had, at any rate, been alone with a boy he was afraid of for four years and not once had anyone wiser told him what to do. That was going to have to be enough.

* * *

The Room of a Thousand Fountains at night was a different place, and the third waterfall from the east door was louder than it looked, and the ledge behind it was dry and narrow and smelled of moss and cold stone, and had been, Obi-Wan now knew, a dead-drop for two dead people for the better part of four months before a third dead person had been let in on it.

He got down on to the ledge with some difficulty and a certain amount of commentary, none of which either of the other two acknowledged, and sat with his back against the rock and the sling against his chest and the water a sheet of black and silver a hand's breadth from his boots.

Anakin sat on his left. Ahsoka sat on his right, on the end of the ledge, where it narrowed, with her knees drawn up and her small boots not reaching the stone below, and the white of her face-markings catching what light there was. Ten. A girl in a sand-coloured tunic with lekku barely past her shoulders and a woman's eyes in her face, and he had understood at last, nine nights ago, why she had frightened him for seven years, and the understanding had not made it stop.

They had been quiet a while. It was the boy who broke it, and he broke it flatly, as a man puts a tool on a table.

"I can kill him."

Nobody asked who.

"I've done it once. I know what it takes. I know where he sleeps, I know where he works, I know what the guards are and what they aren't, and I know what he can do, because I watched him do it for twenty-three years, and I know what I can do, because I did it." He was looking at the water, not at either of them. "He's at 500 Republica eight nights in ten. He's in the Works the other two. I know which two. He doesn't know I know. He thinks I'm a fifteen-year-old with a crush on Mirren Dask and a grudge against Master Windu. I could walk in on the next tenth-day with my saber on my belt and come out with the Republic saved and nobody the wiser, and the only thing stopping me is the two of you."

"Yes," said Obi-Wan. "That is the only thing. I am glad we have established it."

"Master—"

"Not yet."

"Why?"

Obi-Wan looked at the water for a while himself. He was very tired, and his side hurt, and he had lied to Yoda that morning and been allowed to, and all of that was in his voice when he spoke, and he did not try to take it out.

"Because I have sat in the Senate," he said. "You have not. You were a boy there and a weapon after; I have sat through a hundred sessions. If the Chancellor dies tonight, in his bed, in the sanctum of 500 Republica, with a lightsaber wound through him, what the Senate will see is not a Sith Lord. They do not know the word. What they will see is a kindly old man from Naboo, six years in office, who stood up for the Rim and was butchered in his sleep by the religious order he had been so patient with. They will make a martyr of him before the body is cold. And the Jedi who did it—"

"Nobody would know it was—"

"The Jedi who did it," Obi-Wan said, "are the Jedi who are purged. Not in twelve years. In twelve days. You know who is waiting. Dooku is two years from his speech and he will give it two years early with a dead Chancellor to point at. The Trade Federation have their droids on the shelves; we saw the hull-codes. The army on Kamino is not ready, so they will not need the army. They will need a vote. And a Senate that has just watched its Chancellor murdered by a Jedi will give them any vote they like, and the first one will be about us." He stopped. "And he is not alone. You know that better than I do. There is Dooku. There is whoever Dooku has. There was a creature on Naboo that I cut in half, and it got up, and I did not know it for twelve years. If you kill Sidious tonight you kill one Sith, and you do not know how many are left, and neither do I, and the one thing I am sure of is that there is always one more."

Anakin's hands had closed. "So we wait. We wait, and he goes on. He has a chair in the Senate and a boy in his office every tenth day and a war in a drawer. I know what waiting costs. I've paid it. I've *been* it."

"I know."

"You don't—"

"I know," Obi-Wan said. "I sat on a ridge for nineteen years. I am not asking you to wait because I like it. I am asking you to wait because I have seen the alternative, and it was you."

That landed. He had meant it to and he was sorry, which was a thing he could be both of at once now, at fifty-seven, in a body that was thirty-one and bleeding.

Ahsoka had said nothing. She said it now, quietly, over the water.

"You're both wrong about where to start."

They looked at her. She did not look back; she was looking at her own boots.

"It's not him. It's not the Senate. It's the chips." Her voice was a child's, and the words were not. "There are a million of them growing on Kamino, right now, tonight, in tubes, and every one of them has a thing in its head that Sidious put there, and they're *six*. They're six years old. They look twelve. Rex looks twelve. Cody looks twelve." She said the names the way one lays flowers. "You kill Palpatine, Skyguy, and the chips are still there, and the next one who knows the words says them, and it's Dooku, and it happens anyway, only now it happens to an Order that's just murdered the Chancellor. You wait, Master Kenobi, and the chips are still there, and every day you wait they're a day older, and they're still children, they're children like *that* one—" a small sharp nod at the tunnel to the main hall, where somewhere a hundred clans were asleep— "and nobody in this Temple knows they exist. Chips first. Kamino first. Find the thing in their heads and get it out, and then there's no army to use on us, and then you can argue about the Senate all you like."

"Kamino is a season away on a courier," Obi-Wan said. "I have the costings. I have had them four years."

"I know. I'm not saying it's easy. I'm saying it's first."

"And I'm saying," said Anakin, "that while we're pulling chips out of six-year-olds one at a time on a planet nobody can find, he's in that office, and he's *looking* for us. He's got the recording. Dooku's got the recording. He knows what I am. He doesn't know what you are, Snips, and he doesn't know what you are, Master, and that's the only edge we've got, and it's going."

"Then it is going," said Obi-Wan. "It will go whatever we do. The question is what we have bought with it before it does."

They had come round to the beginning. He could feel it; they all could. He had been in enough councils to know the sound of an argument that had found its circle and would run it until someone was too tired to go on, and he was the one who was too tired, and he was not going to say so.

Nobody said anything for a long time. The water went on.

"I'm not agreeing," Anakin said eventually.

"No."

"I'm not agreeing either," said Ahsoka.

"I would be alarmed if you did. It would be the first time in two lives." He let his head go back against the rock. "I am not agreeing with myself, for what it is worth. I am simply the one who has to say *not yet*, because somebody must, and I have the beard for it."

The boy made a sound that was not quite a laugh. On his other side the girl's shoulder, small and warm through the tunic, came to rest against the sling, carefully, on the side that did not hurt.

None of them moved to go.

He had thought, on the refresher floor four years ago, that he would do this alone. He had thought it on the ridge for nineteen years and in the corridor at the end, and he had been wrong about that, at least, and he sat with the two of them in the dark behind the water and did not agree with either of them and did not get up, and neither did they.


## Chapter 28: The Daughter's Bird

Ahsoka had stopped listening somewhere while Obi-Wan kept answering *not yet*, and had started counting instead, which was what she did in rooms where the adults were talking.

The water went on. The ledge was cold through her tunic. On her left Obi-Wan's good side was warm and his sling smelled of bacta and the healers' soap, and on her right, a metre off, Anakin sat with his knees up and his long boy's hands hanging between them, and the two of them talked across the top of her montrals as if she were a low table. They did not mean to. She knew they did not mean to. That was almost the worst of it.

*Kamino is a season away on a courier.* *He's in that office and he's looking for us.* *The question is what we have bought.* She had said the chips were first and she had kept saying it and each time it had gone into the argument the way a stone goes into that pool: a sound, a ring, and then the surface closed. They heard her. They did not hear her. There was a difference and she had spent seven years learning its exact width.

She was ten. She had been ten for most of a year now, and nine before that, and eight, and the body had got used to her long before she had got used to it. Her feet did not reach the floor of the ledge where it dropped; she had tucked them under. Her hands in her lap were a child's hands, the nails bitten, which was not a thing she had ever done in the other life and which she had let herself do here because children bit their nails and it was one less thing to perform. She had commanded a battalion at sixteen. She had run a network for ten years under a name that was not a name, and sat in rooms with men who would have killed her for a tenth of what she knew, and said nothing in the right order until they left. And here she sat between the two people in the galaxy who knew all that, and they talked over her head, with love, with a combined century of grief in their voices, and she hated it with a clean small hatred that surprised her by being so childish.

She let her shoulder rest against the sling, on the side that did not hurt. Obi-Wan did not move away. That was something.

* * *

Anakin walked her back as far as the fountain stair, because it was past the hour when a youngling could be in the corridors alone without a story, and a Padawan escorting a youngling to the dormitories was a story that explained itself.

"You didn't hear me," she said, on the stair.

"I heard you. Chips first."

"You heard the words."

He looked down at her. He had to; the top of her montrals came to his shoulder. She had been taller than this once, nearly his height, and she had been able to hit him in the shoulder without reaching, and she missed that more than she had expected to miss anything about a body.

"Snips, I'm not—"

"You're both doing it. You don't know you're doing it. Every time I say something you both go quiet for a second and then you carry on from where you were, and the second is you deciding whether a ten-year-old gets a vote." She kept her voice low. There was a Guard on the landing above, and the Guard's mask turned, and she thought of another mask, grey and marked, and did not let the thought finish. "I'm not asking you to like it. I'm telling you I've noticed."

"All right."

"That's not hearing it either."

"I know." He rubbed the back of his neck, the boy's gesture, the one that was real. "I'm trying. It's — you're small. I look at you and you're small."

"I was small on the *Venator* too," she said. "I was small when I cut it out of him."

He did not answer that. They had gone over it at Ilum and again on the ledge and he had been there for the end of Fives, he knew the shape of it, but he had not been in the surgery bay with the lights going red and the droid saying *the scan shows nothing* while she held her hand over the place on Rex's skull and said *look again*. Obi-Wan had been told. Anakin had been told. Neither of them had seen Rex's face when he came out of it, the look of a man who has been informed that he is a loaded weapon and has agreed to be carried anyway. That was hers. It was not a thing she could hand across a ledge in a sentence.

There were others. She kept a count of them, the things that were only hers, the way she kept the count of the stairs. Maul in the throne room with the beams coming down, and what he had offered her, and what he had said about Anakin, and how nearly she had believed him; Obi-Wan knew that Maul had lived and that he had killed him at the end, and did not know that the last person Maul had asked for help was a girl of seventeen standing in a dead city, and that she had said no. The faces under the Inquisitors' helmets, the ones she had seen without them. The man Caleb Dume became, and his eyes. She had not told Anakin all of that. Some of it she would never tell him, because it would not help, and because he carried enough.

"Goodnight, Skyguy," she said.

"Night, Snips."

He went up and she went down. On the lower landing the same Pau'an Guard stood at his post behind the black-marked mask, and she passed within a pace of him. She knew what he would become in another life: the Grand Inquisitor, who would hunt her friends across the Rim with a double-bladed saber and a patience she had learned to dread. Once her stomach would have turned and her feet stopped; now she kept walking and did not flinch. He was only a Guard on a stair, a person who had not yet chosen anything, and she decided, quietly, that she would let him be that for as long as he could.

* * *

Master Plo kept his quarters on the walkway side of the Tranquillity Spire, where the Council members who liked the sky had theirs, and she had never once gone to them unasked in seven years. She went the next afternoon, between astrogation and the meal, with the permission she had asked Master Sen for and Sen's *I am surprised at you* still in her ears from the other week. She knocked. She was small enough that she knocked at a height most visitors did not, and she heard him notice it on the other side of the door before it opened.

"Little 'Soka."

"Master Plo. Can I come in?"

He stood back. The room was spare and dim and smelled of the filtered air of his mask, and there was a low table and two cushions and a window that looked out over the whole of the Temple's eastern roof, and she sat on the cushion with her boots not touching the floor and he sat opposite, folding down in that slow way of his, and waited. He was good at waiting. He had waited seven years.

She had rehearsed it on the stair and in the dormitory and in the astrogation class with the instructor's voice going on about Rishi vectors, and all the versions had been wrong, because they had been too clever. Obi-Wan had said: *tell him you are frightened for a boy.* Obi-Wan had told Yoda he believed in a vision. It was a good shape. She took it, and cut it down to a child's size.

"I have dreams," she said. "About what's going to happen. Not — not like other people's dreams. They're true. I know they're true because some of them have already come true and I didn't tell anyone, I just waited and watched them happen." She looked at her hands. That part she did not have to perform. "I asked you once if a dream could be a memory. You remember."

"I remember."

"It wasn't a question. I'm sorry. I didn't know how to ask the real one."

"And what is the real one?"

"Whether you'll believe me. Not now. Later." She made herself look up. The mask gave back nothing, the way it always had, and she had learned long ago to read the stillness instead, the angle of the head. "Some of what I dream is bad, Master Plo. Some of it's — it's for the whole Order. And when it starts to happen, I'm going to come to the Council and tell them, and they're going to look at me the way everyone looks at me, and they're going to decide a child can't know. And they'll be wrong. One day the Council will have to trust a child. I need one of them to remember that I said it first."

The room was very quiet. Below the window the roof of the eastern hall went out in long grey tiles and a line of birds that were not convors sat along the ridge of it, and she watched them because she could not watch him.

"Then I will remember that you said it," Plo Koon said.

That was all. He did not ask what she had dreamed. He did not ask which dreams had come true, or what was going to happen to the Order, or why a child of ten would speak of the Council as though she had sat in rooms with it. He did not ask about the hangar, or the Guard he had never sent, or the leave he had never signed and had said *I have no question* about. He looked at her, and the look went on a long time, longer than a look at a child goes on, and somewhere in the middle of it she understood that it had stopped being a question. It had been a question for seven years. She had felt it on the back of her neck in dojos and refectories and on the *Crucible*'s deck. Now it was something else. It was the look of a man who has decided to know something and not to say it, and to carry the weight of not saying it himself so that a child will not have to.

She loved him. She had loved him since she was three the first time and three the second, and there was nothing to be done about it, and it was terrible, because she knew where it ended. She knew the sky over Cato Neimoidia. She had read the report in a cold room on a ship that no longer existed. She sat on his cushion with her boots off the floor and she could not tell him, and he had just told her, in nine words, that he would not make her.

"Thank you, Master," she said, and her voice was a child's and shook like one, and he let it.

"Eat something before the meal," he said. "You are too thin for your clan. Sen tells me so and I am inclined to believe her."

"Yes, Master Plo."

At the door he put his hand on her head, between the montrals, the way he had on Shili, and she went out under it and down the stair and did not stop walking until she was somewhere nobody could see her, and then she stopped.

* * *

Barriss Offee was twelve and a clan above her and left things behind.

It was a small thing to know about a person. Ahsoka had collected it over seven years without meaning to, the way she had collected the sound of Master Sen's sleeves: Barriss, who was quiet and earnest and whose handwriting on a flimsi was like a diagram, and who forgot her towel on the rail at the end of the dojo hour, every time, and came back for it with her face hot while the next clan was filing in.

Hawkbat had the dojo after Barriss's clan on the fourth day. Ahsoka had learned not to look at her. She had learned it the first year, when looking had made her stomach turn and a healer had asked whether she was eating. So she filed in with Kessa and Wix and Tam Dorreth being loud, and kept her eyes on the floor, and saw the towel.

It was blue-grey and folded and had *OFFEE* stitched in the corner in the healers' neat thread. The rail was at Ahsoka's eye-height. She stood and looked at it and the clan went past her and Drallig's assistant called the first form and she thought: *leave it.* Let it be found by someone else. Let the girl come back in with her face hot and take it in front of twenty ten-year-olds. It was nothing. It was a towel.

In six years that girl would stand in a courtroom and tell the truth about the Order in a voice that would not shake, and it would be the truest thing anyone said that year, and she would have killed people to earn the right to say it, and she would have let Ahsoka hang for it first. Ahsoka knew the exact weight of the cuffs. She knew the sound of the Senate guards' boots, and the shape of the tunnel under the Temple where she had run from Anakin because she could not trust him and had been right not to trust anyone, and the look on Barriss's face at the end when Anakin pulled her saber from her hand and it was green, Ahsoka's own green, and the girl had said — Ahsoka did not let herself remember what the girl had said.

She took the towel off the rail.

It was easy. It weighed nothing. She stood at the dojo door with it and the clan ahead was still in the corridor, a dozen twelve-year-olds in their sand-coloured tunics, and she had to look up to find the one she wanted, and she held it out.

"You left this."

Barriss turned. She had blue eyes and the Mirialan marks across her nose and she was already going red. "Oh — thank you. I always — thank you." She took it. She looked at Ahsoka properly, which she had never done, because there was no reason for a girl of twelve to look properly at a girl of ten. "Tano, isn't it? Hawkbat?"

"Yes."

"Thank you, Tano."

She smiled. It was a shy, real smile, the smile of someone who has not yet done anything, and Ahsoka smiled back with the child's face she had built, and went in to the first form, and held her training saber the right way round for the whole hour because she did not trust her hands.

That night she did not sleep. She lay beside Kessa with her palm flat on her chest and the thin bond faint under it, and did not reach down it, and watched the dormitory ceiling until the light changed. Kindness was cheap. Everyone said so. She had just found out what it cost, and it was a night, and she paid it, and in the morning her eyes hurt and Sen said nothing, which was Sen's way of saying a great deal.

* * *

Caleb Dume found her in the refectory, as he always did, by the method of walking the length of the Bear Clan table and then the length of the Hawkbat table until he reached her, and then sitting down.

He was seven. His hair stuck up at the back. His tunic hem was turned up twice because they had not yet found one that fitted him, and he had a bowl of something he had already half spilled, and he put it down beside hers and said, "They moved the stairs."

"Which stairs?"

"The ones by the Archives. The ones that go down. There's a Guard on them now and you can't go down."

"They didn't move them. They just put a Guard on them."

"That's the same," said Caleb, with the certainty of a boy who has never in his life been contradicted by the facts, and started eating.

She watched him. She could not help it. She had told herself a hundred times that she would stop, that it was unfair to a seven-year-old to be looked at by someone who knew what was coming for him, and she had not stopped, because he kept coming and sitting down.

"Ahsoka."

"What."

"When I'm big I'm going to be—" He stopped and frowned at his spoon. "I forgot."

"That's all right."

"I was going to say something good."

"You were."

"Yeah." He ate. "I'll remember later."

She did not say *you will.* She put her hand briefly on the top of his head, the way Plo had put his on hers, and took it away before he noticed, and finished her meal, and when she got up he got up too and followed her as far as the door and then went to find Bear Clan, because that was as far as he ever followed her, and she had never found out why.

* * *

The roof at dawn was the coldest place in the Temple, and she went up because of that.

She had climbed to it the first year, when she was four and nobody watched a four-year-old at the fifth hour, and had found the place where the eastern hall's roof met the base of the spire and made a lee from the wind. She had come up most mornings since. The convor was always there, somewhere: on the ridge, on the stair-head, on the gutter at the far end. It never flew. It had never, in seven years, come within four metres of her. She had stopped trying to make it, around the time she stopped trying to make a lot of things.

This morning the sky over the Senate District was the colour of the inside of a shell and the air hurt to breathe and she sat with her back to the spire's stone and her knees pulled up inside the tunic, which was a thing you could do at ten and not at thirty-three, and watched the speeder lanes come up out of the dark.

She heard it before she saw it. The small scrape of claws on tile.

It came along the ridge of the roof, hopping, the way it walked, because it did not fly. Green. The owl-face. The feathered crest that went up and down as it turned its head. She did not move. She had learned that much. It came along the ridge and then it came down off the ridge onto the flat where she sat, and crossed the flat, and stopped.

Within reach. For the first time in seven years, if she put out her hand, she could have touched it.

She did not put out her hand.

It looked at her. The eyes were gold and round and knew her. They had known her on Shili, in the grass, before Plo came. They had known her on a ship with no name over a planet where a man with a lightsaber had told her she would die. They had known her at every stage in between, and she had never asked why, because asking was a thing you did when you expected an answer, and she had not expected one.

*A hand on her chest. Light going in. The Daughter's face, which had been so much like a girl's.*

The flashback cut across the morning and was gone. She sat with it.

She had never let herself think it through. She had thought *Mortis* and then stopped, because Mortis was the one place in either life where the rules had not held, and she had wanted so badly, these seven years, for there to be rules. But sit with it. Sit with it on a cold roof with the bird a hand's breadth away.

The Daughter had died on Mortis. The Son had killed her, and in dying she had given what was left of herself to a girl who was also dead, because Anakin had asked and the Father had allowed it, and the girl had got up. Ahsoka had got up. She had carried something of the Daughter out of that place in her body and had carried it for seventeen years without ever quite looking at it, through the war and the Siege and the long dark and Malachor, and on Malachor the temple had come down on her and she had stood under the falling stone and — and then Shili. Grass. Three years old.

The light that had carried her back from her own death at the Son's hand had been the Daughter's. It had still been in her when she stood under the stone. It had been in her at the end.

And the bird had been there on Shili, waiting, before she had even known she was alive again.

"It's you," she said aloud. Her breath went out white. "It was you. She did this."

The convor turned its head the full way round and back, the way it did, and settled its feathers, and did not go away.

It was not an answer. It was the nearest thing to one she had been given in seven years and she took it, and held it, and found that her hands were shaking, which they did not do, which she had trained out of them before she was twenty. The Daughter's life was in her. The Daughter had put it there to save her once and it had saved her twice, and the second time it had not saved her where she stood but had put her somewhere else, somewhere before, and the Daughter's creature had come to sit by her on the grass and had not said a word about it for seven years because it could not, or because she had not asked.

And perhaps not only her. The thought came up cold and clear as the air. If the Daughter's light had done this for Ahsoka, then it had been in the room when Anakin died, wherever he had died, because the Daughter had been Anakin's too, in her way; it had been Anakin who carried her body across the floor to Ahsoka's. And Obi-Wan —

She did not know about Obi-Wan. She sat with that too, honestly. Obi-Wan had been on Mortis for the end. He had stood at the altar with the dagger in his hand while the Daughter's light went out of her and into Ahsoka, and he had never once spoken of it, and nothing of that light had gone into him. She had no thread that ran from the Daughter to him. She would not make one up. She had been Fulcrum for ten years and the first rule of Fulcrum had been that you did not report a thing you wanted to be true.

"I don't know about him," she said to the bird. "I'm not going to pretend I do."

The bird closed its eyes, slowly, and opened them.

The sun came over the edge of the district and the roof went gold, and she was very cold, and small, and it was the first morning in seven years that she had not been alone up there.

* * *

She wrote nothing at the drop. She went to it at the hour they had agreed for the day after a night like the last one, when the Room was emptiest, and counted the doors from the east and the waterfalls from the door, and went in behind the third, and he was already there.

He was sitting with his back to the rock where Obi-Wan had sat. He looked tired in the way the body looked tired, the fifteen-year-old's way, grey under the eyes and the braid coming loose, and he looked up when she came through the water and started to say something easy, and stopped, because of her face.

"What."

"The bird came and sat with me," she said. "This morning. Close enough to touch."

He waited.

"It's her," said Ahsoka.

She watched him take it. She watched him go back through the years to the room with the dais and the Father and the Son, and she saw him find the body on the floor and himself lifting it and the light going from one girl into the other, and she saw the moment he understood, because his face did a thing she had seen it do under a helmet and had not thought she would ever see it do again, and it was not a thing she had words for.

"Then why me?" he said. His voice had cracked, the boy's way, and he did not seem to notice. "I was there when she died."

"Because you were there."

The water went on. He put the heel of his hand against one eye and held it there.

"I don't know how," she said. "I don't know what it is or what it wants or if it wants anything. I don't know why now and not some other year. I don't know if it's her or just — what she left. I'm not going to tell you I know more than I do." She came and sat down on the stone, an arm's length from him, with her feet not touching the drop. "And I don't know about Obi-Wan. Obi-Wan had been in that room, and had never once spoken of it. I can't make it reach him and I'm not going to try. I'm telling you that so you don't ask."

"I wasn't going to ask."

"You were going to think it."

"Yeah." He took his hand down. His eyes were red and he was fifteen and he was forty-five and he looked at her the way he had looked at her across a hangar once, when she had come back for him and he had not deserved it. "She said — the Daughter. She didn't say anything. She never said anything. She just put her hand out."

"I know. I was the one she put it on."

He laughed, once, not well. "Snips."

"I'm not saying it makes anything better."

"No."

"I'm saying it's the first thing that's been *ours*. Not his. Not the Chancellor's. Not the Council's. Something came for us on its own and it's sitting on the roof and it won't fly." She looked at the water, grey and gold where the morning came through it. "I thought you should know before he does. He can have it tonight. I wanted one thing that was just this."

He did not say anything to that for a while. Then he reached across the arm's length and put his hand on her head, between the montrals, the way she had done to Caleb and Plo had done to her, and left it there, and his hand was too big for a boy's and shook very slightly, and she let it, because she was small, and he was there, and she had been there when the Daughter died, and so had he.


## Chapter 29: Interlude: The Recording

The Works did not sleep, exactly; it had merely stopped breathing some decades ago and nobody had come to certify it. Sidious liked that about the place. He stood in the long room above the dead foundry with his hood up and his hands folded in his sleeves and listened to the building tick as the night cooled it, and waited for his apprentice to finish saying what he had come to say.

"Fett delivered it to me in person," Dooku said. "On Serenno. He would not send it."

"A careful man."

"A man who wishes to be paid in person." Dooku set the holoprojector on the table between them and did not sit. He had been standing since he arrived, which was a tell; the Count sat when he was certain of a room. "There is a message attached. I have not edited it."

"I would know if you had."

"Yes," Dooku said. "You would."

Sidious let the small silence that followed go on for exactly as long as it pleased him, and then inclined his head, and Dooku touched the projector.

Jango Fett resolved above the table at a quarter of his height, helmet on, standing in what might have been a ship's hold. He did not introduce himself. He said that the job had been done as specified: the Jedi isolated, taken, held, the droids run as the client's droids were meant to be run, the hostages kept alive. He said that the Jedi had been shot in the tower's defence and that he had not fired the shot, which was in the recording, and that if the client had wanted the man dead the client should have said so. He said that a second Jedi had arrived without appearing in the contract and that the price was therefore revised, and he named a figure, flatly, the way a man names the weather. Then he paused, and Sidious, who had listened to a great many men decide whether to say a thing, watched him decide.

"I have fought Jedi," Fett said. "I have killed one. I know what they do and how long it takes them to do it." The helmet did not move. "That was not one. You want to know what it was, you watch it yourself. I'm not getting any closer to it than I did."

The image cut. There was nothing about a son. There was nothing about the Mandalorian's own retreat, which Sidious already knew was in the file, because Dooku's silence about it had been so very complete.

"The revised figure," Sidious said.

"I have paid it."

"Good. Play the rest."

* * *

The helmet saw what the man saw, and the man had been looking at the door.

The tower room on Oba Diah was round and poorly lit, with drains in the floor and a gantry two storeys up where the Pykes kept their cargo, and the recording held it at the height of a tall man's eyes, swinging a little as Fett moved. Sidious watched the Jedi fall from the gantry, which Dooku had already told him of; watched the Pykes go to the body; watched the Mandalorian step back against the wall, pistol low, the way a man steps back from a thing that is not yet finished. Fett's breathing was audible in the helmet. It was slow. He had been bored.

Then the door.

Sidious had seen the boy perhaps forty times in six years, across a desk, in a garden, once in the undercity with his twelve-year-old heart in his hands. He knew the way the boy came into a room looking for the exit, which was the slave in him, and stood too close to his master, which was the orphan. He had made a study of it.

The thing that came through the door on Oba Diah did not look for the exit. It did not look at the Pykes, or the droids, or the Mandalorian with the pistol. It looked at the floor where the Jedi lay, once, and then it did not look at anything at all, and the first droid was in two pieces before the second had finished turning.

Sidious leant forward very slightly, which he did not often do.

The stroke was overhand. One hand. The blade came down the way a tree comes down, without flourish, without the little rising preparation every Jedi in the galaxy had been taught at nine years old; it simply arrived, and was through, and was somewhere else. A second droid. A third. The boy's off hand did not touch the hilt at any point. He walked through them as though the room were a corridor and they were a narrowing of it. Fett's breath had stopped being slow.

A Pyke fired and the shot went back into him and he folded. The boy did not break stride. He did not look at the man.

He did not look at any of them. That was the thing. Jedi killed with their faces full of regret, Mandalorians with their faces full of arithmetic, his own kind with pleasure, which was honest. The boy killed the way one closes a door behind oneself. The dead were behind him before they had finished falling. He was only ever looking at the next thing.

Then the second Pyke, the tall one, who had got to the Jedi's body with a blade and had the sense to want a hostage.

The boy stopped. He raised his left hand — the hand without the saber, open, the fingers not quite closed — and the Pyke went up.

Up. Off the floor. By the throat, with four metres of nothing between the boy's fingers and the Pyke's neck, and his boots kicking at the air, and the blade clattering down out of a hand that had forgotten it. The boy held him there. He did not squeeze theatrically; there was no fist. He simply held him, with the thumb and two fingers a little apart, the way a man holds a cup, and the Pyke's face went from grey to a darker grey and his heels stopped kicking, and the boy opened his hand and let the body drop and had already turned away before it landed.

Sidious said, "Stop."

Dooku stopped it. The image hung above the table: a boy of fifteen with a Padawan braid behind his right ear and a face still soft at the jaw, standing with one arm out in a dead man's tower, and the Pyke's body not yet on the floor.

Sidious looked at the hand.

He knew what that was. Not in the way the Jedi would know it, if they ever saw this — a Force choke, dark side, a tutting in the Council Chamber. He knew it the way a man knows his own handwriting. The open hand instead of the fist. The thumb apart. The holding, not the crushing, because a crushed throat ends the lesson and a held one teaches it. He had done it himself, in a corridor on Naboo to a Neimoidian who had needed teaching, and a thousand times in the mind, where it was most useful. It was his. The grammar of it was his. He had taught it to no one. Not to the one who had come before, who had been a blade and had no patience for the slow hand; not to the man standing beside him now, who found it vulgar and preferred the saber for the same reason he preferred the harpsichord.

"Go on," he said.

The boy went to the Jedi. He knelt. The helmet did not wait for the rest. There was a roar and a lurch, and the tower room fell away as Fett's jetpack took him up toward the gantry, so that what followed was glimpsed from above and at a slant, foreshortened, through the shimmer of his own exhaust. Sidious watched the boy lift the man — not with his arms but with the Force, one hand held out flat beneath the body as though to steady a tray, the saber dark and clipped at his belt, the boy's knees shaking a little under the effort because he was fifteen and the man was not small — and walk him toward the door, the Jedi borne along a hand's breadth above the floor. The Mandalorian's pistol was already down, holstered or near enough, a gauntlet's edge at the bottom of the frame. The boy did not look up once. He had not spared Fett so much as a glance in the whole of it, and Sidious understood, watching the angle widen as the man rose out of reach, that Fett had understood that perfectly well, and that it was why he had let him go.

The recording ran on through an empty room and a lift and a landing pad and a courier's engines, and Sidious let it, because Dooku was standing very still beside him and he wished to see how long the Count could stand still.

* * *

"Well," Sidious said at last, "that was not a Jedi."

"No."

"Nor a boy."

"He is fifteen," Dooku said. "The braid is real. Kenobi's apprentice. I made certain of it at the reception. I touched the mind, as you know, and I told you what I found."

"You told me a wall. You told me Sith-built."

"I told you what it was."

"You did." Sidious turned, and looked at him properly for the first time since the recording had begun, and let him feel it. "And now you have told me what he does with his hands. Which is also Sith-built, my friend, and not by me."

Dooku did not look away. That was to his credit; the Count had many things to his credit, which was why he was standing in this room and not lying in the foundry below it. He was proud and cultured and believed in his own reasons, and a man who believes in his own reasons can be led by them further than a man who merely obeys. Sidious believed him. Mostly. He believed that Dooku had never met the boy before the Dome Gallery, because Dooku had been angry about it afterward in a way that did not lie, and that the boy's hatred had unsettled him so deeply he had failed to hide it in a crowded room. He believed that Dooku had not taught a fifteen-year-old to hold a man's throat open-handed. He believed it to perhaps nine parts in ten.

The tenth part he kept, the way one keeps a knife in a drawer one never opens. It was pleasant to have it there. He had not had a tenth part about anyone in years.

"There are two of us," he said. "There have been two of us for a thousand years, and there are two of us now, and one of them is you. Someone has made this boy an instrument. Not a Jedi's instrument; they do not teach that stroke, and if that child used it in front of Drallig the old man would still be sitting down. A Sith's instrument. He has been taught by someone who learned what I learned."

"I did not—"

"I know you did not." Gently. A hand on the shoulder, without the hand. "I am thinking aloud, Tyranus. Indulge me."

Dooku inclined his head, and Sidious watched him do it, and went on.

"The first possibility is you. I have set it aside. The second is that the boy has taught himself, from a holocron, from a book, from some scrap the Order forgot to burn; and that is a thing I would like to believe, because it would mean he is mine already and does not know it. But one does not teach oneself to hold a throat *like that*. One is shown. The third possibility—"

He stopped.

He did not stop because he was uncertain. He stopped because the third possibility had a shape, and the shape was a shaft on Naboo with a boy of twenty-five standing at the top of it, and a red blade going out, and a thing falling in two pieces into the dark, and the dark not being, after all, very deep. He had felt it the way one feels a tooth go, from the Senate Rotunda, and he had not gone to look, because the Chancellorship was being handed to him across a floor full of applause, and because one does not go to look. He had not thought about the shaft in a long time. He found he did not wish to finish thinking about it now.

"The third possibility," he said, "I shall keep for myself."

Dooku said nothing. He was wise.

"What matters," Sidious said, "is not who. It is when. I had allowed seven years." He walked to the window, which looked out on the black backs of factories and the single orange light of a maintenance drone going about a job nobody had told it to stop. "Seven years was a sound plan. The boy was to be knighted in a war. The war was to be mine from both ends. His master was to die in it, well, somewhere I could be seen to grieve. The boy was to come to me grieving. One builds a thing like that slowly, Tyranus, the way one builds a Senate majority, a vote at a time, and I have been very happy building it." He turned. "And someone has walked into my garden and done in a season what I had allowed seven years for, and done it *better*, and left me the account."

"Then the plan—"

"The plan is now."

He said it quietly. He had never raised his voice in his life and did not propose to begin over a child.

"The boy is to be taken this year. Or broken this year; I shall have him one way or the other, and I confess I am no longer certain which I would prefer. The master is to be removed in a manner that does not make a martyr of him; you will think about that, and you will think about the Mandalorian, who has seen the boy's back and knows what it cost him not to shoot it, and who will therefore want more money or a long way away, and may have both. The crisis I had meant for the end of my term is to be brought forward. All of it. The Rim, the Federation, the army, the vote. I have been patient for six years. I find I have an appetite."

* * *

Dawn came up over the Senate District the colour of weak tea, and the Chancellor's office was already lit, because the Chancellor was already in it.

He had the recording on the private display set into his desk, the image frozen at the hand, and he looked at it between items of business the way another man might look at a view.

The business was considerable and he did not hurry it. Sly Moore stood at his shoulder with her pad and her pale, patient face and took down what he gave her in the order he gave it; he liked to let the day arrange itself and see what it chose to put first.

The Rim first, then.
