Dooku's Serenno printers had done good work all season: pamphlets in four scripts on the Rim's tariff burden and the Core's indifference, circulated through planetary councils that had never before been told they were owed something. Three worlds had passed resolutions. One had recalled its senator. It had been meant to take two more years to ripen. It would have to ripen faster. He told Moore to have the Trade Federation's Senate liaison to lunch — the new one, the Neimoidian with the grievance — and to let it be understood that the Chancellor's office would not stand in the way of a revised tariff measure on Rim bulk freight, provided it was proposed by someone else. Verrane of Tyrrhus would do. Verrane had asked for Kenobi by name and found it exhilarating, and a man who has been useful once will usually be useful twice to see whether the feeling lasts.

The committee next. Senator Amidala's Rim franchise bill — disclosure of Syndicate holdings in Rim concessions and a tribunal to hear them — was in its second reading in committee, and the committee had, after Oba Diah, grown unaccountably fond of it. Eleven surveyors had been freed by a Jedi and the committee wished to take evidence on Syndicate conduct and had written to the Temple to ask after Padawan Skywalker's health. He had read the letter. He had enjoyed it. He told Moore that the Chancellor was gravely concerned that the tribunal clause overreached the Senate's jurisdiction and would be grateful to see it referred for study, and that the disclosure provisions might be narrowed, in the interest of their passage, to holdings above a threshold that no Syndicate had ever been foolish enough to exceed. Organa would object, in a voice like a good bell, and be listened to, and lose; it was a pattern worth a Chancellor's protecting. Mothma would second him and count the votes and be right about the count, which was the dangerous thing in her, and could wait.

Amidala he thought about a moment longer. He had a list. It was not written anywhere and it was not, as yet, a list of anything but nuisances, the way a gardener keeps a list of what has seeded itself in the wrong bed. Her name had been on it since she was fourteen and had stood up in his Rotunda and called a vote of no confidence against the man she had been sent to beg from, which had been, he still thought, the most useful afternoon of his life. She wrote to the boy. He knew that; he knew that Kenobi had kept one letter back. He moved her name, in his mind, a little higher up the list, not across the line into the other list, because that was a different instrument and one did not reach for it over a tribunal clause. Not yet. The line was a thing one crossed once. Organa's name stood beside hers, in the same small hand, for much the same reason: he was courteous in the way that precedes being tiresome.

The term. He did the arithmetic as he did every morning, and it came out as it always did: the Senate's calendar gave him something under two years; his own reckoning gave him as long as the Republic was frightened; the gap between those figures was the whole of his work. He would close it this year. One needed a war for that, and an army for the war, and the army was six years old and looked twelve and would be ready, the Kaminoans said, in four. They would have to be hurried. Everything would. He found he did not mind.

"One more," he said.

Moore waited.

"An invitation. On the residence card, not the office. To Padawan Anakin Skywalker, care of the Jedi Temple." He looked at the display. The boy's hand. The thumb apart. "The Chancellor would be honoured if Padawan Skywalker would join him for a private supper at 500 Republica on the evening of the twelfth, in recognition of his conduct on Oba Diah, of which the Chancellor has heard a great deal and wishes to hear more. The invitation extends to the Padawan alone, as his master is understood to be convalescent. Send a copy to the Council as a courtesy; address it to Master Windu, who will dislike it, and copy Master Yoda, who will say nothing. Make the hour late enough that he will be hungry."

"Chancellor," Moore said, which was how she said yes.

She went out. The door closed behind her with the soft, expensive sound doors made on this level, and the morning went on arriving across the window, and the Temple sat in the middle of it on the far side of the city with its five spires taking the light.

He looked at the frozen image for a while longer. Fifteen. Soft at the jaw. A Padawan's braid, a little crooked; nobody had fixed it for him that morning. One arm out. A man dying in the air at the end of it, and the boy already looking at the next thing, the way one does, the way he himself had looked, at that age, at a corridor with a dead man in it and the door at the far end.

He had never seen that style in his life. He knew every line of it.

"Who taught you that, my boy?" he said, almost fondly, to the hand.


## Chapter 30: Supper

The card came on the sixth morning after Obi-Wan left the tank for good, and Anakin knew what it was before the courier droid had finished reading the address, because it was cream and thick and carried the residence crest in the corner instead of the office seal; the seal had summoned him near twenty times and nothing good had ever come of it, and the residence card had never come at all, which was worse.

He took it from the droid at the door of their quarters with a boy's hands and turned it over. The hand was Sly Moore's; he had seen it on near twenty cards of the other kind. *The Chancellor would be honoured.* *In recognition of his conduct on Oba Diah.* *The Padawan alone, as his master is understood to be convalescent.* The evening of the twelfth. That was tomorrow.

Obi-Wan read it twice, sitting at the table with his left arm in the sling and his tea going cold, and then put it face down on the table the way he put his list face down, and looked at the window.

"Well," he said.

"I have to go."

"Yes."

"If I don't go, he knows. Near twenty times I've gone. I've gone with a fever. The one time I've got something to hide, I come down with a headache." Anakin heard his own voice: a boy's register, fast, a little high. He made it slow down. "He wrote it so it can't be refused. He wrote *alone* so that it can't be refused and we'd know he'd done it."

"I am aware of what he wrote." Obi-Wan turned the card over again and read the line about convalescence, and something in his mouth went dry and private. "I shall write to the office, of course. I shall say that my health is quite recovered and that I would be glad to accompany my Padawan, as is customary."

"They'll say no."

"They will say no beautifully. I should like to have it in writing."

* * *

The Council had been informed as a courtesy, which meant that the Council had not been asked, and Mace Windu came down to the Padawan corridor himself at the sixth hour to make it understood that he had noticed. He stood in the doorway of their common room and did not sit. He looked at the card on the table without touching it.

"You'll go," he said to Anakin. Not a question.

"Yes, Master."

"Your master is in a sling and the Chancellor of the Republic wants you for supper at his private residence, alone, a fortnight after you brought back a Syndicate's hostages in a stolen courier." Windu's voice was clipped and level. "The office says *in recognition*. The holonet says *heroism*. This Council said *theft*, and we meant it, and I'd like to know which word the Chancellor uses when the door is shut."

"I'll tell you, Master."

"Will you." Windu looked at him for a while. The look was the one from the window, the one Anakin had carried for twenty-three years on the inside of a mask and had thought he would never be able to stand again, and here he was standing it in a tunic that was short at the wrists. "I have been wrong about you before, Skywalker. I still don't know which way. Go and eat his supper. Come back and tell me what he said. All of it."

"Yes, Master."

Windu looked at Obi-Wan, and something passed between them that Anakin could not read, and then he went.

The reply from the Chancellor's office came within the hour, under Moore's hand. The Chancellor was overjoyed to hear of Master Kenobi's recovery. The Chancellor would not dream, however, of taxing it; the hour was late, the residence was a long way from the Temple, and the Chancellor had been assured by the Order's own healers that rest was the one prescription Master Kenobi was disinclined to follow. It was perfectly polite. Obi-Wan read it and laughed once, without any amusement at all, and said, "He has read Che's notes. Of course he has," and filed it.

* * *

They went behind the waterfall after the evening meal, the three of them, and sat on the stone with the water making its wall of noise between them and the Temple, and tried to prepare him. None of it helped, and all three of them knew that it would not before they started, and they did it anyway, because the alternative was to sit there and say nothing.

"He will not touch you in the first hour," Obi-Wan said. He had his back to the rock and his good hand flat on his knee, and he spoke in the voice he used for briefings, the voice Anakin had heard in a hundred tents. "He has never needed to. He will feed you and praise you and tell you what the Council thinks of you, and he will tell you what I think of you, which he will get slightly wrong on purpose so that you will correct him, and in correcting him you will tell him something. Don't correct him. Let him be wrong."

"I know how he talks."

"You know how he talks to a man. You have not sat across a table from him in that body since you came back, not for a whole evening, not alone." Obi-Wan looked at him. "He has had six years of walking in and out of you like a garden, and then one night the gate was locked, and he has been very patient. He is not going to be patient tomorrow. Tomorrow he will put his hand on the door."

"And I'll let him in."

"You will let him into what you built. The boy. All of it, as deep as he likes; he has had that boy before and he will want to see that he still has him. The question is what he does when he reaches the back of the boy and finds the wall."

"He's found the wall. Months ago."

"He has *touched* the wall. He has walked past it, politely, near twenty times, and noted it, and gone away. He has not tried it." Obi-Wan's voice did not change. "Tomorrow he will try it. And you will have to decide, in the second that he does, how strong a wall a fifteen-year-old can have."

Anakin said nothing. The water went on. Somewhere beyond it a bell rang the hour and a class of younglings was called in from the gardens, their voices going up in the long room and then stopping as the door shut.

"Let it hold," Obi-Wan said. "Let it be old and scarred and let him think what he thinks about who built it. Dooku, or the one before. He has already thought it; let him go on thinking it. Whatever you do, do not show him who built it."

"I know."

"I know you know. I am saying it so that it has been said."

Ahsoka had been quiet. She sat with her knees up and her arms around them, small against the rock, her lekku barely past her shoulders and the white of her markings showing in the dark; a girl of ten with a woman's eyes who had sat in a great many briefings too, on the other side of the table, and knew what they were worth. She waited until Obi-Wan had finished.

"Three things," she said. "Don't kill him."

Anakin looked at her.

"I mean it. I know you can. I saw you on the recording in my head every night this tenday whether I wanted to or not, and I know you can put him through that window before the guards have their pikes up. Don't. We talked about it. We haven't agreed, and until we have, you don't."

"I'm not going to kill the Chancellor at supper."

"Second. Don't let him see that you want to." She said it flat. "You've got a face. I know it, Obi-Wan knows it; you think you're sitting there like a stone and the whole room can see you want to put your hand through the table. He has seen that face across a desk since you were nine. If it comes up, look at the food."

"And the third."

"Eat something." Her mouth moved. It was not a smile. "He's made it late so you'll be hungry, you're always hungry, and you'll sit there with a full plate and your stomach making a noise and you won't eat because you're waiting, and he'll watch you not eat. That's a thing he can read. Put something in your mouth. Chew it. It's only food."

"Snips."

"It's only food," she said again, and looked away at the water.

None of it helped. He had known it would not when they climbed the steps, and they had known it; the thing waiting at the end of tomorrow was not a thing that could be briefed. But he sat with the two of them until the hour was very late and the Temple had gone quiet beyond the water, and when Obi-Wan got up, slowly, favouring the side, Ahsoka put her shoulder under his good arm without being asked, and Anakin walked behind them down the long steps and thought, *this is what I have*, and was not sure whether that was a comfort.

* * *

500 Republica had its own sky. The towers of it went up so far that the air traffic lanes threaded between them at three levels, and the upper storeys sat above the lanes in a quiet that money bought. He had been here before, in the other life, in a cloak, at night, by the private lift from the sub-levels. He had never come in through the front.

The front was a lobby of pale stone and a Senate Guard at a desk, blue robes, blue helmet, a face he did not know. The guard read the card and did not look at him and said, "Padawan Skywalker. The Chancellor is expecting you. The lift on the left." There were two more guards at the lift, and two at the top of it when the doors opened on a corridor that was hushed and wide and smelt faintly of a wood that grew on Naboo and nowhere else, and at the end of the corridor there was a door, and in front of the door there was Sly Moore.

She was exactly as he remembered her, which he had stopped finding strange about anything by now and found strange again, standing in front of her. Tall. Pale as the inside of a shell. The white eyes that did not seem to focus on anything, and the long still hands folded at her waist, and the hood back. She had stood at the Emperor's shoulder for years and he had never known what she thought about any of it, or whether she thought.

"Padawan Skywalker," she said. It was barely sound.

"Aide Moore."

She looked at him, or in his direction, a beat longer than she needed to; the attention of a person used to looking at people on another's behalf. Then she turned and opened the door for him and did not come in.

The room was long. That was the first thing: it went away from him further than any room in the Temple quarters, panelled in the pale wood, lit low, and the far end of it was glass, floor to ceiling, and the glass was full of the city at night, and in the middle of the city, placed in the exact centre of the window as if the whole of Coruscant had been built as a frame for it, was the Temple. Five spires. The lights on along the Processional Way. The Council tower with a single window lit near the top.

He stopped in the doorway. He had not meant to.

"Ah," said the Chancellor. "You see I have arranged a view for you. I confess it is my favourite in the apartment. One can always find it, whatever else the city does." He was standing by the table with his hands clasped in front of him, in a long grey robe of the kind he wore in the evenings, which Anakin knew, and his face was lined and kindly and lit from the side by the candles on the table, which he also knew, and his voice was the voice. "Come in, come in. You have had a long day, I expect, and a long week, and I have kept you up far too late. Sit, please. Here, where you can see it."

The table was laid for two. Real linen. Real plates, white and heavy, and on them food that was not Temple food: a whole fish from the lake country of Naboo, baked in salt so that the crust had to be broken; a dish of small roasted roots in something sweet; green shuura cut in halves and bleeding on a plate; bread still warm in a cloth; a jug of water and a bottle of something pale. There was a cup in front of his place, a plain one, metal, with a lip worn smooth. He sat down in front of it and the Temple sat in the window above the Chancellor's shoulder, and the Chancellor sat down across from him with a small contented sigh, like a man taking the weight off, and smiled.

* * *

"I have heard a great deal," the Chancellor said, breaking the salt crust with the back of a spoon so that steam came out, "and I have heard it from three directions, and no two of them agree, which is generally how I know a thing is true. The holonet calls it heroism. I had my office pull the feeds; the word is used eleven times, which is a record for a Jedi of your years. The Council—" he lifted a piece of the fish onto Anakin's plate, white and flaking, and then onto his own "—calls it theft. Master Windu, when he deigned to answer my courtesy, used the word in writing. Theft of a courier. I have great respect for Master Windu's precision." He took up his fork. "I call it initiative. I have been in public life for a long time, Anakin, and I have learned that when a thing is called heroism by the people and theft by the institution, it is generally initiative, and initiative is the one thing institutions cannot bear."

"The courier was logged, Chancellor." A boy's voice. Careful, sullen at the edge. "Master Kenobi gave the order."

"So I understand, and so Master Kenobi has told the Council, and I am sure the Council has believed him, because it suits them to." The old eyes were warm. "I do not need you to tell me anything else. Eat. You are growing out of that tunic as I watch."

He put the fork in the fish. He put a piece of it in his mouth and it was hot and salt and perfectly cooked, and he chewed it, and it was like chewing cloth. He swallowed. He picked up the cup, which had water in it, and held it, and found that his hand wanted to go on holding it and that the metal was cool and the lip was worn, and he kept it in his hand.

"And how is Master Kenobi?"

"Recovering, Chancellor."

"I was so sorry to be unable to receive him tonight. Convalescence is the most tedious of all conditions; I have had my share. But I am glad. Glad for you." A pause of exactly the right length. "It is a great thing to have brought one's master home. I have wondered, you know—in six years, I have wondered—whether you knew how much you were valued, there." A small movement of the head toward the window, toward the spires. "They are not a demonstrative people. It is a virtue, I suppose. But a boy may do a thing in one night that a Knight would be knighted for, and bring eleven people home who would otherwise be in a Pyke's mine, and come back to be confined to his quarters. I found that hard to read. I will be candid; I found it hard not to intervene. I did not. One learns one's place."

"The Council has its reasons, Chancellor."

"The Council always has its reasons. That is what a council is for." He smiled. "Do you know, I had a message from the Senator from Naboo, about you? In the formal way, a committee note; she does everything in the formal way, our Senator. She wished to be assured of Padawan Skywalker's health after the incident. I told her you were in excellent health, as far as I knew, and that I would be seeing you soon and would say so. She was pleased. She remembers a boy on a ship, I think. We all have our boy on a ship." The fork moved. "She is a remarkable young woman. Difficult, in the Senate; she will make a great many enemies. I do what I can."

Anakin looked at the food.

It was all there. Every word of it. He had heard it first in a red-walled office at nine with his feet not reaching the floor, and at twelve in a speeder in the undercity, and at nineteen with the war on and his arm gone and Palpatine's hand on the stump, and at twenty-two in a box at the opera, and in a hundred rooms afterward when there was nothing left of him to groom and the voice had done it anyway, out of habit, the way a man keeps feeding a dog he has already decided to drown. *They do not value you. I do. She is in danger; I do what I can. One learns one's place.* He knew it by heart. He could have said the next sentence before it was said. And the horror of it, the thing that sat in his stomach with the fish, was how good it still was. How it still found the place. The Council *had* confined him. Windu *had* written theft. Obi-Wan *had* sat with a list face down and not shown it to him. None of that was a lie, and the voice took each true thing and turned it a quarter-turn, and the quarter-turn was the whole of the work, and at forty-five with twenty-three years of the suit in his bones and this man's death in his hands he sat in a fifteen-year-old's body and felt it work.

"Yes, Chancellor," he said.

"You are very quiet tonight."

"It's been a long week, Chancellor."

"So it has. So it has." The Chancellor set his fork down. He folded his hands on the linen, old and spotted and very still, and looked at the boy across the table with the candle between them and the Temple in the glass behind him, and his face was the face it had always been, and his voice was the voice, and the room changed.

* * *

It was not a stroll.

Near twenty times in that body he had sat across from this man and felt the touch come: a warmth at the edge of thought, a hand trailed along a garden wall, a man who had the key letting himself in to look at the roses. Six years of it before he came back and six months since. He had built the boy for it, in the first week, in the quarters at night with Obi-Wan asleep on the other side of the wall: a boy's surface, shallow and bright, with everything a boy of fifteen could be expected to keep in it. Palpatine had walked that surface near twenty times and found it, and had looked once at the wall at the back of it, and had gone away.

This was a hand on the door.

It came in through the eyes. That was how he had always come, in the other life, and Anakin had forgotten and remembered in the same second: the pressure behind the eyes, a weight that was not pain, and a taste at the back of the mouth like metal, and the sense—absolute, the way one knows which way is down—of being *looked at* from the inside. The voice did not stop. "I have been thinking," the Chancellor said, warmly, somewhere a long way off, "about what might be done for you, after this," and the hand was on the door and the hand was pushing, and the strength of it was the strength he knew, and it was all there was in the world.

He opened the door.

He let it in. Into the boy. Into the vault he had built, and had deepened since, in the weeks after Ilum, every night, in case: here was the resentment, bright and hot and childish, of being confined to the Temple like a youngling who had wet himself; here was Windu's face at the window and a boy's hatred of it, clean, uncomplicated; here was Bellas in the dojo saying *slave boy* and the memory of the blade a finger from a boy's throat and the shame that came after and the boy not knowing what had got into him; here was a girl called Mirren Dask in the second row of Vos-Kerrick's class and the way she pushed her hair back, and the boy's stupid heart; here was his mother. He let it in to his mother, because it always went to his mother, and he had built her for it: Shmi in the dust with Watto's tin, Shmi in the letters saying *Owen is a good boy, Ani, you would like him*, the boy's helpless longing and his anger that the Order kept him from her and his fear that one day the Council would stop it. And under the mother, newest, laid in on purpose over the last tenday: the tower room on Oba Diah, and the thing that had come out of the boy there, the way his hand had gone out and the Pyke had risen off the floor, and the boy's terror of it afterward, lying awake, not knowing what he was, not daring to ask his master, not daring to ask anyone, and wondering—just wondering, just once, the way a boy would—whether there was anyone who might understand it who was not a Jedi.

He felt the Chancellor find that. He felt the pleasure in it, the pure, almost paternal delight of a man who has turned over a stone in his own garden and found a rare and precious thing. The hand lingered there. It stroked.

And then it went on, as he had known it would, past the boy, to the back of the vault, to the wall.

The wall was old and scarred and built over years. Vokara Che had read it so. Dooku had read it so and gone white. The Chancellor laid his hand on it now, and Anakin felt him read the grammar of it the way a man reads his own handwriting on a page he does not remember writing, and felt the question rise in him, fully formed, cold and interested: *who built this?*

He could have held it. The wall would have held. It had held for twenty-three years against this man's hand, and it would have held tonight. Obi-Wan had said *let it hold*. Ahsoka had said *don't let him see*. He had meant it.

He opened the wall.

One second. He did not think about it; the thinking came afterward, on the bench. It was not the boy who did it and it was not Vader; it was whatever was left after both of them, the thing that had taken the mask off and looked at its son, and it was tired, and it had been looked at from the inside by this man for the whole of its life and it was not going to be looked at that way again. He opened the wall and let the hand through and let it touch, for one second, the thing behind it.

Not a memory. Nothing with a shape to it, nothing that could be told afterwards as a thing seen. A mind that had once held him by the throat, and the grip of it, closing. A fall, long, with no floor in it. Then a silence, very large. Anakin did not give him the shaft, or the blue light, or the old man lifted above his head; those stayed where they were, behind his teeth. He gave him only what they had left behind: the weight of the mind that had decided, and under it the thing that had no name and was not hatred and was not the dark and was not anything this man had ever had the use of. It would reach him as cold, and a dropping away, and quiet. He would not know whose, or where, or when.

Then he closed.

Not slammed. Closed, the way one closes a door on a room where someone is sleeping. The wall came down between them whole and old and scarred and the hand was outside it, and the boy's bright shallow surface came back over the top of it like water finding its level, resentment and Bellas and the girl in the second row, and the pressure behind his eyes went away, and the taste of metal went, and the candle was between them on the table, and the fish was going cold.

The Chancellor had not moved. His hands were still folded on the linen. His face had not changed at all, and that was the terrible thing: it had not changed, and Anakin, who had lived inside this man's moods for twenty-three years the way a fish lives in a current, could feel that under the face there was nothing. Not fear. Not anger. A stillness like the stillness of the moment after a very loud sound, in which a man takes stock of what he has.

Both of them knew that something had been done. Neither could say what, or say it aloud. There was not a sentence in Basic that either of them could have spoken across that table that would not have ended the Republic in the morning, and they both knew that too, and sat in it.

The Chancellor smiled.

"You have grown, my boy," he said.

"Yes, Chancellor."

*The face, in the instant it understood. Not rage; that came later, on the way down. Only the eyes gone wide and the mouth a little open, an old man's face finding out, a half-second before the rest of him did, that the floor had ended. Then the sound in the shaft, the long thinning cry going down and down past the pipework until the dark took it, and after it the silence, which was the largest thing he had ever heard.*

He took a mouthful of the fish. He chewed it. He swallowed it, and drank from the cup, and put the cup down.

"Forgive me," the Chancellor said. "I have kept you far too late, and talked a great deal, as old men do." He rose, and Anakin rose, and the Temple hung in the window between them with its one lit tower. "You must come again, when Master Kenobi is well. The three of us. I should like that very much." He put his hand out, and Anakin took it, and it was dry and warm and did not tremble, and did not hold on. "Moore will see you down. Good night, Anakin."

"Good night, Chancellor."

Sly Moore was outside the door. She had not moved, he thought, in two hours. She walked him to the lift along the corridor that smelt of Naboo wood and the guards at the lift did not look at him and the doors opened, and she stood outside them with her hands folded and her white eyes on him, or near him.

"Padawan," she said, and the doors closed.

* * *

He walked. He could have taken a transport from the foot of the tower; the Temple would have sent one if he had called; he did not call. He walked out through the pale lobby into the Senate District at night.

It was cold at this hour and there was wind at the kerb, coming off the lanes, and the plaza was empty. The Senate dome sat at the end of the long avenue with its lights on. Monument Plaza was past that. He walked, and his boots were loud on the stone, and he thought about nothing at all for the length of the avenue, with great effort, and then he came to a bench by a dry fountain where some senator's statue stood with a hand out to the empty air, and he sat down on it, because his legs had decided to.

He could not stand up.

He tried. He put his hands on the edge of the bench and pushed and the legs did not take it. They were shaking from the inside, in the long muscles, the way a boy's legs shake. His hands were shaking too. He put them between his knees. His face was hot and the wind was cold on it and he was so hungry he felt sick, and he was fifteen, and he had just sat across a table from Darth Sidious for two hours and opened his mind to him on purpose, which was a thing that at forty-five, with everything the suit could give him and everything the dark could, he had never once been able to do. He had never once in twenty-three years let the man in and *chosen* what he saw. He had been looked at. Tonight he had been looked at and had looked back, and the body that had done it was a child's, and it had used everything it had, and now it sat on a bench in the Senate District at the first hour and could not get up.

He did not know how long he sat. A speeder went over low and its lights went across the senator's bronze hand and off.

Someone sat down on the bench beside him.

He knew who it was before he turned his head, from the bond, which he had kept thin all evening and which was suddenly not thin at all, and from the way the person sat: carefully, favouring the left, with a small breath at the end of it. Obi-Wan had his cloak on over the sling and his hood down. His beard needed trimming. He looked across the plaza at the dome and not at Anakin.

"You were told not to come," Anakin said. His voice came out wrong.

"I was told, by the Chancellor's office, not to tax my convalescence. I have been sitting on a bench across the plaza for two hours and eleven minutes in a wind, which is not taxing. It is bracing." He did not look round. "I have sat in Monument Plaza for four years while you were in that office. I did not see why tonight should be the exception."

"He said—" Anakin stopped. He did not know what came next.

"I know what he said. He has been saying it since you were nine. I have heard the half of it that he says about me." Obi-Wan's good hand came out of the cloak and lay on the bench between them, on the cold stone, not touching. "You walked out of his house. Under your own power, down a lift, through a door, across a plaza. That is the whole of the evening, Anakin. The rest is detail."

Anakin looked at the hand on the bench. After a while he put his own on top of it, a boy's hand, narrow, over a man's, and left it there, and neither of them said anything for some time.

"You didn't eat," Obi-Wan said eventually.

"I ate two mouthfuls."

"Two. Well. Ahsoka will want to know. I shall tell her three."

Anakin laughed. It came out of him like a cough, like something breaking loose, and he put his face in his free hand and sat there on the bench with his shoulders going and Obi-Wan's hand under his and the wind coming off the lanes, and when he could stand, Obi-Wan stood with him, slowly, and they walked back to the Temple together, which was a long way, and said nothing at all.

* * *

Jurokk, the Gate Master, let them in at the gate without a word. The Temple inside was dark and warm and smelt of stone and the night-incense.

They did not go to the quarters. Anakin went to the stair that led up and up to the eastern roof and Obi-Wan followed him, slowly, holding the rail, and did not ask why, and did not complain about the steps. He was barely a week out of the tank, and he had walked the whole way from the Senate District on a side that had not finished mending, and now he was climbing to a roof; Anakin heard what it cost him in every breath on the stair and said nothing, because to name it would have been to ask him to stop.

She was there. On the parapet at the eastern edge with the city below her and the night going grey at the far rim of it, a small shape in a sand-coloured tunic with her knees up and her arms round them, the way she had sat behind the waterfall. She had not slept. He could see it from the door. She turned her head when they came out onto the roof and looked at the two of them, the boy and the man, the sling and the hunched shoulders and whatever was on his face, and she did not get up and she did not say anything, and the bond between them, thin as thread, went tight for a moment and then slack.

The convor was with her.

It sat on the parapet a hand's breadth from her hip, green, owl-faced, its feathers lifting a little in the wind, its round eyes open and on him. It had never flown, and it did not fly now. It looked at him the way it had always looked at him, as if it were checking something, and then it closed its eyes, slowly, and opened them again, and went back to looking at the city.

He crossed the roof and sat down on the parapet on her other side, so that the bird was between them. Obi-Wan came after and lowered himself onto the stone on Anakin's other side, with a breath, and let his back rest against the rail. The grey at the rim of the city was turning to something else. Below them the Processional Way had its lights still on and across the whole of Coruscant, a long way off, in the quiet that money bought, there was a tower with one window lit, and he did not look at it.

Nobody said anything. The bird did not move. They sat, the three of them and the convor, and let the morning come.


## Chapter 31: Shmi

The lie was Obi-Wan's, and Anakin stood beside him in the Council chamber and watched him tell it.

It was a small lie, well made. A trade contact of Dex's in Hutt space had passed word of slaver activity on the Mos Espa road, a shipment of Twi'leks moving through Tatooine under Hutt paper, nothing that merited a Knight at full strength but something that ought to be looked at, and Master Kenobi, who was not yet at full strength, proposed that he look at it. A short assignment. A convalescent one, if the Council liked the word. He would take his Padawan, who knew the ground. He said all this standing straight with his left arm held a little too still at his side, the sling gone two days and the shoulder not yet forgiving him for it, and he said it in the voice he used for the Senate, courteous and faintly bored, as if it had not occurred to him that anyone might wonder what a boy born on that planet might want there. Yoda said nothing. Windu looked at Obi-Wan, and then at Anakin, and then at Obi-Wan again, a long flat look with the whole of Oba Diah in it, and said, "Ten days," and that was all. Walking out down the tower stair, Anakin thought that this man had once been unable to lie to a Council to save his own life, and had learnt to do it to save a boy's, and that it was a shared craft between them now, a thing they did well together; and that he was not sure he ought to be proud of how good Obi-Wan had got.

He did not ask why. He had not asked for it. Obi-Wan had come into the common room four nights after the roof, with the list face down on the table between them and the tea going cold, and said, "Your mother is alive," as if it were a thing Anakin might have forgotten, and then, "I think you should see her while she is," and had gone to bed.

* * *

The heat found him on the ramp.

He had known it would. He had told himself so on the way down through the atmosphere, in the cramped Temple courier with the light coming gold through the port and Obi-Wan asleep against the bulkhead with his bad arm cradled in his good one. He had told himself: it will be hot, you were born in it, it is only weather. And then the ramp went down at Mos Eisley and the air came in and laid itself on his face and his hands and the back of his neck like something alive, and the boy's body took a breath and could not find the end of it, and he stood on the ramp with his mouth open like a child who has never seen the sun.

Twenty-three years. The suit had regulated everything. It had kept him at a temperature chosen by a medical droid and had never once let the weather of any world touch what was left of his skin, and here was Tatooine at midday, two suns, the pale one high and the orange one lower, and the heat went through his tunic and found the narrow back and the thin arms and the flush that climbed his throat whether he wished it or not, and he was sweating before he reached the bottom of the ramp. He put his hand up to shade his eyes and the hand was wet. He looked at it.

"It is only weather," Obi-Wan said behind him, mildly, and Anakin realised he had said it aloud, in the ship, more than once.

The last time he had walked Mos Eisley he had been looking for the hut of a dead man, with troopers ahead and behind, and the town had emptied in front of him the way water empties from a cracked basin. He walked the same streets now at the shoulder of the same man, who was thirty-one and limping very slightly and buying a speeder from a Jawa, and the town did not empty. A Rodian jostled him. A child ran past with a water-skin. The smell was the same, dust and animal and machine oil and the sour tang of vaporator brine, and he stood in it and did not shield, for once, against anything.

The speeder was old and the front repulsor dragged left and Anakin drove it, because Obi-Wan's arm would not take the yoke for three hours, and because it was his planet. They went out over the salt flats with the two suns moving round on their left and nothing in any direction but what there had always been. He had last driven this road at nineteen, with his mother a month gone and the Force screaming in his ears; he drove it now with his hands quite steady, and felt the road remembering him.

Anchorhead went past, a few domes and a cantina and a tower for the water. Then the homestead.

* * *

It was smaller than he had kept it.

The dome stood up out of the sand with its entry arch and its two low wings, bleached the colour of the sand, and beyond it, in rows that walked away to the horizon, the vaporators stood with their vanes turning slowly, and the pit of the courtyard was a square of shadow in the ground to the left of the dome with the stair going down into it. He had buried her on the rise beyond the vaporators. There was nothing on the rise. He looked at it, once, as the speeder came in, and then he looked at the man coming up out of the dome, and he put the speeder down.

Cliegg Lars was broad and plain and had both his legs. Anakin had not known he was braced for that until the man came out of the arch and walked to meet them, walked, two legs, a farmer's easy rolling gait across the sand, and something in Anakin's chest that had been clenched for an hour let go. Cliegg had a face the suns had made and a working man's hands and he looked at the two of them, the Jedi and the boy, with the particular courtesy of a man who has never once asked a Jedi for anything and does not intend to begin now.

"Master Kenobi," he said. "She said you'd come when you could. Didn't say it would be today." His eyes went to Anakin and stayed. "You're him."

"Yes, sir."

"You've her look about the eyes." That seemed to be all he meant to say about it. "She's below. Owen." This to the boy who had come up behind him and stopped in the arch with his arms folded. "Take the speeder round. It's dragging a repulsor, anyone can hear it."

Owen Lars was sixteen and already had the shoulders he would have at fifty. He did not move. He looked at Anakin with the look of a boy who has decided that the thing in front of him will bring trouble to his house and means to be ready for it. He looked at Obi-Wan longer. Then he came and took the speeder's control stalk out of Anakin's hand without a word and drove it round the side of the dome, left repulsor dragging, and Anakin stood on his mother's land and tried to remember how to walk.

"Go on," Obi-Wan said quietly. "I'll follow."

He went down the stair into the courtyard.

She was at the bottom of it.

She had heard the speeder and had come out of the kitchen with her hands still wet and she had not dried them, she had stopped at the foot of the stair with her hands held a little away from her sides, dripping, and she was looking up. She wore a work apron over a rough blue dress. Her hair was pulled back and tied and there was grey in it at the temples, two wings of grey that had not been there when he was nine and that he had seen, in the other life, only in a tent in the dark by the light of a fire outside, matted with blood. Her face was lined by the suns. Her eyes were the same. She looked at him coming down the stair, a boy with a braid behind his ear and a sand-coloured tunic dark with sweat, and her mouth opened and nothing came out of it, and then she said, "Ani. Ani." She dried one hand on her apron at last and held it up toward his head, not touching. "I told Cliegg you'd be taller than this."

He had thought he could do this.

He got to the bottom of the stair and she put her wet hands on his face. He was taller than she was. He had not been, the last time she held him, the first time, the time he had thought of every day for thirty-six years. She had to tip her head back. She put her hands on his face and her thumbs under his eyes and looked at him the way she had looked at him at the door of the hovel in Mos Espa with the ship's engines already running behind him, as if she were learning him for the last time, and he put his arms round her and held on.

She was alive. She was warm and she smelt of cooking and the brine and of herself, and her heart was going under his ear where it had gone when he was small enough to be carried, and she was saying his name into his hair. Ani. Ani. He had forty-five years in him and a war and a suit and the deaths of more people than this planet held and he stood in her arms and was nine. He could not help it. It came up through him like water through sand.

"Let me look at you." She held him off. The thumbs again, under his eyes. "You're thin. They don't feed you."

"They feed me."

"You're thin." She looked harder. He saw her find something. He did not know what she had found; he had got his face into the shape of a boy's and his presence down to the size of a boy's and the wall up, and still she looked at him, his mother, who had known every lie he told from the age of three, and her eyes went over him the way they had gone over him when he came in from the podrace yard with his lip split and said he had fallen. "What's happened to you?"

"Nothing. Mum. Nothing's happened. I'm all right."

She looked at him. She did not say that she did not believe him. She never had. She put one hand flat on his chest as if to feel what was going on inside it, and kept it there, and looked, and then she let it go and said, "Well. You're here," and turned him by the shoulder towards the kitchen door, and that was how he knew she had not believed a word.

* * *

Owen found him at the far row an hour before the suns went down.

He had gone to the vaporators because he could not be in the dome. Cliegg had said over the midday water that the two end units on the north row had been giving a third of their yield for a month and that he had a man coming from Anchorhead who had not come, and Anakin had said he would look, in the boy's voice, the eager one, and had gone up the stair into the heat with a tool roll from the shop under his arm and had been glad of it, of the walk out along the rows with the sand burning through the soles of his boots and the vanes creaking and the sweat running into his eyes, because it was a thing to do with his hands.

The vaporators were old GX-8s with the refrigeration coil in the base and the intake vanes on a mast, and the two end units had the same fault, which was not the fault Cliegg thought. He had the base plate off the second one and was lying on his back in the sand under the housing with his arm up inside it to the shoulder when he heard the boots.

"That's the compressor," Owen said, above him. "Told him it was the compressor."

"It's not the compressor."

"It's the compressor. Yield drops when the heat drops. Compressor's not cycling at night." A pause. "You'll have been on Coruscant. They don't have vaporators on Coruscant."

Anakin lay under the housing with his hand on the thing that was wrong, which was a brine-line sensor crusted with salt so that it reported full and shut the coil off early, a two-minute fix with a wire brush, and thought about the many things he could say. He could say that he had built his first vaporator relay at six out of parts Watto threw away. He could say that he knew this boy, that he had come once to this farm in the suit after Yavin and stood over the two graves Luke had dug by the homestead before Yavin, and had not let the troopers touch them. He could say a great many things, and the boy above him was sixteen and had been right about a compressor on this farm before, and lived here, and would go on living here when Anakin had gone, and would be the one who kept it running.

He wiped the sensor with his thumb and let the salt fall where Owen could not see it.

"Could be the compressor," he said. "Show me."

Owen got down. He got down on his knees in the sand and then on his side and put his head in under the housing beside Anakin's, close enough that Anakin could smell him, sweat and sand and the oil they used on the vanes, and he reached up past Anakin's arm and put his hand on the compressor and said, "Here. Feel. Should be ticking over. It's not."

It was not, because the coil was off, because the sensor had told it to be. Anakin put his hand where Owen's was and felt the stillness and said, "You're right," and Owen said, "Told you," and then, after a moment, in a different voice, the voice of a boy who has been right in front of a stranger and finds that it has not satisfied him as he expected, "Dad'll want to know what it needs."

"Cycle it manually tonight and see if it holds. If it's the compressor it'll drop again by morning."

It would not drop again by morning. Owen would come out at dawn and find both units at full yield and would decide that the manual cycle had cleared it, and would tell his father so, and would be believed. Anakin lay beside him under the housing and listened to the vanes turn and did not care. He had been right about everything, once, for nineteen years. It had been a very expensive way to be right.

"She reads them out," Owen said. He was looking up at the housing, not at Anakin. "Your letters. At supper. Dad pretends he's not listening."

"I know. She said."

"You write a lot about speeders."

"There's not much else I'm allowed to write about."

Owen thought about that. "They read them? The Jedi?"

"Someone does."

"Hm." The boy's hand was still on the compressor. "And him. He reads them."

"He lets them go. That's the thing he does. Nobody else would have." Anakin heard the man in his own voice and put the boy back into it. "He's all right. He's — he's all right, Owen."

"He looks at the house," Owen said, "like he's buying it," and got up, and brushed the sand off, and stood for a moment with the suns behind him, sixteen and square and already the shape of the man who would stand at the foot of that stair and say not this year either, and then went back along the row towards the dome without waiting, which was, Anakin understood, a degree. It was more than he had got in the other life, where Owen had put out a hand in a courtyard and said his name with a stranger's care.

He put the base plate back on with the tool from his belt, a slim Temple hydrospanner with his initials scratched in the handle because Padawans lost them, and then, because the far units were a long walk from the dome and it was a thing a boy who liked machines might do, he spent twenty minutes at the top of the mast with the comm relay, so that if the vanes on these two stopped turning the dome would know it. Then he went in.

* * *

Supper was in the dome, in the low room with the curved walls and the table that had been Cliegg's father's, and there were five of them round it, and Anakin watched Obi-Wan be looked at.

He had not thought about it until it was happening. He had thought about the vaporators and the courtyard and the rise beyond the rows and about not shielding and about shielding, and had not thought: Shmi will sit across the table from him. She did. She served the stew and the flatbread and the cup of blue milk each that was all the farm allowed itself, and she sat down across from Obi-Wan Kenobi and put her hands together on the table and looked at him with the whole of her attention, and said, "I wanted to thank you. For the letters. I know what it cost. I know it's not done."

"It is not done," Obi-Wan agreed. "I have been told so several times."

"I don't know what I'd have had without them. I had nine years of him. Then nothing, until the letters. I would have gone on with nothing. People do." Her hands stayed together. "You didn't have to."

"No," Obi-Wan said, and nothing else, and Anakin saw him not say the rest.

He saw it. He knew this man's face better than he had ever known his own, which he had not seen without a mask for twenty-three years, and he watched Obi-Wan sit across a farm table from Luke's grandmother, in Luke's house, with Luke's uncle at sixteen eating stew beside him, and be thanked, and he knew exactly where Obi-Wan was. Not here. Not at this table nineteen years from now with a different woman serving, either; he had never sat at that table. He had stood on a rise in the dark and watched the window and gone home to the hut and the tea things. He had brought the child and handed him over and Owen had not let him in the door, and now Owen's stepmother was giving him bread with her own hands, and Obi-Wan took the bread and said thank you and his left hand, under the table, was holding the edge of the bench.

"He was a good boy," Shmi said. "Is he a good boy, Master Kenobi? He'll tell me he's all right and I'd like to hear it from someone who'd say if he wasn't."

Obi-Wan looked at Anakin across the table. Anakin looked at his bowl.

"He is," Obi-Wan said, "the best I have known. I would not say so in front of him on Coruscant, it would be bad for him. But he is." A breath. "He frightens me, sometimes. He is very able. But he is good, Shmi. I will say that anywhere."

Cliegg grunted. Owen ate. Shmi looked at her son with her head on one side for a long time, and then she got up to fetch the water, and put her hand on the back of his neck as she passed, and left it there a moment, and went on.

* * *

She caught him at the foot of the stair when the others had gone to their beds.

Cliegg kept a farm's hours and Owen with him; the lamps in the dome were out by full dark and Obi-Wan had gone up the stair to the speeder to sleep where the comm was, he said, which was his way of leaving the house to Anakin. He had meant to go after him. He had got as far as the foot of the stair with the courtyard dark round him and the stars in the square of sky above and his mother came out of the kitchen with no lamp and said, "You're off early."

"Before the suns. We have to be in Mos Eisley by midday. Master Obi-Wan has to —"

"Look at slavers. Yes." She came and stood by him. She was in her nightclothes with a shawl over them and her hair down and grey, and she looked, in the starlight, like an old woman, which she was not and never would be. "I won't be up. I'm not going to say goodbye to you twice in a yard, I did it once. So." She put her hand out. "Give me that."

He did not know what she meant. She reached round his belt and took the hydrospanner out of its loop, the slim one with his initials, and turned it over in her hands and put it in the pocket of her shawl.

"Mum —"

"You'll want it back." She said it quite plainly. "So you'll come. That's all. I'm not asking you anything. I'm not asking you what happened to you, I can see that you can't say and I can see that it's bad, and I'm not going to make you lie to me in my own house, I had enough of that from Watto." Her hand came up and found his face in the dark. "You're all right. You said. So you're all right. Go to bed, Ani."

She kissed him on the forehead, which she had to reach for, and went in, and he heard the door of the room she shared with Cliegg close, and he stood in the courtyard of her house with the loop at his belt empty and could not move.

He would not come. He knew it the way he knew his own hands. He would come in four years on a borrowed speeder bike with the Force howling and find the loop at his belt empty and her gone a month, and whether that was this life or the other he could not, standing here, tell. He had told her he was all right. He had told her nothing. He had done the one thing he had come to do, which was to let her hold him, and the other thing, the thing he had known since the ramp at Mos Eisley that he was going to try to do, he had not done yet.

He went up the stair into the dark.

* * *

The rise was a quarter of an hour's walk beyond the last row.

He went out along the north row past the two units he had fixed, their vanes turning now in the cold wind that came off the flats after dark, and past the end of the row where the farm stopped and the planet began, and up the slope of the rise with the sand sliding under his boots, and sat down at the top of it where he had sat before.

There was nothing there.

He had thought he would be able to see it. He had thought the ground would know. He had knelt here with Cliegg's hover-chair whining behind him and Padmé's hand on his shoulder and had put a stone on the sand with her name cut into it; and he had stood here in the suit, after Yavin, with the troopers waiting at the bottom of the rise, and had looked at the stones for a long time and had not been able to kneel, because the suit did not kneel; and he sat now on the same sand under the same stars with the farm a dark shape below and one green light in it, Obi-Wan's comm, and the rise was a rise. It was sand. Nothing had ever been put in it. He put his hand on the ground where her grave would be and it was only ground, and the grave was in him, where it had always been, and the not-grave was under his hand, and he could not tell which was less bearable.

She was in the dome asleep with his hydrospanner in her shawl.

He sat for a long time. The cold came up through him. The boy's body shivered and he let it. He thought of nothing in particular and then of a particular thing, which was a camp. He knew where it was. It would not be where it was now in four years, the Tuskens moved with the seasons and the water, but he knew their range, he had learnt it in the suit from the Imperial survey charts with a thoroughness he had never explained to anyone, and he knew, more or less, within a night's ride, where the band that would take her was lying up this season. There were perhaps forty of them. Some of them, tonight, were children.

He stood up, and went down the far side of the rise, away from the farm.

He had his saber. He did not take it off his belt. He walked out across the flat with the moons on his left and the empty loop at his hip, and he let himself think it through, because he had never once in the other life thought anything through. He could go tonight and be back by the second sun and nobody would know. He could do it cleanly, which he had not done then: one blade, in the dark, the sentries first, the men, and leave the rest. He could make it so that the band which took her did not exist to take her. He had the skill. He had the practice. And he was fifteen and nobody would believe it of him, and the one man who would know had already lied to a Council for him twice.

He walked until the farm's lit window was gone behind the rise and there was nothing but the flat and the moons and the far line of the Jundland dark where the mountains were.

Then he stopped.

He did not know what stopped him. It was not mercy; he had never been able to make mercy out of nothing, and there was nothing out here. It was not the children, or not only. It was something more like arithmetic. He stood on the flat with the wind pushing at him and understood, quite coldly, that he had come here to do the same thing he had done before, four years early and better organised, and that the man who did it better organised was not an improvement on the man who had done it screaming. He had been that man. He had been him in a Temple with the fires going up the stairs. He had been him on a bridge with a boy who called him father. He knew exactly what it felt like to walk into a place with a blade and come out with everyone in it dead, and it felt like this. It felt like standing on a cold flat at night with a good reason. Every one of them had had a good reason.

He stood there until the shivering stopped being the cold.

Then he turned round, and walked back.

* * *

Obi-Wan was waiting by the speeder.

He was sitting on the sand with his back against the rear repulsor housing and his cloak round him and his bad arm cradled in his good one, where the comm light in the cab was a small green point above his head, and he had been there, Anakin saw from the sand round him, for hours. He had not come out to the rise. He had not come after him onto the flat. He had sat here in the cold with the farm dark beside him and the rows of vanes turning and had waited to see which way the boy came back from, or whether he did.

He looked up when Anakin came round the end of the row. He looked at the saber on his belt and at his hands and at his face, in that order, and Anakin let him.

"You didn't go."

"No."

Obi-Wan nodded once, slowly, as if confirming something on a list. He did not say anything else. He did not say well done, or I knew, or anything a master might say to a Padawan who had been out all night with a weapon. He put his good hand down on the sand and pushed himself up, with a breath, and stood beside the speeder with the cloak falling back off his shoulder, and then he turned, not to Anakin, and looked at the homestead.

He looked at it for a long time.

The dome, pale under the moons. The square dark of the pit with the stair going down. The two wings, the entry arch, the rows of vaporators walking away into the dark with their vanes going round, and beyond them, low and black against the stars, the rise. There was no lamp lit in any of it. There was no sound but the vanes and the wind. Obi-Wan stood with his hand on the side of the speeder and his face turned to the farm and did not move, and Anakin stood a little behind him and to one side and watched him look.

He knew what he was seeing. He had never seen it and he knew. He had been handed the whole of it, four nights after the roof, in Obi-Wan's dry flat voice, as a thing that had happened to someone else: the hut, the Wastes, the walks at dusk to a place on a ridge from where the farm could be seen and could not see him. A boy in the yard at dawn with his head under a vaporator housing and his hair going white in the suns, and a man on a ridge who had carried him there as an infant and had never been let in the door, watching him grow, year on year, and going home. Nineteen years. The whole of Luke's childhood, from a ridge.

Obi-Wan was looking at the rise. He was looking at the rows. He was looking at the place at the end of the north row where a boy had lain on his back under a housing this afternoon with his arm up to the shoulder in the works, and Anakin understood that he was not seeing the boy who had lain there. He was seeing another one. He was standing on his ridge.

Anakin did not say anything. He let him look. The vanes went round. The first of the suns was still an hour under the rim of the world, and the two of them stood by the speeder in the cold and the dark, and the one looked, and the other watched him looking, and neither of them moved to wake the house.


## Chapter 32: The Negotiator

Obi-Wan Kenobi stood in the centre of the Council Chamber with both feet flat on the floor and nothing holding him up but his spine, and counted it a small victory that nobody had offered him a chair.

The sling had come off before Tatooine. Vokara Che had taken one look at him on his return, said something in Ryl that he chose not to have understood, and signed him fit to stand for an hour at a time. The shoulder still pulled when he breathed in. He breathed in anyway. Twelve seats, all filled, and the morning coming through the tall windows so that the floor was a wheel of light with him at the hub of it. He had stood here a month ago with a hole in him and told them the courier was his order, and it had held. A man could get used to that, which was the danger.

The report had gone in the night before. Eleven pages, written on the transport home with Anakin asleep across the aisle, long legs folded wrong, the braid come loose from behind his ear. Obi-Wan had written it with the sleeping boy in the corner of his eye and had left out everything that could not be explained.

"Master Kenobi." Windu did not look up from the datapad on his knee. "Your report."

"You have it, Master."

"I have read it. The Council has read it. I would like to hear it."

So he told it, in order: the tower room at Oba Diah; the survey team of eleven, alive, on Eriadu; the droids on the gantry, Trade Federation hull-codes under the paint, six years old and sold on as surplus, which the Pykes did not have the credit to buy by the hundred; the money, which had come from somewhere; and the hunter.

"A Mandalorian," he said. "Silver-blue armour, old pattern, a jetpack. He was on the upper gantry for the whole of it. He did not fire. He did not close. His helmet recorded, and when it was over he left with the recording, and I would very much like to know who he delivered it to."

"You saw that he recorded."

"I saw the indicator. One learns to look for it."

"Mercenaries record," Windu said. "They are paid on proof."

"They are. And a man in that armour costs more than the Pyke Syndicate has ever paid for anything that was not spice. He was not hired to fight. He was hired to watch." Obi-Wan let that sit for one breath and no longer. "Senator Verrane of Tyrrhus asked for me by name, through the Chancellor's liaison, on a matter that any Knight in this Temple could have handled. I was sent. I was shot. I suggest to the Council, with respect, that the Pykes were paid to kill a Jedi on Oba Diah, and I would like us to ask who benefits."

The light moved on the floor. Plo Koon, who had not moved since Obi-Wan came in, did not move now.

"The Pykes are criminals," Windu said. "Criminals have killed Jedi before. They will again. They had a Republic survey team and a Jedi came to take it from them, and they did what they are. Your Mandalorian is a mercenary; there are three hundred of them on the Outer Rim and half of them wear old armour. Senator Verrane has been checked. His office, his accounts, his staff, his committee seat and his tariff. He is a man from a poor world who wanted a famous Jedi to rescue his voters and got one." A pause, dry as the voice. "You were asked for by name because you are the one they had heard of."

"I'm flattered."

"You should be concerned." Windu set the datapad down. "You have given this Council a Senator who is clean, a hunter who did nothing, and droids that anyone with a cargo hauler could have bought at Cato Neimoidia for scrap prices. And you have asked us to see a conspiracy. I do not see one. I see a Jedi who was shot and who would like it to have meant something."

That was fair, and it was meant to hurt, and it did neither. The Council had been wrong in this room so many times in his memory that one more did not cost him anything.

"Then I withdraw the word conspiracy," he said, "and I'll settle for the word *paid*. Someone paid the Pykes. Someone paid the hunter. I only ask that when the next Jedi is asked for by name on a matter beneath them, the Council remembers that I asked."

"Noted." Windu turned a page he did not need to turn. "And the slaver on Tatooine. The mission that took you out there. What did it find?"

"Sand, Master. In quantity. The slaver had sold up and gone before I arrived."

"Master Kenobi." Plo Koon's voice came through the mask the way it always did, as if from a room further off. "The hunter recorded. Have you considered what, precisely? A boy's style, perhaps. A lift made with an open hand."

Obi-Wan looked at him. Behind the goggles there was nothing to read, and he had known Plo Koon for twenty years and in another life had put his name on a list.

"Me," he said. "Falling, mostly. I would not have thought it worth the price of the armour."

"No," Plo said, and left it there, and Obi-Wan understood that it had been both a question and a kindness, and that Plo Koon knew exactly what that helmet had caught of the boy, the style no Master had taught and the hand held open, and had decided for reasons of his own to let the answer stand.

Yoda had said nothing. He sat with his hands on the head of his stick and his ears a little lowered and his eyes on Obi-Wan's face, and he had not spoken once, not to agree with Windu and not to disagree, and the silence was not absence. Obi-Wan had learned to read it over four years of lying to the old Master. Yoda was not waiting for the Council to finish. He was waiting for the while to end.

"The report is received," Windu said. "The Council finds the deaths on Oba Diah the work of the Pyke Syndicate acting in its own interest. The matter of the hunter is referred to the Judicial Department. The matter of the Senator is closed. The matter of your courier," and his eyes came up for the first time, "is on the file, in your words, and will stay there."

"I would expect nothing less."

"Filed. Thank you, Master Kenobi."

He bowed, from the waist, with the shoulder complaining, and went out, and the door sighed shut behind him on twelve people who had heard everything he had said and nothing he knew.

* * *

The letter was waiting in the quarters when he got back, routed through the Archives as all correspondence from the Lost Twenty was, with Jocasta Nu's seal on the slip and, he suspected, her eye on the content. Anakin was in the dojo. He put the kettle on, because that was what one did, and keyed the holo.

Text, not voice; Dooku had chosen. Blue, in a script so even it looked set rather than written. Obi-Wan read it twice.

*Master Kenobi,*

*I learn, late and by the usual roundabout channels, that you were injured on Oba Diah and have been some weeks in the care of the Temple healers. I write to say that I am sorry for it. Qui-Gon would have been sorrier, and angrier, and would have said so in fewer words than I am able to manage.*

*I have for some years intended to ask you to Serenno. There are papers of his here that the Order did not want when he died and that I think you ought to see, and there is a conversation about his legacy, what he taught and what he was not permitted to finish, that I would prefer to have in a room and not across a comm. If your recovery allows it, the house is quiet in the autumn and the air is a great deal better than Coruscant's. You would be welcome to bring the boy. I knew Qui-Gon's opinion of him. I have never had the chance to form my own.*

*With regard, and with the hope that it does not come too late to be of use,*

*Dooku.*

The kettle clicked off. He did not get up.

It was a beautiful letter. He let himself feel it, because it was true and Dooku had written it to be felt. The old man had loved Qui-Gon as much as he had loved anything, and six years into the service of the thing in the Senate he still did, and the invitation was made of that love and of the hook inside it, and the two could not be separated, and Dooku would not have wanted them to be.

*You would be welcome to bring the boy.*

He sat with it. He had lain on his back in a hangar on Geonosis in another life, leg opened, arm opened, and watched this man's blade lift over him with a courtesy that had not altered by a hair, and been saved by a boy who lost half an arm in the saving. Three years after that, on the floor of a dying ship, the same boy had crossed two sabers at the old man's neck and been told, very quietly, by the voice in the chair, to do it. Obi-Wan had not seen that. Anakin had told him, on a roof, with his face turned away. *He had no hands. The Chancellor said do it, and I did it, and I think I knew then.*

And now the old man wanted to see the boy. In a quiet house, in the autumn, with the air good, and whatever he had felt in that boy's mind at the reception waiting to be looked at properly.

Obi-Wan made the tea and wrote the reply by hand, which took most of an hour, because it had to be perfect and because the shoulder made his writing slant.

*Count Dooku,*

*Your letter reached me this morning and I was glad of it: for its kindness, and because it is the first word I have had of Qui-Gon's papers, which I had believed lost with the rest of him. I am grateful to know they are not.*

*I am unable to travel at present. The healers are of the opinion that a man who has been shot should not be permitted to enjoy himself; the Council is of the healers' opinion; and I find that between the two of them there is very little opinion left for me to hold. My Padawan's studies also keep him on Coruscant. He has fallen behind this year, which is my fault and not his, and I am not inclined to compound it with a holiday, however good the air.*

*May I ask after the papers by a slower road? If copies could be sent to the Archives in Qui-Gon's name, Madame Nu would have them where he would have wished them kept, and I would read them with the care they deserve, and with the care you have shown in keeping them.*

*Qui-Gon spoke of you often. He never said anything of you that I would not repeat.*

*With regard, and in his memory,*

*Obi-Wan Kenobi.*

He read it back. He took out one adjective. He put it back. He sent it through the Archives, to Jocasta Nu's attention, so that there would be a record that Master Kenobi had been offered Serenno and the boy, and had declined the one and not seen the other, and had been impeccably polite throughout.

The holo chimed before he had set down the stylus: Jocasta Nu, in miniature, acknowledging the correspondence and noting, in the same level breath, that Padawan Skywalker's trace in the Archive query log was still logged and still unreported, and what did he wish her to do with it? "I set the boy that exercise," Obi-Wan said, which was near enough to true that it cost him no flicker, "and I would be obliged if you left it logged under my name." She inclined her head a fraction, said that the Archives did not alter entries, only append them, and let it stand.

Then he drank the tea, which had gone cold, and went to find the other two.

* * *

The third waterfall by the east door fell into its pool from a height of twelve metres and made, behind itself, a ledge of wet black stone that nobody used, because it was cold and the only way to it was a rim of rock with the water on one side and the drop on the other. Ahsoka had found it, of course. She was sitting on it now with her knees up and her boots not quite reaching the edge, ten years old and small against the stone, the lekku to her shoulders beaded with spray, and Anakin was beside her with his back to the wall and his long legs out, and between the two of them, under the moss, was the sample box that held their letters.

Obi-Wan came along the rim with one hand on the rock and did not look down, and when he reached them Ahsoka moved over without being asked, and he sat.

"Well?" Anakin said.

"Filed."

"All of it?"

"Pykes acting in their own interest. The hunter to the Judicial Department. The Senator closed. Master Plo asked me what the Mandalorian had been recording and I told him it was me, falling, and he let me." He took the pad out of his robe. "And this came."

He read them Dooku's letter. He read them his reply. The water fell past the end of the ledge in a sheet, and the light came through it green, and when he had finished neither of them said anything for a moment.

"He wants me," Anakin said.

"He wants to see you. In a quiet house. With the air good."

"He wants to see whether I'll try to kill him." The boy's voice was flat and old. "He felt it. At the reception. He put his hand in and he found something that knew him, and he's been turning it over for two months, and he's just asked you to bring it to his house so he can look at it in a good light."

"Yes."

"And you said no."

"I said no in the most courteous terms available to the language, and I copied Jocasta Nu, so that if anything happens to either of us in the next year there is a record that the Count of Serenno asked for you and was refused." Obi-Wan put the pad away. "It is the only thing I can do with a letter like that. Decline it beautifully and make sure somebody saw."

"He'll write again."

"Each one kinder than the last, and each one mentioning you less. That's how it's done." He shifted the shoulder. "Now. I would like, if the two of you can bear it, to be dull for half an hour. We have been reacting since Oba Diah and I want to stop."

Ahsoka said, "Go on, then," in the voice she used when nobody else could hear her, the woman's dryness out of a child's mouth.

"One." He held up a finger. "Anakin's cover is above everything. Above mine. Above hers. Above the chips, above Kamino, above the Chancellor. The reason we are still sitting here is that the only two people who know what he is are a Jedi who lies to the Council and a youngling who lies to Master Plo, and the moment that changes, every clock in the galaxy starts running at once." He looked at the boy. "No more Oba Diahs."

"You were shot."

"I was. And you cut a droid in half with a stroke Cin Drallig has never taught, and choked a man to death with an open hand in front of a camera, and Mace Windu has your report with the word *theft* in it and the Chancellor has whatever that camera saw. If I am shot again, Anakin, you will let me be shot. You will fight like a talented boy who is frightened, because that is what you are supposed to be, and if I die of it you will grieve like one, and you will not, under any circumstances, let the thing out of its box in front of a witness."

The boy's jaw worked. He was fifteen; it was visible. The flush came up his neck and he looked at the water and not at Obi-Wan and said, after a while, "Fine."

"The audiences continue. Every tenth day. The surface he finds is the surface you've built, and the wall stays where it is, because if the wall comes down he will know, and if the wall changes he will know, and the only thing that has saved us so far is that he cannot see what is behind it and has concluded someone else built it. Let him go on thinking that. Let him think it's Dooku."

"He's stopped thinking that."

"Then let him think whatever he thinks, and give him nothing new to think it with. You sandbag. You lose to Orrin Bellas in the dojo on alternate weeks. You are bad at astrogation."

"I'm not bad at astrogation."

"You are now."

Ahsoka made a sound that might have been a laugh. "He's never known how to be fifteen."

"I know how to be fifteen."

"You know how to be fifteen the way I know how to be ten," she said. "Badly, and from the outside."

"Two." Obi-Wan held up a second finger before it could become a quarrel, because it was the kind of quarrel they could have for an hour and had. "Sifo-Dyas."

That stopped them both.

"He is on the Order's books as missing. Not dead: missing. Nobody has touched the file in five years, because he was a strange man with strange opinions and the Council was glad, I think, to stop looking. Sifo-Dyas was Qui-Gon's friend, and Dooku's; the three of them were a story in this Temple once." He let the water fill the pause. "I am going to petition to reopen it. As a personal matter. A Knight asking, on his late master's account, that an old friend of his master's not be left on a shelf. It will be granted because it costs nothing and because Master Yoda will want to know why I asked, and he will not ask me."

"And the file," Anakin said slowly, "goes to Ord Mantell."

"The file goes to Ord Mantell, where there is a docket with his name on it from the year he vanished, and from Ord Mantell the docket goes toward the Rishi Maze, and from the Rishi Maze a man with a legitimate writ and a good reason can go anywhere he likes and come back and say what he found. I will not have to explain how I knew. The file will have known for me."

"Kamino," Ahsoka said.

"Kamino."

"And when you get there."

"That," Obi-Wan said, "is the part I wanted to be dull about."

She had turned to face him, small square shoulders, chin up, and under it the woman who had cut a chip out of a man's head on a ship full of men who wanted her dead and had never once, in the telling, raised her voice.

"They're six," she said. "Rex is six. The first batch. They look twelve and they're six, and every one of them has a thing in his head the size of a fingernail that will make him shoot you when a man on Coruscant says a number into a comm. You asked me on the roof what I'd do first, and I told you, and I'm telling you again. The chips. Before Sidious, before Dooku, before anything. We find the army and we take the chips out."

"Of how many?"

"All of them."

"There are two hundred thousand of them, Ahsoka, if the Kaminoans are to be believed, and a million more by the time anyone needs them."

"Then we start."

"And the Kaminoans, who report to a man named Tyranus whose face they have never seen, will notice when a Jedi begins cutting open their product, and will tell him, and he will tell the Chancellor." He said it gently. He had no gentler way. "I believed, until the two of you told me otherwise, that they turned because they were ordered to and were afraid not to.
