I have had to learn that they were made to. I do not know what one does with an army of children who have been made to. I would rather know than not. I am not certain that is the same as being ready."

"You don't have to be ready," she said. "You have to go."

"I am going. I only wanted it said, on this ledge, that I am going without a plan for the end of it, so that when the end of it arrives nobody can say I promised one."

Anakin had said nothing through this. He was looking at the water, and Obi-Wan, who had been told about a dying man's head in the boy's lap and a chip in a bloody cloth, saw that he was on that ship and not on this ledge at all.

"Three," he said, and the boy came back. "Senator Amidala."

The silence that followed had a different quality from the others. Anakin did not move. He did not look away from the water and he did not look at it either; he simply went still, the way the body goes still when it is waiting to find out whether it is going to be hit.

"She has written through the Senate liaison to ask after Padawan Skywalker's health since the incident on Oba Diah. Her committee is taking evidence on Syndicate conduct in the Rim. I am going to answer her myself: my Padawan is in excellent health and has no account of his own to give, which is true, and the committee may have my evidence, in person, as often as it likes. And I am going to make it clear, in the way one does, that anything the Senator wishes to know about Oba Diah, or about Jedi matters generally, she may have from the Temple, through me, at any hour. So that she need never ask the Chancellor's office. So that the Chancellor's office need never have a reason to send for her."

"You'll be her channel," Ahsoka said.

"I will be her channel. It is the only kind of distance I can manage. I cannot keep her out of the Senate and I cannot keep him out of the Chancellor's office, but I can make the Jedi the easier door, and a busy woman takes the easier door." He looked at Anakin. "If you have an objection, now is the time."

Anakin said nothing.

Ahsoka watched him say nothing. Obi-Wan watched her watch him. She had known the woman, in a life she had told them about in pieces, and had liked her; and the boy had told them, on the roof, in a voice like a door, what he had done to her on a landing platform. Neither of them said any of this. The water fell. After a while the boy said, "No. No objection," and it was in his fifteen-year-old voice, the performing one, and Ahsoka looked away from him as if she had been told something.

"Four," Obi-Wan said. "Dooku."

"Nothing," Anakin said at once.

"Nothing. Yes. He is the one who can reach Sidious, which makes him the one we cannot touch and the one who will, eventually, come to us. He has written. He will write again. He will find a reason to be on Coruscant and a reason to be in a room with you, and when that day comes we will have had a year to decide what you are going to let him find. Until then we leave him entirely alone and we are very, very polite." He eased the shoulder. "Those are the four. Cover; Sifo-Dyas; the Senator; the Count. Is there a fifth?"

"Yes," said Ahsoka. "You, falling off things."

"That is not a plan. That is a hobby."

"Stop it."

"I have stopped. I was shot while stopping. It is remarkably hard to stop in a way that satisfies everyone."

She did laugh, then, very small, and put her hand over her mouth as if a Master might hear it through twelve metres of falling water; and Anakin, beside her, did not laugh, but the corner of his mouth went, and the bond at the back of Obi-Wan's mind, thin as wire, warmed by one degree. It would have to be enough. There was nothing else on the ledge but the three of them, the box under the moss, and the long cold year coming down on them like the water.

* * *

Windu was in the antechamber when he came up for the afternoon session. That was not an accident; nobody waited in that round grey room who had not meant to wait. Windu was standing at the window with his hands behind his back and did not turn round when the lift opened.

"Master Kenobi."

"Master Windu."

"Walk with me."

They went out onto the walkway below the chamber, open to the air, the Senate District laid out beyond the rail with the great dome catching the light. Obi-Wan did not look at the dome. He had looked at it from here in another life, on the last morning of the Order, and did not need to again.

Windu walked to the rail and stopped. He stood for a while with the wind in his robes.

"The boy frightens me," he said.

Obi-Wan waited.

"I have sat on this Council across from Masters I did not trust and been right. I have sat across from children and been wrong, and I would like to be wrong now." The clipped voice did not rise; it never did. "He killed two men on Oba Diah. One of them was found with his throat crushed and no mark on him. The other had been cut through a droid first. I have seen the droid. The plating was eight centimetres and it was one stroke, and Cin Drallig looked at the cut and said he had not taught it and did not know anyone alive who had. Those are the facts in your report. You wrote them yourself."

"I did."

"Vokara Che walked that boy's mind in the spring with his consent and the Council's, and came back and told me there was a wall in it that was old and scarred and had been built over years. Years, Obi-Wan. He is fifteen. Something has been building in that boy for longer than you have had him, and I do not know what it is, and I do not know who taught him to shield like a thing that has been hunted, and I do not know who the Chancellor sees every tenth day when he looks at him." He turned from the rail. "I do not think you are lying to me. I think you are not telling me. I have learned the difference. And I do not know, any more, whether I am afraid of what the boy is, or of what you are doing to keep it from us."

The wind came along the walkway. Obi-Wan Kenobi, who had written this man's name on a list four years ago, stood with his bad shoulder to the rail and found that he had, after all, only one honest thing to say, and that the Negotiator in him, who had spent the morning turning twelve Masters and the afternoon turning a Count, had nothing to add to it.

"He frightens me too," he said. "That's why I'm staying."

Windu looked at him. It went on for some time. Obi-Wan let himself be looked at, which was harder than anything he had done in the chamber, and did not drop his eyes, and did not reach for a joke, and did not explain.

Then Windu nodded, once, as if a figure had been entered somewhere and had been found to balance, and turned, and went in to the session without another word, and left him on the walkway with the wind.


## Chapter 33: The Ledger

The training sabers were kept in a rack by the east door of the third dojo, hilts up, twenty of them, and Anakin Skywalker handed them out one at a time because Drallig had told him, four years ago in another life and a month ago in this one, that a child who takes a weapon from a rack takes it like a thing; a child who is given one takes it like a trust.

So he stood by the rack in the morning light and gave them out. Hawkbat Clan came through the door in the order they always came, which was not an order anyone had set. Tam Dorreth first, because Tam Dorreth was first through every door in the Temple and would be until something taught him otherwise, loud already, telling Brannick Sel about a speeder he had seen from the refectory window. Brannick, whose head-tails were not yet long enough to tie back and kept swinging into his eyes. Kessa Ruun, green and solemn, who said "Thank you, Padawan Skywalker" with the whole of her ten years behind it. Ilyo, the Rodian girl, whose wrist was too thin for the hilt and who gripped it the way a child grips a pole on a moving tram. Wix, the Ithorian boy, both mouths humming under his breath, a sound like a distant generator. Fourteen more. He knew every name and every hand.

And at the back, last, the Tano child, who took her saber from him without looking up and turned it in her small fist as she walked away so that the emitter pointed behind her, down along her forearm, the way it should not be held and the way she had held one since the first week he had been here. There was a hilt on her belt now, beside the training saber's empty clip, that had not been there a month ago: plain, a little long for her, the metal still new enough to show the file marks where Huyang had let her finish it herself. She did not touch it. Nobody had told her not to wear it to initiates' class and so she wore it.

Drallig watched from the far wall with his arms folded. He had watched from the far wall every class since Oba Diah. He had never said why and Anakin had never asked.

"Lines," the battlemaster said.

They made their lines. Four rows of five, the children at arm's length from one another so that a stumble would not take a neighbour's eye, boots squeaking on the mat, Tam Dorreth still talking until Drallig looked at him and the talking stopped as if a tap had been shut. Anakin took the front, where the assistant stood, facing them. It put the whole clan in front of him at once. Twenty children in sand-coloured tunics with the hems let down twice and the belts tied by someone else that morning. Twenty unlit hilts.

"First position," he said.

They lifted them.

He had done this perhaps twenty mornings now, in this body. The first time, on the first morning, he had seen the hall on fire behind them and had put his hand on the rack to keep standing. The second time he had seen it. The tenth. The twentieth, with his voice not shaking, which was the worst of it. He had learned to let it come and to teach through it. He had thought that was the most that could be done.

Twenty children raised their sabers and he saw twenty children raising their sabers.

He did not notice it at once. He corrected Ilyo's wrist. He told Brannick to get his hair out of his eyes or he would tie it back himself, and Brannick grinned, and the Tano child's mouth moved at the corner in the back row. He walked the first line looking at feet. Then he came to the end of the line and turned to walk the second and something in him, some long-trained part that stood always at the door of the next moment waiting for the fire, said: *where is it?* And he realised that for the length of a walked line there had been nothing in the corner of his eye but the dojo wall, and the grey Coruscant morning in the high windows, and Drallig.

That was when it came. Not the whole of it. One line, cutting in the way it always did, the way a blade comes through a door.

*The boy's hands up, both of them, the way they were taught, and the blue light on his face, and his mouth open to ask.*

He stood with it. Tam Dorreth's saber drifted out of position and he said, "Tam," and Tam corrected it. His own hands were cold. He breathed, which still, after seven months, startled him: the air going in on its own, and out, no valve, no hiss. He breathed again. The dojo came back around the edges of the thing and then over it, as water comes back over a stone when the wave that bared it has gone, and the stone was still there under the water, and would be, and the water was over it.

It passed.

He had not known it could do that. He had thought the choice was between seeing it and not seeing it, and that he would see it until he died. He had not understood that there was a third thing, which was to see it and have it pass, and to find at the end of it twenty children with their sabers up and the next thing to do being to tell Wix to stop humming.

"Wix," he said. "Stop humming."

Wix stopped humming. The children laughed, which they were not supposed to do in Drallig's hall, and Drallig let them, which he had never done.

They went through the first six forms. He showed the parry before the stroke, because a child who learns to hit before he learns to be hit will hit everything, and he put them in pairs and let them sting each other. Tam Dorreth yelped. Kessa Ruun took a bruise across the knuckles from Ilyo and did not yelp, and looked at her hand as if it had surprised her, and he knelt and showed her where her guard had been and where it should have been, and she nodded with her lips pressed together and went back and did it right. Children were children. They tired by the third pairing and he could see it in their shoulders, the sabers sinking, the feet coming together, the thing that made Drallig say *lines* in that voice. He gave them water. He was fifteen and his own shoulders ached from holding a training saber at arm's length to demonstrate, which a man could have done all morning and a boy could not.

The Tano child was paired with Kessa, as always. She let Kessa win twice and then, in the third pass, forgot herself, or chose not to remember, and turned the reversed saber under Kessa's guard with a motion that nobody in the room had taught her and nobody in the room could have, and touched Kessa very lightly on the hip and stepped back and looked, briefly, at the floor.

Drallig saw it. Anakin watched Drallig see it. The battlemaster's face did not change, except that something went into it and stayed, the way a figure goes into a ledger.

"Enough," Drallig said. "Rack them."

They racked them. They went out in the order they had come in, Tam first, loud again the moment he reached the corridor, Wix humming, Ilyo rubbing her wrist. The Tano child went last and did not look at him and he did not look at her, and the thin thread between them, the one they had made on the nineteenth night and kept faint enough that nobody would ever feel it who was not inside it, carried for a moment something that was not a word. He let it go by.

Drallig came off the wall.

He was a square, grey man with a swordsman's stillness and a face that gave nothing to anyone, and he had been the second person to die under Vader's blade in the Temple and the only one who had made him work for it. He stood now by the rack and counted the hilts with his eyes, twenty, as he did after every class, and then looked at the Padawan beside him who had put them there.

"You watch their feet," he said.

"Yes, Master."

"Most Padawans who assist in this hall watch the blade. The blade is what they were praised for." He paused. "You correct the feet, and the feet are what will keep them alive. I do not know where you learned to do that. I did not teach it to you. I taught it to your master, and I am not certain he took it in."

Anakin said nothing. There was nothing safe to say.

"I looked at a cut on Oba Diah," Drallig said, "and I told Master Windu that I did not know anyone alive who could have made it. That was true." He looked at the rack. "I will tell you something else that is true, which I have not told Master Windu, because he did not ask. In thirty years I have had perhaps four assistants in this hall who could teach. It is not the same skill as the other. It is nearly the opposite, and I have come to think that is because a man who can do the first thing has very little reason to learn patience." He turned to go. "Whoever you are, Padawan, you have had a reason. Same time in three days."

He went out. Anakin stood by the rack with the morning on the mat and the smell of twenty children's sweat going out of the air, and for a while was only there, which was new.

* * *

He wrote the letter at the common-room table before the midday meal, because the courier went at the second hour and Obi-Wan was paying for it out of a stipend that did not have much left in it after the Council's penalty.

He wrote it by hand. He had always written them by hand; Obi-Wan had said at the beginning that a thing written by hand could be read only by someone who cared enough to decipher it. The hand that held the stylus was fifteen and still unsure of its own size. The letters came out large and a little uneven, a boy's letters, and he let them.

*Mother,*

*We are back. The journey was fine and Obi-Wan's arm is out of the sling, though he still holds it as if it were in one when he thinks nobody is looking, and I have told him so.*

*I am teaching the little ones again. Hawkbat Clan, the same twenty. There is a boy who talks through everything and a girl who hums, except she is a boy, it is hard to tell with Ithorians, and a girl called Kessa who got hit on the hand today and did not cry, and I thought of you, because you would not have cried either, and then I thought you would have told me not to say so in a letter.*

*I am well. I am sleeping. That is true. I know you looked at me in the kitchen as if it was not true and I am telling you it is.*

*I have not got the hydrospanner back. I am not going to ask for it. You said I would want it and so I would come, and that is right, and I would rather it stayed where it is until I do. Tell Owen the compressor is fine and the fault was where I said it was, and that if the dome alarm goes before dawn it is the far north row and not the house, and not to get up for it, and to get up for it anyway.*

*The initials are mine. I scratched them with a file when I was eleven. The A is bad.*

*I will write again in ten days. Obi-Wan says to send his regards and to say that the tea was very good, and he means it, I have seen him with bad tea.*

*Ani.*

He read it through once. Every line in it was true that could be true. The ones that could not be were not in it, and the space where they would have gone was the shape of a man of forty-five sitting in a kitchen on Tatooine with his mother's hand on his wrist and the thing she had seen in his face, which she had not asked about and he could not have answered. The letter went around that space the way the vaporator rows went around the rock on the north line. It was a boy's letter. He folded it and put it in the courier sleeve and sealed it with his thumb, and did not read it again, because he knew that if he did he would see the space and not the letter.

* * *

Obi-Wan gave him the list after supper.

They had eaten in the quarters, which they did more often now, the two small rooms and the common room between them that had been home for four years and were, this month, something more exact than home. Obi-Wan had made tea. The sling was off and the left shoulder still did not lift properly and he had stopped pretending it did, in here, and had let Anakin take the pot from him without a word about it. The lamp was on. The city went on outside the window with its lights and its traffic lanes stacked up into the dark, and the Senate dome was out there somewhere to the north with its own light on, and neither of them looked at it.

Obi-Wan put the cup down and went into his room and came out with a notebook.

It was flimsi. A plain cheap Temple-issue notebook of the kind the Archives gave out by the box for taking notes in a reading room, grey cover, the corners gone soft. He had seen it before, on the third night after Oba Diah when the three of them had laid their lists out one beside another and nobody had read anyone else's, and face down on the shelf by Obi-Wan's bed every morning since, with a cup on it. He had never opened it.

Obi-Wan put it on the table between the cups and sat down and did not push it across.

"I began it on the third night," he said. "After I woke. You know that. I did not know what else to do with the nights. I wrote down everyone I could remember and where, and when I remembered another one I wrote that one down, and when I was told one by someone, later, in the years after, I wrote that down too, with the date I was told. There are forty-one. I have read it at night, only at night, and never in the day, for four years." He stopped. He had a way of stopping before the thing he meant to say, as if to give it room. "I kept it so I would not forget what we are preventing."

Anakin pulled it across and opened it.

The handwriting was Obi-Wan's and it was four years old. That was the first thing. The hand on the first page was the hand of a man who had been awake for three days in a body he did not recognise, pressing too hard, the letters leaning, a tremor in the downstrokes that Obi-Wan would have been ashamed of in a report. By the third page it had steadied. By the tenth it was the hand Anakin knew, neat, upright, a little impatient at the ends of words, the hand that wrote on the backs of mission orders and on the margins of his Padawan's astrogation homework. The list was in no order that he could see. It was in the order of remembering.

*Cin Drallig. The Temple.*

That was the first line. He had known it would be and it still went in under the ribs like a parry that was not there.

*Jurokk. The Temple, the gate.*

*Depa Billaba. Kaller. Her Padawan survived.*

*Plo Koon. Over Cato Neimoidia. His own men.*

*Ki-Adi-Mundi. Mygeeto.*

*Aayla Secura. Felucia.*

*Luminara Unduli. Taken alive; Kashyyyk. I was told the rest later and do not know if it is true.*

They went on. Some had places and some did not. Some had a date beside them in a different ink, the date Obi-Wan had learned of it, nineteen years before the Death Star or ten or two, a date that was now meaningless and that he had written anyway because he was a man who put dates on things. Some had a word after them, a single word, *clones* or *Temple* or *unknown*, and one had *me*, and he read that one twice before he understood it was Obi-Wan's note against his own name on the page of a man whose death he had been told of and could not have prevented and had counted himself responsible for anyway.

He went through to the end. Forty-one. He went through again, more slowly, because he was looking for something, and did not find it.

"I'm not in it," he said.

"No."

"Why not?"

Obi-Wan did not look at the notebook. He looked at the window. "I tried," he said. "Every night for the first month. I would get to the end and sit with the stylus and I could not find the page it belonged on. There was a page for the ones who died and a page I did not keep for the one who did it and I could not put you on either. So I did not put you anywhere." A pause. "I am aware that this is not the same as leaving you out."

Anakin closed the notebook and then opened it again at the last written page, where there was most of a side of flimsi still blank under the forty-first name, and held out his hand.

Obi-Wan gave him the stylus.

"These are the ones you don't have," he said. "The ones after. You wouldn't have had them. You were on the ridge." He bent over the page and his hand, writing, was not the hand that had written the letter at midday. It was smaller and quicker and it did not make a boy's letters. "Jocasta Nu. Coruscant, outside the Temple. She came back for a holocron and he found her." He wrote it. "Kirak Infil'a. Al'doleem. The first. He beat the thing that came for him nearly to death, and then it came back." He wrote it. "A Padawan on the Archive stairs. The night itself. I don't know her name. I never did. She had a blue blade and she stood on the fourth step so that she would have the height." He wrote *Padawan, Archive stairs, the night* and under it, without a name, drew a short line where the name would be. "Mon Cala. Four. Cabarria, one; I never learned it either. Chandrila, two, together. A Knight on Dathomir who had gone to hide among the Nightsisters, and the Nightsisters. Vuvur."

He wrote until the page was full. He did not count them. Obi-Wan did not ask him to. When he put the stylus down his hand was steady and his face was not, and he let it not be, in here, where the only person who could see it had seen the other thing.

"There are more," he said. "There were more than I ever knew. The Inquisitors took their own and did not always report. I have only the ones I saw." He turned the notebook round so that it faced Obi-Wan and pushed it back across the table. "Those are mine."

Obi-Wan looked at the page for a long time. He did not touch it. Then he said, "There should be three," and Anakin, who had been thinking it and had not wanted to be the one to say it, reached inward along the thin thread, the one the length of the Temple, and tugged.

* * *

She came in twenty minutes, by the service corridor, at an hour when a youngling should have been asleep in the Hawkbat dormitory; a Temple Guard who saw a child in a sand tunic in that corridor would assume an errand for a Master, because what else would she be doing. She knocked twice and once. Obi-Wan opened the door and she came past him into the lamplight and looked at the table and understood it before anyone spoke.

She was ten. He never stopped seeing it and never stopped being unable to see past it: she came to his shoulder, and he was not tall; her lekku were barely past her collar; she had to climb up onto the chair, not sit on it, and she did, and set her grey flimsi on the table beside the notebook without being asked, and the green hilt knocked against the chair-leg as she settled, and she put her hand over it the way a child puts a hand over a thing that she has been told not to bring and has brought.

"All three," she said.

"All three," Obi-Wan said.

She pulled the notebook over and read it, as he had, straight through. She read faster. He watched her get to Plo Koon and stop, and go on. He watched her get to *Depa Billaba. Kaller. Her Padawan survived* and put her finger on the line and hold it there, and not say Caleb's name, and not say the other one either, and take her finger away. She read his page. She did not say anything about his page.

"Mine are mostly not Jedi," she said.

"Then write them."

She took the stylus. Her hand was the smallest of the three and the most certain, because she had had seven years to learn this body and had learned it the way she learned everything, completely. She wrote in Basic, not Togruti, because the list was to be read.

*Jesse. Mandalore. He was chipped, and he tried to kill me, and he died in the crash. I put his helmet on a pole with the others.*

*The Venator. All of them. The 332nd. I buried as many as I could find. I did not count. I should have.*

*Maul's list is on my flimsi and I am not copying it in, because they are his.*

She stopped. She looked at Obi-Wan's forty-one and at the one line among them she had held her finger on, and wrote nothing beside it, because it was already written. Then she bent over the page again and wrote, in letters that pressed harder than the rest:

*Rex. Alive. He got out with me. He is on Kamino now and he is six years old and he is on no list that anyone has made and I am putting him on this one.*

"This is a list of the dead," Anakin said. He said it gently. He was not arguing.

"Then it needs one who isn't," she said, "or it's only a list." She put the stylus down. "He's the reason I know the ones above him. Jesse would have shot me if Rex hadn't got between us first. I'm not writing Jesse without writing Rex. You can take it out when I'm gone."

"Nobody is taking anything out," Obi-Wan said.

They copied it in. It took the better part of an hour. Anakin's datapad, the one with the cipher on it, keyed to a serial number from a machine that did not yet exist. He opened a new file beside the cipher and did not encode it, because this one was going to be read by three people who already knew everything in it, and he keyed in Obi-Wan's forty-one from the flimsi while Obi-Wan read them out, and then his own page, and then Ahsoka's, and then she gave him the grey flimsi and he keyed in her eight Togruti lines beside a column where she wrote what each one was in Basic, and she did it without hesitating, which told him she had decided in the corridor. Then Obi-Wan held out his hand and Anakin, after a moment, opened the cipher and gave him the key, and Obi-Wan read *Things that are different* in silence, forty-some entries now, the last of which was in a different hand and said *I do not know which is worse*, and did not ask whose hand, and copied it in.

Three lists. One datapad. The flimsi notebook lay beside it with the stylus on top, and when the copying was done Obi-Wan turned it face down, out of four years' habit, and then after a moment turned it face up again and left it.

"Will you tell Yoda?" Anakin said.

Obi-Wan looked at the notebook.

"When he can hear it," he said.

Ahsoka climbed down off the chair. She put her hand on the datapad once, flat, the way she had put it on the hilt, and took it away. She did not look at Anakin and he did not look at her and the thread between them said what it said. At the door she turned and said to Obi-Wan, in the careful child's voice she used in corridors, "Thank you, Master Kenobi," for the Guard who might be listening, and then in her own voice, very low, "He'd have put himself on it. Rex. If he'd made one. That's why I did," and went.

Obi-Wan sat a moment looking at the door, and then he reached into his sleeve and laid something beside the notebook that he had evidently been carrying all evening. It was a letter, flimsi folded in three and gone soft at the creases, the seal never broken, and the years were in it the way they were in the notebook's corners. On the outside, in a hand Anakin knew and had not seen in a very long time, it said *Padawan Skywalker, care of Master Kenobi.* "I found it in my own files the year I came," Obi-Wan said, in the tone he kept for the condition of a hyperdrive. "The man I was before had put it away unopened, and I have not known what to do with it since, which is not a thing I admit often." Anakin turned it over once, with the seal under his thumb, and thought of a woman saying *I never heard back*, and put it inside his tunic unopened.

* * *

The lamp had been on a long time. The tea was cold. Obi-Wan did not get up to make more, which was how Anakin knew that he had understood there was a thing still on the table that was not the notebook.

He had been sitting with it for seven months, and for twenty-three years before that in a body that could not sit, and he had once thought that if he ever had a chance to say it he would say it somewhere with the sky in it. He had not thought it would be a common room with two cold cups and a flimsi notebook. He found that this was better. Nothing in the room could burn.

"You said you loved me," he said. "On the bank."

Obi-Wan did not move. The shoulder that did not lift stayed where it was.

"I did."

Anakin looked at him. He was fifteen and it was in his face and his hands and the way his knees did not reach the floor properly from the chair, and under it he was the other thing, and he let both of them be there, because there was nobody in the room who did not know. His voice when it came was not a boy's and not the one he used for the Chancellor. It was the one from the bank, the one that had said the other thing, which he had never taken back because there had not been a mouth to take it back with.

"Say it where I can hear it."

Obi-Wan put his good hand flat on the table, beside the notebook, not on it.

He had never been a man who said things twice. He had said them once, exactly, and let them stand, and had spent twenty years on a ridge learning what it cost to have said one of them to a man who was on fire and could not have heard it over the sound of himself. Anakin had heard it. He had always heard it. That was the thing Obi-Wan did not know and was not going to be told tonight, because tonight was not for what Anakin had heard.

"You were my brother, Anakin," Obi-Wan said. "I loved you."

The city went on outside the window.

He did not say it the way it had been said on the bank. There was no lava in it and no turning away. He said it looking at the boy across the table, and he said it in the tense it had been said in, because it was the thing that had been asked for and he was a precise man, and then, after a moment in which neither of them breathed, he said, more quietly, in a voice he had heard once, in another life, "I have not stopped," and took his hand off the table.

Anakin did not answer. There was no line for it. He sat in the lamplight with the air going in and out of him on its own, which still startled him, and let it.

After a while Obi-Wan reached over and turned the lamp down, not off, and neither of them moved to go to bed, and the notebook lay face up between the cups with the stylus across it, and the datapad beside it dark, and the Temple around them full of children asleep.


## Chapter 34: What the Dead Remember

Ahsoka Tano sat on the eastern parapet with her boots hanging over four hundred storeys of nothing and the convor an arm's length to her left, and watched the sky decide what colour it was going to be.

It had not decided. Over the Senate dome it was the grey of old flimsi, and over the Works, where the light came from, it was going the yellow-brown it went every morning before the lanes filled, and between the two the air held still the way it did only at this hour, when the Temple was asleep under her and the city not yet awake. She had the roof to herself. She had had it to herself most mornings for seven years, which was a long time to keep a secret from a building, and the building had never once given her away.

The convor had not moved since she climbed up. It sat on the stone with its feathers tucked and its round yellow eyes half shut, looking at nothing, or at the same nothing she was looking at. She did not reach for it. She had learned on Shili, at three, in a body that could barely carry her to the edge of the village, that it did not like to be reached for, and that if she sat still enough for long enough it would come within reach on its own, and that this was the whole of what it offered and had to be enough.

It was enough. She was ten. She had been ten before and she had been thirty-three, and this morning she was both at once with no seam between them, which had happened only twice in seven years and both times on a roof. She could feel the one inside the other: the small strong body with its montrals barely past her brow and its lekku not yet to her collarbones, that had to look up at every adult in the Temple and most of the children; and inside it the woman who had stood in a temple with the floor going out from under her and a black mask looking down, and had said the thing she had said. The body was cold. She had not brought a cloak. The woman did not mind the cold, because the last time she had been warm it had meant fire.

The saber lay across her knees, unlit. It was a child's first saber, the hilt a little long for her fingers because Huyang built for the hand she would have and not the one she had, and she had wound the grip with cord from the *Crucible*'s stores to make it hold. Inside it the crystal was green. She had known it would be green before she went into the cave; she had stood in the dark on Ilum with her hand out and felt it answer and had not been surprised, and when it came up out of the ice into her palm it had been the colour of the convor's wing and she had closed her hand on it and said nothing. She had said nothing since. The others knew it was green because they had eyes. They did not know she had wanted it to be, that she had gone into the cave half afraid it would come up blue, that when it did not she had sat down on the ice with her forehead on her knees until Kessa Ruun found her and thought she was crying from the cold. It was a small thing. It was hers. There were not many of those, and somewhere in the fourth year she had decided to keep one.

She had decided other things since. Most of them on this roof, most of them before the light. She had decided, the night she felt him arrive like a struck bell, that she would find out, and she had. She had decided on Ilum, with his hand on the back of her neck and both of them shaking, that she would not cut the thread. She had decided at the table in Kenobi's quarters, with the three of them bent over a datapad and forty-one names and then more, that she would be the one who knew where the chips were, because the other two had never had a hand inside a clone's skull and she had. And she had decided, somewhere between then and now, what she was going to be this time, which was not what she had been. Not the one who left. Not the one who came back too late with two white blades and a plan. She would be the one who was there. In the room. Early. Small enough to be overlooked and old enough to use it. If that meant four more years in a sand-coloured tunic letting Tam Dorreth win at sabers, she would let him win. She had let worse things happen for worse reasons.

Rex was six. She thought it the way she thought it every morning, as a fact to be checked and put back. Six years old on a world of rain, in a body that looked twelve, in a line of boys with his face, with a thing behind his eye that had not been asked for and would be used. She had carried him out of a crashed Venator and buried the others under their own helmets and she had been thirty-three then, and she was ten, and he was six, and there was time. That was the whole of what the morning offered. There was time.

Plo would be awake by now. He rose before the clan did and sat in the small room off the Hawkbat dormitory with his mask off and the air of Dorin hissing into it, and if she came to the door he would put the mask on before he answered, so that she would not have to see him breathe. He had known for seven years that she was more than she showed and had never once asked her to show it. He would have to be told. Not today. Soon. She owed him that, and she owed him the thing she had said to him in the sling-weeks, that one day the Council would have to trust a child, and he had said he would remember she said it. She intended that he should.

Barriss Offee was twelve, a clan above, and walked the Temple corridors as if they had been built to her measure. Ahsoka had decided to be kind to her, plainly and early, before there was anything to be kind about. And Caleb Dume was seven and had taken to following her at a distance of exactly three paces, as if by accident; she let him, and did not turn round.

The light came over the Works and found the dome and ran along it, and the convor opened its eyes.

She did not look at it. She looked at the hangar mouth, far down on the eastern face, where the courier shuttle would be out on the apron by now with a boy under it who was not a boy, and she thought the name she had for him and did not say it, not even here, not even to a bird. She had said it last on a floor that was falling. She would say it again when it was earned.

The convor turned its head, the whole of it, the way they did, and looked at her with both eyes. Then it settled its feathers and closed them again, within reach, and she let it be there, and did not reach.

* * *

Obi-Wan Kenobi drank his tea standing, which he did not usually do, at the window of the common room, which he did, and watched the morning take the city one tower at a time.

The cup was the chipped one. He had two and this was the one he preferred, for reasons he would not have been able to give the Council. The tea was the Alderaanian that Dex kept under the counter and overcharged him for. His left arm ached where the sling had been, in the dull, honest way of a thing healing properly, and he had stopped noticing it except at this hour, when he noticed everything. The Senate dome was gold. The Works beyond it were brown. Four hundred storeys down, the lanes were beginning to fill with the first of the day's traffic, the long slow freight barges that ran before the speeders, and he watched one of them go by at the level of the Temple's knees and thought, as he thought every morning and would not have admitted to, that it was a strange thing to have seen all this burn and then to see it, every morning, not burning.

The message had come in overnight on the Senate-liaison channel, which meant the Council's clerks had already read it, which meant it had been written to be read. It was from the office of the Senator for Naboo. It announced, in the Senator's own hand and the Senate's own tense, the formation of a select committee to take evidence on labour practices in the Outer Rim territories, with particular reference to contract indenture, the conduct of licensed syndicates, and the recent detention of a Republic survey team on Oba Diah; it noted that the Jedi Order had been party to the recovery of that team; and it asked, in the courteous way that left no room to decline without saying so, whether the Order might see fit to provide an adviser to sit with the committee during its evidence sessions. Then it said, below the seal, in a different voice: *I hope Padawan Skywalker is fully recovered. I have had no reply to my earlier enquiry and I do not take silence as an answer. P. A.*

He had read it three times. The third time he had smiled, which he had not meant to.

He put the cup down on the table, beside the datapad, and took up his own and began to answer. He answered as her channel, because that was what he was and because the clerks would read this one too. The Order was honoured to be asked. The request would be laid before the Council at its next sitting, and he was confident of its reception; the Council had, he wrote, an interest in the conduct of licensed syndicates that was more than academic, and he thought she would understand him. If an adviser were appointed he would see to it that the appointment did not depend on the convenience of any one office, which she would also understand. He did not say he would sit with the committee himself. He did not know whether he would be allowed to, and he had learned in four years to promise only what he could do alone.

Then he looked at the postscript for a while and wrote, under his own name and not the channel's: *The Padawan is in excellent health and has no account to share, which I take to be the ordinary condition of a boy of fifteen and not a comment on his correspondents. He is, this morning, inside a courier shuttle. I am assured this is a repair.* And after a moment, because she had been twenty once and he had not known her then, and because the thing he knew about her end was not hers to carry yet: *You were right to ask twice. Most people do not.*

He read it over. It was dry and it was careful and it said nothing that could be quoted in a committee or a Chancellor's office, and under it, where only she would look, it was warm. He sent it.

He had lodged a petition with the Council before the tea, asking that the file on Master Sifo-Dyas be reopened as Qui-Gon's friend and not as the Order's problem, and over the last of it he thought the Senator's committee a fair answer to Verrane's tariff.

Anakin's datapad lay by the cup where the boy had left it last night, dark, the stylus across the flimsi notebook beside it, both face up. Inside the datapad was one file. He had watched it made. Forty-one names in his own hand and then more in two others, the three lists become one thing in plain Basic, no cipher, because the boy had said that if it was going to be found it should at least be read, and the girl had said nothing and added a name and then underlined it, alive. He held the key to the cipher. He did not need it. He did not open the file. He had read those names every night for four years, face down by day, and last night he had read them once more in company and found that in company they were lighter, not because there were fewer but because there were more hands. He would not read them this morning. This morning he would let the datapad be dark and the notebook be face up and the names be what they were, which was a list of work to be done, and not what they had been, which was a confession.

He drank the last of the tea cold.

He had tried again, in the night, when the boy was finally asleep in the next room and the bond had gone the thin even grey that meant no dreams. He had sat on the floor with his back to the wall and his hands open on his knees and reached, the way he had been taught in a desert by a voice that had not come until the third year, and he had reached further than he had in a long time, and there had been nothing. No voice. No sense of a presence withheld. Only the Temple's own quiet and the sound of a boy breathing through a wall. He had sat with it a while. Four years ago it had been the worst of all the silences; worse than the Council's, worse than the Chancellor's. This morning, standing at the window with the city not burning, he found that he did not mind it. If Qui-Gon was out there he would come when he came. If he was not, then the thing that had sent three of them back had done it without him, and that was its business. Obi-Wan had spent twenty years waiting to be told what to do by someone wiser. He had been told, eventually, and he had done it, and he had watched what it cost. He thought he might manage without, for a while. He had a Padawan and a committee and a girl on a roof and forty-one names of his own that two others had added to, and that was enough to be going on with.

The Chancellor's next audience was in four days. He had counted it the way he counted the tenth days, from the one before. He would walk the boy as far as the Senate and wait in Monument Plaza, and when the boy came out he would look at him once, as he always did, and the boy would say *fine*, as he always did, and they would walk back across the plaza together and say nothing, and it would have been one more audience that he had not managed to end and had not left the boy alone for. Two hundred and some, now, one every tenth day for six years. One more. He would be there for the one more. It was not a plan. It was a habit, and he had learned on a ridge above a farm that a habit kept long enough became the thing itself.

He rinsed the cup and set it upside down to dry, and left the datapad where it was.

* * *

Anakin Skywalker had both hands inside the compensator housing of a courier shuttle up to the wrists, and the housing was cold, and his hands were not.

That was the thing he noticed. He noticed it last. He had noticed the breath first, the night he woke, and the cold second, and the water in the refresher third, and then, over seven months, in no order, everything else: the way a stair felt in the knees, the way a tunic hem caught on a belt, the way sweat ran, the way a flush came up the neck when Drallig looked at him, the way sleep took him under like a hand and let him go in the morning without asking leave. He had been startled by all of it. That had worn down, lately, to something quieter. He was a boy of fifteen lying on his back on a hangar floor with the shuttle's belly a hand's width above his face and grease on his forearms and the smell of coolant in his nose and his hands, his two hands, working a bolt loose by feel in the dark, and nothing hurt. Nothing in him hurt. That was all. He noted it the way he would have noted a reading on a gauge: nominal. Then he went back to the bolt.

The compensator had been drifting for a month. Knight Thane had logged it as a drift and done nothing about it because the courier had nowhere to be, and then the courier had had somewhere to be, which was a Council run out to Bothawui in ten days, and a drifting compensator on the long Outer Rim legs would put the pilot's teeth through his tongue. So Anakin had asked. He had asked Thane at the hangar office with his braid behind his ear and his hands behind his back, the way a Padawan asked, and Thane had looked at him for a long moment with the face of a man who had written *repulsor test, on the log already* in his own hand over a theft he had not seen and could not now unsee, and had said, "Bay four. Don't touch anything else." Thane was in the office now with the door open and his back to the bay and had not turned round once. He was pretending, with the whole of his body, not to know anything. He was good at it. Anakin had known men who were worse.

"Number three," he said, to the dark under the shuttle.

A hand came in under the lip of the hull and put a number three driver into his palm. It was a small hand. It was already holding the number three before he spoke, because its owner had been watching him strip the housing for ten minutes and knew what came next, and was ten years old and could not possibly know what came next, and had arranged to be in bay four at this hour by telling the Hawkbat Clan master she was observing a mechanics practical for an assignment, which was true in the way that most things she said were true. She sat on an upturned crate by the landing strut in a sand-coloured tunic with her boots not reaching the floor and her lekku not reaching her collarbones and the youngling's blankness on her face that she could put on like a hood, and she handed him tools, and pretended not to be anyone.

"You're going to strip it," she said.

"I'm not going to strip it."

"You're turning it the wrong way."

"It's a left-hand thread."

"On a Kuati housing? They don't put left-hand threads on a Kuati housing. Anybody knows that."

"Anybody," Anakin said, and turned it the other way, and it came.

There was a pause under the shuttle, in which a boy's hands worked and a girl's heels knocked gently against a crate.

"Who taught you about compensators?" he said.

"Nobody. I'm ten."

"Right."

"I read a lot."

"Right." He got the housing plate off and slid it out and she took it from him without being asked and set it on the floor on a rag so the mating face stayed clean, which was what you did, and nobody taught a youngling that either. "Hand me the—"

The coupling tool was already in his palm.

He lay still a moment with it in his hand. Not because anything was wrong. Because it was so exactly the thing it was. There had been a war, once, with a fleet in it and a woman beside him with two blades who knew what he wanted before he did and told him when he was turning it the wrong way, and the war was not here and the fleet was not built and the woman was sitting on a crate with her feet not touching the floor, and the thing between them was here anyway, in a hangar, at the size of two children, with no name on it. It did not need one. He put the coupling tool to the drift bearing and felt it seat, and heard her hear it seat, and neither of them said anything, which was also a thing they had used to do.

Out at the hangar mouth, on the lip where the apron met the air, something green sat in the new sun. He saw it over the edge of the hull when he turned his head. It did not fly. It did not look at him. He looked at it for the length of a breath and went back to the bearing, and when he looked again it was still there, and he let it be.

What he was for, now. He had asked it on the first night with his face in a pillow and had not got an answer and had stopped asking around the third month, because the asking was itself the old thing, the wanting to be told. He was lying under a shuttle. He was fixing it so a letter could go to a woman on a farm who kept his hydrospanner on a hook so that he would come back for it. He was keeping, in the next week and the one after, a boy in the Hawkbat dormitory from waking with a stair and a gate and a circle of chairs in his head. He was walking into a red room in four days with a surface on his mind and a wall behind it, and walking out again. He was a Padawan with a braid and a master with a notebook and a youngling on a crate who was going to be in the room early this time, and none of that was a reason, and he had stopped needing one. He did not believe he deserved to be here. He had stopped needing to believe that too. He was here. Padmé's old letter lay against his chest inside his tunic, still unopened, and did not ask to be. He turned the bearing a quarter and it bit.

"Try it," he said.

She slid off the crate and went up the ramp with the boots of a ten-year-old girl loud on the metal and he heard the compensator spin up, whine, catch, and hold.

"Nominal," she said from inside, in a child's voice, and then, lower, where only the hull could hear it, "told you."

He lay on the floor with grease to the elbows and his two hands open on his chest, and breathed, and did not resolve anything, and was here.

* * *

In the Works, in a room with no window, Dooku stood before the boy's face and waited to be told what it meant.

It was very large. The projector threw it a metre high against the bare ferrocrete, blue-white, and the Mandalorian's helmet had caught it at the one instant that mattered: the gantry, the grey light, a Padawan of fifteen with his lightsaber lowered in his right hand and his left hand open and raised, and his face. It was the face that had stopped the recording. His master had run it through once at speed and once slow and then had stopped it here, on the boy, and had been looking at it for some time without speaking, and Dooku, who had seen that face across a reception hall and felt its hatred find him like a hand on the back of the neck, had been looking at the floor.

The face was not a boy's. That was all that could be said of it, and his master had not said it. It was not angry. There was no strain in it, no effort, none of the bared-teeth concentration of a child doing a thing beyond him. It was calm. It was the calm of a man who had done this before and had done it so often that there was no longer anything in it to feel. The eyes were a Padawan's eyes, blue, young; the thing behind them was not. Dooku had killed men. He had stood in rooms with his master while his master did things that he did not watch. He had not seen that calm on any face but one, and that one was standing beside him in the dark with its hands folded in its sleeves, and did not have it now.

What it had, Dooku could not read. He had never been able to. He had thought, six years ago, that it was contempt, and had been glad to serve it because contempt was at least a kind of attention. He knew better now. His master was looking at the boy's open hand the way a man looks at his own signature on a document he does not remember signing.

The room was cold. The Works were always cold; the heat had gone out of them a century ago with the foundries, and what was left was ferrocrete and rust and the long sound of wind in ducts. The old man did not feel it. Dooku did, in the joints of his hands, and did not move.

"He showed me something at supper," Sidious said, to the image and not to Dooku. "A second of it. He let me put my hand on a thing that had held me by the throat, and then he closed the door on my fingers, and smiled." He said it the way another man might speak of a wine. Dooku, who had touched the boy once and had not slept well since, kept his face still and his hands still and did not ask what it had been.

"Find out what happened to Maul," Sidious said.

Dooku did not look up at once. He let the words stand in the cold a moment, because he was a precise man and the words were not precise, and he had learned that when his master was imprecise it was never an accident. Maul. The Zabrak. The thing his master had made before him and lost on Naboo, cut in half by a Padawan of twenty-five on a reactor catwalk, six years ago; a name his master had not spoken in his hearing in six years of service, and had spoken now, in front of this face, in the tone of a man asking for a ledger to be checked.

He was aware of being tested and did not know on what. He answered as he always answered, in the voice he used for the Senate and for his master, which were the same voice.

"Maul is dead, Master."

Sidious did not turn his head. He went on looking at the boy's hand, open, with the grey light of Oba Diah in the palm of it and three droids gone to pieces at the edge of the frame. His own hands stayed in his sleeves. When he spoke it was gently, almost kindly, the voice of a man who has waited a long time for something to be interesting and has at last been obliged.

"Then find out what else is."
